additionally, we find it helpful to introduce several notation frequently used in the studies of markov chains. instead of operating under the usual  $\ell_2$  norm, the stationary distribution  $\pi$  equips us with a new set of norms. specifically, for a strictly positive probability vector  $\pi = [\pi_i]_{1\leq i\leq n}$ , any vector  $\pmb {x} = [x_i]_{1\leq i\leq n}$  and any matrix  $\mathbf{a}$ , it is useful to introduce the vector norm  $\| \pmb {x}\|_{\pi}\coloneqq \sqrt{\sum_{i}\pi_{i}x_{i}^{2}}$  and the corresponding matrix norm  $\| a\|_{\pi}\coloneqq \sup_{\| x\|_{\pi} = 1}\| ax\|_{\pi}$ .

# 2.5.2 perturbation of the leading eigenvector

now we are ready to present the perturbation bound for the leading left eigenvector of a probability transition matrix, a result originally developed in chen et al. (2019b).

theorem 2.10. consider the settings in section 2.5.1. suppose that  $p^{\star}$  represents a reversible markov chain, whose stationary distribution vector  $\pi^{\star}$  is strictly positive. assume that

$$
\left\| \boldsymbol {e} \right\| _ {\pi^ {\star}} <   1 - \max  \left\{\lambda_ {2} \left(\boldsymbol {p} ^ {\star}\right), - \lambda_ {n} \left(\boldsymbol {p} ^ {\star}\right) \right\}. \tag {2.32}
$$

then one has

$$
\left\| \boldsymbol {\pi} - \boldsymbol {\pi} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {\pi^ {\star}} \leq \frac {\left\| \boldsymbol {\pi} ^ {\star^ {\top}} \boldsymbol {e} \right\| _ {\pi^ {\star}}}{1 - \max  \left\{\lambda_ {2} (\boldsymbol {p} ^ {\star}), - \lambda_ {n} (\boldsymbol {p} ^ {\star}) \right\} - \left\| \boldsymbol {e} \right\| _ {\pi^ {\star}}}.
$$

the similarity between theorem 2.10 and corollary 2.8 is noteworthy. indeed, recalling that the largest eigenvalue of the probability transition matrix  $p^{\star}$  is precisely 1, one might view  $1 - \max \left\{\lambda_{2}(p^{\star}), - \lambda_{n}(p^{\star})\right\}$  as the gap between the first and the second largest eigenvalues of  $p^{\star}$  (in magnitude), akin to the eigengap  $|\lambda_r^\star | - |\lambda_{r + 1}^\star |$  in corollary 2.8 (with  $r = 1$ ). in words, theorem 2.10 guarantees that as long as the size of the perturbation matrix  $e$  is not too large, the perturbation of the leading left eigenvector—or equivalently, the perturbation of the stationary distribution of the associated markov chain—is proportional to the size of the noise when projected onto the direction  $\pi^{\star}$ , as measured by  $\| \pmb{\pi}^{\star \top}\pmb {e}\|_{\pi^{\star}}$ . as we shall demonstrate in section 3.6, this perturbation theory delivers powerful techniques for analyzing the ranking problem described previously in chapter 1.

remark 2.3. sensitivity and perturbation analyses for the steady-state distributions of markov chains have been studied in the literature; see, e.g., mitrophanov (2005), liu (2012), jiang et al. (2017), and rudolf and schweizer (2018) and the references therein.

# 2.5.3 proof of theorem 2.10

since  $\pi^{\star}$  and  $\pi$  denote respectively the leading left eigenvectors of  $p^{\star}$  and  $p$ , namely,

$$
\boldsymbol {\pi} ^ {\star^ {\top}} \boldsymbol {p} ^ {\star} = \boldsymbol {\pi} ^ {\star^ {\top}}, \qquad \text {a n d} \qquad \boldsymbol {\pi} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {p} = \boldsymbol {\pi} ^ {\top},
$$

the perturbation  $\pi -\pi^{\star}$  admits the following decomposition

$$
\begin{array}{l} \boldsymbol {\pi} ^ {\top} - \boldsymbol {\pi} ^ {\star \top} = \boldsymbol {\pi} ^ {\top} p - \boldsymbol {\pi} ^ {\star \top} p ^ {\star} = (\boldsymbol {\pi} - \boldsymbol {\pi} ^ {\star}) ^ {\top} p + \boldsymbol {\pi} ^ {\star \top} (p - p ^ {\star}) \\ = \left(\boldsymbol {\pi} - \boldsymbol {\pi} ^ {\star}\right) ^ {\top} \left(\boldsymbol {p} - \boldsymbol {p} ^ {\star}\right) + \left(\boldsymbol {\pi} - \boldsymbol {\pi} ^ {\star}\right) ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {p} ^ {\star} + \boldsymbol {\pi} ^ {\star \top} \left(\boldsymbol {p} - \boldsymbol {p} ^ {\star}\right) \\ = (\boldsymbol {\pi} - \boldsymbol {\pi} ^ {\star}) ^ {\top} (\boldsymbol {p} - \boldsymbol {p} ^ {\star}) + (\boldsymbol {\pi} - \boldsymbol {\pi} ^ {\star}) ^ {\top} (\boldsymbol {p} ^ {\star} - \boldsymbol {1} \boldsymbol {\pi} ^ {\star \top}) + \boldsymbol {\pi} ^ {\star \top} (\boldsymbol {p} - \boldsymbol {p} ^ {\star}). \\ \end{array}
$$

here, the last relation hinges upon the fact that  $\pmb{\pi}$  and  $\pmb{\pi}^{\star}$  are probability vectors, and hence  $(\pmb{\pi} - \pmb{\pi}^{\star})^{\top}\mathbf{1} = 1 - 1 = 0$ . apply the triangle inequality with respect to the norm  $\| \cdot \|_{\pmb{\pi}^{\star}}$  to obtain

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \boldsymbol {\pi} - \boldsymbol {\pi} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {\pi^ {\star}} \leq \left\| \left(\boldsymbol {\pi} - \boldsymbol {\pi} ^ {\star}\right) ^ {\top} \left(\boldsymbol {p} - \boldsymbol {p} ^ {\star}\right) \right\| _ {\pi^ {\star}} + \left\| \left(\boldsymbol {\pi} - \boldsymbol {\pi} ^ {\star}\right) ^ {\top} \left(\boldsymbol {p} ^ {\star} - 1 \boldsymbol {\pi} ^ {\star^ {\top}}\right) \right\| _ {\pi^ {\star}} \\ + \left\| \pi^ {\star \top} \left(\boldsymbol {p} - \boldsymbol {p} ^ {\star}\right) \right\| _ {\pi^ {\star}} \\ \leq \left(\| p - p ^ {\star} \| _ {\pi^ {\star}} + \left\| p ^ {\star} - 1 \pi^ {\star \top} \right\| _ {\pi^ {\star}}\right) \| \pi - \pi^ {\star} \| _ {\pi^ {\star}} \\ + \left\| \pi^ {\star^ {\top}} \left(\boldsymbol {p} - \boldsymbol {p} ^ {\star}\right) \right\| _ {\pi^ {\star}}, \\ \end{array}
$$

where the last line relies on the definition of the matrix norm  $\| \cdot \|_{\pi^{\star}}$ . rearranging terms, we are left with

$$
\left\| \boldsymbol {\pi} - \boldsymbol {\pi} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {\pi^ {\star}} \leq \frac {\left\| \boldsymbol {\pi} ^ {\star \top} \left(\boldsymbol {p} - \boldsymbol {p} ^ {\star}\right) \right\| _ {\pi^ {\star}}}{1 - \left\| \boldsymbol {p} - \boldsymbol {p} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {\pi^ {\star}} - \left\| \boldsymbol {p} ^ {\star} - \boldsymbol {1} \boldsymbol {\pi} ^ {\star \top} \right\| _ {\pi^ {\star}}},
$$

with the proviso that  $\| p - p^{\star}\|_{\pi^{\star}} + \| p^{\star} - 1\pi^{\star^{\top}}\|_{\pi^{\star}} < 1$ . the proof would then be completed as long as one could justify that

$$
\left\| \boldsymbol {p} ^ {\star} - 1 \boldsymbol {\pi} ^ {\star^ {\top}} \right\| _ {\boldsymbol {\pi} ^ {\star}} = \max  \left\{\lambda_ {2} \left(\boldsymbol {p} ^ {\star}\right), - \lambda_ {n} \left(\boldsymbol {p} ^ {\star}\right) \right\}. \tag {2.33}
$$

proof of the identity (2.33). let  $\pmb{\pi}^{\star} = [\pi_i^\star]_{1 \leq i \leq n}$ , and define a diagonal matrix  $\mathbf{ii}^{\star} = \mathrm{diag}([\pi_1^{\star}, \dots, \pi_n^{\star}]) \in \mathbb{r}^{n \times n}$ . from the definition of the norm  $\| \cdot \|_{\pmb{\pi}^{\star}}$  (both the matrix version and the vector version), it is easily seen that for any matrix  $\mathbf{a}$

$$
\begin{array}{l} \| \boldsymbol {a} \| _ {\pi^ {\star}} = \sup  _ {\boldsymbol {x} \neq \mathbf {0}} \frac {\| \boldsymbol {a} \boldsymbol {x} \| _ {\pi^ {\star}}}{\| \boldsymbol {x} \| _ {\pi^ {\star}}} = \sup  _ {\boldsymbol {x} \neq \mathbf {0}} \frac {\| \left(\pi^ {\star}\right) ^ {1 / 2} \boldsymbol {a} \left(\pi^ {\star}\right) ^ {- 1 / 2} \left(\pi^ {\star}\right) ^ {1 / 2} \boldsymbol {x} \| _ {2}}{\| \left(\pi^ {\star}\right) ^ {1 / 2} \boldsymbol {x} \| _ {2}} \\ = \sup  _ {\boldsymbol {v} \neq \boldsymbol {0}} \frac {\left\| \left(\boldsymbol {\pi} ^ {\star}\right) ^ {1 / 2} \boldsymbol {a} \left(\boldsymbol {\pi} ^ {\star}\right) ^ {- 1 / 2} \boldsymbol {v} \right\| _ {2}}{\left\| \boldsymbol {v} \right\| _ {2}} = \left\| \left(\boldsymbol {\pi} ^ {\star}\right) ^ {1 / 2} \boldsymbol {a} \left(\boldsymbol {\pi} ^ {\star}\right) ^ {- 1 / 2} \right\|, \tag {2.34} \\ \end{array}
$$

with  $\| \cdot \|$  the usual spectral norm, where the last line replaces  $(\mathbf{\pi}^{\star})^{1 / 2}\pmb {x}$  with  $\pmb{v}$ . consequently, we obtain

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| p ^ {\star} - 1 \pi^ {\star \top} \right\| _ {\pi^ {\star}} = \left\| \left(\pi^ {\star}\right) ^ {1 / 2} \left(p ^ {\star} - 1 \pi^ {\star \top}\right) \left(\pi^ {\star}\right) ^ {- 1 / 2} \right\| \\ = \left\| s ^ {\star} - \pi_ {1 / 2} ^ {\star} \left(\pi_ {1 / 2} ^ {\star}\right) ^ {\top} \right\|, \\ \end{array}
$$

where we define  $s^{\star} \coloneqq (\pi^{\star})^{1/2} p^{\star}(\pi^{\star})^{-1/2}$  and  $\pi_{1/2}^{\star} \coloneqq \left[\sqrt{\pi_i^{\star}}\right]_{1 \leq i \leq n}$ . several basic properties regarding  $s^{\star}$  are in order; see brémaud (2013, chapter 6.2).

(a) since  $p^{\star}$  represents a reversible markov chain with stationary distribution  $\pi^{\star}$ , the matrix  $s^{\star}$  is symmetric, whose eigenvalues are real-valued. this can be verified by the detailed balance equations (2.31).  
(b) given that  $s^{\star}$  is obtained via a similarity transformation of  $p^{\star}$ , we see that  $s^{\star}$  and  $p^{\star}$  share the same set of eigenvalues. this can easily be verified from the definition of eigenvectors:

$$
\boldsymbol {s} ^ {\star} \boldsymbol {\xi} = \lambda \boldsymbol {\xi} \quad \longleftrightarrow \quad \boldsymbol {p} ^ {\star} \left(\pi^ {\star}\right) ^ {- 1 / 2} \boldsymbol {\xi} = \lambda \left(\pi^ {\star}\right) ^ {- 1 / 2} \boldsymbol {\xi}.
$$

(c) in particular,  $\lambda_1(s^\star) = \lambda_1(p^\star) = 1$ , and  $\pi_{1/2}^\star$  is precisely the eigenvector of  $s^\star$  associated with  $\lambda_1(s^\star) = 1$ . thus, from the eigendecomposition of the symmetric matrix  $s^\star$ , it is easy to see that the eigenvalues of  $s^\star - \pi_{1/2}^\star(\pi_{1/2}^\star)^\top$  are  $0, \lambda_2(s^\star), \dots, \lambda_n(s^\star)$ .

taking the preceding facts collectively, we reach

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \boldsymbol {s} ^ {\star} - \boldsymbol {\pi} _ {1 / 2} ^ {\star} \left(\boldsymbol {\pi} _ {1 / 2} ^ {\star}\right) ^ {\top} \right\| \stackrel {{\text {(i)}}} {{=}} \max  \left\{\left| \lambda_ {2} (\boldsymbol {s} ^ {\star}) \right|, \left| \lambda_ {n} (\boldsymbol {s} ^ {\star}) \right| \right\} \\ = \max  \left\{\lambda_ {2} (\boldsymbol {s} ^ {\star}), - \lambda_ {n} (\boldsymbol {s} ^ {\star}) \right\} \stackrel {{\text {(i i)}}} {{=}} \max  \left\{\lambda_ {2} (\boldsymbol {p} ^ {\star}), - \lambda_ {n} (\boldsymbol {p} ^ {\star}) \right\}. \\ \end{array}
$$

here, (i) relies on property (c), while (ii) follows from property (b). this concludes the proof.

# 2.6 appendix: proofs of auxiliary lemmas in section 2.2

# 2.6.1 proof of lemma 2.4

given that singular values are unitarily invariant, it suffices to look at the singular values of the following matrix

$$
\left[ \begin{array}{c} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\top} \\ \boldsymbol {u} _ {\perp} ^ {\top} \end{array} \right] (\boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\top} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star^ {\top}}) [ \boldsymbol {u} _ {\perp} ^ {\star}, \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} ] = \left[ \begin{array}{c c} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {u} _ {\perp} ^ {\star} & \mathbf {0} \\ \mathbf {0} & - \boldsymbol {u} _ {\perp} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \end{array} \right]. \tag {2.35}
$$

consequently, the singular values of  $uu^{\top} - u^{\star}u^{\star \top}$  are composed of those of  $u^{\top}u_{\perp}^{\star}$  and those of  $u_{\perp}^{\top}u^{\star}$  combined. it then boils down to characterizing the spectrum of  $u^{\top}u_{\perp}^{\star}$  and  $u_{\perp}^{\top}u^{\star}$ .

to pin down the singular values of  $\pmb{u}^{\top}\pmb{u}_{\perp}^{\star}$ , we first turn attention to the eigenvalues of  $\pmb{u}^{\top}\pmb{u}_{\perp}^{\star}\pmb{u}_{\perp}^{\star \top}\pmb{u}$ . assuming that the svd of  $\pmb{u}^{\top}\pmb{u}^{\star}$  is given by  $\pmb{x}\pmb{\sigma}\pmb{y}^{\top}$  (where  $\pmb{x}$  and  $\pmb{y}$  are  $r\times r$  orthonormal matrices, and  $\pmb{\sigma}$  is diagonal), we can derive

$$
\begin{array}{l} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {u} _ {\perp} ^ {\star} \boldsymbol {u} _ {\perp} ^ {\star \top} \boldsymbol {u} = \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\top} \left(\boldsymbol {i} _ {n} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star \top}\right) \boldsymbol {u} = \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {u} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star \top} \boldsymbol {u} \\ = \boldsymbol {i} _ {r} - \boldsymbol {x} \boldsymbol {\sigma} ^ {2} \boldsymbol {x} ^ {\top} = \boldsymbol {x} \left(\boldsymbol {i} _ {r} - \cos^ {2} \boldsymbol {\theta}\right) \boldsymbol {x} ^ {\top} \\ = \boldsymbol {x} (\sin^ {2} \theta) \boldsymbol {x} ^ {\top}. \tag {2.36} \\ \end{array}
$$

here, the penultimate identity follows from our construction (cf. (2.5)), where we define  $\cos \theta \coloneqq \mathrm{diag}([\cos \theta_1, \dots, \cos \theta_r])$ . therefore, for any  $1 \leq i \leq r$ , the  $i$ -th largest singular value of  $\pmb{u}^{\top}\pmb{u}_{\perp}^{\star}$  obeys

$$
\sigma_ {i} \left(\boldsymbol {u} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {u} _ {\perp} ^ {\star}\right) = \sqrt {\lambda_ {i} \left(\boldsymbol {u} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {u} _ {\perp} ^ {\star} \boldsymbol {u} _ {\perp} ^ {\star \top} \boldsymbol {u}\right)} = \sin \theta_ {r + 1 - i},
$$

which results from the ordering in (2.6). this means that, if  $r \leq n - r$ , then the singular values of  $u^{\top}u_{\perp}^{\star}$  are precisely given by  $\{\sin \theta_i\}_{1 \leq i \leq r}$ . repeating this argument reveals that the singular values of  $u_{\perp}^{\top}u^{\star}$  are also  $\{\sin \theta_i\}_{1 \leq i \leq r}$  if  $r \leq n - r$ .

combining the above observations thus completes the proof.

# 2.6.2 proof of lemma 2.5

a closer inspection of the proof of lemma 2.4 (in particular, (2.36) and the orthonormality of  $\mathbf{x}$ ) reveals that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {u} _ {\perp} ^ {\star} \right\| = \sqrt {\left\| \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {u} _ {\perp} ^ {\star} \boldsymbol {u} _ {\perp} ^ {\star \top} \boldsymbol {u} \right\|} = \sqrt {\left\| \boldsymbol {x} (\sin^ {2} \theta) \boldsymbol {x} ^ {\top} \right\|} = \| \sin \theta \|, \\ \left\| \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {u} _ {\perp} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {\mathrm {f}} = \sqrt {\operatorname {t r} (\boldsymbol {u} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {u} _ {\perp} ^ {\star} \boldsymbol {u} _ {\perp} ^ {\star^ {\top}} \boldsymbol {u})} = \sqrt {\operatorname {t r} (\boldsymbol {x} (\sin^ {2} \theta) \boldsymbol {x} ^ {\top})} \\ = \sqrt {\operatorname {t r} (\boldsymbol {x} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {x} \sin^ {2} \boldsymbol {\theta})} = \sqrt {\operatorname {t r} (\sin^ {2} \boldsymbol {\theta})} = \| \sin \boldsymbol {\theta} \| _ {\mathrm {f}}, \\ \end{array}
$$

where we have used the basic property  $\operatorname{tr}(\mathbf{a}\mathbf{b}) = \operatorname{tr}(\mathbf{b}\mathbf{a})$ . similarly,

$$
\left\| \boldsymbol {u} _ {\perp} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \right\| = \| \sin \boldsymbol {\theta} \|, \quad \text {a n d} \quad \left\| \boldsymbol {u} _ {\perp} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {\mathrm {f}} = \| \sin \boldsymbol {\theta} \| _ {\mathrm {f}}.
$$

note that the above identities hold for all  $1 \leq r \leq n$ . in addition, the relation (2.35) tells us that

$$
\left\| \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\top} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star^ {\top}} \right\| = \max  \left\{\left\| \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {u} _ {\perp} ^ {\star} \right\|, \left\| \boldsymbol {u} _ {\perp} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \right\| \right\}; \tag {2.37a}
$$

$$
\left\| \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\top} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star \top} \right\| _ {\mathrm {f}} = \left(\left\| \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {u} _ {\perp} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {\mathrm {f}} ^ {2} + \left\| \boldsymbol {u} _ {\perp} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {\mathrm {f}} ^ {2}\right) ^ {1 / 2}. \tag {2.37b}
$$

putting the above identities together immediately establishes the advertised results.

# 2.6.3 proof of lemma 2.6

as before, suppose that the svd of  $\pmb{u}^{\top}\pmb{u}^{\star}$  is given by  $\pmb{x}\pmb{\sigma}\pmb{y}^{\top}$ , where  $\pmb{x}$  and  $\pmb{y}$  are  $r\times r$  orthonormal matrices whose columns contain the left singular vectors and the right singular vectors of  $\pmb{u}^{\top}\pmb{u}^{\star}$ , respectively, and  $\pmb{\sigma} \in \mathbb{r}^{r\times r} = \cos \theta$  is a diagonal matrix whose diagonal entries correspond to the singular values of  $\pmb{u}^{\top}\pmb{u}^{\star}$ .

the spectral norm upper bound. we first observe that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {x} \boldsymbol {y} ^ {\top} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \right\| ^ {2} = \left\| \left(\boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {x} \boldsymbol {y} ^ {\top} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star}\right) ^ {\top} \left(\boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {x} \boldsymbol {y} ^ {\top} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star}\right) \right\| \\ = \left\| 2 i _ {r} - y x ^ {\top} u ^ {\top} u ^ {\star} - u ^ {\star \top} u x y ^ {\top} \right\| \\ = \left\| 2 \boldsymbol {i} _ {r} - \boldsymbol {y} \boldsymbol {x} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {x} \boldsymbol {\sigma} \boldsymbol {y} ^ {\top} - \boldsymbol {y} \boldsymbol {\sigma} \boldsymbol {x} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {x} \boldsymbol {y} ^ {\top} \right\| \\ = 2 \| \boldsymbol {y} (\boldsymbol {i} _ {r} - \boldsymbol {\sigma}) \boldsymbol {y} ^ {\top} \| = 2 \| \boldsymbol {i} _ {r} - \boldsymbol {\sigma} \|. \tag {2.38} \\ \end{array}
$$

here, the penultimate line relies on the singular value decomposition  $\pmb{u}^{\top}\pmb{u}^{\star} = \pmb{x}\pmb{\sigma}\pmb{y}^{\top}$ , while the two identities in the last line result from the orthonormality of  $\pmb{x}$  and  $\pmb{y}$ , respectively. in addition, note that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \boldsymbol {i} _ {r} - \boldsymbol {\sigma} \right\| = \left\| \boldsymbol {i} _ {r} - \cos \boldsymbol {\theta} \right\| \leq \left\| \boldsymbol {i} _ {r} - \cos^ {2} \boldsymbol {\theta} \right\| \\ = \| \sin^ {2} \theta \| = \| \sin \theta \| ^ {2}. \\ \end{array}
$$

this taken together with (2.38) leads to

$$
\min _ {\boldsymbol {r} \in \mathcal {o} ^ {r \times r}} \| \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {r} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \| \leq \| \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {x} \boldsymbol {y} ^ {\top} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \| \leq \sqrt {2} \| \sin \theta \|,
$$

where the first inequality holds since  $\mathbf{x}$  and  $\mathbf{y}$  are both orthonormal matrices and hence  $\mathbf{x}\mathbf{y}^{\top}$  is also orthonormal.

the spectral norm lower bound. on the other hand, we make the observation that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \min  _ {\boldsymbol {r} \in \mathcal {o} ^ {r \times r}} \| \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {r} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \| ^ {2} = \min  _ {\boldsymbol {r} \in \mathcal {o} ^ {r \times r}} \| (\boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {r} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star}) ^ {\top} (\boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {r} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star}) \| \\ = \min  _ {\boldsymbol {r} \in \mathcal {o} ^ {r \times r}} \| \boldsymbol {r} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {r} + \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star \top} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} - \boldsymbol {r} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star \top} \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {r} \| \\ = \min  _ {\boldsymbol {r} \in \mathcal {o} ^ {r \times r}} \| 2 \boldsymbol {i} _ {r} - \boldsymbol {r} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {x} \boldsymbol {\sigma} \boldsymbol {y} ^ {\top} - \boldsymbol {y} \boldsymbol {\sigma} \boldsymbol {x} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {r} \|, \tag {2.39} \\ \end{array}
$$

where the last relation holds since  $x \sigma y^{\top}$  is the svd of  $u^{\top} u^{\star}$ . continue the derivation to obtain

$$
\begin{array}{l} (2. 3 9) \stackrel {\text {(i)}} {=} \min  _ {\boldsymbol {q} \in \mathcal {o} ^ {r \times r}} \| 2 \boldsymbol {i} _ {r} - \boldsymbol {q} \boldsymbol {\sigma} \boldsymbol {y} ^ {\top} - \boldsymbol {y} \boldsymbol {\sigma} \boldsymbol {q} ^ {\top} \| \\ \stackrel {\mathrm {(i i)}} {=} \min  _ {\boldsymbol {q} \in \mathcal {o} ^ {r \times r}} \| 2 \boldsymbol {q} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {q} - \boldsymbol {q} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {q} \boldsymbol {\sigma} \boldsymbol {y} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {q} - \boldsymbol {q} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {y} \boldsymbol {\sigma} \boldsymbol {q} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {q} \| \\ = \min  _ {q \in \mathcal {o} ^ {r \times r}} \| 2 \boldsymbol {i} _ {r} - \boldsymbol {\sigma} \boldsymbol {y} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {q} - \boldsymbol {q} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {y} \boldsymbol {\sigma} \| \\ \stackrel {(\mathrm {i i i})} {=} \min  _ {\boldsymbol {o} \in \mathcal {o} ^ {r \times r}} \| 2 \boldsymbol {i} _ {r} - \boldsymbol {\sigma} \boldsymbol {o} - \boldsymbol {o} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\sigma} \|. \tag {2.40} \\ \end{array}
$$

here, (i) follows by setting  $\pmb{q} = \pmb{r}^{\top}\pmb{x}$  (since both  $\pmb{x}$  and  $\pmb{r}$  are orthonormal matrices), (ii) results from the unitary invariance of the spectral norm, whereas (iii) holds by setting  $\pmb{o} = \pmb{y}^{\top}\pmb{q}$ . moreover,

recognizing that  $\| \sigma o\| \leq \| \sigma \| \cdot \| o\| \leq 1$  (and hence  $2i_{r} - \sigma o - o^{\top}\sigma \succeq 0$ ), one can obtain

$$
\begin{array}{l} \min _ {\boldsymbol {o} \in \mathcal {o} ^ {r \times r}} \left\| 2 \boldsymbol {i} _ {r} - \boldsymbol {\sigma} \boldsymbol {o} - \boldsymbol {o} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\sigma} \right\| = \min _ {\boldsymbol {o} \in \mathcal {o} ^ {r \times r}} \lambda_ {\max } \left(2 \boldsymbol {i} _ {r} - \boldsymbol {\sigma} \boldsymbol {o} - \boldsymbol {o} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\sigma}\right) \\ = \min  _ {\boldsymbol {o} \in \mathcal {o} ^ {r \times r}} \max  _ {\boldsymbol {u}: \| \boldsymbol {u} \| _ {2} = 1} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\top} (2 \boldsymbol {i} _ {r} - \boldsymbol {\sigma} \boldsymbol {o} - \boldsymbol {o} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\sigma}) \boldsymbol {u} \\ = \min  _ {\boldsymbol {o} \in \mathcal {o} ^ {r \times r}} \max  _ {\boldsymbol {u}: \| \boldsymbol {u} \| _ {2} = 1} (2 - 2 \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\sigma} \boldsymbol {o} \boldsymbol {u}) \\ \geq \min  _ {\boldsymbol {o} \in \mathcal {o} ^ {r \times r}} \left(2 - 2 \boldsymbol {e} _ {r} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\sigma} \boldsymbol {o} \boldsymbol {e} _ {r}\right) \\ = 2 - 2 \cos \theta_ {r} \max  _ {\boldsymbol {o} \in \mathcal {o} ^ {r \times r}} \boldsymbol {e} _ {r} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {o} \boldsymbol {e} _ {r} \\ \geq 2 - 2 \cos \theta_ {r} = 4 \sin^ {2} \left(\theta_ {r} / 2\right). \tag {2.41} \\ \end{array}
$$

here, the inequality follows by taking  $\mathbf{u}$  to be  $\pmb{e}_r$  (recall that by construction,  $\sigma_r = \cos \theta_r \geq 0$  is the smallest singular value of  $\pmb{\sigma}$ ), and the penultimate line holds by combining the facts  $|\pmb{e}_r^\top \pmb{o} \pmb{e}_r| \leq \| \pmb{o} \| = 1$  and  $\pmb{e}_r^\top \pmb{e}_r = 1$ . putting (2.41) and (2.40) together yields

$$
\begin{array}{l} \min _ {\pmb {r} \in \mathcal {o} ^ {r \times r}} \left\| \pmb {u} \pmb {r} - \pmb {u} ^ {\star} \right\| \geq \sqrt {4 \sin^ {2} (\theta_ {r} / 2)} = 2 \sin (\theta_ {r} / 2) = \| 2 \sin (\theta / 2) \| \\ \geq \left\| \sin \theta \right\|, \\ \end{array}
$$

where we again use the inequality  $2\sin (\theta /2)\geq \sin \theta$  for all  $\theta \in [0,\pi /2]$

finally, invoking the relation  $\| \sin \theta \| = \| uu^{\top} - u^{\star}u^{\star \top}\|$  (see lemma 2.5) establishes the claimed spectral norm bounds.

the frobenius norm upper bound. regarding the frobenius norm upper bound, one sees that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {x} \boldsymbol {y} ^ {\top} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {\mathrm {f}} ^ {2} = \left\| \boldsymbol {u} \right\| _ {\mathrm {f}} ^ {2} + \left\| \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {\mathrm {f}} ^ {2} - 2 \operatorname {t r} \left(\boldsymbol {y} \boldsymbol {x} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star}\right) \\ \stackrel {\mathrm {(i)}} {=} r + r - 2 \operatorname {t r} \left(\boldsymbol {y} \boldsymbol {x} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {x} \boldsymbol {\sigma} \boldsymbol {y} ^ {\top}\right) \stackrel {\mathrm {(i i)}} {=} 2 r - 2 \operatorname {t r} (\boldsymbol {\sigma}), \tag {2.42} \\ \end{array}
$$

where (i) holds since  $\pmb{u}$  and  $\pmb{u}^{\star}$  are both  $n\times r$  matrices with orthonormal columns, and (ii) follows since  $\pmb{x}^{\top}\pmb {x} = \pmb{y}^{\top}\pmb {y} = \pmb{i}$  (and hence  $\operatorname {tr}(\pmb {y}\pmb {x}^{\top}\pmb {x}\pmb {\sigma}\pmb {y}^{\top}) = \operatorname {tr}(\pmb {y}^{\top}\pmb {y}\pmb {x}^{\top}\pmb {x}\pmb {\sigma}) = \operatorname {tr}(\pmb {\sigma}))$ ). furthermore,

$$
\begin{array}{l} 2 r - 2 \operatorname {t r} (\boldsymbol {\sigma}) \stackrel {\text {(i i i)}} {=} 2 \sum_ {i} (1 - \cos \theta_ {i}) \leq 2 \sum_ {i} (1 - \cos^ {2} \theta_ {i}) \\ = 2 \left\| \sin \boldsymbol {\theta} \right\| _ {\mathrm {f}} ^ {2} = \left\| \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\top} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star^ {\top}} \right\| _ {\mathrm {f}} ^ {2}, \\ \end{array}
$$

where (iii) holds by construction (cf. (2.5)), and the last identity results from lemma 2.5. this taken collectively with (2.42) reveals that

$$
\min _ {\boldsymbol {r} \in \mathcal {o} ^ {r \times r}} \| \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {r} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \| _ {\mathrm {f}} ^ {2} \leq \| \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {x} \boldsymbol {y} ^ {\top} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \| _ {\mathrm {f}} ^ {2} \leq \| \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\top} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star^ {\top}} \| _ {\mathrm {f}} ^ {2},
$$

where the first inequality holds since  $\mathbf{x}$  and  $\mathbf{y}$  are both orthonormal matrices and hence  $\mathbf{x}\mathbf{y}^{\top}$  is also orthonormal.

# 2.7. notes

the frobenius norm lower bound. with regards to the frobenius norm lower bound, it is seen that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \min  _ {\boldsymbol {r} \in \mathcal {o} ^ {r \times r}} \| \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {r} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \| _ {\mathrm {f}} ^ {2} = \min  _ {\boldsymbol {r} \in \mathcal {o} ^ {r \times r}} \left\{\| \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {r} \| _ {\mathrm {f}} ^ {2} + \| \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \| _ {\mathrm {f}} ^ {2} - 2 \langle \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {r}, \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \rangle \right\} \\ \stackrel {\mathrm {(i)}} {=} 2 \min  _ {\boldsymbol {r} \in \mathcal {o} ^ {r \times r}} \left\{r - \langle \boldsymbol {r}, \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \rangle \right\} \\ \stackrel {(\mathrm {i i})} {=} 2 \min  _ {\boldsymbol {r} \in \mathcal {o} ^ {r \times r}} \left\{r - \left\langle \boldsymbol {r}, \boldsymbol {x} \boldsymbol {\sigma} \boldsymbol {y} ^ {\top} \right\rangle \right\}, \tag {2.43} \\ \end{array}
$$

where (i) holds since  $\| u\|_{\mathrm{f}} = \| u^{\star}\|_{\mathrm{f}} = \sqrt{r}$ , and (ii) relies on the svd  $x\sigma y^{\top}$  of  $u^{\top}u^{\star}$ . continue the derivation to obtain

$$
\begin{array}{l} (2. 4 3) \stackrel {\text {(i i i)}} {=} 2 \min  _ {q \in \mathcal {o} ^ {r \times r}} \left\{r - \langle q, \cos \theta \rangle \right\} \stackrel {\text {(i v)}} {\geq} 2 \min  _ {q \in \mathcal {o} ^ {r \times r}} \left\{r - \| q \| \| \cos \theta \| _ {*} \right\} \\ = 2 \left(r - \sum_ {i} \cos \theta_ {i}\right). \tag {2.44} \\ \end{array}
$$

here, (iii) sets  $\pmb{q} = \pmb{x}^{\top}\pmb{r}\pmb{y}$  and identifies  $\pmb{\sigma}$  as  $\cos \theta$ , (iv) comes from the elementary inequality  $\langle \pmb{a},\pmb{b}\rangle \leq \| \pmb{a}\| \| \pmb{b}\|_{*}$ , whereas the last line follows since  $\cos \theta_{i} \geq 0$ . additionally, it is easily seen that

$$
\begin{array}{l} (2. 4 4) = 2 \sum_ {i} (1 - \cos \theta_ {i}) = 4 \sum_ {i} \sin^ {2} (\theta_ {i} / 2) \\ \geq \sum_ {i} \sin^ {2} \theta_ {i} = \frac {1}{2} \| \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\top} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star \top} \| _ {\mathrm {f}} ^ {2}, \tag {2.45} \\ \end{array}
$$

where the penultimate relation follows from the elementary inequality  $2\sin(\theta/2) \geq \sin \theta$  (which holds for any  $0 \leq \theta \leq \pi/2$ ), and the last line invokes lemma 2.5. combining the inequalities (2.44) and (2.45), we establish the claimed lower bound.

# 2.7 notes

additional resources on matrix perturbation theory. matrix perturbation theory is a firmly established topic that has been extensively studied in the past several decades. two classic books that offer in-depth discussions of perturbation theory for eigenspaces and singular subspaces are stewart and sun (1990) and sun (1987). other valuable resources on this topic include bhatia (2013) and horn and johnson (2012). the exposition herein is largely influenced by the excellent lecture notes by montanari (2011) and hsu (2016). in addition, the book (kato, 2013) offers a more abstract treatment of perturbation theory from the viewpoint of linear operators. several variants of the sin theorem amenable to statistical analysis are available in the statistics literature as well (e.g., yu et al. (2015), vu and lei (2013), cai and zhang (2018), and zhang et al. (2021)).

extensions. we point out several well-known extensions of the theorems presented in this chapter. to begin with, the current exposition restricts attention to the real case for simplicity, while in fact all results herein generalize to the complex-valued case (stewart and sun, 1990). in addition, theorem 2.9 together with lemma 2.6 reveals the existence of two rotation matrices  $\mathbf{r}_u$  and  $\mathbf{r}_v$  obeying

$$
\max  \left\{\| \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {r} _ {u} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \| _ {\mathrm {f}}, \| \boldsymbol {v} \boldsymbol {r} _ {v} - \boldsymbol {v} ^ {\star} \| _ {\mathrm {f}} \right\} \leq \frac {\sqrt {2} \max  \left\{\| \boldsymbol {e} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \| _ {\mathrm {f}} , \| \boldsymbol {e} \boldsymbol {v} ^ {\star} \| _ {\mathrm {f}} \right\}}{\sigma_ {r} ^ {\star} - \sigma_ {r + 1} ^ {\star} - \| \boldsymbol {e} \|},
$$

but falls short of illuminating the connection between  $\mathbf{r}_u$  and  $\mathbf{r}_v$ . an extension derived in dopico (2000) establishes a similar perturbation bound even when  $\mathbf{r}_u$  and  $\mathbf{r}_v$  are taken to be the same rotation matrix.

# applications of  $\ell_{2}$  perturbation theory to data science

this chapter develops tailored spectral methods for several important applications arising in statistics, machine learning and signal processing. as it turns out, these methods are all variations of a common recipe: extracting the information of interest from the eigenspace (resp. singular spaces) and eigenvalues (resp. singular values) of a certain matrix  $m$  properly constructed from data. the inspiration stems from the observation that: the corresponding quantities of  $m^{\star} = \mathbb{e}[m]$  — when properly constructed and under appropriate statistical models—might faithfully reveal the information being sought after. the classical  $\ell_2$  perturbation theory introduced in chapter 2, when paired with modern probabilistic tools reviewed in section 3.1, uncovers appealing performance of spectral methods in numerous applications by controlling the size of the perturbation  $e\coloneqq m - m^{\star}$ . the vignettes in this chapter provide ample evidence regarding the benefits of harnessing the statistical nature of the acquired data.

# 3.1 preliminaries: matrix tail bounds

in order to invoke the  $\sin \theta$  theorems (theorems 2.7 and 2.9), an important ingredient lies in developing a tight upper bound on the spectral norm  $\| \pmb{e}\|$  of the perturbation matrix  $\pmb{e}$ . this is where statistical/probabilistic tools play a major role. rather than presenting an encyclopedia of probabilistic techniques (which can be gleaned from tropp (2015), vershynin (2017), boucheron et al. (2013), wainwright (2019), tropp (2011), raginsky and sason (2013), and howard et al. (2020)), this monograph singles out only two useful matrix concentration inequalities that suffice for the applications considered herein.

# the (truncated) matrix bernstein inequality

the first result is an extension of the celebrated matrix bernstein inequality (oliveira, 2009; tropp, 2012; hopkins et al., 2016). this is an elegant and convenient tail bound for the sum of independent random matrices, resulting in effective performance guarantees for a diverse array of statistical applications. we refer the interested reader to tropp (2015) for a highly accessible introduction of the classical matrix bernstein inequality, and hopkins et al. (2016, section a.2.2) for a proof of the

truncated variant stated in theorem 3.1.

theorem 3.1 (truncated matrix bernstein). let  $\{x_i\}_{1 \leq i \leq m}$  be a sequence of independent real random matrices with dimension  $n_1 \times n_2$ . suppose that for all  $1 \leq i \leq m$ ,

$$
\mathbb {p} \left\{\| x _ {i} - \mathbb {e} [ x _ {i} ] \| \geq l \right\} \leq q _ {0} \tag {3.1a}
$$

$$
\left\| \mathbb {e} \left[ \boldsymbol {x} _ {i} \right] - \mathbb {e} \left[ \boldsymbol {x} _ {i} \mathbb {1} \left\{\| \boldsymbol {x} _ {i} \| <   l \right\} \right] \right\| \leq q _ {1} \tag {3.1b}
$$

hold for some quantities  $0 \leq q_0 \leq 1$  and  $q_1 \geq 0$ . in addition, define the matrix variance statistic  $v$  as

$$
\begin{array}{l} v := \max \left\{\left\| \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {m} \mathbb {e} \big [ (\pmb {x} _ {i} - \mathbb {e} [ \pmb {x} _ {i} ]) (\pmb {x} _ {i} - \mathbb {e} [ \pmb {x} _ {i} ]) ^ {\top} \big ] \right\|, \right. \\ \left. \left\| \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {m} \mathbb {e} \left[ \left(\boldsymbol {x} _ {i} - \mathbb {e} \left[ \boldsymbol {x} _ {i} \right]\right) ^ {\top} \left(\boldsymbol {x} _ {i} - \mathbb {e} \left[ \boldsymbol {x} _ {i} \right]\right) \right] \right\| \right\}. \tag {3.2} \\ \end{array}
$$

then for all  $t \geq mq_1$ , one has

$$
\mathbb {p} \left(\left\| \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {m} (\pmb {x} _ {i} - \mathbb {e} [ \pmb {x} _ {i} ]) \right\| \geq t\right) \leq (n _ {1} + n _ {2}) \exp \left(\frac {- (t - m q _ {1}) ^ {2} / 2}{v + l (t - m q _ {1}) / 3}\right) + m q _ {0}.
$$

remark 3.1. note that when the  $x_{i}$ 's are i.i.d. zero-mean random matrices, the matrix variance statistic simplifies to

$$
v = m \max \left\{\| \mathbb {e} [ \boldsymbol {x} _ {i} \boldsymbol {x} _ {i} ^ {\top} ] \|, \| \mathbb {e} [ \boldsymbol {x} _ {i} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {x} _ {i} ] \| \right\}.
$$

to make it more user-friendly, we record a straightforward consequence of theorem 3.1 as follows.

corollary 3.2. suppose the assumptions of theorem 3.1 hold, and set  $n \coloneqq \max \{n_1, n_2\}$ . for any  $a \geq 2$ , with probability exceeding  $1 - 2n^{-a + 1} - mq_0$  one has

$$
\left\| \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {m} \left(\boldsymbol {x} _ {i} - \mathbb {e} \left[ \boldsymbol {x} _ {i} \right]\right) \right\| \leq \sqrt {2 a v \log n} + \frac {2 a}{3} l \log n + m q _ {1}. \tag {3.3}
$$

in order to make effective use of the above results (particularly when handling unbounded random matrices), it is advisable to take  $l$  as a high-probability bound on  $\| x_i - \mathbb{e}[x_i] \|$ . the rationale is simple: by properly truncating  $x_i$  based on the level  $l$ , we end up with a bounded sequence that is more convenient to work with while not deviating much from the original sequence. in particular, if all  $\| x_i - \mathbb{e}[x_i] \|$  are bounded by a deterministic quantity which is set to be  $l$ , then both  $q_0$  and  $q_1$  vanish, thus eliminating the need of enforcing truncation. in this case, corollary 3.2 simplifies to a user-friendly version of the standard matrix bernstein inequality, which we record below for ease of reference.

corollary 3.3 (matrix bernstein). let  $\{x_i\}_{1 \leq i \leq m}$  be a set of independent real random matrices with dimension  $n_1 \times n_2$ . suppose that

$$
\mathbb {e} \left[ \boldsymbol {x} _ {i} \right] = \mathbf {0}, \quad \text {a n d} \quad \| \boldsymbol {x} _ {i} \| \leq l, \quad \text {f o r a l l} i. \tag {3.4}
$$

set  $n \coloneqq \max \{n_1, n_2\}$ , and recall the definition of variance statistic in (3.2). for any  $a \geq 2$ , with probability exceeding  $1 - 2n^{-a + 1}$  one has

$$
\left\| \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {m} x _ {i} \right\| \leq \sqrt {2 a v \log n} + \frac {2 a}{3} l \log n. \tag {3.5}
$$

by virtue of the above inequalities, the key to bounding  $\| \sum_{i} x_{i} \|$  largely lies in controlling the following two crucial quantities:

$$
\sqrt {v \log n} \qquad \text {a n d} \qquad l \log n,
$$

where the former depends on the number  $m$  of random matrices involved.

# spectral norm of random matrices with independent entries

an important family of random matrices that merits special attention comprises the ones with independent random entries, that is, matrices of the form  $\mathbf{x} = [x_{i,j}]_{1\leq i,j\leq n}$  with independent  $x_{i,j}$ 's. while the spectral norm of such a matrix can also be analyzed via matrix bernstein (by treating  $\mathbf{x}$  as the sum of independent random matrices  $x_{i,j}\pmb {e}_i\pmb {e}_j^\top$ ), this approach is typically loose in terms of the logarithmic factor. motivated by the abundance of such random matrices in practice, we record below a strengthened non-asymptotic spectral norm bound, which is of significant utility and is tighter than what matrix bernstein has to offer for this case.

theorem 3.4. consider a symmetric random matrix  $\mathbf{x} = [x_{i,j}]_{1\leq i,j\leq n}$  in  $\mathbb{r}^{n\times n}$ , whose entries are independently generated and obey

$$
\mathbb {e} \left[ x _ {i, j} \right] = 0, \quad \text {a n d} \quad | x _ {i, j} | \leq b, \quad 1 \leq i, j \leq n. \tag {3.6}
$$

define

$$
\nu := \max  _ {i} \sum_ {j} \mathbb {e} \left[ x _ {i, j} ^ {2} \right]. \tag {3.7}
$$

then there exists some universal constant  $c > 0$  such that for any  $t \geq 0$ ,

$$
\mathbb {p} \left\{\| \boldsymbol {x} \| \geq 4 \sqrt {\nu} + t \right\} \leq n \exp \left(- \frac {t ^ {2}}{c b ^ {2}}\right). \tag {3.8}
$$

this result, which appeared in bandeira and van handel (2016, remark 3.13), can be established via tighter control of the expected spectral norm in conjunction with talagrand's concentration inequality. two remarks are in order.

- first, it is easy to see that the result extends to asymmetric matrices with independent entries, using the standard "dilation trick" (see, e.g., (tropp, 2015, section 2.1.17)). specifically, for an asymmetric random matrix  $\mathbf{x} \in \mathbb{r}^{n_1 \times n_2}$ , let us introduce the symmetric dilation  $s(\mathbf{x})$  of  $\mathbf{x}$ :

$$
\mathcal {s} (\boldsymbol {x}) := \left[ \begin{array}{c c} \boldsymbol {0} & \boldsymbol {x} \\ \boldsymbol {x} ^ {\top} & \boldsymbol {0} \end{array} \right] \in \mathbb {r} ^ {(n _ {1} + n _ {2}) \times (n _ {1} + n _ {2})},
$$

which enjoys the desired symmetry and can be analyzed directly using theorem 3.4. the resulting bound on  $\| s(\boldsymbol{x})\|$  can be translated back to  $\| \boldsymbol{x}\|$  via the elementary identity  $\| \boldsymbol{x}\| = \| \mathcal{s}(\boldsymbol{x})\|$ . for conciseness, we will occasionally apply theorem 3.4 directly to asymmetric matrices without invoking the dilation trick.

- as a useful corollary, if we know a priori that  $\mathbb{e}[x_{i,j}^2 ]\leq \sigma^2$  for all  $1\leq i,j\leq n$ , then theorem 3.4 implies that

$$
\left\| \boldsymbol {x} \right\| \leq 4 \sigma \sqrt {n} + \tilde {c} b \sqrt {\log n} \tag {3.9}
$$

with probability at least  $1 - n^{-8}$  for some constant  $\tilde{c} > 0$ . to see this, it suffices to set  $\tilde{c} = \sqrt{9c}$  and take  $t = b\sqrt{9c\log n}$  in (3.8).

remark 3.2. the inequality (3.9) continues to hold if we replace  $n^{-8}$  with  $n^{-\alpha}$  for any positive constant  $\alpha > 0$ . here and below, we often go with the artificial choice like  $n^{-8}$  since it is small enough for our purpose.

# 3.2 low-rank matrix denoising

to catch a glimpse of the effectiveness of the approach we have introduced, let us start by trying it out on a warm-up example: low-rank matrix denoising.

# 3.2.1 problem formulation and algorithm

consider an unknown rank- $r$  symmetric matrix  $m^{\star} \in \mathbb{r}^{n \times n}$  with eigendecomposition  $m^{\star} = u^{\star} \lambda^{\star} u^{\star \top}$ , where the columns of  $u^{\star} \in \mathbb{r}^{n \times r}$  are orthonormal, and  $\lambda^{\star} \in \mathbb{r}^{r \times r}$  is a diagonal matrix containing the nonzero eigenvalues  $\{\lambda_i^{\star}\}$  of  $m^{\star}$ . assume that  $|\lambda_1^{\star}| \geq |\lambda_2^{\star}| \geq \dots \geq |\lambda_r^{\star}| > 0$ . suppose that we observe a noisy copy

$$
\boldsymbol {m} = \boldsymbol {m} ^ {\star} + \boldsymbol {e},
$$

where  $\mathbf{e} = [e_{i,j}]_{1\leq i,j\leq n}$  is a symmetric noise matrix. it is assumed that the entries  $\{e_{i,j}\}_{i\geq j}$  are independently generated obeying

$$
e _ {i, j} \stackrel {\text {i . i . d .}} {\sim} \mathcal {n} (0, \sigma^ {2}), \quad i \geq j. \tag {3.10}
$$

the aim is to estimate the eigenspace  $\pmb{u}^{\star}$  from the data matrix  $\pmb{m}$ . despite its simplicity, this problem has been extensively studied in the literature (koltchinskii and xia, 2016; bao et al., 2021; ding, 2020; xia, 2021; li et al., 2021a). it also bears close relevance to the famous angular/phase synchronization problem (singer, 2011; bandeira et al., 2017).

in order to estimate the low-rank factors specified by  $\pmb{u}^{\star}$ , a natural scheme is to resort to the rank-  $r$  leading eigenspace of the data matrix  $\pmb{m}$ . more precisely, denote by  $\lambda_1, \dots, \lambda_n$  the eigenvalues of  $\pmb{m}$  sorted by their magnitudes, i.e.,

$$
\left| \lambda_ {1} \right| \geq \left| \lambda_ {2} \right| \geq \dots \geq \left| \lambda_ {n} \right|, \tag {3.11}
$$

and let  $\pmb{u}_1, \dots, \pmb{u}_n$  represent the associated eigenvectors. this spectral method returns  $\pmb{u} = [\pmb{u}_1, \dots, \pmb{u}_r] \in \mathbb{r}^{n \times r}$  as an estimate of  $\pmb{u}^{\star}$ .

# 3.2.2 performance guarantees

statistical accuracy of the spectral estimate. we now examine the accuracy of the above spectral estimate. towards this, a key step lies in bounding the spectral norm of the noise matrix  $\pmb{e}$ . we claim for the moment that (which will be established in section 3.2.3)

$$
\left\| \boldsymbol {e} \right\| \leq 5 \sigma \sqrt {n} \tag {3.12}
$$

with probability at least  $1 - o(n^{-8})$ . armed with this claim and the fact  $\lambda_{r + 1}^{\star} = 0$ , we are in a situation where it is quite easy to see how the davis-kahan theorem applies. according to corollary 2.8, with probability greater than  $1 - o(n^{-8})$  one has

$$
\operatorname {d i s t} \left(\boldsymbol {u}, \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star}\right) \leq \frac {2 \| \boldsymbol {e} \|}{\left| \lambda_ {r} ^ {\star} \right|} \leq \frac {1 0 \sigma \sqrt {n}}{\left| \lambda_ {r} ^ {\star} \right|}, \tag {3.13}
$$

provided that the noise variance is sufficiently small obeying  $\sigma \sqrt{n} \leq \frac{1 - 1 / \sqrt{2}}{5} |\lambda_r^\star|$  so that  $\| \pmb{e} \| \leq (1 - 1 / \sqrt{2}) |\lambda_r^\star|$ .

tightness and optimality. the tightness of the statistical guarantee (3.13) can be assessed when compared with the minimax lower bound. for instance, it is well-known in the literature (e.g., cheng et al. (2021, theorem 3)) that: even for the case with  $r = 1$ , one cannot hope to achieve  $\mathrm{dist}(\widehat{\pmb{u}},\pmb{u}^{\star}) = o(\sigma \sqrt{n} /|\lambda_r^{\star}|)$  in a minimax sense—regardless of the estimator  $\widehat{\pmb{u}}$  in use. consequently, the spectral method turns out to be orderwise statistically optimal for low-rank matrix denoising.

additional useful results: eigenvalue and matrix estimation. before concluding, we record several immediate consequences of the above analysis that will be useful later on. specifically, assuming that  $\sigma \sqrt{n} \leq \frac{1 - 1 / \sqrt{2}}{5} |\lambda_r^\star|$ , we see from weyl's inequality (cf. lemma 2.2) that

$$
\left| \lambda_ {i} \right| \leq \| \boldsymbol {e} \| \leq 5 \sigma \sqrt {n}, \quad \text {f o r a l l} i \geq r + 1 \tag {3.14}
$$

with probability  $1 - o(n^{-8})$ .

we further remark on the euclidean statistical accuracy when estimating the unknown matrix  $m^{\star}$  using  $\widehat{m} \coloneqq u\lambda u^{\top}$ , where  $\lambda \coloneqq \mathrm{diag}([\lambda_1,\dots ,\lambda_r])$ . it is seen from the triangle inequality that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {\lambda} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\top} - \boldsymbol {m} ^ {\star} \right\| \leq \left\| \boldsymbol {m} - \boldsymbol {m} ^ {\star} \right\| + \left\| \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {\lambda} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\top} - \boldsymbol {m} \right\| \\ = \| \boldsymbol {e} \| + | \lambda_ {r + 1} | \leq 2 \| \boldsymbol {e} \|, \tag {3.15} \\ \end{array}
$$

where the last inequality relies on (3.14). since the rank of  $u\lambda u^{\top} - m^{\star}$  is at most  $2r$ , with probability at least  $1 - o(n^{-8})$  one has

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {\lambda} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\top} - \boldsymbol {m} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {\mathrm {f}} \leq \sqrt {2 r} \left\| \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {\lambda} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\top} - \boldsymbol {m} ^ {\star} \right\| \leq 2 \sqrt {2 r} \| \boldsymbol {e} \| \\ \leq 1 0 \sigma \sqrt {2 n r}. \tag {3.16} \\ \end{array}
$$

# 3.2.3 proof of the inequality (3.12) on  $\| e\|$

we plan to employ theorem 3.4. given that gaussian entries are unbounded, we introduce a truncated copy  $\widetilde{\pmb{e}} = [\widetilde{e}_{i,j}]_{1\leq i,j\leq n}$  defined as follows

$$
\widetilde {e} _ {i, j} := e _ {i, j} \mathbb {1} \left\{\left| e _ {i, j} \right| \leq 5 \sigma \sqrt {\log n} \right\}, \quad 1 \leq i, j \leq n. \tag {3.17}
$$

two properties are in place.

- it is readily seen from the property of gaussian distributions that

$$
\mathbb {p} \left\{e _ {i, j} = \widetilde {e} _ {i, j} \right\} \geq 1 - n ^ {- 1 2}, \quad 1 \leq i, j \leq n,
$$

which combined with the union bound leads to

$$
\mathbb {p} \left\{\boldsymbol {e} = \widetilde {\boldsymbol {e}} \right\} \geq 1 - n ^ {- 1 0}. \tag {3.18}
$$

- given that  $b \coloneqq \max_{i,j} |\widetilde{e}_{i,j}| \leq 5\sigma \sqrt{\log n}$ , we can invoke theorem 3.4 (or more directly, (3.9)) to demonstrate that

$$
\| \tilde {\boldsymbol {e}} \| \leq 4 \sigma \sqrt {n} + o (b \log n) \leq 5 \sigma \sqrt {n}
$$

for sufficiently large  $n$ , with probability exceeding  $1 - o(n^{-8})$ . here, we implicitly use the fact that  $\mathbb{e}[\tilde{e}_{i,j}^2 ]\leq \mathbb{e}[e_{i,j}^2 ] = \sigma^2$ .

combining the above two observations implies that

$$
\left\| \boldsymbol {e} \right\| = \left\| \widetilde {\boldsymbol {e}} \right\| \leq 5 \sigma \sqrt {n}
$$

with probability exceeding  $1 - o(n^{-8})$ , as claimed.

# 3.3 principal component analysis and factor models

principal component analysis (pca) and factor models (jolliffe, 1986; lawley and maxwell, 1962; fan et al., 2020b)—which serve as an effective unsupervised learning tool for exploring and understanding data—arise frequently in data-intensive applications in economics, finance, psychology, signal processing, speech, neuroscience, traffic data analysis, among other things (stock and watson, 2002; mccrae and john, 1992; scharf, 1991; chen et al., 2015a; balzano et al., 2018; fan et al., 2021b). pca and factor models not only allow for dimensionality reduction, but also provide intermediate means for data visualization, noise removal, anomaly detection, and other downstream tasks. in this section, we investigate a simple, yet broadly applicable, factor model.

# 3.3.1 problem formulation and assumptions

dependence of high-dimensional measurements is a stylized feature in data science. to model the dependence among observed high-dimensional data, we assume that there are latent factors that drive the dependence, with a loading matrix that describes how each component depends on the latent factors and an idiosyncratic noise that captures the remaining part. to set the stage, imagine we have collected a set of  $n$  independent sample vectors  $\pmb{x}_i \in \mathbb{r}^p$ ,  $1 \leq i \leq n$  obeying

$$
\boldsymbol {x} _ {i} = \boldsymbol {l} ^ {\star} \boldsymbol {f} _ {i} + \boldsymbol {\eta} _ {i}, \quad 1 \leq i \leq n. \tag {3.19}
$$

here,  $\pmb{f}_i \in \mathbb{r}^r$  is a vector of latent factors,  $\pmb{l}^{\star} \in \mathbb{r}^{p \times r}$  represents a factor loading matrix that is not known a priori, whereas  $\pmb{\eta}_i \in \mathbb{r}^p$  stands for additive random noise or the idiosyncratic part that cannot be explained by the latent factor  $\pmb{f}_i$ . informally, the samples  $\{\pmb{x}_i\}$  are, in some sense, assumed to be approximately embedded in a low-dimensional subspace encoded by the loading matrix  $\pmb{l}^{\star}$ , which describes how each component of data  $\pmb{x}_i$  depends on the factor  $\pmb{f}_i$  and captures the inter-dependency across different variables. in the language of pca, the subspace spanned by  $\pmb{l}^{\star}$  specifies the  $r$  principal components underlying this sequence of data samples. a common goal thus amounts to estimating the subspace spanned by the loading matrix  $\pmb{l}^{\star}$  and the latent factors  $\{\pmb{f}_i\}$ . in the pca literature, the subspace represented by  $\pmb{l}^{\star}$  is commonly referred to as the principal subspace.

in this monograph, we concentrate on the following tractable statistical model for pedagogical reasons. see fan et al. (2020b, chapter 10) for more general settings (including, say, heavy-tailed distributions and non-isotropic noise covariance matrices).

assumption 3.1. the vectors  $\pmb{f}_i$  and  $\pmb{\eta}_i$ $(1 \leq i \leq n)$  are all independently generated according to

$$
\boldsymbol {f} _ {i} \stackrel {\text {i . i . d .}} {\sim} \mathcal {n} (\boldsymbol {0}, \boldsymbol {i} _ {r}), \quad \text {a n d} \quad \boldsymbol {\eta} _ {i} \stackrel {\text {i . i . d .}} {\sim} \mathcal {n} (\boldsymbol {0}, \sigma^ {2} \boldsymbol {i} _ {p}). \tag {3.20}
$$

moreover, we assume without loss of generality that  $\pmb{l}^{\star} = \pmb{u}^{\star}(\pmb{\lambda}^{\star})^{1/2}$ , where the columns of  $\pmb{u}^{\star} \in \mathbb{r}^{p \times r}$  are composed of orthonormal vectors, and  $\pmb{\lambda}^{\star} = \mathrm{diag}\big([ \lambda_1^{\star}, \dots, \lambda_r^{\star} ]\big)$  is an  $r$ -dimensional diagonal matrix obeying  $\lambda_1^{\star} \geq \dots \geq \lambda_r^{\star} > 0$ . throughout this section, we denote by

$$
\kappa := \lambda_ {1} ^ {\star} / \lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}
$$

the condition number of the low-rank matrix  $l^{\star}l^{\star \top} = u^{\star}\lambda^{\star}u^{\star \top}$

# 3.3.2 algorithm

as a starting point, it is readily seen under assumption 3.1 that

$$
\boldsymbol {x} _ {i} \sim \mathcal {n} (\boldsymbol {0}, m ^ {\star}) \quad \text {w i t h} \boldsymbol {m} ^ {\star} := \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \boldsymbol {\lambda} ^ {\star} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star \top} + \sigma^ {2} \boldsymbol {i} _ {p}. \tag {3.21}
$$

in brief, the covariance matrix  $m^{\star}$  is a low-rank matrix superimposed by a scaled identity matrix; for this reason, this model is also frequently referred to as the spiked covariance model (johnstone, 2001). the key takeaway is that the top-  $r$  eigenspace of the covariance matrix  $m^{\star}$  in (3.21) coincides with the  $r$ -dimensional principal subspace being sought after (i.e., the one spanned by  $l^{\star}$  or  $u^{\star}$ ).

the above observation motivates a simple spectral algorithm, which begins by computing a sample covariance matrix

$$
\boldsymbol {m} := \frac {1}{n} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \boldsymbol {x} _ {i} \boldsymbol {x} _ {i} ^ {\top}, \tag {3.22}
$$

followed by computation of the rank- $r$  eigendecomposition  $\mathbf{u} \mathbf{\lambda} \mathbf{u}^{\top}$  of  $\mathbf{m}$ . here,  $\mathbf{\lambda} \in \mathbb{r}^{r \times r}$  is a diagonal matrix whose diagonal entries entail the  $r$  largest eigenvalues  $\lambda_1 \geq \dots \geq \lambda_r$  of  $\mathbf{m}$ , and  $\mathbf{u} := [\mathbf{u}_1, \dots, \mathbf{u}_r] \in \mathbb{r}^{p \times r}$  with  $\mathbf{u}_i$  representing the eigenvector of  $\mathbf{m}$  associated with  $\lambda_i$ . the spectral algorithm studied herein then returns  $\mathbf{u}$  as the estimate for the principal subspace  $\mathbf{u}^\star$ .

remark 3.3. in the presence of missing data or heteroskedastic noise (meaning that the variance of the noise entries varies across different entries), the second part of the covariance matrix  $m^{\star}$  (i.e.,  $\sigma^2 i_p$  in (3.21)) might no longer be a scaled identity. under such circumstances, one might need to carefully adjust the diagonal entries of  $m$  in order for the algorithm to succeed; see, e.g., lounici (2014), loh and wainwright (2012), zhang et al. (2021), cai et al. (2021a), zhu et al. (2019), and yan et al. (2021). the reader might consult section 3.9 for an introduction to a commonly adopted diagonal deletion idea to address the aforementioned issue.

# 3.3.3 performance guarantees

this subsection develops statistical guarantees for the spectral method described above by invoking the eigenspace perturbation theory introduced previously. the first step is to establish a connection between the sample covariance  $\pmb{m}$  and the true covariance  $\pmb{m}^{\star}$ . defining  $\pmb{f} \coloneqq [\pmb{f}_1, \dots, \pmb{f}_n] \in \mathbb{r}^{r \times n}$  and  $\pmb{z} \coloneqq [\pmb{\eta}_1, \dots, \pmb{\eta}_n] \in \mathbb{r}^{p \times n}$ , one can easily compute that

$$
\boldsymbol {m} = \frac {1}{n} \left(\boldsymbol {l} ^ {\star} \boldsymbol {f} + \boldsymbol {z}\right) \left(\boldsymbol {l} ^ {\star} \boldsymbol {f} + \boldsymbol {z}\right) ^ {\top} = \boldsymbol {m} ^ {\star} + \boldsymbol {e}, \tag {3.23}
$$

where  $m^{\star}$  is defined in (3.21), and

$$
\begin{array}{l} \boldsymbol {e} := \boldsymbol {l} ^ {\star} \left(\frac {1}{n} \boldsymbol {f} \boldsymbol {f} ^ {\top} - \boldsymbol {i} _ {r}\right) \boldsymbol {l} ^ {\star \top} + \frac {1}{n} \boldsymbol {l} ^ {\star} \boldsymbol {f} \boldsymbol {z} ^ {\top} + \frac {1}{n} \boldsymbol {z} \boldsymbol {f} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {l} ^ {\star \top} \\ + \left(\frac {1}{n} \boldsymbol {z} \boldsymbol {z} ^ {\top} - \sigma^ {2} \boldsymbol {i} _ {p}\right). \tag {3.24} \\ \end{array}
$$

to apply the davis-kahan theorem, we are in need of controlling the size of the perturbation matrix  $\pmb{e}$ . this is achieved by the following lemma, whose proof is deferred to section 3.3.4.

lemma 3.5. consider the settings in section 3.3.1. suppose that  $n \geq cr\log^3 (n + p)$  for some sufficiently large constant  $c > 0$ . then with probability exceeding  $1 - o((n + p)^{-10})$ , one has

$$
\| \boldsymbol {e} \| \lesssim \left(\lambda_ {1} ^ {\star} \sqrt {\frac {r}{n}} + \sigma \sqrt {\frac {\lambda_ {1} ^ {\star} p}{n}} + \sigma^ {2} \sqrt {\frac {p}{n}} + \frac {\sigma^ {2} p \log^ {\frac {3}{2}} (n + p)}{n}\right) \log^ {\frac {1}{2}} (n + p).
$$

with lemma 3.5 in place, we are ready to present the following theorem that controls the estimation error of the spectral algorithm.

theorem 3.6. consider the settings in section 3.3.1. suppose that  $n \geq c(\kappa^2 r + r\log^2 (n + p) + \frac{\kappa\sigma^2p}{\lambda_r^\star} +\frac{\sigma^4p}{(\lambda_r^\star)^2})\log^3 (n + p)$  for some sufficiently large constant  $c > 0$ . then with probability at least  $1 - o((n + p)^{-10})$ , the following holds:

$$
\operatorname {d i s t} \left(\boldsymbol {u}, \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star}\right) \lesssim \left(\frac {\sigma}{\sqrt {\lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}}} \sqrt {\frac {\kappa p}{n}} + \frac {\sigma^ {2}}{\lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}} \sqrt {\frac {p}{n}} + \kappa \sqrt {\frac {r}{n}}\right) \log^ {\frac {1}{2}} (n + p). \tag {3.25}
$$

remark 3.4. the third term  $\kappa \sqrt{(r\log(n + p)) / n}$  on the right-hand side of (3.25) arises due to the randomness of  $\{\pmb{f}_i\}$  but not that of  $\{\pmb{\eta}_i\}$ . if our goal is instead to estimate the eigenspace of  $l^{\star}(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i}\pmb{f}_{i}\pmb{f}_{i}^{\top})l^{\star \top}$  as opposed to that of  $l^{\star}l^{\star \top}$ , then this term can be erased.

to interpret what theorem 3.6 conveys, we include a few remarks in the sequel, focusing on the simple scenario where  $\kappa = o(1)$ . in view of remark 3.4, we shall ignore the term  $\kappa \sqrt{(r\log(n + p)) / n}$  in the discussion below.

linear vs. quadratic dependency on the noise level. in comparison to the matrix denoising task (cf. section 3.2.2) where  $\mathrm{dist}(\pmb {u},\pmb{u}^{\star})$  scales linearly with the noise level  $\sigma$  (cf. (3.13)), the above performance guarantees for pca exhibit contrasting behavior in two different regimes depending on the strength of the signal-to-noise ratio (snr), measured in terms of  $\lambda_r^\star /\sigma^2$ :

- when the snr is sufficiently large with  $\lambda_r^\star /\sigma^2\gtrsim 1$ , then the dominant factor in (3.25) is the term  $\sigma \left(\frac{p\log(n + p)}{\lambda_r^\star n}\right)^{1 / 2}$ , which scales linearly with the noise level.  
- when the snr drops below the threshold  $\lambda_r^\star /\sigma^2\lesssim 1$  , then the term  $\frac{\sigma^2}{\lambda_r^\star}\sqrt{\frac{p\log(n + p)}{n}}$  which scales quadratically with the noise level—enters the picture and becomes the dominant effect

in truth, the quadratic term emerges since our spectral method operates upon the sample covariance matrix, which inevitably contains second moments of the noise components.

tightness and optimality. natural questions arise as to whether the performance guarantees in theorem 3.6 are tight, and whether the statistical accuracy can be further improved by designing more intelligent algorithms. these questions can be addressed by looking into the fundamental statistical limits. as established in the literature (zhang et al., 2021; cai et al., 2021a), one cannot hope to achieve

$$
\operatorname {d i s t} \left(\widehat {\boldsymbol {u}}, \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star}\right) = o \left(\frac {\sigma}{\sqrt {\lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}}} \sqrt {\frac {p}{n}} + \frac {\sigma^ {2}}{\lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}} \sqrt {\frac {p}{n}}\right) \tag {3.26}
$$

in a minimax sense, regardless of the choice of the estimator  $\widehat{\pmb{u}}$ ; see, e.g., zhang et al. (2021, theorem 2) for a precise statement. comparing (3.26) with theorem 3.6 reveals the near statistical optimality of the spectral method (modulo some log factor), and confirms the tightness of the eigenspace perturbation theory when applied to this problem.

proof of theorem 3.6. we first make the observation that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \lambda_ {1} (m ^ {\star}) \geq \dots \geq \lambda_ {r} (m ^ {\star}) > \lambda_ {r + 1} (m ^ {\star}) = \dots = \lambda_ {p} (m ^ {\star}) = \sigma^ {2} > 0, \\ \text {a n d} \quad \lambda_ {r} (\boldsymbol {m} ^ {\star}) - \lambda_ {r + 1} (\boldsymbol {m} ^ {\star}) = \lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}. \\ \end{array}
$$

the davis-kahan  $\sin \theta$  theorem (cf. corollary 2.8) thus implies that: if the perturbation size obeys  $\| \pmb{e} \| \leq (1 - 1 / \sqrt{2}) \lambda_r^{\star}$ , then one has

$$
\begin{array}{l} \operatorname {d i s t} \left(\boldsymbol {u}, \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star}\right) \leq \frac {2 \| \boldsymbol {e} \|}{\lambda_ {r} \left(\boldsymbol {m} ^ {\star}\right) - \lambda_ {r + 1} \left(\boldsymbol {m} ^ {\star}\right)} = \frac {2 \| \boldsymbol {e} \|}{\lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}} \\ \lesssim \frac {1}{\lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}} \left(\lambda_ {1} ^ {\star} \sqrt {\frac {r}{n}} + \sigma \sqrt {\frac {\lambda_ {1} ^ {\star} p}{n}} + \sigma^ {2} \sqrt {\frac {p}{n}} + \frac {\sigma^ {2} p \log^ {\frac {3}{2}} (n + p)}{n}\right) \log^ {\frac {1}{2}} (n + p) \\ \asymp \left(\kappa \sqrt {\frac {r}{n}} + \frac {\sigma}{\sqrt {\lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}}} \sqrt {\frac {\kappa p}{n}} + \frac {\sigma^ {2}}{\lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}} \sqrt {\frac {p}{n}}\right) \log^ {\frac {1}{2}} (n + p). \\ \end{array}
$$

here, the penultimate inequality results from lemma 3.5; the last line is valid as long as  $n \gtrsim (\sigma^2 / \lambda_1^\star)p\log^3 (n + p)$  —a condition that would hold under the assumption of this theorem—so that the fourth term is dominated by the second one in the parenthesis of the penultimate line. finally, it is immediately seen from lemma 3.5 that the condition  $\| \pmb{e}\| \leq (1 - 1/\sqrt{2})\lambda_r^\star$  would hold under the assumption of this theorem.

# 3.3.4 proof of lemma 3.5

we start by applying the triangle inequality to (3.24) as follows

$$
\begin{array}{l} \| \boldsymbol {e} \| \leq \| \boldsymbol {l} ^ {\star} \| ^ {2} \left\| \frac {1}{n} \boldsymbol {f} \boldsymbol {f} ^ {\top} - \boldsymbol {i} _ {r} \right\| + \| \boldsymbol {l} ^ {\star} \| \left\| \frac {1}{n} \boldsymbol {f} \boldsymbol {z} ^ {\top} \right\| + \left\| \frac {1}{n} \boldsymbol {z} \boldsymbol {f} ^ {\top} \right\| \| \boldsymbol {l} ^ {\star} \| \\ + \left\| \frac {1}{n} \boldsymbol {z} \boldsymbol {z} ^ {\top} - \sigma^ {2} \boldsymbol {i} _ {p} \right\|. \tag {3.27} \\ \end{array}
$$

in order to develop an upper bound on this quantity, one needs to control the spectral norm of  $\frac{1}{n}\pmb {f}\pmb{f}^{\top} - \pmb{i}_r$ ,  $\frac{1}{n}\pmb {f}\pmb {z}^{\top}$ ,  $\frac{1}{n}\pmb {z}\pmb {f}^{\top}$  and  $\frac{1}{n}\pmb {z}\pmb {z}^{\top} - \sigma^{2}\pmb{i}_{p}$ . all of these terms share similar randomness structure, namely, they are all averages of independent zero-mean random matrices. as a result, the truncated matrix bernstein inequality in corollary 3.2 becomes applicable. in what follows, we shall only demonstrate how to control the size of  $\frac{1}{n}\pmb {f}\pmb {z}^{\top}$ ; the other terms can be bounded similarly.

write  $\pmb{f}\pmb{z}^{\top} = \sum_{i=1}^{n} \pmb{f}_{i} \pmb{\eta}_{i}^{\top}$ . since the entries of  $\pmb{f}\pmb{z}^{\top}$  might be unbounded, we start by identifying an appropriate truncation level. from standard properties about gaussian distributions and the union bound, it is straightforward to verify that

$$
\mathbb {p} \left\{\| \boldsymbol {f} _ {i} \| _ {\infty} \leq 5 \sqrt {\log (n + p)} \text {a n d} \| \boldsymbol {\eta} _ {i} \| _ {\infty} \leq 5 \sigma \sqrt {\log (n + p)} \right\} \geq 1 - (n + p) ^ {- 1 1. 5}.
$$

one can further derive

$$
\left\| \boldsymbol {f} _ {i} \boldsymbol {\eta} _ {i} ^ {\top} \right\| \leq \left\| \boldsymbol {f} _ {i} \right\| _ {2} \left\| \boldsymbol {\eta} _ {i} \right\| _ {2} \leq \sqrt {r p} \left\| \boldsymbol {f} _ {i} \right\| _ {\infty} \left\| \boldsymbol {\eta} _ {i} \right\| _ {\infty} \leq 2 5 \sqrt {r p} \sigma \log (n + p)
$$

with probability greater than  $1 - (n + p)^{-11.5}$ . in other words, with the choice  $l \coloneqq 25\sqrt{rp}\sigma \log (n + p)$  one has

$$
\mathbb {p} \left\{\| \boldsymbol {f} _ {i} \boldsymbol {\eta} _ {i} ^ {\top} \| \geq l \right\} \leq (n + p) ^ {- 1 1. 5} =: q _ {0}.
$$

additionally, the symmetry of gaussian distributions implies

$$
\mathbb {e} \left[ \boldsymbol {f} _ {i} \boldsymbol {\eta} _ {i} ^ {\top} \right] - \mathbb {e} \left[ \boldsymbol {f} _ {i} \boldsymbol {\eta} _ {i} ^ {\top} \mathbb {1} \left\{\| \boldsymbol {f} _ {i} \boldsymbol {\eta} _ {i} ^ {\top} \| <   l \right\} \right] = 0.
$$

to invoke the truncated bernstein inequality, it remains to determine the variance statistic. towards this end, letting  $\pmb{b}_i = \pmb{f}_i\pmb{\eta}_i^\top$ , we observe that

$$
\mathbb {e} \left[ \boldsymbol {b} _ {i} \boldsymbol {b} _ {i} ^ {\top} \right] = \mathbb {e} \left[ \boldsymbol {f} _ {i} \boldsymbol {\eta} _ {i} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\eta} _ {i} \boldsymbol {f} _ {i} ^ {\top} \right] = \mathbb {e} \left[ \boldsymbol {\eta} _ {i} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\eta} _ {i} \right] \mathbb {e} \left[ \boldsymbol {f} _ {i} \boldsymbol {f} _ {i} ^ {\top} \right] = p \sigma^ {2} \boldsymbol {i} _ {r},
$$

$$
\mathbb {e} \left[ \boldsymbol {b} _ {i} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {b} _ {i} \right] = \mathbb {e} \left[ \boldsymbol {\eta} _ {i} \boldsymbol {f} _ {i} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {f} _ {i} \boldsymbol {\eta} _ {i} ^ {\top} \right] = \mathbb {e} \left[ \boldsymbol {f} _ {i} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {f} _ {i} \right] \mathbb {e} \left[ \boldsymbol {\eta} _ {i} \boldsymbol {\eta} _ {i} ^ {\top} \right] = r \sigma^ {2} \boldsymbol {i} _ {p},
$$

thus leading to

$$
v := \max \left\{\left\| \sum_ {i} \mathbb {e} [ \boldsymbol {b} _ {i} \boldsymbol {b} _ {i} ^ {\top} ] \right\|, \left\| \sum_ {i} \mathbb {e} [ \boldsymbol {b} _ {i} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {b} _ {i} ] \right\| \right\} = n p \sigma^ {2},
$$

where we use the fact that  $r \leq p$ . taking these bound together and applying the truncated matrix bernstein theorem (see corollary 3.2) demonstrate that if  $n \gtrsim r\log^3 (n + p)$ , one has

$$
\begin{array}{l} \frac {1}{n} \| \boldsymbol {f} \boldsymbol {z} ^ {\top} \| \lesssim \frac {1}{n} \sqrt {v \log (n + p)} + \frac {1}{n} l \log (n + p) \\ \asymp \sigma \sqrt {\frac {p \log (n + p)}{n}} + \frac {\sqrt {r p}}{n} \sigma \log^ {2} (n + p) \asymp \sigma \sqrt {\frac {p \log (n + p)}{n}} \tag {3.28a} \\ \end{array}
$$

with probability at least  $1 - o\big((n + p)^{-10}\big) - nq_0 = 1 - o\big((n + p)^{-10}\big)$ .

repeating the above analysis yields that: if  $n \gtrsim r\log^3 (n + p)$ , with probability at least  $1 - o((n + p)^{-10})$  one has

$$
\left\| \frac {1}{n} \boldsymbol {f} \boldsymbol {f} ^ {\top} - \boldsymbol {i} _ {r} \right\| \lesssim \sqrt {\frac {r \log (n + p)}{n}}, \tag {3.28b}
$$

$$
\left\| \frac {1}{n} \boldsymbol {z} \boldsymbol {z} ^ {\top} - \sigma^ {2} \boldsymbol {i} _ {p} \right\| \lesssim \sigma^ {2} \sqrt {\frac {p \log (n + p)}{n}} + \frac {\sigma^ {2} p \log^ {2} (n + p)}{n}. \tag {3.28c}
$$

note that we do not get rid of the second term on the right-hand side of (3.28c) since we do not assume  $n \gtrsim p\log^3 (n + p)$ .

substituting the above results (3.28) into (3.27) and recognizing the basic fact  $\| l^{\star}\| = \| u^{\star}(\lambda^{\star})^{1 / 2}\| \leq$ $\| (\pmb {\lambda}^{\star})^{1 / 2}\| = \sqrt{\lambda_1^{\star}}$ , we conclude that

$$
\| \boldsymbol {e} \| \lesssim \left(\lambda_ {1} ^ {\star} \sqrt {\frac {r}{n}} + \sigma \sqrt {\frac {\lambda_ {1} ^ {\star} p}{n}} + \sigma^ {2} \sqrt {\frac {p}{n}} + \frac {\sigma^ {2} p \log^ {\frac {3}{2}} (n + p)}{n}\right) \log^ {\frac {1}{2}} (n + p).
$$

# 3.4 graph clustering and community recovery

next, we move on to a central problem that permeates data science applications: clustering. an important formulation that falls under this category is graph clustering or community recovery, which aims to cluster individuals into different communities based on pairwise measurements of their relationships, each of which reveals information about whether or not two individuals belong to the same community (abbe, 2017); see figure 3.1 for an illustration. there has been a recent explosion of interest in this problem, due to its wide applicability in, say, social network analysis (azaouzi et al., 2019), image segmentation (browet et al., 2011), shape mapping in computer vision (huang and guibas, 2013), haplotype phasing in genome sequencing (chen et al., 2016a), to name just a few. this section explores the capability of spectral methods in application to graph clustering; we will revisit the clustering problem again in section 3.5 for another common formulation.

![](images/756d548b222845bacf401e2ebb8005c34327adf43d24f12978920fc1bcd7d14b.jpg)  
figure 3.1: illustration of graph clustering and community recovery, where one wishes to cluster all nodes into two communities based on the edges in the graph.

# 3.4.1 problem formulation and assumptions

in this section, we formulate the graph clustering problem via the well-renowned stochastic block model (sbm) introduced in holland et al. (1983)—an idealized generative model that commonly serves as a theoretical benchmark for evaluating community recovery algorithms.

consider an undirected graph  $\mathcal{g} = (\mathcal{v},\mathcal{e})$  that comprises  $n$  vertices, where  $\mathcal{v}$  and  $\mathcal{e}$  denote the vertex set and the edge set of  $\mathcal{g}$ , respectively. the  $n$  vertices, labelled by  $1,\dots ,n$ , exhibit community structures and can be grouped into two non-overlapping communities of equal sizes. here and throughout,  $n$  is assumed to be an even number, so that each community contains exactly  $n / 2$  vertices. to encode the community memberships, we assign  $n$  binary-valued variables  $x_{i}^{\star}\in \{1, - 1\}$ $(1\leq i\leq n)$  to the vertices in a way that

$$
x _ {i} ^ {\star} = \left\{ \begin{array}{l l} 1, & \text {i f v e r t e x i b e l o n g s t o t h e 1 s t c o m m u n i t y}, \\ - 1, & \text {o t h e r w i s e}. \end{array} \right.
$$

the sbm assumes that the set  $\mathcal{e}$  of (undirected) edges is generated randomly based on the community memberships of the incident vertices. to be precise, each pair  $(i,j)$  of vertices is connected by an edge independently with probability  $p$  (resp.  $q$ ) if  $i$  and  $j$  belong to the same community (resp. different communities). the resultant connectivity pattern is represented by an adjacency matrix  $\mathbf{a} = [a_{i,j}]_{1\leq i,j\leq n}\in \{0,1\}^{n\times n}$ , such that for each pair  $(i,j)$ ,

$$
a _ {i, j} = \left\{ \begin{array}{l l} 1, & \text {i f} (i, j) \in \mathcal {e}, \\ 0, & \text {o t h e r w i s e .} \end{array} \right. \tag {3.29}
$$

by convention, we take the diagonal entries to be  $a_{i,i} = 0$  for all  $1 \leq i \leq n$ . as a remark, the matrix  $\mathbf{a}$  is symmetric since  $\mathcal{g}$  is an undirected graph, with upper triangular elements being realizations of independent bernoulli random variables with mean either  $p$  (if two nodes are in the same community) or  $q$  (otherwise). in addition, it is assumed throughout that  $p > q > 0$ , implying that there are in expectation more within-community edges than across-community edges.

based on the adjacency matrix  $\mathbf{a}$  generated by the sbm, the goal is to identify the latent community memberships of the vertices. to phrase it in mathematical terms, the aim is to reconstruct the vector  $\pmb{x}^{\star} = [x_{i}^{\star}]_{1\leq i\leq n}\in \{1, - 1\}^{n}$  modulo the global sign, namely, recovering either  $\pmb{x}^{\star}$  or  $-\pmb{x}^{\star}$ . this is all one can hope for, as there is absolutely no basis to distinguish the names of two groups.

# 3.4.2 algorithm: spectral clustering

now we describe a spectral method. to simplify presentation, it is assumed without loss of generality that:  $x_{i}^{\star} = 1$  for any  $1 \leq i \leq n / 2$ , and  $x_{i}^{\star} = -1$  for any  $i > n / 2$ .

a starting point for the algorithm design is to examine the mean of the adjacency matrix, given as follows

$$
\mathbb {e} [ \boldsymbol {a} ] = \left[ \begin{array}{l l} p \boldsymbol {\mathbf {1}} _ {n / 2} \boldsymbol {\mathbf {1}} _ {n / 2} ^ {\top} & q \boldsymbol {\mathbf {1}} _ {n / 2} \boldsymbol {\mathbf {1}} _ {n / 2} ^ {\top} \\ q \boldsymbol {\mathbf {1}} _ {n / 2} \boldsymbol {\mathbf {1}} _ {n / 2} ^ {\top} & p \boldsymbol {\mathbf {1}} _ {n / 2} \boldsymbol {\mathbf {1}} _ {n / 2} ^ {\top} \end{array} \right] - p \boldsymbol {i}.
$$

as revealed by the above calculation, the matrix constructed below

$$
\boldsymbol {m} = \boldsymbol {a} - \frac {p + q}{2} \mathbf {1} _ {n} \mathbf {1} _ {n} ^ {\top} + p \boldsymbol {i} \tag {3.30}
$$

exhibits an approximate rank-1 structure, in the sense that its mean

$$
\boldsymbol {m} ^ {\star} := \mathbb {e} [ \boldsymbol {m} ] = \frac {p - q}{2} \left[ \begin{array}{c} \mathbf {1} _ {n / 2} \\ - \mathbf {1} _ {n / 2} \end{array} \right] \left[ \begin{array}{l l} \mathbf {1} _ {n / 2} ^ {\top} & - \mathbf {1} _ {n / 2} ^ {\top} \end{array} \right] \tag {3.31}
$$

is a rank-1 matrix. the leading eigenvalue of  $m^{\star}$  and its associated eigenvector are given respectively by

$$
\lambda^ {\star} := \frac {(p - q) n}{2}, \quad \text {a n d} \quad \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} := \frac {1}{\sqrt {n}} \left[ \begin{array}{c} \mathbf {1} _ {n / 2} \\ - \mathbf {1} _ {n / 2} \end{array} \right]. \tag {3.32}
$$

crucially, the eigenvector  $\pmb{u}^{\star}$  encapsulates the precise community structure we seek to recover: all positive entries of  $\pmb{u}^{\star}$  correspond to vertices from one community, while the remaining ones form another community.

inspired by the above calculation, a candidate spectral clustering algorithm consists of eigendecomposition followed by entrywise rounding:

1. compute the leading eigenvector  $\pmb{u}$  of  $m$  (constructed in (3.30));  
2. compute the estimate  $\pmb {x} = [x_i]_{1\leq i\leq n}$  such that for any  $1\leq i\leq n$

$$
x _ {i} = \operatorname {s g n} \left(u _ {i}\right) = \left\{ \begin{array}{l l} 1, & \text {i f} u _ {i} > 0, \\ - 1, & \text {i f} u _ {i} \leq 0. \end{array} \right. \tag {3.33}
$$

in words, the community memberships are estimated in accordance with the signs of the entries of the leading eigenvector of  $m$ , namely, the entries with the same signs are declared to come from the same cluster.

remark 3.5. the above algorithm requires prior knowledge of the parameters  $p$  and  $q$  when constructing  $m$ . it is also feasible to develop a "model-agnostic" alternative by, for instance, looking at the second eigenvector of  $a$  (since the second eigenvector of  $\mathbb{e}[a]$  turns out to be precisely  $\pmb{u}^{\star}$ ), which does not rely on prior information about  $p$  and  $q$  at all; see, e.g., abbe et al. (2020b) for details. here, we adopt the above model-dependent version primarily for convenience of exposition.

# 3.4.3 performance guarantees: almost exact recovery

the spectral method enjoys appealing statistical guarantees for recovering the community structure of the sbm, which can be readily obtained by invoking the  $\ell_2$  eigenvector perturbation theory. to demonstrate this, we begin by developing an upper bound on the spectral norm of the perturbation matrix  $e\coloneqq m - m^{\star}$ , postponing the proof to section 3.4.4.

lemma 3.7. consider the settings in section 3.4.1, and suppose that  $np \gtrsim \log n$ . then with probability at least  $1 - o(n^{-8})$ , one has

$$
\| \boldsymbol {e} \| \lesssim \sqrt {n p}. \tag {3.34}
$$

this spectral norm bound, in conjunction with the davis-kahan  $\sin \theta$  theorem, leads to the following theoretical support for the spectral method introduced in section 3.4.2.

theorem 3.8. consider the setting in section 3.4.1, and suppose that

$$
p \gtrsim \frac {\log n}{n}, \quad \text {a n d} \quad \sqrt {\frac {p}{n}} = o (p - q). \tag {3.35}
$$

with probability exceeding  $1 - o(n^{-8})$ , the spectral method achieves

$$
\frac {1}{n} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \mathbb {1} \left\{x _ {i} = x _ {i} ^ {\star} \right\} = 1 - o (1), \quad \text {o r} \quad \frac {1}{n} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \mathbb {1} \left\{x _ {i} = - x _ {i} ^ {\star} \right\} = 1 - o (1).
$$

it is noteworthy that the metric

$$
\min \left\{\frac {1}{n} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \mathbb {1} \{x _ {i} \neq x _ {i} ^ {\star} \}, \frac {1}{n} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \mathbb {1} \{x _ {i} \neq - x _ {i} ^ {\star} \} \right\}
$$

can be understood as the mis-clustering rate. in a nutshell, theorem 3.8 asserts that with the assistance of simple rounding (i.e., the  $\mathsf{sgn}(\cdot)$  operation), the spectral method allows for almost exact community recovery—namely, correctly clustering all but a vanishing fraction of the vertices—assuming satisfaction of condition (3.35). note that "almost exact recovery" is also referred to as "weak consistency" in the literature (abbe, 2017).

let us take a moment to interpret the recovery condition in (3.35). the first requirement in condition (3.35) ensures the presence of sufficiently many edges in the observed graph, while still permits the graph to be fairly sparse (with average vertex degrees as low as the order of  $\log n$ ). the second requirement in condition (3.35)—which imposes a lower bound on the separation between the edge densities  $p$  and  $q$ —guarantees that the within-community edges can be adequately differentiated from across-community edges. as a more concrete example, consider the scenario where  $p \asymp (\log n)/n$  (so that each vertex is only expected to be incident to  $o(\log n)$  edges). in this case, the second requirement in condition (3.35) can be translated into

$$
p - q \gg \sqrt {\log n} / n, \qquad \mathrm {i f} p \asymp (\log n) / n.
$$

this indicates that the separation  $p - q$  is allowed to be considerably smaller than the edge densities, even in this low-edge-density regime. in comparison, in another extreme case with  $p \asymp 1$  (so that each vertex is likely to be connected with a constant fraction of other vertices), the second requirement in condition (3.35) reads

$$
p - q \gg 1 / \sqrt {n}, \quad \text {i f} p \asymp 1,
$$

thereby allowing the edge density difference to be even  $\sqrt{n}$  times smaller than the edge densities themselves.

it is worth highlighting that the spectral method is not merely capable of correctly clustering all but a diminishing fraction of vertices; in fact, it allows for simultaneous and exact recovery for all vertices under slightly modified conditions. establishing this stronger assertion requires developing a significantly strengthened  $\ell_{\infty}$ -based eigenvector perturbation theory, which will be elucidated in section 4.5. the discussion about the statistical optimality of this spectral method is postponed to section 4.5 as well.

proof of theorem 3.8. it is readily seen from lemma 3.7 that with with probability at least  $1 - o(n^{-8})$ ,

$$
\left\| \boldsymbol {e} \right\| \leq \left(1 - \frac {1}{\sqrt {2}}\right) \frac {n (p - q)}{2} = \left(1 - \frac {1}{\sqrt {2}}\right) \lambda^ {\star},
$$

provided that condition (3.35) holds. here,  $\lambda^{\star}$  is defined in (3.32). apply corollary 2.8 to yield that with probability at least  $1 - o(n^{-8})$ ,

$$
\operatorname {d i s t} \left(\boldsymbol {u}, \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star}\right) \leq \frac {2 \| \boldsymbol {e} \|}{\lambda^ {\star}} \lesssim \frac {\sqrt {n p}}{n (p - q)} = o (1), \tag {3.36}
$$

where the last relation follows from condition (3.35).

assume, without loss of generality, that  $\| \pmb{u} - \pmb{u}^{\star}\|_{2} = \mathrm{dist}\big(\pmb{u},\pmb{u}^{\star}\big)$ . we shall pay attention to the set

$$
\mathcal {n} := \left\{i \mid | u _ {i} - u _ {i} ^ {\star} | \geq 1 / \sqrt {n} \right\}.
$$

in view of the rounding procedure: for any  $i$  obeying  $x_{i} \neq x_{i}^{\star}$ , one necessarily has  $\operatorname{sgn}(u_i) \neq \operatorname{sgn}(u_i^{\star})$  thus indicating that  $|u_{i} - u_{i}^{\star}| \geq |u_{i}^{\star}| = 1 / \sqrt{n}$  and hence  $i \in \mathcal{n}$ . combining the  $\ell_2$  bound (3.36) and the definition of  $\mathcal{n}$ , we can easily verify that

$$
| \mathcal {n} | \leq \frac {\left\| \boldsymbol {u} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2}}{(1 / \sqrt {n}) ^ {2}} = o (n),
$$

which in turn leads to the advertised result

$$
