\frac {1}{n} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \mathbb {1} \left\{x _ {i} \neq x _ {i} ^ {\star} \right\} \leq \frac {1}{n} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \mathbb {1} \left\{| u _ {i} - u _ {i} ^ {\star} | \geq \frac {1}{\sqrt {n}} \right\} = \frac {| \mathcal {n} |}{n} = o (1).
$$

# 3.4.4 proof of lemma 3.7

we intend to apply theorem 3.4 to establish this lemma. first, observe from the definition  $\mathbf{e} = m - m^{\star} = a - \mathbb{e}[a]$  that

$$
\left| e _ {i, j} \right| \leq \max  _ {i, j} \left| a _ {i, j} \right| = 1.
$$

in addition, the variance of  $e_{i,j}$  is upper bounded by

$$
\mathbb {e} \left[ e _ {i, j} ^ {2} \right] = \mathsf {v a r} (a _ {i, j}) \leq \mathbb {e} \left[ a _ {i, j} ^ {2} \right] \stackrel {\mathrm {(i)}} {\leq} \max  \{p, q \} \stackrel {\mathrm {(i i)}} {=} p
$$

for any  $(i,j)$ , where (i) follows since  $a_{i,j}$  is a bernoulli random variable with mean either  $p$  or  $q$ , and (ii) is due to the assumption  $p > q$ . the bound (3.9) and the condition  $np \gtrsim \log n$  thus imply that

$$
\left\| \boldsymbol {e} \right\| = \left\| \boldsymbol {m} - \mathbb {e} [ \boldsymbol {m} ] \right\| \lesssim \sqrt {n p} + o (\sqrt {\log n}) \asymp \sqrt {n p} \tag {3.37}
$$

with probability exceeding  $1 - o(n^{-8})$ .

# 3.5 clustering in gaussian mixture models

this section is also concerned with clustering, with the aim of grouping unlabeled data points into a few clusters (so that the data within the same cluster share similar characteristics). in contrast to the graph clustering setting in section 3.4 where only pairwise measurements are available, this section assumes direct access to data samples for each individual. spectral methods—possibly with the aid of subsequent refinement like  $k$ -means—continue to be remarkably effective for this setting, achieving practical success in, say, image segmentation (shi and malik, 2000), text separation (reynolds and rose, 1995), climate modeling (lin et al., 2017), and heterogeneity modeling in precision medicine and marketing (fan et al., 2014). motivated by the empirical successes, understanding the theoretical properties of spectral clustering has garnered growing attention recently. in particular, gaussian mixture models emerge as a succinct model of attack, providing elegant yet intuitive abstractions to pivotal quantities that dictate the feasibility of spectral clustering.

# 3.5.1 gaussian mixture models and assumptions

model and goal. imagine that we have collected  $n$  independent samples  $\{\pmb{x}_i\}_{1 \leq i \leq n}$ , generated from a mixture of  $r$  spherical gaussians with respective centers  $\pmb{\theta}_1^{\star}, \dots, \pmb{\theta}_r^{\star} \in \mathbb{r}^p$ . more precisely, for each sample vector  $\pmb{x}_i \in \mathbb{r}^p$ , we assume the existence of a predetermined, yet a priori unknown, cluster membership variable  $\xi_i^{\star} \in [r]$  such that

$$
\boldsymbol {x} _ {i} = \left\{ \begin{array}{c c} \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {1} ^ {\star} + \boldsymbol {\eta} _ {i}, & \text {i f} \xi_ {i} ^ {\star} = 1, \\ \vdots & \vdots \\ \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {r} ^ {\star} + \boldsymbol {\eta} _ {i}, & \text {i f} \xi_ {i} ^ {\star} = r, \end{array} \right. \tag {3.38}
$$

where the noise vector  $\pmb{\eta}_i\sim \mathcal{n}(\mathbf{0},\pmb{i}_p)$  is independently generated across the samples. in words,  $\xi_i^\star$  indicates which gaussian component a sample is generated from. clustering in this gaussian mixture model can, therefore, be posed as recovering the set of cluster membership variables  $\{\xi_i^\star\}_{1\leq i\leq n}$  (modulo the global permutation ambiguity).

assumptions. to simplify our exposition, we impose the following assumptions throughout this section. as a worthy note, this assumption is often non-essential and can be significantly relaxed, which we shall remark on momentarily in remark 3.8.

assumption 3.2. the centers are independently generated obeying

$$
\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i} ^ {\star} \stackrel {{\text {i . i . d .}}} {{\sim}} \mathcal {n} \left(\mathbf {0}, \frac {\delta^ {2}}{2 p} \boldsymbol {i} _ {p}\right), \qquad 1 \leq i \leq r
$$

for some parameter  $\delta > 0$ .

under this assumption, standard gaussian concentration inequalities (vershynin, 2017) tell us that, with high probability (for large  $p$  and  $n = \mathrm{poly}(p)$ ),

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \pmb {\theta} _ {i} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} = (1 + o (1)) \frac {\delta^ {2}}{2}, \qquad \left| \pmb {\theta} _ {i} ^ {\star^ {\top}} \pmb {\theta} _ {j} ^ {\star} \right| = o (\delta^ {2}), \\ \left\| \pmb {\theta} _ {i} ^ {\star} - \pmb {\theta} _ {j} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} = \left\| \pmb {\theta} _ {i} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} + \left\| \pmb {\theta} _ {j} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} - 2 \pmb {\theta} _ {i} ^ {\star \top} \pmb {\theta} _ {j} ^ {\star} = (1 + o (1)) \delta^ {2} \\ \end{array}
$$

hold for any pair  $i \neq j$ , where  $o(1)$  denotes a vanishingly small quantity as  $p$  approaches infinity. the indication is that the parameter  $\delta$  reflects (approximately) the separation between any pair of centers.

for simplicity of presentation, it is further assumed that there are exactly  $n / r$  samples drawn from each of the  $r$  gaussian components. without loss of generality, we assume that

$$
\xi_ {i} ^ {\star} = l, \quad \text {i f} \left\lceil \frac {i}{r} \right\rceil = l \tag {3.39}
$$

for any  $1 \leq i \leq n$ , where the ceiling function  $\lceil x \rceil$  represents the least integer greater than or equal to the number  $x \in \mathbb{r}$ . in other words, the first batch of  $n / r$  samples is drawn from the first gaussian component, the second batch comes from the second component, and so on. it is worth pointing out that this assumed assignment information (3.39) is unavailable when running the spectral clustering algorithm.

# 3.5.2 algorithm and rationale

motivation: spectral structure of the data matrix. in order to develop a spectral clustering algorithm, it is instrumental to first examine the spectral feature of the following data matrix

$$
\boldsymbol {x} := \left[ \boldsymbol {x} _ {1}, \dots , \boldsymbol {x} _ {n} \right] = \mathbb {e} [ \boldsymbol {x} ] + \underbrace {\left[ \boldsymbol {\eta} _ {1} , \cdots , \boldsymbol {\eta} _ {n} \right]} _ {=: z}. \tag {3.40}
$$

clearly,  $\mathbb{e}[\pmb {x}]\in \mathbb{r}^{p\times n}$  exhibits a rank-  $r$  structure:

$$
\mathbb {e} [ x ] = \left[ \theta_ {1} ^ {\star}, \dots , \theta_ {1} ^ {\star}, \theta_ {2} ^ {\star}, \dots , \theta_ {2} ^ {\star}, \dots , \theta_ {r} ^ {\star}, \dots , \theta_ {r} ^ {\star} \right] = \theta^ {\star} f ^ {\star \top},
$$

where we define

$$
\boldsymbol {\theta} ^ {\star} := \left[ \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {1} ^ {\star}, \dots , \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {r} ^ {\star} \right] \in \mathbb {r} ^ {p \times r}, \quad \boldsymbol {f} ^ {\star} := \left[ \begin{array}{c c c c} \mathbf {1} _ {\frac {n}{r}} & & & \\ & \mathbf {1} _ {\frac {n}{r}} & & \\ & & \ddots & \\ & & & \mathbf {1} _ {\frac {n}{r}} \end{array} \right] \in \mathbb {r} ^ {n \times r}. \tag {3.41}
$$

similarly, the gram matrix  $\mathbf{x}^{\top}\mathbf{x}$  also inherits this rank-  $r$  structure in the following sense (albeit in the form of a "spiked" structure due to the presence of noise):

$$
\mathbb {e} \left[ \boldsymbol {x} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {x} \right] = \mathbb {e} [ \boldsymbol {x} ] ^ {\top} \mathbb {e} [ \boldsymbol {x} ] + \mathbb {e} \left[ \boldsymbol {z} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {z} \right] = \boldsymbol {f} ^ {\star} \boldsymbol {\theta} ^ {\star \top} \boldsymbol {\theta} ^ {\star} \boldsymbol {f} ^ {\star \top} + p \boldsymbol {i} _ {n}. \tag {3.42}
$$

recognizing that  $\pmb{f}^{\star}$  encodes all the cluster membership information, one is motivated to attempt information extraction from the rank- $r$  eigenspace of  $\pmb{x}^{\top}\pmb{x}$ , akin to the pca algorithm introduced in section 3.3.2.

algorithm: spectral clustering followed by  $k$ -means. with the preceding spectral properties in mind, we are ready to present a spectral clustering algorithm tailored to this gaussian mixture model. given that the eigenspace of  $\mathbf{x}^{\top}\mathbf{x}$  might only approximate  $\mathbf{f}^{\star}$  up to global rotation, we include a follow-up  $k$ -means scheme (macqueen, 1967) to produce a valid clustering outcome based on the spectral estimate.

1. compute the leading rank-  $r$  eigenspace  $\pmb {u}\in \mathbb{r}^{n\times r}$  of  $\pmb {x}^{\top}\pmb{x}$

2. compute  $\mathbf{y} = \mathcal{p}\big(\mathbf{u}\mathbf{u}^{\top}\big)\in \mathbb{r}^{n\times n}$ , where the operator  $\mathcal{p}(\cdot)$  projects each column onto the unit sphere, i.e.,

$$
\mathcal {p} (\boldsymbol {z}) := \left[ \frac {\boldsymbol {z} _ {1}}{\| \boldsymbol {z} _ {1} \| _ {2}}, \dots , \frac {\boldsymbol {z} _ {n}}{\| \boldsymbol {z} _ {n} \| _ {2}} \right]
$$

for any matrix  $\mathbf{z} = [\pmb{z}_1, \dots, \pmb{z}_n]$ . as will be discussed below, the projection step is not necessary, and we can also simply take  $\mathbf{y} = \mathbf{u}\mathbf{u}^{\top}$ .

3. let  $\pmb{y}_i$  represent the  $i$ -th column of  $\pmb{y}$ , and apply the  $k$ -means algorithm (with  $k = r$ ) to the vectors  $\{\pmb{y}_i\}_{1 \leq i \leq n}$  to find the cluster centers and cluster labels for all individuals; namely, we compute

$$
\left(\left\{\hat {\xi} _ {i} \right\} _ {i = 1} ^ {n}, \left\{\widehat {\boldsymbol {\vartheta}} _ {i} \right\} _ {i = 1} ^ {r}\right) = \underset {\xi_ {1}, \dots , \xi_ {n} \in [ r ], \vartheta_ {1}, \dots , \vartheta_ {r} \in \mathbb {r} ^ {n}} {\arg \min } \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \| \boldsymbol {y} _ {i} - \boldsymbol {\vartheta} _ {\xi_ {i}} \| _ {2} ^ {2}. \tag {3.43}
$$

the algorithm then returns  $\{\widehat{\xi}_i\}_{1\leq i\leq n}$  as the clustering result. interestingly, step 1 bears similarity with the spectral algorithm for graph clustering, since we essentially generate a pairwise similarity measurement for each pair  $(i,j)$  using the inner product  $\langle \pmb {x}_i,\pmb {x}_j\rangle$

remark 3.6. the  $k$ -means formulation (3.43)—which minimizes the sum of squared distance between each data point and the center of its associated cluster—is an integer program and intractable in general (aloise et al., 2009). fortunately, computationally feasible solutions are available either under sufficient minimum center separation or when suitably initialized (lloyd, 1982; vempala and wang, 2004; lu and zhou, 2016; peng and wei, 2007; awasthi et al., 2015; mixon et al., 2017; iguchi et al., 2017). an in-depth account of this computational aspect is beyond the scope of this monograph, and the interested reader is referred to li et al. (2020b) and löffler et al. (2021) for details.

further explanations. we take a moment to explain why  $k$ -means is applied to the columns of  $\mathbf{y}$ . recall that the central object the spectral algorithm seeks to approximate is the leading rank- $r$  eigenspace of  $\pmb{f}^{\star}\pmb{\theta}^{\star \top}\pmb{\theta}^{\star}\pmb{f}^{\star \top}$  (cf. (3.42)). for convenience, suppose we have the eigendecomposition  $\theta^{\star \top}\theta^{\star} = u_{\theta}\sigma_{\theta}u_{\theta}^{\top}$ , where  $u_{\theta} \in \mathcal{o}^{r\times r}$  is orthonormal and  $\sigma_{\theta} \in \mathbb{r}^{r\times r}$  is diagonal. this results in the decomposition

$$
\boldsymbol {f} ^ {\star} \boldsymbol {\theta} ^ {\star \top} \boldsymbol {\theta} ^ {\star} \boldsymbol {f} ^ {\star \top} = \frac {n}{r} \cdot \left(\underbrace {\sqrt {\frac {r}{n}} \boldsymbol {f} ^ {\star} \boldsymbol {u} _ {\theta}} _ {=: u ^ {\star}}\right) \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\theta} \left(\sqrt {\frac {r}{n}} \boldsymbol {f} ^ {\star} \boldsymbol {u} _ {\theta}\right) ^ {\top}. \tag {3.44}
$$

apparently, the matrix  $u^{\star} \in \mathbb{r}^{n \times r}$  defined above has orthonormal columns and, as a result, represents the eigenspace of  $f^{\star} \theta^{\star \top} \theta^{\star} f^{\star \top}$ . the idea is that if the spectral estimate  $u$  approximates  $u^{\star} \in \mathbb{r}^{n \times r}$  well, then the matrices  $\sqrt{\frac{n}{r}} u u^{\top}$  and  $y$  constructed above are hopefully close to the following matrix

$$
\boldsymbol {y} ^ {\star} := \sqrt {\frac {n}{r}} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star^ {\top}} = \sqrt {\frac {r}{n}} \left[ \begin{array}{c c c} \mathbf {1} _ {\frac {n}{r}} \mathbf {1} _ {\frac {n}{r}} ^ {\top} & & \\ & \ddots & \\ & & \mathbf {1} _ {\frac {n}{r}} \mathbf {1} _ {\frac {n}{r}} ^ {\top} \end{array} \right]. \tag {3.45}
$$

as can be easily seen, the data points belonging to the same ground-truth cluster are associated with identical columns in  $\mathbf{y}^{\star}$ ; for instance, each of the first  $n / r$  samples—which belongs to the first cluster—corresponds to a column of  $\mathbf{y}^{\star}$  given by  $\sqrt{\frac{r}{n}}\left[\begin{array}{c}\mathbf{1}_{n / r}\\ \mathbf{0}\end{array}\right]$ . therefore, clustering the columns of

$\mathbf{y}$  via  $k$ -means is expected to unveil the underlying cluster structure, provided that  $\mathbf{y}$  is sufficiently close to  $\mathbf{y}^{\star}$ . in summary, spectral estimation (steps 1-2) effectively leads to a new vector  $\mathbf{y}_i$  for each point, which enjoys substantially enhanced signal-to-noise ratio compared to  $\mathbf{x}_i$  and boosts the chance for  $k$ -means to succeed.

we shall also explain the projection operation enforced in step 2 of the algorithm. given that each column of  $\mathbf{y}^{\star}$  has unit  $\ell_{2}$  norm, projecting each column of  $\mathbf{u}\mathbf{u}^{\top}$  onto the unit sphere ensures that no column of  $\mathbf{y}$  has an abnormal size. note, however, that this projection step is non-essential and is introduced here primarily to simplify the mathematical analysis. spectral clustering is expected to succeed even in the absence of such a projection step (löffler et al., 2021).

remark 3.7. another variation of spectral clustering is to directly apply the  $k$ -means algorithm to cluster the rows of  $\pmb{u}$  (or some properly rescaled version of them) (löffler et al., 2021). to explain the rationale, we note that under the assumption (3.39),  $\pmb{u}^{\star}$  necessarily consists of  $r$  blocks of identical rows as follows:

$$
\boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} = \sqrt {\frac {r}{n}} \left( \begin{array}{c} \mathbf {1} _ {n / r} \boldsymbol {\nu} _ {1} ^ {\top} \\ \vdots \\ \mathbf {1} _ {n / r} \boldsymbol {\nu} _ {r} ^ {\top} \end{array} \right),
$$

where  $\pmb{\nu}_{1}^{\top},\dots ,\pmb{\nu}_{r}^{\top}$  are the orthonormal rows of the matrix  $u_{\theta}$  (cf. (3.44)). consequently, clustering the rows of  $\pmb{u}^{\star}$  reveals exactly the true cluster assignments of all individuals. the idea of our spectral analysis below applies to this method as well; we leave it to the reader as an exercise.

# 3.5.3 performance guarantees

now, we turn to characterizing the clustering performance of the above spectral algorithm. we shall focus attention on the mis-clustering rate as the performance metric. as the cluster labels in  $[r]$  can be arbitrarily permuted, the mis-clustering rate associated with the labels  $\{\widehat{\xi}_i\}$  returned by our algorithm is defined as

$$
\ell_ {\mathrm {m i s}} \left(\left\{\widehat {\xi} _ {i} \right\}, \left\{\xi_ {i} ^ {\star} \right\}\right) := \min  _ {\phi \in \pi} \frac {1}{n} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \mathbb {1} \left\{\phi (\widehat {\xi} _ {i}) \neq \xi_ {i} ^ {\star} \right\},
$$

where  $\pi$  is the set of permutations of  $[r]$ . in words, this metric captures the average number of mislabeled data points, after accounting for global permutation. for notational convenience, we shall set  $m^{\star} \coloneqq \mathbb{e}\left[x^{\top}x\right]$  and  $e \coloneqq x^{\top}x - \mathbb{e}\left[x^{\top}x\right]$  throughout this section.

the first step towards analyzing the statistical accuracy of the spectral algorithm lies in developing a perturbation bound on  $\| uu^{\top} - u^{\star}u^{\star \top}\|$ , where  $u^{\star}$  (cf. (3.44)) represents the leading rank- $r$  eigenspace of  $m^{\star}$ . this can be accomplished via the davis-kahan theorem, which requires us to first control the size of the perturbation  $\pmb{e}$ .

lemma 3.9. consider the settings in section 3.5.1, and suppose  $p \gtrsim r\log^3 (n + p)$ . then with probability at least  $1 - o((n + p)^{-10})$ , one has

$$
\| \boldsymbol {e} \| \lesssim \frac {\delta n \sqrt {\log (n + p)}}{\sqrt {r}} + \sqrt {n p \log (n + p)} + n \log^ {2} (n + p).
$$

the proof of this lemma can be found in section 3.5.4. equipped with the above perturbation bound, we are ready to present our statistical guarantees for spectral clustering.

theorem 3.10. consider the setting and assumptions in section 3.5.1, and suppose that  $r = o(1)$  and  $p \gtrsim \log^3 n$ . with probability at least  $1 - o(p^{-10})$ , the mis-clustering rate of the spectral algorithm in section 3.5.2 achieves

$$
\ell_ {\operatorname {m i s}} \left(\left\{\widehat {\xi} _ {i} \right\}, \left\{\xi_ {i} ^ {\star} \right\}\right) = o (1),
$$

with the proviso that

$$
\log (n + p) = o (\delta) \quad \text {a n d} \quad \left(\frac {p \log (n + p)}{n}\right) ^ {1 / 4} = o (\delta). \tag {3.46}
$$

before embarking on the proof of this theorem, we discuss briefly the implications of this theorem. in order to ensure a vanishingly small mis-clustering rate, it suffices for the center separation  $\delta$  to exceed

$$
\delta \gtrsim \left\{ \begin{array}{l l} \mathrm {p o l y} \log (n + p), & \text {i f} p \leq n, \\ \left(\frac {p}{n}\right) ^ {1 / 4} \mathrm {p o l y} \log (n + p), & \text {i f} p \geq n. \end{array} \right.
$$

this separation condition matches the minimax lower bound up to some logarithmic term (cai and zhang, 2018; ndaoud, 2018). in particular, in the high-dimensional case where  $p \geq n$ , the required separation condition changes fairly gracefully with the aspect ratio  $p / n$ .

remark 3.8. as alluded to previously, assumption 3.2 can be significantly relaxed. for example, the gaussianity assumption therein is unnecessary; (almost) exact clustering is plausible once the minimum center separation exceeds a certain threshold, regardless of how  $\{\pmb{\theta}_i^{\star}\}$  are generated. to achieve this generality, however, the algorithm might need to be properly modified. roughly speaking, in addition to  $\pmb{u}$ , it is sensible to also exploit information contained in the eigenvalues of  $\pmb{x}^{\top}\pmb{x}$  (which is crucial for, say, the scenario where all centers  $\{\pmb{\theta}_i^{\star}\}$  are perfectly aligned except for the scaling factors). we recommend the readers to löffler et al. (2021) for detailed discussions.

proof of theorem 3.10. the proof consists of two steps: controlling the perturbation  $\| uu^{\top} - u^{\star}u^{\star \top}\|_{\mathrm{f}}$  (and hence  $\| y - y^{\star}\|_{\mathrm{f}}$ ), and demonstrating that the follow-up  $k$ -means performs well.

the first step is to control  $\| \pmb{u}\pmb{u}^{\top} - \pmb{u}^{\star}\pmb{u}^{\star \top}\|_{\mathrm{f}}$ , built upon lemma 3.9 and a lower bound on the spectral gap of  $m^{\star}$ . observe that

$$
\lambda_ {r} (m ^ {\star}) - \lambda_ {r + 1} (m ^ {\star}) = \lambda_ {\min} (f ^ {\star} \theta^ {\star \top} \theta^ {\star} f ^ {\star \top}) = \frac {n}{r} \lambda_ {\min} (\theta^ {\star \top} \theta^ {\star}),
$$

where the last identity holds since  $\pmb{f}^{\star}\pmb{f}^{\star\top} = \frac{n}{r}\pmb{i}_r$  according to the definition (3.41). this motivates us to look at the spectral property of  $\theta^{\star\top}\theta^{\star}$ . given that  $\theta^{\star}$  is composed of i.i.d. gaussian entries (cf. assumption 3.2), invoking the bound (3.28c) with proper rescaling gives

$$
\left\| \theta^ {\star^ {\top}} \theta^ {\star} - \mathbb {e} \left[ \theta^ {\star^ {\top}} \theta^ {\star} \right] \right\| \lesssim \delta^ {2} \left(\sqrt {\frac {r \log p}{p}} + \frac {r \log^ {2} p}{p}\right) \leq \frac {\delta^ {2}}{4}
$$

with probability exceeding  $1 - o(p^{-10})$ , provided that  $p \geq c_2 r \log^2 p$  for some sufficiently large constant  $c_2 > 0$ . further, it is self-evident that  $\mathbb{e}[\theta^{\star \top} \theta^{\star}] = \frac{1}{2} \delta^2 i_r$ . weyl's inequality (see lemma 2.2) then guarantees that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \lambda_ {\min } \left(\theta^ {\star^ {\top}} \theta^ {\star}\right) \geq \lambda_ {\min } \left(\mathbb {e} \left[ \theta^ {\star^ {\top}} \theta^ {\star} \right]\right) - \left\| \theta^ {\star^ {\top}} \theta^ {\star} - \mathbb {e} \left[ \theta^ {\star^ {\top}} \theta^ {\star} \right] \right\| \\ \geq \delta^ {2} / 2 - \delta^ {2} / 4 = \delta^ {2} / 4. \\ \end{array}
$$

combine the preceding inequalities to arrive at

$$
\lambda_ {r} \left(m ^ {\star}\right) - \lambda_ {r + 1} \left(m ^ {\star}\right) = \frac {n}{r} \lambda_ {\min } \left(\theta^ {\star \top} \theta^ {\star}\right) \geq \frac {n \delta^ {2}}{4 r}. \tag {3.47}
$$

by virtue of lemma 3.9 and (3.47), if the following condition

$$
\delta \geq c _ {1} \max \left\{\left(\frac {r ^ {2} p \log (n + p)}{n}\right) ^ {1 / 4}, \sqrt {r} \log (n + p) \right\}
$$

holds for some large enough constant  $c_1 > 0$ , then it is guaranteed that  $\| \pmb{e} \| \leq (1 - 1 / \sqrt{2})(\lambda_r(\pmb{m}^\star) - \lambda_{r+1}(\pmb{m}^\star))$ . this in turn allows us to invoke the davis-kahan theorem (namely, corollary 2.8) to obtain

$$
\left\| \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\top} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star^ {\top}} \right\| _ {\mathrm {f}} \leq \frac {\sqrt {2 r} \| \boldsymbol {e} \|}{\lambda_ {r} (\boldsymbol {m} ^ {\star}) - \lambda_ {r + 1} (\boldsymbol {m} ^ {\star})} \leq \sqrt {r} \varepsilon \tag {3.48}
$$

with probability exceeding  $1 - o(p^{-8})$ , where the last line arises from (3.47) and lemma 3.9, and

$$
\varepsilon \asymp \frac {\delta \sqrt {r \log (n + p)} + r \sqrt {\frac {p \log (n + p)}{n}} + r \log^ {2} (n + p)}{\delta^ {2}}.
$$

from the construction of  $\mathbf{y}$  and (3.45), one can propagate the bound (3.48) to  $\| \mathbf{y} - \mathbf{y}^{\star}\|_{\mathrm{f}}$  as follows:

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \boldsymbol {y} - \boldsymbol {y} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {\mathrm {f}} ^ {2} \stackrel {\text {(i)}} {=} \left\| \mathcal {p} \left(\sqrt {\frac {n}{r}} \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\top}\right) - \boldsymbol {y} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {\mathrm {f}} ^ {2} \stackrel {\text {(i i)}} {\leq} 4 \left\| \sqrt {\frac {n}{r}} \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\top} - \boldsymbol {y} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {\mathrm {f}} ^ {2} \\ = \frac {4 n}{r} \| \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\top} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star \top} \| _ {\mathrm {f}} ^ {2} \lesssim \varepsilon^ {2} n. \tag {3.49} \\ \end{array}
$$

here, the first identity (i) holds since the operator  $\mathcal{p}$  is invariant to global scaling. regarding the inequality (ii), it follows from standard inequality regarding euclidean projection (e.g., soltanolkotabi (2019, lemma 15)), which we postpone to the end of this proof.

the next step then amounts to translating the perturbation bound (3.49) into clustering accuracy guarantees (after  $k$ -means is applied). this is accomplished through the following key lemma, to be established in section 3.5.4.

lemma 3.11. suppose that the matrix  $\mathbf{y}$  obtained in the spectral algorithm in section 3.5.2 satisfies

$$
\left\| \mathbf {y} - \mathbf {y} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {\mathrm {f}} ^ {2} \leq \varepsilon^ {2} n, \tag {3.50}
$$

where  $\varepsilon > 0$  is a quantity obeying  $\varepsilon \leq c_3 r^{-4}$  for some sufficiently small constant  $c_3 > 0$ . then the mis-clustering rate obeys

$$
\ell_ {\operatorname {m i s}} \left(\left\{\widehat {\xi} _ {i} \right\}, \left\{\xi_ {i} ^ {\star} \right\}\right) \leq 2 r \varepsilon^ {1 / 4}.
$$

as a consequence of lemma 3.11, the mis-clustering rate is  $o(1)$  as long as  $\varepsilon r^4 = o(1)$ , a condition that is guaranteed under the assumptions (3.46) and  $r = o(1)$ . this establishes theorem 3.10.

proof of the inequality (ii) in (3.49). for any vector  $\pmb{v}$  residing in the unit sphere and any other vector  $\pmb{w}$ , we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \mathcal {p} (\boldsymbol {w}) - \boldsymbol {w} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} = \left\| \mathcal {p} (\boldsymbol {w}) - \boldsymbol {v} + \boldsymbol {v} - \boldsymbol {w} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} \\ = \| \mathcal {p} (\boldsymbol {w}) - \boldsymbol {v} \| _ {2} ^ {2} + \| \boldsymbol {v} - \boldsymbol {w} \| _ {2} ^ {2} + 2 \langle \mathcal {p} (\boldsymbol {w}) - \boldsymbol {v}, \boldsymbol {v} - \boldsymbol {w} \rangle \\ \geq \| \mathcal {p} (\boldsymbol {w}) - \boldsymbol {v} \| _ {2} ^ {2} + \| \mathcal {p} (\boldsymbol {w}) - \boldsymbol {w} \| _ {2} ^ {2} + 2 \langle \mathcal {p} (\boldsymbol {w}) - \boldsymbol {v}, \boldsymbol {v} - \boldsymbol {w} \rangle , \\ \end{array}
$$

where the last inequality follows since  $\mathcal{p}$  denotes the projection onto the unit sphere and  $\pmb{v}$  lies in the unit sphere. cancelling out the common term  $\| \mathcal{p}(\pmb{w}) - \pmb{w}\|_2^2$  and invoking cauchy-schwarz lead to

$$
\| \mathcal {p} (\boldsymbol {w}) - \boldsymbol {v} \| _ {2} ^ {2} \leq - 2 \langle \mathcal {p} (\boldsymbol {w}) - \boldsymbol {v}, \boldsymbol {v} - \boldsymbol {w} \rangle \leq 2 \| \mathcal {p} (\boldsymbol {w}) - \boldsymbol {v} \| _ {2} \| \boldsymbol {v} - \boldsymbol {w} \| _ {2},
$$

and therefore,

$$
\| \mathcal {p} (\boldsymbol {w}) - \boldsymbol {v} \| _ {2} \leq 2 \| \boldsymbol {w} - \boldsymbol {v} \| _ {2}, \quad \text {o r} \quad \| \mathcal {p} (\boldsymbol {w}) - \boldsymbol {v} \| _ {2} ^ {2} \leq 4 \| \boldsymbol {w} - \boldsymbol {v} \| _ {2} ^ {2}.
$$

this inequality clearly extends to the matrix counterpart, thus establishing the claimed result.

# 3.5.4 proof of auxiliary lemmas

proof of lemma 3.9. to begin with, let us decompose  $\pmb{e}$  as follows

$$
\begin{array}{l} \boldsymbol {e} = \boldsymbol {x} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {x} - \mathbb {e} \left[ \boldsymbol {x} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {x} \right] = \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} ^ {\star} \boldsymbol {f} ^ {\star^ {\top}} + z\right) ^ {\top} \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} ^ {\star} \boldsymbol {f} ^ {\star^ {\top}} + z\right) - \mathbb {e} \left[ \boldsymbol {x} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {x} \right] \\ = \boldsymbol {f} ^ {\star} \boldsymbol {\theta} ^ {\star \top} \boldsymbol {z} + \boldsymbol {z} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\theta} ^ {\star} \boldsymbol {f} ^ {\star \top} + \boldsymbol {z} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {z} - p \boldsymbol {i} _ {n}, \tag {3.51} \\ \end{array}
$$

where we have used the notation in (3.40) and (3.41), as well as the identity (3.42). as it turns out, similar terms have already been controlled in the proof for pca (see section 3.3.4). more precisely, the first term in (3.51) obeys

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \boldsymbol {f} ^ {\star} \theta^ {\star \top} \boldsymbol {z} \right\| \stackrel {{\text {(i)}}} {{=}} \sqrt {\frac {n}{r}} \left\| \theta^ {\star \top} \boldsymbol {z} \right\| \stackrel {{\text {(i i)}}} {{\lesssim}} \sqrt {\frac {n}{r}} \cdot \frac {\delta}{\sqrt {p}} \sqrt {n p \log (n + p)} \\ \succ \frac {\delta n \sqrt {\log (n + p)}}{\sqrt {r}} \\ \end{array}
$$

with probability exceeding  $1 - o\big((n + p)^{-10}\big)$ , provided that  $p \gtrsim r \log^3 (n + p)$ . here, the first relation (i) holds true since  $\sqrt{\frac{r}{n}} f^{\star}$  contains orthonormal columns and the spectral norm is unitarily invariant, while (ii) invokes the high-probability bound (3.28a). when it comes to the third term of (3.51), the bound (3.28c) readily implies that

$$
\left\| \boldsymbol {z} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {z} - p \boldsymbol {i} _ {n} \right\| \lesssim \sqrt {n p \log (n + p)} + n \log^ {2} (n + p)
$$

with probability at least  $1 - o((n + p)^{-10})$ . substituting the preceding two bounds into (3.51) and applying the triangle inequality, we reach

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \boldsymbol {e} \right\| \leq 2 \left\| \boldsymbol {f} ^ {\star} \boldsymbol {\theta} ^ {\star^ {\top}} \boldsymbol {z} \right\| + \left\| \boldsymbol {z} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {z} - p \boldsymbol {i} _ {n} \right\| \\ \lesssim \frac {\delta n \sqrt {\log (n + p)}}{\sqrt {r}} + \sqrt {n p \log (n + p)} + n \log^ {2} (n + p). \\ \end{array}
$$

proof of lemma 3.11 (analysis for  $k$ -means). given the class labels  $\{\xi_i\}_{i=1}^n$ , the optimization of the cluster centers  $\{\pmb{\vartheta}_i\}_{i=1}^r$  in the  $k$ -means formulation (3.43) is achieved by the sample means of each cluster. thus, the  $k$ -means formulation (3.43) can be equivalently posed as solving

$$
\underset {\mathcal {c} \in \xi} {\text {m i n i m i z e}} \sum_ {l = 1} ^ {r} \sum_ {i \in \mathcal {c} _ {l}} \left\| \boldsymbol {y} _ {i} - \frac {1}{| \mathcal {c} _ {l} |} \sum_ {j \in \mathcal {c} _ {l}} \boldsymbol {y} _ {j} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} \tag {3.52}
$$

where  $\mathcal{c} = \{\mathcal{c}_1, \dots, \mathcal{c}_r\}$  represents the cluster assignment, and  $\xi$  denotes the set of all  $r$ -partitions of  $[n]$  (i.e.,  $r$  disjoint subsets whose union equals  $[n]$ ).

in order to tackle this formulation, a key ingredient of the proof lies in the following deviation bound that allows one to replace  $\pmb{y}_i$  with the truth  $\pmb{y}_i^\star$ , as long as the cluster size is sufficiently large.

claim 3.1. consider any set  $s \subseteq [n]$  with cardinality  $c_{s}n$  for some quantity  $c_{s} > 0$ . suppose that (3.50) holds with  $\varepsilon \leq c_s^2$ . then one has

$$
\left| \sum_ {i \in \mathcal {s}} \left\| \boldsymbol {y} _ {i} - \frac {1}{| \mathcal {s} |} \sum_ {j \in \mathcal {s}} \boldsymbol {y} _ {j} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} - \sum_ {i \in \mathcal {s}} \left\| \boldsymbol {y} _ {i} ^ {\star} - \frac {1}{| \mathcal {s} |} \sum_ {j \in \mathcal {s}} \boldsymbol {y} _ {j} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} \right| \leq 6 c _ {s} \sqrt {\varepsilon} n. \tag {3.53}
$$

with claim 3.1 in place, we are positioned to establish lemma 3.11 by contradiction; that is, we intend to demonstrate that any cluster assignment that differs too much from the ground-truth clusters cannot possibly be the  $k$ -means solution. in what follows, we denote by  $\mathcal{c}_l^\star$  the  $l$ -th ground-truth cluster  $(1 \leq l \leq r)$ , and let  $\{\mathcal{c}_1, \dots, \mathcal{c}_r\}$  represent the minimizer of (3.52) whenever it is clear from the context.

step 1: developing an upper bound on (3.52). to begin with, we derive an upper bound on the optimal objective value of (3.52), which serves as a reference in assessing the (sub)-optimality of other cluster assignments. by virtue of claim 3.1 and the assumption  $|\mathcal{c}_l^\star| = n / r$ , one has

$$
\left| \sum_ {i \in \mathcal {c} _ {l} ^ {\star}} \left\| \boldsymbol {y} _ {i} - \frac {1}{| \mathcal {c} _ {l} ^ {\star} |} \sum_ {j \in \mathcal {c} _ {l} ^ {\star}} \boldsymbol {y} _ {j} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} - \sum_ {i \in \mathcal {c} _ {l} ^ {\star}} \left\| \boldsymbol {y} _ {i} ^ {\star} - \frac {1}{| \mathcal {c} _ {l} ^ {\star} |} \sum_ {j \in \mathcal {c} _ {l} ^ {\star}} \boldsymbol {y} _ {j} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} \right| \leq \frac {6 \sqrt {\varepsilon} n}{r},
$$

with the proviso that  $\varepsilon \leq 1 / r^2$ . note that by construction, for the "ideal" fitting, one has  $\sum_{i\in \mathcal{c}_l^\star}\| \pmb {y}_i^\star -$ $\frac{1}{|\mathcal{c}_l^\star|}\sum_{j\in \mathcal{c}_l^\star}\pmb {y}_j^\star \| _2^2 = 0$ . using this fact and summing the above inequality over all  $1\leq l\leq r$ , we arrive at

$$
\sum_ {l = 1} ^ {r} \sum_ {i \in \mathcal {c} _ {l} ^ {\star}} \left\| \boldsymbol {y} _ {i} - \frac {1}{\left| \mathcal {c} _ {l} ^ {\star} \right|} \sum_ {j \in \mathcal {c} _ {l} ^ {\star}} \boldsymbol {y} _ {j} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} \leq 6 \sqrt {\varepsilon} n. \tag {3.54}
$$

consequently, due to the assumed optimality of  $\mathcal{c}$  w.r.t. (3.52), replacing  $\{\mathcal{c}_l^\star\}_{l=1}^r$  in (3.54) by  $\{\mathcal{c}_l\}_{l=1}^r$  can only further improve the objective value:

$$
\begin{array}{l} \sum_ {l = 1} ^ {r} \sum_ {i \in \mathcal {c} _ {l}} \left\| \boldsymbol {y} _ {i} - \frac {1}{| \mathcal {c} _ {l} |} \sum_ {j \in \mathcal {c} _ {l}} \boldsymbol {y} _ {j} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} \leq \sum_ {l = 1} ^ {r} \sum_ {i \in \mathcal {c} _ {l} ^ {\star}} \left\| \boldsymbol {y} _ {i} - \frac {1}{| \mathcal {c} _ {l} ^ {\star} |} \sum_ {j \in \mathcal {c} _ {l} ^ {\star}} \boldsymbol {y} _ {j} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} \\ \leq 6 \sqrt {\varepsilon} n. \tag {3.55} \\ \end{array}
$$

step 2: showing that no cluster can be too large. suppose that there exists a cluster  $\mathcal{c}_l$  ( $1 \leq l \leq r$ ) that is too large in the sense that

$$
\left| \mathcal {c} _ {l} \right| \geq \frac {(1 + c _ {\varepsilon}) n}{r} \tag {3.56}
$$

for some quantity  $c_{\varepsilon} > 0$ . we would like to show that this is impossible unless  $c_{\varepsilon}$  is small; in fact, in light of claim 3.1, we need only to establish a lower bound on the second term in (3.53) (with

$s = \mathcal{c}_l)$  so that it leads to a contradiction with the upper bound (3.55). towards this end, we start with the elementary decomposition of the sum of squared errors:

$$
\begin{array}{l} \sum_ {i \in \mathcal {c} _ {l}} \left\| \boldsymbol {y} _ {i} ^ {\star} - \frac {1}{| \mathcal {c} _ {l} |} \sum_ {j \in \mathcal {c} _ {l}} \boldsymbol {y} _ {j} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} = \left(\sum_ {i \in \mathcal {c} _ {l}} \| \boldsymbol {y} _ {i} ^ {\star} \| _ {2} ^ {2}\right) - | \mathcal {c} _ {l} | \cdot \left\| \frac {1}{| \mathcal {c} _ {l} |} \sum_ {j \in \mathcal {c} _ {l}} \boldsymbol {y} _ {j} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} \\ = \left| \mathcal {c} _ {l} \right| \left(1 - \left\| \frac {1}{\left| \mathcal {c} _ {l} \right|} \sum_ {j \in \mathcal {c} _ {l}} \boldsymbol {y} _ {j} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2}\right), \tag {3.57} \\ \end{array}
$$

where we have invoked the fact that  $\| \pmb{y}_i^\star \|_2 = 1$ . it thus comes down to controlling  $\left\| \sum_{j \in \mathcal{c}_l} \pmb{y}_j^\star \right\|_2^2$ . for notational convenience, for any  $1 \leq \tau \leq r$ , we set  $n_{l,\tau} \coloneqq |\mathcal{c}_l \cap \mathcal{c}_\tau^\star|$  (namely, the number of points in  $\mathcal{c}_l$  coming from the  $\tau$ -th ground-truth cluster), and let  $\pmb{y}_{(\tau)}^\star$  represent the vector associated with the  $\tau$ -th cluster (namely,  $\pmb{y}_{(\tau)}^\star = \pmb{y}_j^\star$  for any  $j \in \mathcal{c}_\tau^\star$ ). armed with this set of notation, we can write

$$
\left\| \frac {1}{| \mathcal {c} _ {l} |} \sum_ {j \in \mathcal {c} _ {l}} \boldsymbol {y} _ {j} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} = \left\| \sum_ {\tau = 1} ^ {r} \frac {n _ {l , \tau}}{| \mathcal {c} _ {l} |} \boldsymbol {y} _ {(\tau)} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} = \sum_ {\tau = 1} ^ {r} \left\| \frac {n _ {l , \tau}}{| \mathcal {c} _ {l} |} \boldsymbol {y} _ {(\tau)} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} = \frac {\sum_ {\tau = 1} ^ {r} n _ {l , \tau} ^ {2}}{| \mathcal {c} _ {l} | ^ {2}}. \tag {3.58}
$$

here, the penultimate identity holds since  $\langle \pmb{y}_{(i)}^{\star},\pmb{y}_{(j)}^{\star}\rangle = 0$  for any  $i\neq j$ , while the last relation relies on the fact that  $\| \pmb{y}_{(\tau)}^{\star}\|_{2} = 1$ . using  $0\leq n_{l,\tau}\leq n / r$  and  $\sum_{\tau = 1}^{r}n_{l,\tau} = |\mathcal{c}_l|$ , we have

$$
(3. 5 8) \leq \frac {(\max _ {\tau} n _ {l , \tau}) (\sum_ {\tau} n _ {l , \tau})}{| \mathcal {c} _ {l} | ^ {2}} \leq \frac {(n / r) \cdot | \mathcal {c} _ {l} |}{| \mathcal {c} _ {l} | ^ {2}} \leq \frac {1}{1 + c _ {\varepsilon}},
$$

where the first inequality comes from the basic fact that  $\| \pmb{a}\| _2^2\leq \| \pmb {a}\|_\infty \| \pmb {a}\| _1$  for any vector  $\pmb{a}$ , and the last relation arises from the assumed cardinality constraint on  $\mathcal{c}_l$  (cf. (3.56)).

substitution into (3.57) yields

$$
\sum_ {i \in \mathcal {c} _ {l}} \left\| \boldsymbol {y} _ {i} ^ {\star} - \frac {1}{| \mathcal {c} _ {l} |} \sum_ {j \in \mathcal {c} _ {l}} \boldsymbol {y} _ {j} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} \geq | \mathcal {c} _ {l} | \left(1 - \frac {1}{1 + c _ {\varepsilon}}\right) \geq c _ {\varepsilon} \frac {n}{r}, \tag {3.59}
$$

where the last inequality again arises from the assumption (3.56). this in turn demonstrates that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \sum_ {l = 1} ^ {r} \sum_ {i \in \mathcal {c} _ {l}} \left\| \boldsymbol {y} _ {i} - \frac {1}{| \mathcal {c} _ {l} |} \sum_ {j \in \mathcal {c} _ {l}} \boldsymbol {y} _ {j} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} \geq \sum_ {i \in \mathcal {c} _ {l}} \left\| \boldsymbol {y} _ {i} - \frac {1}{| \mathcal {c} _ {l} |} \sum_ {j \in \mathcal {c} _ {l}} \boldsymbol {y} _ {j} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} \\ \geq \sum_ {i \in \mathcal {c} _ {l}} \left\| \boldsymbol {y} _ {i} ^ {\star} - \frac {1}{| \mathcal {c} _ {l} |} \sum_ {j \in \mathcal {c} _ {l}} \boldsymbol {y} _ {j} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} - 6 \frac {| \mathcal {c} _ {l} |}{n} \sqrt {\varepsilon} n \tag {3.60} \\ \geq c _ {\varepsilon} \frac {n}{r} - 6 \sqrt {\varepsilon} n > 6 \sqrt {\varepsilon} n, \\ \end{array}
$$

where (3.60) results from claim 3.1 when  $\varepsilon \leq 1 / r^2$ , and the last inequality follows as long as  $12\sqrt{\varepsilon} < c_{\varepsilon} / r$ . comparing this with (3.55) leads to contradiction with the optimality assumption of  $\{\mathcal{c}_l\}$ .

step 3: showing that no cluster can be too small. suppose now that there exists a cluster  $\mathcal{c}_i$  ( $1 \leq i \leq r$ ) obeying

$$
| \mathcal {c} _ {i} | \leq \frac {\left(1 - c _ {\varepsilon} (r - 1)\right) n}{r}.
$$

then from the pigeonhole principle, one can find another cluster  $\mathcal{c}_l$  ( $1 \leq l \leq r$ ) with cardinality exceeding

$$
| \mathcal {c} _ {l} | \geq \frac {(1 + c _ {\varepsilon}) n}{r};
$$

otherwise the total size obeys  $\sum_{k=1}^{r} |\mathcal{c}_k| < \frac{(1 - c_\varepsilon(r-1))n}{r} + (r-1) \frac{(1 + c_\varepsilon)n}{r} \leq n$  and  $\{\mathcal{c}_l\}$  is infeasible. the above condition on  $|\mathcal{c}_l|$  coincides with the assumption (3.56) in step 2, which, as a result of previous arguments, cannot possibly hold. to conclude, for all  $1 \leq i \leq r$ , one necessarily has

$$
\left| \mathcal {c} _ {i} \right| > \frac {\left(1 - c _ {\varepsilon} (r - 1)\right) n}{r} \geq \frac {\left(1 - c _ {\varepsilon} r\right) n}{r}. \tag {3.61}
$$

step 4: showing that each  $\mathcal{c}_l$  is mainly composed of points from a true (and distinct) cluster. suppose that there exists a cluster  $\mathcal{c}_l$  ( $1 \leq l \leq r$ ) whose dominant component obeys

$$
\max _ {1 \leq \tau \leq r} n _ {l, \tau} \leq (1 - 2 r c _ {\varepsilon}) n / r,
$$

where we recall that  $n_{l,\tau} = |\mathcal{c}_l\cap \mathcal{c}_\tau^\star |.$  under this assumption, we have

$$
(3. 5 8) \leq \frac {\left(\max _ {\tau} n _ {l , \tau}\right) \left(\sum_ {\tau} n _ {l , \tau}\right)}{\left| \mathcal {c} _ {l} \right| ^ {2}} \leq \frac {\left[ (1 - 2 r c _ {\varepsilon}) n / r \right] \cdot \left| \mathcal {c} _ {l} \right|}{\left| \mathcal {c} _ {l} \right| ^ {2}} \leq \frac {1 - 2 r c _ {\varepsilon}}{1 - r c _ {\varepsilon}},
$$

where the last inequality relies on the lower bound (3.61). substitution into (3.57) gives

$$
\begin{array}{l} \sum_ {i \in \mathcal {c} _ {l}} \left\| \boldsymbol {y} _ {i} ^ {\star} - \frac {1}{| \mathcal {c} _ {l} |} \sum_ {j \in \mathcal {c} _ {l}} \boldsymbol {y} _ {j} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} \geq | \mathcal {c} _ {l} | \left(1 - \frac {1 - 2 r c _ {\varepsilon}}{1 - r c _ {\varepsilon}}\right) \overset {\mathrm {(i)}} {\geq} \frac {(1 - r c _ {\varepsilon}) n}{r} \cdot \frac {r c _ {\varepsilon}}{1 - r c _ {\varepsilon}} \\ = c _ {\varepsilon} n > 1 2 \sqrt {\varepsilon} n, \\ \end{array}
$$

where (i) arises again from (3.61), and the last relation is valid once  $c_{\varepsilon} > 12\sqrt{\varepsilon}$ . this taken collectively with the inequality (3.60) yields

$$
\begin{array}{l} \sum_ {l = 1} ^ {r} \sum_ {i \in \mathcal {c} _ {l}} \left\| \boldsymbol {y} _ {i} - \frac {1}{| \mathcal {c} _ {l} |} \sum_ {j \in \mathcal {c} _ {l}} \boldsymbol {y} _ {j} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} \geq \sum_ {i \in \mathcal {c} _ {l}} \left\| \boldsymbol {y} _ {i} ^ {\star} - \frac {1}{| \mathcal {c} _ {l} |} \sum_ {j \in \mathcal {c} _ {l}} \boldsymbol {y} _ {j} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} - 6 \sqrt {\varepsilon} n \\ > 6 \sqrt {\varepsilon} n, \\ \end{array}
$$

which, however, contradicts the upper bound (3.55). consequently, the dominant component in every cluster  $1 \leq l \leq r$  must obey

$$
\max  _ {1 \leq \tau \leq r} n _ {l, \tau} > (1 - 2 r c _ {\varepsilon}) n / r. \tag {3.62}
$$

in particular, if  $2rc_{\varepsilon} < 1/2$ , then  $\max_{1 \leq \tau \leq r} n_{l,\tau} > n/(2r)$ . an immediate consequence is that: the dominant components of the clusters  $\{\mathcal{c}_l\}$  must come from distinct ground-truth clusters.

step 5: putting all this together. armed with the preceding bound (3.62) and the remark thereafter, it is straightforward to verify the following result on the mis-clustering rate:

$$
\ell_ {\mathfrak {m i s}} \big (\{\widehat {\xi} _ {i} \}, \{\xi_ {i} ^ {\star} \} \big) \leq 1 - \frac {\sum_ {l = 1} ^ {r} \max _ {1 \leq \tau \leq r} n _ {l , \tau}}{n} \leq 2 r c _ {\varepsilon}.
$$

finally, setting  $c_{\varepsilon} = \varepsilon^{1/4}$  leads to the advertised result, provided that  $\varepsilon \leq c_3 r^{-4}$  for some sufficiently small constant  $c_3 > 0$ .

proof of claim 3.1. before proceeding, let us take a quick look at how many columns of  $\mathbf{y}$  might deviate considerably from their counterparts in  $\mathbf{y}^{\star}$ . to be precise, let us introduce the following set

$$
\mathcal {n} _ {\text {l a r g e}} := \left\{i \mid \| \boldsymbol {y} _ {i} - \boldsymbol {y} _ {i} ^ {\star} \| _ {2} ^ {2} \geq \varepsilon \right\}. \tag {3.63}
$$

clearly, its cardinality is necessarily bounded above by

$$
\left| \mathcal {n} _ {\text {l a r g e}} \right| \leq \frac {\left\| \boldsymbol {y} - \boldsymbol {y} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {\mathrm {f}} ^ {2}}{\varepsilon} \leq \frac {\varepsilon^ {2} n}{\varepsilon} = \varepsilon n. \tag {3.64}
$$

the starting point of the proof is the elementary identities

$$
\begin{array}{l} \sum_ {i \in \mathcal {s}} \left\| \boldsymbol {y} _ {i} - \frac {1}{| \mathcal {s} |} \sum_ {j \in \mathcal {s}} \boldsymbol {y} _ {j} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} - \sum_ {i \in \mathcal {s}} \left\| \boldsymbol {y} _ {i} ^ {\star} - \frac {1}{| \mathcal {s} |} \sum_ {j \in \mathcal {s}} \boldsymbol {y} _ {j} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} \\ = \sum_ {i \in \mathcal {s}} \| \boldsymbol {y} _ {i} \| _ {2} ^ {2} - | \mathcal {s} | \cdot \left\| \frac {1}{| \mathcal {s} |} \sum_ {j \in \mathcal {s}} \boldsymbol {y} _ {j} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} - \left(\sum_ {i \in \mathcal {s}} \| \boldsymbol {y} _ {i} ^ {\star} \| _ {2} ^ {2} - | \mathcal {s} | \cdot \left\| \frac {1}{| \mathcal {s} |} \sum_ {j \in \mathcal {s}} \boldsymbol {y} _ {j} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2}\right) \\ = | \mathcal {s} | \cdot \left(\left\| \frac {1}{| \mathcal {s} |} \sum_ {j \in \mathcal {s}} \boldsymbol {y} _ {j} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} - \left\| \frac {1}{| \mathcal {s} |} \sum_ {j \in \mathcal {s}} \boldsymbol {y} _ {j} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2}\right), \tag {3.65} \\ \end{array}
$$

where the last inequality follows from the fact that  $\| \pmb{y}_i\| _2 = \| \pmb{y}_i^\star \| _2 = 1$ . to bound the right-hand side of (3.65), we make the observation that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \frac {1}{| \mathcal {s} |} \sum_ {j \in \mathcal {s}} \left(\boldsymbol {y} _ {j} - \boldsymbol {y} _ {j} ^ {\star}\right) \right\| _ {2} \leq \frac {1}{| \mathcal {s} |} \sum_ {j \in \mathcal {s} \backslash \mathcal {n} _ {\text {l a r g e}}} \| \boldsymbol {y} _ {j} - \boldsymbol {y} _ {j} ^ {\star} \| _ {2} + \frac {1}{| \mathcal {s} |} \sum_ {j \in \mathcal {s} \cap \mathcal {n} _ {\text {l a r g e}}} \| \boldsymbol {y} _ {j} - \boldsymbol {y} _ {j} ^ {\star} \| _ {2} \\ \stackrel {\text {(i)}} {\leq} \sqrt {\varepsilon} + \frac {2 \left| \mathcal {n} _ {\text {l a r g e}} \right|}{| s |} \stackrel {\text {(i i)}} {\leq} \sqrt {\varepsilon} + \frac {2 \varepsilon}{c _ {s}} \leq 3 \sqrt {\varepsilon}. \tag {3.66} \\ \end{array}
$$

here, (i) holds true since any column outside  $\mathcal{n}_{\mathrm{large}}$  satisfies  $\| \pmb{y}_j - \pmb{y}_j^\star \|_2 < \sqrt{\varepsilon}$ , and any column coming from  $\mathcal{n}_{\mathrm{large}}$  obeys  $\| \pmb{y}_j - \pmb{y}_j^\star \|_2 \leq \| \pmb{y}_j \|_2 + \| \pmb{y}_j^\star \|_2 = 2$ ; (ii) follows from (3.64) and the assumption  $|\mathcal{s}| = c_s n$ ; and the last inequality relies on the assumption  $\varepsilon \leq c_s^2$ . in addition,

$$
\left\| \frac {1}{| \mathcal {s} |} \sum_ {j \in \mathcal {s}} \boldsymbol {y} _ {j} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {2} \leq \frac {1}{| \mathcal {s} |} \sum_ {j \in \mathcal {s}} \| \boldsymbol {y} _ {j} ^ {\star} \| _ {2} = 1; \tag {3.67a}
$$

$$
\left\| \frac {1}{| \mathcal {s} |} \sum_ {j \in \mathcal {s}} \boldsymbol {y} _ {j} \right\| _ {2} \leq \frac {1}{| \mathcal {s} |} \sum_ {j \in \mathcal {s}} \| \boldsymbol {y} _ {j} \| _ {2} = 1. \tag {3.67b}
$$

combining (3.66) and (3.67) and applying the triangle inequality, we arrive at

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left| \left\| \frac {1}{| \mathcal {s} |} \sum_ {j \in \mathcal {s}} \boldsymbol {y} _ {j} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} - \left\| \frac {1}{| \mathcal {s} |} \sum_ {j \in \mathcal {s}} \boldsymbol {y} _ {j} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} \right| \\ = \left| \left\| \frac {1}{| \mathcal {s} |} \sum_ {j \in \mathcal {s}} \boldsymbol {y} _ {j} \right\| _ {2} - \left\| \frac {1}{| \mathcal {s} |} \sum_ {j \in \mathcal {s}} \boldsymbol {y} _ {j} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {2} \right| \cdot \left| \left\| \frac {1}{| \mathcal {s} |} \sum_ {j \in \mathcal {s}} \boldsymbol {y} _ {j} \right\| _ {2} + \left\| \frac {1}{| \mathcal {s} |} \sum_ {j \in \mathcal {s}} \boldsymbol {y} _ {j} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {2} \right| \\ \leq 2 \left| \left\| \frac {1}{| \mathcal {s} |} \sum_ {j \in \mathcal {s}} \boldsymbol {y} _ {j} \right\| _ {2} - \left\| \frac {1}{| \mathcal {s} |} \sum_ {j \in \mathcal {s}} \boldsymbol {y} _ {j} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {2} \right| \\ \leq 2 \left\| \frac {1}{| \mathcal {s} |} \sum_ {j \in \mathcal {s}} \left(\boldsymbol {y} _ {j} - \boldsymbol {y} _ {j} ^ {\star}\right) \right\| _ {2} \leq 6 \sqrt {\varepsilon}. \tag {3.68} \\ \end{array}
$$

finally, plugging in (3.68) into (3.65), we conclude that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left| \sum_ {i \in \mathcal {s}} \left\| \boldsymbol {y} _ {i} - \frac {1}{| \mathcal {s} |} \sum_ {j \in \mathcal {s}} \boldsymbol {y} _ {j} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} - \sum_ {i \in \mathcal {s}} \left\| \boldsymbol {y} _ {i} ^ {\star} - \frac {1}{| \mathcal {s} |} \sum_ {j \in \mathcal {s}} \boldsymbol {y} _ {j} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} \right| \\ = | \mathcal {s} | \cdot \left| \left\| \frac {1}{| \mathcal {s} |} \sum_ {j \in \mathcal {s}} \boldsymbol {y} _ {j} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} - \left\| \frac {1}{| \mathcal {s} |} \sum_ {j \in \mathcal {s}} \boldsymbol {y} _ {j} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} \right| \\ \leq 6 \sqrt {\varepsilon} | \mathcal {s} | = 6 c _ {s} \sqrt {\varepsilon} n \\ \end{array}
$$

as claimed, where the last relation holds true since  $|\mathcal{s}| = c_{s}n$ .

# 3.6 ranking from pairwise comparisons

the ranking task—which seeks to identify a consistent ordering of several items based on (partially) revealed preference information about them—is encountered in numerous contexts including web search, crowd sourcing, social choice, peer grading, and so on (dwork et al., 2001; chen et al., 2013; caplin and nalebuff, 1991; shah et al., 2013). of particular interest is the "preference-based" observation model, in which we are only given relative comparisons of a few items (as opposed to individual scores of them). in practice, comparison data of this kind abound, partly because humans often find it easier to make a preference over two or a couple of items than to assign specific ratings to many individual ones. the emergence of crowdsourcing platforms such as amazon mechanical turk further widens the availability of comparison data, where binary judgments over pairs of items are often solicited from a pool of non-experts. in this section, we concentrate on pairwise comparisons and explore the potential of spectral methods for the ranking task.

# 3.6.1 the bradley-terry-luce model and assumptions

to formulate the problem in a statistically sound manner, we introduce a classical parametric model, called the bradley-terry-luce (btl) model (bradley and terry, 1952; ford jr, 1957; luce, 2012), to describe the generating process of pairwise comparisons.

latent preference scores. imagine that there are  $n$  items to be ranked. a key component of the btl model is the assignment of a latent preference score to each item; more concretely, the btl model hypothesizes on the existence of an unseen preference score vector

$$
\boldsymbol {w} ^ {\star} = \left[ w _ {1} ^ {\star}, w _ {2} ^ {\star}, \dots , w _ {n} ^ {\star} \right] ^ {\top}, \tag {3.69}
$$

with  $w_{i}^{\star} > 0$  assigned to the  $i$ -th item ( $1 \leq i \leq n$ ). the ranks of these items are therefore determined exclusively by their (relative) preference scores: an item with a larger score is ranked higher. throughout this section, we denote by  $\kappa$  a sort of condition number as follows

$$
\kappa := \frac {\operatorname* {m a x} _ {1 \leq i \leq n} w _ {i} ^ {\star}}{\operatorname* {m i n} _ {1 \leq i \leq n} w _ {i} ^ {\star}}. \tag {3.70}
$$

pairwise comparisons. equipped with the aforementioned score vector, the btl model posits that: the probability of an item winning a paired comparison is determined entirely by the relative

scores of the two items involved. to be precise, when comparing every pair  $(i,j)$  of items, the model assumes that

$$
\mathbb {p} \left\{\text {i t e m} j \text {i s p r e f e r e d o v e r i t e m} i \right\} = \frac {w _ {j} ^ {\star}}{w _ {i} ^ {\star} + w _ {j} ^ {\star}}, \tag {3.71}
$$

asserting that an item assigned a higher preference score is more likely to win. in this section, we assume access to a comparison between every pair of items. to be precise, for each pair  $(i,j)$ $(1\leq i < j\leq n)$ , we observe an independent binary comparison outcome  $y_{i,j}$  following the btl model (3.71):

$$
y _ {i, j} = \left\{ \begin{array}{l l} 1, & \text {w i t h p r o b a b i l i t y} \frac {w _ {j} ^ {\star}}{w _ {i} ^ {\star} + w _ {j} ^ {\star}}, \\ 0, & \text {o t h e r w i s e}, \end{array} \right.
$$

where  $y_{i,j} = 1$  means item  $j$  beats item  $i$  and  $y_{i,j} = 0$  otherwise. by convention, we set  $y_{i,j} = 1 - y_{j,i}$  for all  $i > j$ .

goal. with the btl parametric model in mind, a natural strategy is to start by estimating the underlying scores  $\{w_i^{\star}\}$  based on the pairwise comparisons in hand, followed by a ranking step performed in accordance with the estimated scores. in this section, we shall focus on characterizing the statistical accuracy of spectral methods in accomplishing the meta task of preference score estimation, and will remark in passing on the ranking step that follows. obviously, from (3.71), we can only hope for estimating  $\{w_i^{\star}\}$  up to some global scaling ambiguity.

# 3.6.2 a spectral ranking algorithm

at first glance, the recipe we have introduced for designing spectral methods seems to have no direct bearing on the btl model. somewhat unexpectedly, a closer inspection unveils an intimate connection between the btl model and a reversible markov chain, whose stationary distribution embodies crucial information about the score vector of interest. this in turn lays a solid foundation for the spectral algorithm described below, originally developed by negahban et al. (2016).

the first step is to convert the pairwise comparison data  $\{y_{i,j}\}_{i\neq j}$  into a probability transition matrix  $p = [p_{i,j}]_{1\leq i,j\leq n}$ , in a way that

$$
p _ {i, j} = \left\{ \begin{array}{l l} \frac {1}{n} y _ {i, j}, & \text {i f} i \neq j, \\ 1 - \sum_ {j: j \neq i} \frac {1}{n} y _ {i, j}, & \text {o t h e r w i s e .} \end{array} \right. \tag {3.72}
$$

by construction of  $p$ , all of its entries are non-negative and the entries in each row sum up to one, thus confirming that  $p$  is a probability transition matrix. the spectral algorithm then computes the leading left eigenvector  $\pi$  of  $p$ , returning it as the estimate for the underlying score vector  $w^{\star}$ .

to make sense of the rationale behind this algorithm, it is helpful to look at the mean  $p^{\star} = [p_{i,j}^{\star}]_{1 \leq i,j \leq n} \coloneqq \mathbb{e}[p]$ , which obeys

$$
p _ {i, j} ^ {\star} = \left\{ \begin{array}{l l} \frac {1}{n} \frac {w _ {j} ^ {\star}}{w _ {i} ^ {\star} + w _ {j} ^ {\star}}, & \text {i f} i \neq j, \\ 1 - \frac {1}{n} \sum_ {j: j \neq i} \frac {w _ {j} ^ {\star}}{w _ {i} ^ {\star} + w _ {j} ^ {\star}}, & \text {o t h e r w i s e .} \end{array} \right. \tag {3.73}
$$

clearly, this matrix  $p^{\star}$  is a probability transition matrix as well. as can be straightforwardly verified, the vector  $\pmb{\pi}^{\star} = [\pi_{i}^{\star}]_{1\leq i\leq n}$  defined by

$$
\pi^ {\star} = \frac {1}{\mathbf {1} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {w} ^ {\star}} \boldsymbol {w} ^ {\star} \tag {3.74}
$$

satisfies the following conditions:

-  $\pi^{\star}$  is a probability vector (i.e.,  $\pi_i^\star \geq 0$  for all  $i$  and  $\sum_{i} \pi_{i}^{\star} = 1$ );  
-  $\pi^{\star}$  satisfies the detailed balance equations as follows:

$$
\pi_ {i} ^ {\star} p _ {i, j} ^ {\star} = \pi_ {j} ^ {\star} p _ {j, i} ^ {\star}, \qquad \mathrm {f o r a l l} (i, j).
$$

classical markov chain theory (brémaud, 2013) thus tells us that  $p^{\star}$  represents a reversible markov chain, whose stationary distribution is precisely given by  $\pi^{\star}$  (this can easily be verified using the definition of the stationary distribution) and corresponds to the normalized preference scores. as a consequence, we hold the intuition that:  $\pi$  is close to  $\pi^{\star}$  and hence  $w^{\star}$  up to some global scaling—as long as  $p$  approximates  $p^{\star}$  reasonably well.

# 3.6.3 performance guarantees

this subsection develops theoretical support for the above spectral ranking algorithm, based on the eigenvector perturbation theory developed previously for probability transition matrices in section 2.5. for notational convenience, we shall use  $\pmb{e} \coloneqq \pmb{p} - \pmb{p}^{\star}$  to denote the difference of the above two transition matrices of interest.

by virtue of theorem 2.10, the perturbation of the stationary distribution of a reversible markov chain  $p^{\star}$  is dictated by two important quantities: (i) the spectral gap  $1 - \max \left\{\lambda_{2}(p^{\star}), - \lambda_{n}(p^{\star})\right\}$ , and (ii) the noise size  $\| e\|_{\pi^{\star}}$  (recall the definition of  $\| \cdot \|_{\pi^{\star}}$  in section 2.5.1). these two quantities are controlled respectively via the following two lemmas, whose proofs can be found in section 3.6.4.

lemma 3.12. consider the settings and notation in sections 3.6.1 and 3.6.2. it follows that

$$
1 - \max \left\{\lambda_ {2} (\pmb {p} ^ {\star}), - \lambda_ {n} (\pmb {p} ^ {\star}) \right\} \geq \frac {1}{2 \kappa^ {2}},
$$

where we recall the definition of  $\kappa$  in (3.70).

lemma 3.13. consider the settings and notation in sections 3.6.1 and 3.6.2, and recall that  $\pmb{e} := \pmb{p} - \pmb{p}^{\star}$ . with probability at least  $1 - o(n^{-8})$ ,

$$
\| \boldsymbol {e} \| _ {\pi^ {\star}} \leq \sqrt {\kappa} \| \boldsymbol {e} \| \lesssim \sqrt {\frac {\kappa \log n}{n}}.
$$

now we are well prepared to assess the quality of the spectral estimate, as summarized below, whose proof is given at the end of this subsection.

theorem 3.14. consider the settings and algorithm in sections 3.6.1 and 3.6.2. suppose that  $n \geq c\kappa^5 \log n$  for some sufficiently large constant  $c > 0$ . then with probability exceeding  $1 - o(n^{-8})$ , one has

$$
\frac {\| \boldsymbol {\pi} - \boldsymbol {\pi} ^ {\star} \| _ {2}}{\| \boldsymbol {\pi} ^ {\star} \| _ {2}} \lesssim \kappa^ {2. 5} \sqrt {\frac {\log n}{n}}. \tag {3.75}
$$

given the construction (3.74) of  $\pi^{\star}$ , this theorem implies the existence of a scalar  $z > 0$  such that

$$
\frac {\| z \pmb {\pi} - \pmb {w} ^ {\star} \| _ {2}}{\| \pmb {w} ^ {\star} \| _ {2}} \lesssim \kappa^ {2. 5} \sqrt {\frac {\log n}{n}}
$$

holds with high probability. it is worth noting that one cannot possibly retrieve the global scaling factor  $z$ , due to the invariance of the btl observation model under global scaling (cf. (3.71)).

to interpret the effectiveness of this theorem, consider, for example, the case when  $\kappa = o(1)$  (so that all the latent scores  $w_{i}^{\star}$  are about the same order). theorem 3.14 tells us that the relative estimation error of  $\pi$  is vanishing as the number  $n$  of items increases. as it turns out, this statistical error rate (3.75) is near minimax-optimal up to a logarithmic factor; see negahban et al. (2016, theorem 3). in fact, with a more careful analysis, one can further eliminate this extra log  $n$  factor and establish (orderwise) minimax optimality of this algorithm, as has been done in chen et al. (2019b, theorem 5.2).

caution needs to be exercised, however, that high score estimation accuracy alone does not necessarily imply appealing ranking accuracy. for instance, if the goal is to identify the top- $k$  ranked items—a problem commonly referred to as "top- $k$  ranking" (chen and suh, 2015)—then the ranking accuracy also relies heavily on the separation between the score of the  $k$ -th ranked item and that of the  $(k + 1)$ -th ranked item (namely, whether the set of top- $k$  ranked items is sufficiently distinguishable from the remaining ones). fortunately, the spectral ranking algorithm introduced in this section remains minimax optimal when it comes to top- $k$  ranking, through a refined  $\ell_{\infty}$  perturbation theory to be introduced in chapter 4. the interested reader is referred to chen et al. (2019b) for details.

proof of theorem 3.14. invoke theorem 2.10 to see that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \| \pmb {\pi} - \pmb {\pi} ^ {\star} \| _ {\pmb {\pi} ^ {\star}} \leq \frac {\left\| \pmb {\pi} ^ {\star^ {\top}} \pmb {e} \right\| _ {\pmb {\pi} ^ {\star}}}{1 - \max \left\{\lambda_ {2} (\pmb {p} ^ {\star}) , - \lambda_ {n} (\pmb {p} ^ {\star}) \right\} - \| \pmb {e} \| _ {\pmb {\pi} ^ {\star}}} \\ \leq 4 \kappa^ {2} \| \boldsymbol {\pi} ^ {\star \top} \boldsymbol {e} \| _ {\boldsymbol {\pi} ^ {\star}}, \tag {3.76} \\ \end{array}
$$

provided that

$$
1 - \max  \left\{\lambda_ {2} \left(\boldsymbol {p} ^ {\star}\right), - \lambda_ {n} \left(\boldsymbol {p} ^ {\star}\right) \right\} - \| \boldsymbol {e} \| _ {\pi^ {\star}} \geq 1 / \left(4 \kappa^ {2}\right). \tag {3.77}
$$

from lemma 3.12 and lemma 3.13, we know that condition (3.77) holds true with probability at least  $1 - o(n^{-8})$ , with the proviso that  $n \geq c\kappa^5 \log n$  for some sufficiently large constant  $c > 0$ .

additionally, letting  $\pi_{\min}^{\star} \coloneqq \min_{i} \pi_{i}^{\star}$  and  $\pi_{\max}^{\star} \coloneqq \max_{i} \pi_{i}^{\star}$ , we can easily see from the definition of  $\| \cdot \|_{\pi^{\star}}$  (i.e.,  $\| \pmb{v} \|_{\pi^{\star}} = \sqrt{\sum_{i} \pi_{i}^{\star} v_{i}^{2}}$  for any vector  $\pmb{v}$ ) that

$$
\| \boldsymbol {v} \| _ {\pi^ {\star}} \stackrel {{\text {(i)}}} {\leq} \sqrt {\pi_ {\max} ^ {\star}} \| \boldsymbol {v} \| _ {2}, \quad \text {a n d} \quad \| \boldsymbol {v} \| _ {2} \stackrel {{\text {(i i)}}} {\leq} \frac {1}{\sqrt {\pi_ {\min} ^ {\star}}} \| \boldsymbol {v} \| _ {\pi^ {\star}},
$$

which allows us to further obtain

$$
\begin{array}{l} \| \boldsymbol {\pi} - \boldsymbol {\pi} ^ {\star} \| _ {2} \leq \frac {1}{\sqrt {\pi_ {\operatorname* {m i n}} ^ {\star}}} \| \boldsymbol {\pi} - \boldsymbol {\pi} ^ {\star} \| _ {\pi^ {\star}} \leq \frac {4 \kappa^ {2}}{\sqrt {\pi_ {\operatorname* {m i n}} ^ {\star}}} \| \boldsymbol {\pi} ^ {\star \top} \boldsymbol {e} \| _ {\pi^ {\star}} \leq 4 \kappa^ {2. 5} \| \boldsymbol {\pi} ^ {\star \top} \boldsymbol {e} \| _ {2} \\ \leq 4 \kappa^ {2. 5} \| \boldsymbol {e} \| \| \boldsymbol {\pi} ^ {\star} \| _ {2}. \\ \end{array}
$$

here, the first inequality comes from (ii), the second inequality is a consequence of (3.76), whereas the third one results from (i). the proof is then completed by applying the high-probability bound  $\| \pmb{e}\| \lesssim \sqrt{(\log n) / n}$  derived in lemma 3.13.

# 3.6.4 proof of auxiliary lemmas

before delving into the proof, we state a general comparison theorem, which is attributed to diaconis and saloff-coste (1993), that relates the spectral gap of a reversible markov chain with that of another (possibly more tractable) reversible chain. we refer the interested reader to negahban et al. (2016, lemma 6) for a proof of the following result.

lemma 3.15. consider two reversible markov chains over the state space  $\{1,2,\dots ,n\}$ . let  $p$  and  $\pi$  (resp.  $\widetilde{p}$  and  $\widetilde{\pi}$ ) denote the transition matrix and the stationary distribution of the first (resp. second) chain. in addition, set

$$
\alpha := \min _ {i, j} \frac {\pi_ {i} p _ {i , j}}{\widetilde {\pi} _ {i} \widetilde {p} _ {i , j}}, \qquad \mathrm {a n d} \qquad \beta := \max _ {i} \frac {\pi_ {i}}{\widetilde {\pi} _ {i}}.
$$

then one has

$$
\begin{array}{l} \frac {1 - \max  \left\{\lambda_ {2} (\boldsymbol {p}) , - \lambda_ {n} (\boldsymbol {p}) \right\}}{1 - \max  \left\{\lambda_ {2} (\tilde {\boldsymbol {p}}) , - \lambda_ {n} (\tilde {\boldsymbol {p}}) \right\}} \geq \frac {\alpha}{\beta}. \end{array}
$$

armed with this comparison lemma, we are ready to present the proof of lemma 3.12.

proof of lemma 3.12. in order to control the spectral gap with the aid of lemma 3.15, we construct an auxiliary transition matrix

$$
\boldsymbol {q} ^ {\star} = \frac {1}{n} \mathbf {1 1} ^ {\top},
$$

which clearly corresponds to a reversible markov chain with stationary distribution  $\pmb{u}^{\star} = (1 / n)\cdot \mathbf{1}$ . the eigengap of this newly constructed reversible markov chain is

$$
1 - \max  \left\{\lambda_ {2} (\boldsymbol {q} ^ {\star}), - \lambda_ {n} (\boldsymbol {q} ^ {\star}) \right\} = 1,
$$

since  $\lambda_2(q^\star) = \lambda_n(q^\star) = 0$ . therefore, we only need to bound  $\alpha$  and  $\beta$ .

recalling the construction of  $p^{\star}$  in (3.73), we can straightforwardly check that

$$
\pi_ {i} ^ {\star} p _ {i, j} ^ {\star} = \frac {1}{n} \frac {\pi_ {i} ^ {\star} \pi_ {j} ^ {\star}}{\pi_ {i} ^ {\star} + \pi_ {j} ^ {\star}} \geq \frac {1}{2 n} \min \{\pi_ {i} ^ {\star}, \pi_ {j} ^ {\star} \}
$$

for every  $i \neq j$ , and in addition,

$$
\pi_ {i} ^ {\star} p _ {i, i} ^ {\star} = \pi_ {i} ^ {\star} \left[ 1 - \sum_ {j: j \neq i} \frac {1}{n} \frac {w _ {j} ^ {\star}}{w _ {i} ^ {\star} + w _ {j} ^ {\star}} \right] \geq \pi_ {i} ^ {\star} \left[ 1 - \sum_ {j: j \neq i} \frac {1}{n} \right] = \frac {1}{n} \pi_ {i} ^ {\star}.
$$

combining the previous two inequalities, we obtain

$$
\min _ {i, j} \left(\pi_ {i} ^ {\star} p _ {i, j} ^ {\star}\right) \geq \frac {1}{2 n} \min _ {1 \leq i \leq n} \pi_ {i} ^ {\star} = \frac {1}{2 n \kappa} \max _ {1 \leq i \leq n} \pi_ {i} ^ {\star} \geq \frac {1}{2 n ^ {2} \kappa},
$$

where the last relation holds since  $\max_i\pi_i^\star \geq \frac{1}{n}\sum_i\pi_i^\star = \frac{1}{n}$ . this together with the construction of  $q^{\star}$  further leads to

$$
\alpha := \min _ {i, j} \frac {\pi_ {i} ^ {\star} p _ {i , j} ^ {\star}}{u _ {i} ^ {\star} q _ {i , j} ^ {\star}} = n ^ {2} \min _ {i, j} \left(\pi_ {i} ^ {\star} p _ {i, j} ^ {\star}\right) \geq \frac {1}{2 \kappa}.
$$

in regard to  $\beta$ , it is seen that

$$
\beta := \max _ {i} \frac {\pi_ {i} ^ {\star}}{u _ {i} ^ {\star}} = n \max _ {i} \pi_ {i} ^ {\star} = n \kappa \min _ {i} \pi_ {i} ^ {\star} \leq \kappa ,
$$

where the final inequality follows since  $\min_i\pi_i\leq \frac{1}{n}\sum_i\pi_i = \frac{1}{n}$ . with the preceding bounds on  $\alpha$  and  $\beta$  in place, lemma 3.15 informs us that

$$
\frac {1 - \max  \left\{\lambda_ {2} (\boldsymbol {p} ^ {\star}) , - \lambda_ {n} (\boldsymbol {p} ^ {\star}) \right\}}{1 - \max  \left\{\lambda_ {2} (\boldsymbol {q} ^ {\star}) , - \lambda_ {n} (\boldsymbol {q} ^ {\star}) \right\}} \geq \frac {\alpha}{\beta} \geq \frac {1}{2 \kappa^ {2}}.
$$

this together with the aforementioned eigengap for  $q^{\star}$  establishes the advertised result.

proof of lemma 3.13. let  $d \coloneqq \mathrm{diag}(\sqrt{\pi_1^\star}, \dots, \sqrt{\pi_n^\star})$ . we have seen from the proof in section 2.5.3 (cf. (2.34)) that

$$
\| \boldsymbol {e} \| _ {\pi^ {\star}} = \| \boldsymbol {d e d} ^ {- 1} \| \leq \| \boldsymbol {d} \| \| \boldsymbol {e} \| \| \boldsymbol {d} ^ {- 1} \| = \frac {\sqrt {\max _ {i} \pi_ {i} ^ {\star}}}{\sqrt {\min _ {i} \pi_ {i} ^ {\star}}} \| \boldsymbol {e} \| = \sqrt {\kappa} \| \boldsymbol {e} \|,
$$

where  $\kappa$  is defined in (3.70). therefore, it suffices to bound  $\| \pmb{e}\|$

by construction of  $\pmb{p}$  and  $\pmb{p}^{\star}$  (see (3.72) and (3.73)), we see that

$$
e _ {i, j} = p _ {i, j} - p _ {i, j} ^ {\star} = \frac {1}{n} \left(y _ {i, j} - \mathbb {e} \left[ y _ {i, j} \right]\right) \tag {3.78}
$$

for any  $i \neq j$ . in addition, for all  $1 \leq i \leq n$ , it follows that

$$
e _ {i, i} = p _ {i, i} - p _ {i, i} ^ {\star} = - \sum_ {j: j \neq i} e _ {i, j} = - \frac {1}{n} \sum_ {j: j \neq i} \left(y _ {i, j} - \mathbb {e} \left[ y _ {i, j} \right]\right). \tag {3.79}
$$

in view of these identities, we shall decompose the matrix  $\pmb{e}$  into three parts: the upper triangular part (denoted by  $\pmb{e}_{\mathrm{upper}}$ ), the diagonal part (denoted by  $\pmb{e}_{\mathrm{diag}}$ ), and the lower triangular part (denoted by  $\pmb{e}_{\mathrm{lower}}$ ). clearly, the triangle inequality gives

$$
\left\| \boldsymbol {e} \right\| \leq \left\| \boldsymbol {e} _ {\text {u p p e r}} \right\| + \left\| \boldsymbol {e} _ {\text {d i a g}} \right\| + \left\| \boldsymbol {e} _ {\text {l o w e r}} \right\|. \tag {3.80}
$$

in the sequel, we deal with these three terms separately.

let us start with the diagonal part  $\pmb{e}_{\mathrm{diag}}$ . in view of the definition of the spectral norm, we know that

$$
\| \boldsymbol {e} _ {\mathrm {d i a g}} \| = \max _ {1 \leq i \leq n} | e _ {i, i} | = \max _ {1 \leq i \leq n} \big | \sum_ {j: j \neq i} e _ {i, j} \big |,
$$

where the last relation arises from (3.79). fix any  $i$ , then it is easily seen that  $\sum_{j:j\neq i}e_{i,j}$  is a sum of independent zero-mean random variables  $\{e_{i,j}\}$ , which can be controlled via the bernstein inequality. specifically, observe that

$$
\max _ {j: j \neq i} | e _ {i, j} | = \max _ {j: j \neq i} \frac {1}{n} \big | y _ {i, j} - \mathbb {e} [ y _ {i, j} ] \big | \leq \frac {1}{n} =: b _ {1}
$$

and, in addition,

$$
v _ {1} := \sum_ {j: j \neq i} \mathbb {e} \left[ e _ {i, j} ^ {2} \right] = \frac {1}{n ^ {2}} \sum_ {j: j \neq i} \operatorname {v a r} \left(y _ {i, j}\right) \leq \frac {1}{n},
$$

where the last inequality follows since the variance of a bernoulli random variable is no larger than 1. apply the bernstein inequality (cf. corollary 3.3) and the union bound over  $1 \leq i \leq n$  to demonstrate that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \| \boldsymbol{e}_{\text{diag}}\| = \max_{1\leq i\leq n}\big|\sum_{j:j\neq i}e_{i,j}\big|\lesssim \sqrt{v_{1}\log n} +b_{1}\log n \\ \lesssim \sqrt {\frac {\log n}{n}} + \frac {\log n}{n} \asymp \sqrt {\frac {\log n}{n}} \\ \end{array}
$$

![](images/fa7560def7db21969c7506277118fe1bfecf6fcde34ba68e0be8bb27ecb546ec.jpg)  
figure 3.2: illustration of phase retrieval and solving quadratic systems of equations, where only the intensities of linear measurements are collected. here,  $\mathbf{a} = [a_{1},\dots ,a_{m}]^{\top}$ , and  $|z|^2 \coloneqq [|z_1|^2,\dots ,|z_m|^2 ]^\top$  for any vector  $\mathbf{z} = [z_i]_{1\leq i\leq m}$ .

holds with probability at least  $1 - o(n^{-8})$ .

we now move on to the upper triangular part  $\pmb{e}_{\mathrm{upper}}$ , whose entries  $\{e_{i,j}\}_{i < j}$  are independent. invoking theorem 3.4 (see the remark about asymmetric version right after theorem 3.4) with the bounds on  $b_{1}$  and  $v_{1}$  established above, we arrive at

$$
\| \boldsymbol {e} _ {\mathrm {u p p e r}} \| \lesssim \sqrt {v _ {1}} + b _ {1} \log n \lesssim \sqrt {\frac {1}{n}} + \frac {\log n}{n} \asymp \sqrt {\frac {1}{n}}
$$

with probability at least  $1 - o(n^{-8})$ . similar arguments lead to the same upper bound on  $\| e_{\mathrm{lower}} \|$ , which we omit for brevity.

substituting the upper bounds on  $\| e_{\mathrm{diag}}\|$ ,  $\| e_{\mathrm{upper}}\|$  and  $\| e_{\mathrm{lower}}\|$  into (3.80), we immediately establish the desired bound.

# 3.7 phase retrieval and solving quadratic systems of equations

phase retrieval is a fundamental problem arising in numerous imaging applications such as x-ray crystallography, diffraction imaging, and so on (fienup, 1982; shechtman et al., 2015; candès et al., 2013; candès et al., 2015b; jaganathan et al., 2016). in physics, phase retrieval is concerned with estimating a specimen by observing the intensities (or squared modulus) of the diffracted waves scattered by the object without knowing their phases. the advent of this problem is attributed to the physical limitation that the optical sensors are unable to record the phases of the diffracted waves. put another way, in phase retrieval, we only have access to measurements that are quadratic functions of the object of interest, and aim at estimating the unknown object up to global phase. this gives rise to the problem of solving quadratic systems of equations, to be formulated below.

# 3.7.1 problem formulation and assumptions

suppose that we are interested in reconstructing an unknown signal  $\pmb{x}^{\star}\in \mathbb{r}^{n}$ , but only have access to a collection of  $m$  quadratic measurements on the linear combinations of its entries as follows:

$$
y _ {i} = \left(\boldsymbol {a} _ {i} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {x} ^ {\star}\right) ^ {2}, \quad 1 \leq i \leq m, \tag {3.81}
$$

where  $\pmb{a}_i = [a_{i,1},\dots ,a_{i,n}]^\top \in \mathbb{r}^n$  is the design vector known a priori. see figure 3.2 for an illustration of this measurement model. the question is: when can we hope to reconstruct  $\pmb{x}^{\star}$ , in an accurate and efficient fashion, on the basis of these nonlinear equations?

as is well known, solving quadratic systems of equations is, in general, np hard. additional assumptions are therefore needed to enable tractable recovery. here, we adopt a gaussian design model commonly studied in the literature.

assumption 3.3. the design vectors  $\{a_i\}_{1\leq i\leq m}$  are independently generated obeying  $a_{i}$ $\mathcal{n}(\mathbf{0},i_n)$

# 3.7.2 algorithm

the gaussian design model (cf. assumption 3.3) allows meaningful estimation of the unknown object  $\pmb{x}^{\star}$  via the (by now) familiar spectral method. let us start by arranging the data into the following matrix

$$
\boldsymbol {m} := \frac {1}{m} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {m} y _ {i} \boldsymbol {a} _ {i} \boldsymbol {a} _ {i} ^ {\top} = \frac {1}{m} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {m} \left(\boldsymbol {a} _ {i} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {x} ^ {\star}\right) ^ {2} \boldsymbol {a} _ {i} \boldsymbol {a} _ {i} ^ {\top}, \tag {3.82}
$$

which can be viewed as a weighted sample covariance matrix of the design vectors  $\{\pmb{a}_i\}$ . the spectral method then estimates  $\pmb{x}^{\star}$  by

$$
\boldsymbol {x} = \sqrt {\frac {\lambda_ {1}}{3}} \boldsymbol {u} _ {1}, \tag {3.83}
$$

where  $\pmb{u}_1$  (resp.  $\lambda_1 = \lambda_1(m)$ ) indicates the leading eigenvector (resp. eigenvalue) of the matrix  $m$ . this simple approach has been suggested for phase retrieval since the work of netrapalli et al (2015).

to explain the rationale of this approach, it is instrumental to look at the mean of  $m$  under assumption 3.3. specifically, simple calculation (which we include at the end of this subsection) gives

$$
\boldsymbol {m} ^ {\star} := \mathbb {e} [ \boldsymbol {m} ] = \mathbb {e} \left[ \left(\boldsymbol {a} _ {i} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {x} ^ {\star}\right) ^ {2} \boldsymbol {a} _ {i} \boldsymbol {a} _ {i} ^ {\top} \right] = 2 \boldsymbol {x} ^ {\star} \boldsymbol {x} ^ {\star \top} + \| \boldsymbol {x} ^ {\star} \| _ {2} ^ {2} \boldsymbol {i} _ {n}. \tag {3.84}
$$

it is self-evident that (a) the leading eigenvector of  $m^{\star}$  is precisely given by  $\pm \pmb{x}^{\star} / \| \pmb{x}^{\star}\|_{2}$ , and (b) the leading eigenvalue of  $m^{\star}$  is given by  $3\| \pmb{x}^{\star}\|_{2}^{2}$  by (3.84). from now on, we shall set

$$
\boldsymbol {u} _ {1} ^ {\star} := \boldsymbol {x} ^ {\star} / \| \boldsymbol {x} ^ {\star} \| _ {2}, \quad \text {a n d} \quad \lambda_ {1} ^ {\star} := 3 \| \boldsymbol {x} ^ {\star} \| _ {2} ^ {2}, \tag {3.85}
$$

which implies  $\pmb{x}^{\star} = \sqrt{\lambda_1^{\star} / 3}\pmb{u}_1^{\star}$  and hence explains the estimator constructed in (3.83). the above argument further hints that: the spectral estimate  $\pmb{x}$  converges to the ground truth  $\pm \pmb{x}^{\star}$  in the large-sample limit with  $m\to \infty$  (so that  $m\rightarrow \mathbb{e}[m] = m^{\star}$ ). the question, however, boils down to where this algorithm stands in the more realistic finite-sample scenario.

remark 3.9. the expression (3.85) indicates that  $\pmb{x}^{\star} = \| \pmb{x}^{\star}\|_{2}\pmb{u}_{1}^{\star}$ . from the law of large numbers one expects

$$
\frac {1}{m} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {m} y _ {i} \rightarrow \mathbb {e} [ y _ {i} ] = \mathbb {e} \left[\left(\boldsymbol {a} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {x} ^ {\star}\right) ^ {2} \right] = \| \boldsymbol {x} ^ {\star} \| _ {2} ^ {2},
$$

with probability approaching one. thus, an alternative estimator is

$$
\widehat {\boldsymbol {x}} = \left(\frac {1}{m} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {m} y _ {i}\right) ^ {1 / 2} \boldsymbol {u} _ {1}. \tag {3.86}
$$

derivation of (3.84). the  $(i,j)$ -th entry of  $\mathbb{e}[m]$  is given by

$$
\mathbb {e} [ m _ {j, k} ] = \mathbb {e} \big [ \big ((\pmb {a} _ {i} ^ {\top} \pmb {x} ^ {\star}) ^ {2} \pmb {x} ^ {\star} \pmb {x} ^ {\star \top} \big) _ {j, k} \big ] = \mathbb {e} \big [ (a _ {i, 1} x _ {i, 1} ^ {\star} + \dots + a _ {i, n} x _ {i, n} ^ {\star}) ^ {2} a _ {i, j} a _ {i, k} \big ].
$$

expanding terms and using the moments of gaussian variables yield

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathbb {e} \left[ m _ {j, k} \right] = \mathbb {e} \left[ 2 a _ {i, j} ^ {2} a _ {i, k} ^ {2} x _ {i, j} ^ {\star} x _ {i, k} ^ {\star} \right] = 2 x _ {i, j} ^ {\star} x _ {i, k} ^ {\star} \quad \text {i f} j \neq k; \\ \mathbb {e} [ m _ {j, j} ] = \mathbb {e} \bigl [ a _ {i, j} ^ {4} \bigl (x _ {i, j} ^ {\star} \bigr) ^ {2} \bigr ] + \sum_ {l: l \neq j} \mathbb {e} \bigl [ a _ {i, l} ^ {2} a _ {i, j} ^ {2} \bigl (x _ {i, l} ^ {\star} \bigr) ^ {2} \bigr ] = 3 \bigl (x _ {i, j} ^ {\star} \bigr) ^ {2} + \sum_ {l: l \neq j} \bigl (x _ {i, l} ^ {\star} \bigr) ^ {2} \\ = 2 \left(x _ {i, j} ^ {\star}\right) ^ {2} + \| \boldsymbol {x} ^ {\star} \| _ {2} ^ {2}. \\ \end{array}
$$

putting these together leads to the expression (3.84).

# 3.7.3 performance guarantees

developing theoretical support for the aforementioned spectral method hinges upon characterizing the proximity of  $\lambda_{1}$  and  $\lambda_1^\star$  and that of  $\pmb{u}_{1}$  and  $\pmb{u}_1^{\star}$ , both of which rely largely on bounding  $m - m^{\star}$ . in what follows, we start by controlling  $\| m - m^{\star}\|$ , with the proof postponed to section 3.7.5.

lemma 3.16. consider the settings in section 3.7.1. there exist sufficiently large constants  $c, c > 0$  such that if  $m \geq cn\log^3 m$ , then with probability at least  $1 - o(m^{-10})$  one has

$$
\left\| \boldsymbol {m} - \boldsymbol {m} ^ {\star} \right\| \leq c \sqrt {\frac {n \log^ {3} m}{m}} \| \boldsymbol {x} ^ {\star} \| _ {2} ^ {2} \leq \frac {1}{1 0} \| \boldsymbol {x} ^ {\star} \| _ {2} ^ {2}. \tag {3.87}
$$

remark 3.10. the sample size requirement can be further relaxed to  $m \geq cn\log n$  with a more careful treatment (candes et al., 2015b; ma et al., 2020). for the sake of conciseness, however, we do not strive to shave the log factors here.

with the above bound in mind, we are ready to characterize the statistical accuracy of the spectral method for phase retrieval.

theorem 3.17. suppose the assumptions of lemma 3.16 hold, then with probability at least  $1 - o(m^{-10})$ , the following holds

$$
\min  \left\{\| \boldsymbol {x} - \boldsymbol {x} ^ {\star} \| _ {2}, \| \boldsymbol {x} + \boldsymbol {x} ^ {\star} \| _ {2} \right\} \leq 3 c \sqrt {\frac {n \log^ {3} m}{m}} \| \boldsymbol {x} ^ {\star} \| _ {2}.
$$

as can be seen from theorem 3.17, when the number  $m$  of measurements obeys  $m \gg n \log^3 m$ , the relative accuracy of the spectral estimates (i.e.,  $\min \{\| \pmb{x} - \pmb{x}^\star \|_2, \| \pmb{x} + \pmb{x}^\star \|_2\} / \| \pmb{x}^\star \|_2$ ) becomes considerably smaller than 1, thus indicating consistent estimation. this should be contrasted with the minimax lower bounds derived in the literature (cai and zhang, 2015; eldar and mendelson, 2014), which assert that no estimator can achieve a vanishingly small relative estimation error if  $m$  is orderwise smaller than  $n$ . all this corroborates the power of spectral methods for solving the phase retrieval problem.

proof of theorem 3.17. lemma 3.16 and weyl's inequality (see lemma 2.2) yield

$$
\left| \lambda_ {1} - \lambda_ {1} ^ {\star} \right| \leq \| m - m ^ {\star} \| \leq c \sqrt {\frac {n \log^ {3} m}{m}} \| \boldsymbol {x} ^ {\star} \| _ {2} ^ {2} \leq \| \boldsymbol {x} ^ {\star} \| _ {2} ^ {2}, \tag {3.88}
$$

as a result, by using  $\lambda_1^\star = 3\| \pmb{x}^\star \|_2^2$ , we have

$$
\lambda_ {1} \geq \lambda_ {1} ^ {\star} - \| \boldsymbol {x} ^ {\star} \| _ {2} ^ {2} = 3 \| \boldsymbol {x} ^ {\star} \| _ {2} ^ {2} - \| \boldsymbol {x} ^ {\star} \| _ {2} ^ {2} = 2 \| \boldsymbol {x} ^ {\star} \| _ {2} ^ {2}. \tag {3.89}
$$

in addition, note that  $\lambda_1^\star = \lambda_1(m^\star) = 3\| \pmb{x}^\star \|_2^2$  and  $\lambda_i(m^\star) = \| \pmb{x}^\star \|_2^2$  for all  $i \geq 2$ . the bound (3.87) on  $m - m^\star$  indicates that

$$
\left\| \boldsymbol {m} - \boldsymbol {m} ^ {\star} \right\| \leq (1 - 1 / \sqrt {2}) \bigl [ \lambda_ {1} (\boldsymbol {m} ^ {\star}) - \lambda_ {2} (\boldsymbol {m} ^ {\star}) \bigr ],
$$

which allows one to invoke the davis-kahan sin  $\theta$  theorem (cf. corollary 2.8) to obtain

$$
\operatorname {d i s t} \left(\boldsymbol {u} _ {1}, \boldsymbol {u} _ {1} ^ {\star}\right) \leq \frac {2 \| \boldsymbol {m} - \boldsymbol {m} ^ {\star} \|}{\lambda_ {1} (\boldsymbol {m} ^ {\star}) - \lambda_ {2} (\boldsymbol {m} ^ {\star})} \leq 2 c \sqrt {\frac {n \log^ {3} m}{m}}. \tag {3.90}
$$

without loss of generality, we shall assume  $\| \pmb{u}_1 - \pmb{u}_1^\star \|_2 = \mathrm{dist}(\pmb{u}_1, \pmb{u}_1^\star)$  in the sequel.
