now we are ready to control our target quantity  $\mathrm{dist}(\pmb{x},\pmb{x}^{\star})$ . in view of the definition (3.83) of  $\pmb{x}$ , one has

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \boldsymbol {x} - \boldsymbol {x} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {2} = \left\| \sqrt {\lambda_ {1} / 3} \boldsymbol {u} _ {1} - \left\| \boldsymbol {x} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {2} \boldsymbol {u} _ {1} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {2} \\ \leq \left\| \left(\sqrt {\lambda_ {1} / 3} - \| \boldsymbol {x} ^ {\star} \| _ {2}\right) \boldsymbol {u} _ {1} \right\| _ {2} + \| \boldsymbol {x} ^ {\star} \| _ {2} \| \boldsymbol {u} _ {1} - \boldsymbol {u} _ {1} ^ {\star} \| _ {2} \\ \leq \left| \sqrt {\lambda_ {1} / 3} - \| \boldsymbol {x} ^ {\star} \| _ {2} \right| + 2 c \sqrt {\frac {n \log^ {3} m}{m}} \| \boldsymbol {x} ^ {\star} \| _ {2}. \tag {3.91} \\ \end{array}
$$

here, the second line applies the triangle inequality, and the last line arises from the facts  $\| \pmb{u}_1\| _2 = 1$  and (3.90). it then boils down to controlling  $|\sqrt{\lambda_1 / 3} -\| \pmb{x}^{\star}\|_{2}|$ , for which (3.88) and (3.89) prove useful. a little algebra reveals that

$$
\left| \sqrt {\lambda_ {1} / 3} - \| \boldsymbol {x} ^ {\star} \| _ {2} \right| = \frac {1}{\sqrt {3}} \frac {\left| \lambda_ {1} - 3 \| \boldsymbol {x} ^ {\star} \| _ {2} ^ {2} \right|}{\sqrt {\lambda_ {1}} + \sqrt {3} \| \boldsymbol {x} ^ {\star} \| _ {2}} \leq c \sqrt {\frac {n \log^ {3} m}{m}} \| \boldsymbol {x} ^ {\star} \| _ {2}, \tag {3.92}
$$

where the last relation relies on the bounds (3.88) and (3.89).

taking collectively (3.91) and (3.92) concludes the proof.

# 3.7.4 extensions

the spectral algorithm described in section 3.7.2, while enjoying appealing statistical guarantees, is improvable in multiple aspects. in this subsection, we briefly discuss two central issues: sample efficiency and robustness against outliers.

# improving sample efficiency

thus far, the spectral algorithm we have discussed requires the sample size to exceed  $m \gtrsim n \log^3 m$ . while this can be improved to  $m \gtrsim n \log n$  via tighter analysis (candès et al., 2015b; ma et al., 2020), it remains suboptimal due to the presence of the log factor. what happens in the sample-starved regime where the sample size  $m$  is on the same order as the number  $n$  of unknowns? is it possible to achieve the information-theoretic sampling limit for this problem? as it turns out, in order to attain the desired statistical accuracy in the sample-starved regime, we have to modify the standard recipe by applying appropriate preprocessing steps before forming the data matrix  $m$ , as we shall explain momentarily.

why is the algorithm in section 3.7.2 suboptimal? before introducing the improved spectral algorithm, we take a closer look at the lower bound of the approximation error  $\| m - m^{\star}\|$  for the sample-starved regime. clearly,

$$
\| \boldsymbol {m} \| \geq \frac {\boldsymbol {a} _ {j} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {m} \boldsymbol {a} _ {j}}{\| \boldsymbol {a} _ {j} \| _ {2} ^ {2}} = \frac {1}{m} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {m} y _ {i} \frac {(\boldsymbol {a} _ {i} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {a} _ {j}) ^ {2}}{\| \boldsymbol {a} _ {j} \| _ {2} ^ {2}} \geq \frac {1}{m} y _ {j} \| \boldsymbol {a} _ {j} \| _ {2} ^ {2}
$$

holds for any  $1 \leq j \leq m$ . taking  $j = i^{*} \coloneqq \arg \max_{i} y_{i}$ , we obtain

$$
\left\| \boldsymbol {m} \right\| \geq \frac {\left(\max  _ {i} y _ {i}\right) \left\| \boldsymbol {a} _ {i *} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2}}{m}. \tag {3.93}
$$

under the i.i.d. gaussian design,  $\{y_i / \| \pmb{x}^\star \|_2^2\}_{1 \leq i \leq m}$  forms a collection of i.i.d.  $\chi^2$  random variables with 1 degree of freedom. classical gaussian concentration results (ferguson, 1996; vershynin, 2017) tell us that

$$
\max _ {1 \leq i \leq m} y _ {i} = (2 + o (1)) \| \boldsymbol {x} ^ {\star} \| _ {2} ^ {2} \log m, \quad \| \boldsymbol {a} _ {i} \| _ {2} ^ {2} = (1 + o (1)) n, 1 \leq i \leq m
$$

with probability approaching one as  $n$  grows, as long as  $m = \mathrm{poly}(n)$ . substitution into (3.93) implies that

$$
\| \boldsymbol {m} \| \geq (2 + o (1)) \frac {n \log m}{m} \| \boldsymbol {x} ^ {\star} \| _ {2} ^ {2} \gg \| \boldsymbol {x} ^ {\star} \| _ {2} ^ {2}
$$

once  $m \ll n \log m$ , which combined with (3.84) further yields

$$
\left\| m - m ^ {\star} \right\| \geq \left\| m \right\| - \left\| m ^ {\star} \right\| = \left\| m \right\| - 3 \left\| x ^ {\star} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} \gg \left\| m ^ {\star} \right\|.
$$

in other words, the deviation between  $m$  and  $m^{\star}$  is not as well-controlled as desired in the regime with  $m \ll n \log m$ , and hence classical matrix perturbation theory (e.g., the davis-kahan theorem) does not support the use of the spectral algorithm based on  $m$  in this case.

spectral methods with data preprocessing. the above diagnosis suggests a natural remedy: since the culprit lies in the large influence  $\max_i y_i$  has brought to bear on the leading eigenvector, it is advisable to downweight the effect of any excessively large  $y_i$ . this is precisely the key idea behind the truncated spectral method proposed by chen and candès (2017)—as well as other variations proposed thereafter—that provably improves the sample efficiency of spectral methods.

more specifically, instead of using the matrix  $m$  constructed in (3.82), we resort to a properly preprocessed data matrix

$$
\boldsymbol {m} _ {\mathcal {t}} := \frac {1}{m} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {m} \mathcal {t} \left(y _ {i}\right) \boldsymbol {a} _ {i} \boldsymbol {a} _ {i} ^ {\top} \tag {3.94}
$$

with  $\mathcal{t}$  some preprocessing function, and produce, by (3.86), an estimate

$$
\boldsymbol {x} _ {\mathcal {t}} = \left(\frac {1}{m} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {m} y _ {i}\right) ^ {1 / 2} \boldsymbol {u} _ {1, \mathcal {t}}, \tag {3.95}
$$

where  $\pmb{u}_{1,\mathcal{t}}$  denotes the leading eigenvector of  $m_{\mathcal{t}}$ . a few representative examples of  $\mathcal{t}$  are in order.

- mean-based truncation (chen and candès, 2017):

$$
\mathcal {t} (y) := y \mathbb {1} \left\{y \leq \alpha_ {1} \bar {y} \right\}, \quad \bar {y} := \frac {1}{m} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {m} y _ {i}, \tag {3.96}
$$

where  $\alpha_{1} > 0$  is some sufficiently large constant;

- median-based truncation (zhang et al., 2016; zhang et al., 2018a):

$$
\mathcal {t} (y) := y \mathbb {1} \left\{y \leq \alpha_ {2} y _ {\text {m e d}} \right\}, \quad y _ {\text {m e d}} := \operatorname {m e d i a n} \left(\left\{y _ {i} \right\}\right), \tag {3.97}
$$

where  $\alpha_{2} > 0$  is some sufficiently large constant;

orthogonality promotion (wang et al., 2018a; duchi and ruan, 2019)

$$
\mathcal {t} (y) := \mathbb {1} \{y \geq y _ {\left(\alpha_ {3} m\right)} \}, \tag {3.98}
$$

where  $y_{(1)} \geq y_{(2)} \geq \dots \geq y_{(m)}$  denote the order statistics of  $\{y_i\}$ , and  $0 < \alpha_3 < 1$  is some properly chosen constant;

- optimal preprocessing (mondelli and montanari, 2019; luo et al., 2019):

$$
\mathcal {t} (y) := \frac {y / \bar {y} - 1}{y / \bar {y} + \sqrt {2 m / n} - 1}, \quad \bar {y} := \frac {1}{m} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {m} y _ {i}. \tag {3.99}
$$

remark 3.11. to be more precise,  $\mathcal{t}(y)$  depends not only on  $y$  but also some statistics about  $\{y_i\}$  (e.g., empirical mean). here, we suppress the dependency on such additional statistics mainly to simplify notation.

in words, the first two choices discard any measurement  $y_{i}$  that is too large (compared to the order of either the empirical mean or empirical median), the third one selects a subset of measurements that are most aligned with the unknown signal and scales their contributions to a measurement-invariant level, while the last one effectively behaves as a shrinkage operator once  $y_{i}$  rises above the empirical mean. the following theorem—which was first established in chen and candès (2017) for the version (3.96) and subsequently extended to other alternatives (zhang et al., 2016; wang et al., 2018a; lu and li, 2020; mondelli and montanari, 2019; luo et al., 2019)—confirms the effectiveness and importance of proper preprocessing in enabling order-optimal sample complexity. the interested reader is referred to these papers for the proofs.

theorem 3.18. consider the settings in section 3.7. fix any constant  $\varepsilon >0$ , and suppose  $m\geq c_0n$  for some sufficiently large constant  $c_{0} > 0$  that is independent of  $n$  and  $m$  but possibly dependent on  $\varepsilon$ . then the spectral estimate (3.95) equipped with the above choices of  $\mathcal{t}$  obeys

$$
\min  \left\{\| \boldsymbol {x} _ {\mathcal {t}} - \boldsymbol {x} ^ {\star} \| _ {2}, \| \boldsymbol {x} _ {\mathcal {t}} + \boldsymbol {x} ^ {\star} \| _ {2} \right\} \leq \varepsilon \| \boldsymbol {x} ^ {\star} \| _ {2}
$$

with probability at least  $1 - o(n^{-2})$ , provided that the parameters  $\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3}$  are suitably chosen in (3.96)-(3.98).

to demonstrate the practicability of preprocessing, we depict in figure 3.3 the numerical performance of the mean-truncated spectral method (i.e., the choice (3.96)) in comparison to the vanilla version described in section 3.7.2. these numerical experiments corroborate the clear advantage of proper preprocessing in the sample-starved regime.

finally, we remark that in addition to order-wise statistical guarantees, lu and li (2020) further pinned down sharp characterization of the error bounds (including the pre-constants) in this sample-starved regime. leveraging such sharp analyses, mondelli and montanari (2019) identified the information-theoretic optimal choice (3.99), in the sense that it leads to an estimate strictly better than a random guess (a.k.a. weak recovery) whenever it is information-theoretically possible. luo et al. (2019) further showed that this choice is uniformly optimal, meaning that it leads to the smallest principal angle between  $\pmb{x}_{\mathcal{t}}$  and  $\pmb{x}^{\star}$  uniformly over all sampling ratios when  $m$  is on the same order of  $n$ .

# robustness vis-à-vis adversarial outliers

another practical consideration that merits special attention is that the collected samples are sometimes susceptible to adversarial entries (due to, say, sensor failures or malicious attacks). to formulate this in more formal terms, consider the following modified measurement model (zhang et al., 2016; hand, 2017; hand and voroninski, 2016):

$$
y _ {i} = \left\{ \begin{array}{l l} \left(\boldsymbol {a} _ {i} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {x} ^ {\star}\right) ^ {2}, & i \notin \mathcal {s} _ {\text {o u t l i e r}}, \\ \text {a r b i t r a r y ,} & i \in \mathcal {s} _ {\text {o u t l i e r}}. \end{array} \right. \tag {3.100}
$$

here,  $s_{\text{outlier}} \subseteq \{1, \dots, m\}$  represents the unknown subset of indices associated with outliers, which is of cardinality  $|s_{\text{outlier}}| = \alpha m$  for some  $0 < \alpha < 1$ . in particular, the measurements coming from  $s_{\text{outlier}}$  might be corrupted arbitrarily. the goal is to reliably estimate  $x^{\star}$  even when the measurements are grossly corrupted.

unfortunately, the vanilla spectral method presented in section 3.7.2 might not function properly even in the presence of a single outlier; for instance, if the magnitude of this outlier is excessively large, then the leading eigenvector of  $m$  will be heavily biased by this outlier. as a result, the spectral method needs to be properly adjusted in order to combat the adverse effect of outliers.

to circumvent this issue, we first remind the readers of a classical finding in robust statistics (huber, 2004): the median statistic is oftentimes robust against adversarial outliers. leveraging this finding to the phase retrieval context, one might naturally employ the median of the measurements  $\{y_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq m}$  as a tool to help detect any excessively large outlier. in fact, this is precisely the idea behind the median-truncated scheme presented in (3.97), whose capability in dealing with outliers has been established in zhang et al. (2016) for phase retrieval and li et al. (2020c) for low-rank matrix recovery.

theorem 3.19. consider the measurement model in (3.100), and the i.i.d. gaussian design in assumption 3.3. fix any  $\varepsilon > 0$ . there exist some constants  $c_0 > 0$  and  $0 < c_1 < 1$  such that if  $m \geq c_0 n$  and  $\alpha \leq c_1$ , then the spectral estimate (3.95) equipped with (3.97) obeys

$$
\min  \left\{\| \boldsymbol {x} _ {\mathcal {t}} - \boldsymbol {x} ^ {\star} \| _ {2}, \| \boldsymbol {x} _ {\mathcal {t}} + \boldsymbol {x} ^ {\star} \| _ {2} \right\} \leq \varepsilon \| \boldsymbol {x} ^ {\star} \| _ {2}
$$

with probability at least  $1 - o(n^{-2})$ .

in a nutshell, theorem 3.19 reveals that a median-truncated spectral method achieves consistent estimation even when a constant fraction of the measurements are corrupted in an arbitrary manner. all this is guaranteed to happen even when the number  $m$  of samples is on the same order as  $n$ , thus further enhancing the resilience of spectral methods in the presence of adversarial corruptions. the interested reader is referred to zhang et al. (2016) for the proof of this theorem.

# 3.7.5 proof of auxiliary lemmas

proof of lemma 3.16. given that  $\{a_i\}$  is rotationally invariant, we assume without loss of generality that  $x^{\star} = e_{1}$ , where  $e_1$  is the first standard basis vector. thus, our task can be translated into bounding

$$
\boldsymbol {m} - \boldsymbol {m} ^ {\star} = \frac {1}{m} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {m} \left\{a _ {i, 1} ^ {2} \boldsymbol {a} _ {i} \boldsymbol {a} _ {i} ^ {\top} - (2 \boldsymbol {e} _ {1} \boldsymbol {e} _ {1} ^ {\top} + \boldsymbol {i} _ {n}) \right\} =: \frac {1}{m} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {m} \left(\boldsymbol {b} _ {i} - \mathbb {e} [ \boldsymbol {b} _ {i} ]\right),
$$

where  $a_{i,1}$  denotes the first entry of the vector  $\pmb{a}_i$ , and  $\pmb{b}_i \coloneqq a_{i,1}^2 \pmb{a}_i \pmb{a}_i^\top$ .

in order to deal with the unboundedness of gaussian random variables, we resort to the truncated matrix bernstein inequality, which requires us to first set a proper truncation level. in view of the gaussianity of  $\mathbf{a}_i$  and the union bound, one has  $\| \mathbf{a}_i \|_{\infty} \leq 5 \sqrt{\log m}$  for all  $1 \leq i \leq m$  with probability at least  $1 - m^{-11.5}$ ; on this event, one would have

$$
\left\| \boldsymbol {b} _ {i} \right\| = \left| a _ {i, 1} \right| ^ {2} \left\| \boldsymbol {a} _ {i} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} \leq n \left\| \boldsymbol {a} _ {i} \right\| _ {\infty} ^ {4} \leq 5 ^ {4} n \log^ {2} m. \tag {3.101}
$$

therefore, taking  $l \coloneqq 5^4 n\log^2 m$  leads to

$$
\mathbb {p} \{\| \boldsymbol {b} _ {i} \| \geq l \} \leq m ^ {- 1 1. 5} =: q _ {0}.
$$

further, truncating at this level does not incur much bias; to be precise, we claim that (with the proof deferred to the end of this subsection)

$$
q _ {1} := \left\| \mathbb {e} \left[ \boldsymbol {b} _ {i} \mathbb {1} \left\{\| \boldsymbol {b} _ {i} \| <   l \right\} - \mathbb {e} [ \boldsymbol {b} _ {i} ] \right] \right\| \lesssim m ^ {- 3}. \tag {3.102}
$$

the next step is to characterize the variance statistic. towards this end, it is first seen from the definition of  $\pmb{b}_i$  that

$$
\mathbb {e} \left[ \left(\boldsymbol {b} _ {i} - \mathbb {e} [ \boldsymbol {b} _ {i} ]\right) ^ {2} \right] \preceq \mathbb {e} \left[ \boldsymbol {b} _ {i} ^ {2} \right] = \mathbb {e} \left[ a _ {i, 1} ^ {4} \| \boldsymbol {a} _ {i} \| _ {2} ^ {2} \boldsymbol {a} _ {i} \boldsymbol {a} _ {i} ^ {\top} \right]. \tag {3.103}
$$

as can be easily verified,  $\mathbb{e}\big[a_{i,1}^4\| \pmb {a}_i\| _2^2\pmb {a}_i\pmb {a}_i^\top \big]$  is a diagonal matrix, whose diagonal entries obey

$$
\left(\mathbb {e} \left[ a _ {i, 1} ^ {4} \| \boldsymbol {a} _ {i} \| _ {2} ^ {2} \boldsymbol {a} _ {i} \boldsymbol {a} _ {i} ^ {\top} \right]\right) _ {l, l} = \mathbb {e} \left[ a _ {i, 1} ^ {4} a _ {i, l} ^ {2} \sum_ {j} a _ {i, j} ^ {2} \right] = \sum_ {j} \mathbb {e} \left[ a _ {i, 1} ^ {4} a _ {i, l} ^ {2} a _ {i, j} ^ {2} \right] \lesssim n
$$

for any  $1 \leq l \leq n$ , where the last relation follows from the property of standard gaussians. this taken together with (3.103) gives

$$
v := \left\| \sum_ {i} \mathbb {e} \big [ (\boldsymbol {b} _ {i} - \mathbb {e} [ \boldsymbol {b} _ {i} ]) ^ {2} \big ] \right\| \leq \sum_ {i} \max  _ {l} \left| \left(\mathbb {e} \big [ a _ {i, 1} ^ {4} \| \boldsymbol {a} _ {i} \| _ {2} ^ {2} \boldsymbol {a} _ {i} \boldsymbol {a} _ {i} ^ {\top} \big ]\right) _ {l, l} \right| \lesssim m n.
$$

invoking the truncated bernstein inequality in corollary 3.2 then yields: with probability at least  $1 - o(m^{-10}) - mq_0 = 1 - o(m^{-10})$ , one has

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \boldsymbol {m} - \boldsymbol {m} ^ {\star} \right\| \lesssim \frac {1}{m} \sqrt {v \log m} + \frac {l}{m} \log m + \frac {m q _ {1}}{m} \\ \lesssim \sqrt {\frac {n \log m}{m}} + \frac {n \log^ {3} m}{m} + \frac {1}{m ^ {3}} \lesssim \sqrt {\frac {n \log^ {3} m}{m}} \\ \end{array}
$$

as desired, with the proviso that  $m \gtrsim n\log^3 m$ .

proof of the inequality (3.102). we begin by employing the relation (3.101) to help modify the truncation event as follows:

$$
\begin{array}{l} q _ {1} = \left\| \mathbb {e} \left[ b _ {i} \mathbb {1} \left\{\left\| b _ {i} \right\| <   l \right\} - \mathbb {e} [ b _ {i} ] \right] \right\| = \left\| \mathbb {e} \left[ b _ {i} \mathbb {1} \left\{\left\| b _ {i} \right\| \geq l \right\} \right] \right\| \\ \leq \left\| \mathbb {e} \left[ \boldsymbol {b} _ {i} \mathbb {1} \left\{n \| \boldsymbol {a} _ {i} \| _ {\infty} ^ {4} \geq l \right\} \right] \right\| = \left\| \mathbb {e} \left[ \boldsymbol {b} _ {i} \mathbb {1} \left\{\| \boldsymbol {a} _ {i} \| _ {\infty} \geq \overline {{l}} \right\} \right] \right\|, \\ \end{array}
$$

where we define  $\overline{l} \coloneqq (l / n)^{1/4} = 5\sqrt{\log m}$ . it is easily seen that  $\mathbb{e}\big[b_i \mathbb{1}\{\| \pmb{a}_i \|_{\infty} \geq \overline{l}\}\big]$  is a diagonal matrix and, therefore,

$$
\begin{array}{l} q _ {1} \leq \max  _ {l} \left| \left(\mathbb {e} \left[ \boldsymbol {b} _ {i} \mathbb {1} \{\| \boldsymbol {a} _ {i} \| _ {\infty} \geq \bar {l} \} \right]\right) _ {l, l} \right| \stackrel {{\text {(i)}}} {=} \max  _ {l} \mathbb {e} \left[ a _ {i, 1} ^ {2} a _ {i, l} ^ {2} \mathbb {1} \{\| \boldsymbol {a} _ {i} \| _ {\infty} \geq \bar {l} \} \right] \\ \stackrel {\mathrm {(i i)}} {\leq} 0. 5 \max _ {l} \mathbb {e} \big [ (a _ {i, 1} ^ {4} + a _ {i, l} ^ {4}) \mathbb {1} \{\| \boldsymbol {a} _ {i} \| _ {\infty} \geq \overline {{l}} \} \big ] = \mathbb {e} \big [ a _ {i, 1} ^ {4} \mathbb {1} \{\| \boldsymbol {a} _ {i} \| _ {\infty} \geq \overline {{l}} \} \big ] \\ \leq \mathbb {e} \left[ a _ {i, 1} ^ {4} \mathbb {1} \left\{\left| a _ {i, 1} \right| \geq \bar {l} \right\} \right] + \mathbb {e} \left[ a _ {i, 1} ^ {4} \mathbb {1} \left\{\max  _ {j \neq 1} \left| a _ {i, j} \right| \geq \bar {l} \right\} \right], \tag {3.104} \\ \end{array}
$$

where (i) relies on the definition of  $b_i$ , and (ii) comes from the am-gm inequality. with regards to the first term of (3.104), observe that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathbb {e} \left[ a _ {i, 1} ^ {4} \mathbb {1} \left\{| a _ {i, 1} | \geq 5 \sqrt {\log m} \right\} \right] = \int_ {5 \sqrt {\log m}} ^ {\infty} \frac {2 \xi^ {4}}{\sqrt {2 \pi}} e ^ {- \xi^ {2} / 2} d \xi \\ \leq 2 \int_ {5 \sqrt {\log m}} ^ {\infty} \frac {e ^ {- \xi^ {2} / 4}}{\sqrt {2 \pi}} \mathrm {d} \xi = 2 \mathbb {p} \{| a _ {i, 1} | \geq 2. 5 \sqrt {\log m} \} \lesssim \frac {1}{m ^ {3}} \\ \end{array}
$$

for  $m$  sufficiently large, where we have used the fact that  $\xi^4 e^{-\xi^2 /2} \leq e^{-\xi^2 /4}$  for  $\xi \geq 5\sqrt{\log m}$ . regarding the second term of (3.104), note that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathbb {e} \left[ a _ {i, 1} ^ {4} \mathbb {1} \left\{\max  _ {j \neq 1} | a _ {i, j} | \geq \bar {l} \right\} \right] = \mathbb {e} \left[ a _ {i, 1} ^ {4} \right] \mathbb {e} \left[ \mathbb {1} \left\{\max  _ {j \neq 1} | a _ {i, j} | \geq \bar {l} \right\} \right] \\ = 3 \mathbb {p} \big \{\max  _ {j \neq 1} \left| a _ {i, j} \right| \geq 5 \sqrt {\log m} \big \} \lesssim m ^ {- 1 0}, \\ \end{array}
$$

where the first identity uses the independence between  $a_{i,1}$  and  $\{a_{i,j}\}_{j \neq 1}$ . substituting the preceding bounds into (3.104) establishes (3.102).

# 3.8 matrix completion

a pressing challenge often encountered in data science applications is estimation and learning in the face of missing data. to elucidate how to tackle this challenge via spectral methods, we delve into the renowned matrix completion problem in this section, followed by another application called tensor completion in section 3.9.

imagine that one observes a small subset of the entries in a large unknown matrix and seeks to fill in all missing entries. an archetypal example is collaborative filtering, where one aims to predict the users' preferences on a collection of products based on partially revealed user-product ratings. see figure 3.4 for an illustration. the problem, often referred to as matrix completion, is apparently ill-posed in general, as there are (much) fewer measurements than the unknowns.

fortunately, if the matrix of interest exhibits certain low-dimensional structure, then reliable recovery becomes feasible. a commonly encountered example of this kind concerns the case when the target matrix enjoys a low-rank structure. again, take collaborative filtering for example: the user-product rating matrix might be well explained by a relatively small number of latent factors connecting users' preferences with products' attributes, thus resulting in an approximately low-rank matrix. motivated by its fundamental importance, recent years have witnessed a flurry of research activity in studying low-rank matrix completion (candes and recht, 2009; keshavan et al., 2010; gross, 2011); see chen and chi (2018) and davenport and romberg (2016) for overviews of recent developments. in the sequel, we present a simple yet effective approach enabled by the spectral method, originally proposed in achlioptas and mcsherry (2007) and keshavan et al. (2010).

# 3.8.1 problem formulation and assumptions

suppose that we are interested in estimating an  $n_1 \times n_2$  rank- $r$  matrix  $m^{\star} = [m_{i,j}^{\star}]_{1 \leq i \leq n_1, 1 \leq j \leq n_2}$ . without loss of generality, we assume

$$
n _ {1} \leq n _ {2}.
$$

denote by  $m^{\star} = u^{\star}\sigma^{\star}v^{\star \top}$  the svd of  $m^{\star}$ , where the columns of  $u^{\star} \in \mathbb{r}^{n_1 \times r}$  (resp.  $v^{\star} \in \mathbb{r}^{n_2 \times r}$ ) are the left (resp. right) singular vectors of  $m^{\star}$ , and  $\sigma^{\star}$  is a diagonal matrix whose diagonal

![](images/c19c8a511206d28dbdfa3119e1dc4ac3034fb3febbc21bdbd4774a29b0513c97.jpg)  
figure 3.4: illustration of matrix completion, where each “ $\checkmark$ ” stands for an observed entry and each “?” represents a missing entry.

entries are the singular values of  $m^{\star}$ . we define the condition number of the matrix  $m^{\star}$  to be  $\kappa := \sigma_{1}(m^{\star}) / \sigma_{r}(m^{\star})$ .

to capture the presence of missing data, we introduce an index subset  $\omega \subseteq [n_1] \times [n_2]$ , such that each entry  $m_{i,j}^{\star}$  is observed if and only if  $(i,j) \in \omega$ . the goal is to reconstruct the singular subspaces  $\pmb{u}^{\star}$  and  $\pmb{v}^{\star}$ , as well as the full matrix  $\pmb{m}^{\star}$ , based on entries observed over the sampling set  $\omega$ .

random sampling. apparently, not all sampling patterns admit reliable estimation. for instance, if  $\omega$  contains only entries in the top half of the matrix, then there is in general no hope to predict the bottom half of the matrix. in order to allow for meaningful matrix completion, this monograph focuses on a natural random observation model commonly adopted in the literature, as formulated below.

assumption 3.4 (random sampling). each entry of  $m^{\star}$  is observed independently with probability  $0 < p < 1$ , namely, each  $(i,j) \in [n_1] \times [n_2]$  is included in  $\omega$  independently with probability  $p$ .

under this model, we shall view the expected number of observed entries—namely,  $pn_1n_2$ —as the sample size. in truth, as long as  $p$  is not overly small, the number of observed entries is expected to concentrate around its mean  $pn_1n_2$ .

incoherence conditions. caution needs to be exercised, however, that the random sampling model alone does not guarantee effective recovery of an arbitrary low-rank matrix  $m^{\star}$ . consider, for example, the following rank-1 matrix  $m^{\star}$  containing a single nonzero entry:

$$
\left[ \begin{array}{c c c c} 1 & 0 & \dots & 0 \\ 0 & 0 & \dots & 0 \\ \vdots & \vdots & \ddots & \vdots \\ 0 & 0 & \dots & 0 \end{array} \right].
$$

if  $p = o(1)$ , then with probability  $1 - p = 1 - o(1)$ , the sampling pattern will fail to include the nonzero entry  $m_{1,1}^{\star}$ , thus ruling out the possibility of faithful matrix recovery. consequently, one needs to make sure that the sampling pattern does not suppress too much useful information. towards this end, the pioneering work candès and recht (2009) and candès and tao (2010) singled out an incoherence parameter that plays a vital role.

definition 3.1. the incoherence parameter  $\mu$  of the matrix  $m^{\star} \in \mathbb{r}^{n_1 \times n_2}$  is defined as

$$
\mu := \max \left\{\frac {n _ {1} \| \pmb {u} ^ {\star} \| _ {2 , \infty} ^ {2}}{r}, \frac {n _ {2} \| \pmb {v} ^ {\star} \| _ {2 , \infty} ^ {2}}{r} \right\}.
$$

remark 3.12. recognizing the following basic relation

$$
\frac {r}{n _ {1}} = \frac {1}{n _ {1}} \| \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \| _ {\mathrm {f}} ^ {2} \leq \| \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \| _ {2, \infty} ^ {2} \leq \| \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \| ^ {2} = 1
$$

and an analogous one for  $v^{\star}$ , we have  $1 \leq \mu \leq \max \{n_1, n_2\} / r = n_2 / r$ .

in words, a small  $\mu$  indicates that the energy of the singular vectors is spread out across different elements, namely, the singular subspace of  $m^{\star}$  is not too "aligned" with any of the standard basis vectors, thus ensuring that entrywise observations provide somewhat equalized information about the full spectrum of  $m^{\star}$ . the following lemma summarizes a few immediate consequences of this definition, with the proof deferred to section 3.8.4.

lemma 3.20. assume that  $m^{\star} \in \mathbb{r}^{n_1 \times n_2}$  is  $\mu$ -incoherent. then the following relations hold

$$
\begin{array}{l} \| m ^ {\star} \| _ {2, \infty} \leq \sqrt {\mu r / n _ {1}} \| m ^ {\star} \|; \quad \| m ^ {\star^ {\top}} \| _ {2, \infty} \leq \sqrt {\mu r / n _ {2}} \| m ^ {\star} \|; (3.105a) \\ \left\| m ^ {\star} \right\| _ {\infty} \leq \mu r \left\| m ^ {\star} \right\| / \sqrt {n _ {1} n _ {2}}. (3.105b) \\ \end{array}
$$

additional notation. we find it convenient to introduce a euclidean projection operator  $\mathcal{p}_{\omega}$ :  $\mathbb{r}^{n_1\times n_2}\mapsto \mathbb{r}^{n_1\times n_2}$  such that

$$
\left[ \mathcal {p} _ {\omega} (\boldsymbol {a}) \right] _ {i, j} = \left\{ \begin{array}{l l} a _ {i, j}, & \text {i f} (i, j) \in \omega \\ 0, & \text {e l s e} \end{array} \right. \tag {3.106}
$$

for any matrix  $\mathbf{a} = [a_{i,j}] \in \mathbb{r}^{n_1 \times n_2}$ . with this notation in place, matrix completion amounts to recovering  $\mathbf{m}^{\star}$  on the basis of  $\mathcal{p}_{\omega}(\mathbf{m}^{\star})$ .

# 3.8.2 algorithm

to apply the spectral method, the first step is to form a reasonable approximation  $m$  of the unknown matrix  $m^{\star}$ . by virtue of the random sampling model (cf. assumption 3.4), a candidate approximation can be obtained from the observed data matrix via inverse probability weighting:

$$
\boldsymbol {m} := p ^ {- 1} \mathcal {p} _ {\omega} \left(\boldsymbol {m} ^ {\star}\right). \tag {3.107}
$$

the rationale is that  $m$  forms an unbiased estimate of the ground truth, namely,

$$
\mathbb {e} [ m ] = m ^ {\star},
$$

where the expectation is taken over the randomness in  $\omega$ .

as a result, the proposed spectral method proceeds by computing the rank-  $r$  svd  $\pmb{u}\pmb{\sigma}\pmb{v}^{\top}$  of the matrix  $\pmb{m}$  constructed in (3.107), and employing  $\pmb{u} \in \mathbb{r}^{n_1 \times r}$ ,  $\pmb{v} \in \mathbb{r}^{n_2 \times r}$  and  $\pmb{u}\pmb{\sigma}\pmb{v}^{\top}$  as estimates of  $\pmb{u}^{\star}$ ,  $\pmb{v}^{\star}$  and  $\pmb{m}^{\star}$ , respectively.

# 3.8.3 performance guarantees

as before, whether the subspace  $\mathbf{u}$  (resp.  $\mathbf{v}$ ) is close to  $\mathbf{u}^{\star}$  (resp.  $\mathbf{v}^{\star}$ ) relies crucially on the size of the perturbation  $\| m - m^{\star} \|$ . therefore, we begin by developing an upper bound on this quantity; the proof is based on the matrix bernstein inequality and is postponed to section 3.8.4.

lemma 3.21. consider the settings in section 3.8.1. suppose that  $n_2p \geq c\mu r\log n_2$  for some constant  $c > 0$ . then with probability at least  $1 - o(n_2^{-10})$ , the matrix  $m$  constructed in (3.107) obeys

$$
\left\| m - m ^ {\star} \right\| \lesssim \sqrt {\frac {\mu r \log n _ {2}}{n _ {1} p}} \left\| m ^ {\star} \right\|.
$$

with this perturbation bound in place, we are equipped to apply wedin's  $\sin \theta$  theorem to obtain the following results. the condition on the sample size in theorem 3.22 is stronger than that in lemma 3.21, as we need to control the eigengap in the following theorem.

theorem 3.22. consider the settings in section 3.8.1. suppose that  $n_1p \geq c_1\kappa^2\mu r\log n_2$  for some sufficiently large constant  $c_1 > 0$ . then with probability exceeding  $1 - o(n_2^{-10})$ ,

$$
\max  \left\{\operatorname {d i s t} \left(\boldsymbol {u}, \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star}\right), \operatorname {d i s t} \left(\boldsymbol {v}, \boldsymbol {v} ^ {\star}\right) \right\} \lesssim \kappa \sqrt {\frac {\mu r \log n _ {2}}{n _ {1} p}}.
$$

proof. as a direct consequence of lemma 3.21, one has

$$
\left\| m - m ^ {\star} \right\| \lesssim \sqrt {\frac {\mu r \log n _ {2}}{n _ {1} p}} \left\| m ^ {\star} \right\| \leq \left(1 - \frac {1}{\sqrt {2}}\right) \sigma_ {r} (m ^ {\star}),
$$

provided that  $n_1p \geq c_1\kappa^2\mu r\log n_2$  for some large enough constant  $c_1 > 0$ . apply wedin's theorem (cf. (2.26)) and lemma 3.21 to obtain

$$
\max  \left\{\operatorname {d i s t} \left(\boldsymbol {u}, \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star}\right), \operatorname {d i s t} \left(\boldsymbol {v}, \boldsymbol {v} ^ {\star}\right) \right\} \leq \frac {2 \| \boldsymbol {m} - \boldsymbol {m} ^ {\star} \|}{\sigma_ {r} (\boldsymbol {m} ^ {\star})} \lesssim \kappa \sqrt {\frac {\mu r \log n _ {2}}{n _ {1} p}}
$$

as claimed.

![](images/fb45881a3bc0e9ad44acdb9e39fc6a46f1c9173d0a8810cb7180f499e4150778.jpg)

as an important implication of theorem 3.22, once the sample size exceeds

$$
p n _ {1} n _ {2} \gg \kappa^ {2} \mu r n _ {2} \log n _ {2},
$$

then the spectral estimate achieves consistent estimation in the sense that

$$
\max  \left\{\operatorname {d i s t} \left(\boldsymbol {u}, \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star}\right), \operatorname {d i s t} \left(\boldsymbol {v}, \boldsymbol {v} ^ {\star}\right) \right\} = o (1).
$$

given that  $p n_{1}n_{2} \gtrsim \mu n_{2}r\log n_{2}$  is an information-theoretic sampling requirement for reliable matrix completion when  $r = o(n_1 / \log n_2)$  (candès and tao, 2010), theorem 3.22 confirms the near optimality of spectral methods—in terms of the scaling with  $n_1$ ,  $n_2$  and  $p$ —when it comes to consistent subspace estimation.

before moving forward to the proof, we further characterize the statistical accuracy of  $\pmb{u} \pmb{\sigma} \pmb{v}^{\top}$  in estimating the unknown matrix  $m^{\star}$ . accomplishing this only requires lemma 3.21, without any need of the singular subspace perturbation theory. this result will also come in handy when we turn to discussing entrywise estimation accuracy in chapter 4.

theorem 3.23. consider the settings in section 3.8.1. suppose that  $n_2p \geq c\mu r\log n_2$  for some sufficiently large constant  $c > 0$ . then with probability at least  $1 - o(n_2^{-10})$ , one has

$$
\| \pmb {u} \pmb {\sigma} \pmb {v} ^ {\top} - \pmb {m} ^ {\star} \| _ {\mathrm {f}} \lesssim \sqrt {\frac {\mu r ^ {2} \log n _ {2}}{n _ {1} p}} \| \pmb {m} ^ {\star} \|.
$$

proof. first, note

$$
\left\| \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {\sigma} \boldsymbol {v} ^ {\top} - \boldsymbol {m} ^ {\star} \right\| \leq \left\| \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {\sigma} \boldsymbol {v} ^ {\top} - \boldsymbol {m} \right\| + \left\| \boldsymbol {m} - \boldsymbol {m} ^ {\star} \right\| \leq 2 \| \boldsymbol {m} - \boldsymbol {m} ^ {\star} \|,
$$

where the first inequality comes from the triangle inequality, and the second inequality follows from the fact that  $\boldsymbol{u} \boldsymbol{\sigma} \boldsymbol{v}^{\top}$  is the best rank-  $r$  approximation to  $\boldsymbol{m}$ , i.e.,

$$
\| \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {\sigma} \boldsymbol {v} ^ {\top} - \boldsymbol {m} \| = \min  _ {\boldsymbol {z}: \operatorname {r a n k} (\boldsymbol {z}) \leq r} \| \boldsymbol {z} - \boldsymbol {m} \| \leq \| \boldsymbol {m} - \boldsymbol {m} ^ {\star} \|.
$$

additionally, it is observed that  $\pmb{u} \pmb{\sigma} \pmb{v}^{\top} - \pmb{m}^{\star}$  has rank at most  $2r$ , which implies

$$
\left\| \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {\sigma} \boldsymbol {v} ^ {\top} - \boldsymbol {m} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {\mathrm {f}} \leq \sqrt {2 r} \left\| \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {\sigma} \boldsymbol {v} ^ {\top} - \boldsymbol {m} ^ {\star} \right\| \leq 2 \sqrt {2 r} \left\| \boldsymbol {m} - \boldsymbol {m} ^ {\star} \right\|.
$$

this combined with lemma 3.21 immediately concludes the proof.

![](images/1e53e7fee8360396522b36f2961cb6bfa56e8c56a05aeb47d6369b01c6b0a6f8.jpg)

# 3.8.4 proof of auxiliary lemmas

proof of lemma 3.20. first of all, the  $\| \cdot \|_{2,\infty}$  norm of  $m^{\star}$  can be upper bounded by

$$
\| m ^ {\star} \| _ {2, \infty} = \| u ^ {\star} \sigma^ {\star} v ^ {\star \top} \| _ {2, \infty} \leq \| u ^ {\star} \| _ {2, \infty} \| \sigma^ {\star} \| \| v ^ {\star} \| \leq \sqrt {\frac {\mu r}{n _ {1}}} \| m ^ {\star} \|.
$$

here, the first inequality arises from the elementary bounds  $\| \boldsymbol{a}\boldsymbol{b}\|_{2,\infty} \leq \| \boldsymbol{a}\|_{2,\infty}\| \boldsymbol{b}\|$  and  $\| \boldsymbol{a}\boldsymbol{b}\| \leq \| \boldsymbol{a}\| \| \boldsymbol{b}\|$ , whereas the last relation uses definition 3.1, the orthonormality of  $v^{\star}$ , and identifies  $\| \sigma^{\star}\|$  with  $\| m^{\star}\|$ . the bound on  $\| m^{\star\top}\|_{2,\infty}$  can be derived analogously and is omitted for brevity. in addition, the matrix  $m^{\star}$  is elementwise bounded by

$$
\| m ^ {\star} \| _ {\infty} = \| u ^ {\star} \sigma^ {\star} v ^ {\star^ {\top}} \| _ {\infty} \leq \| u ^ {\star} \| _ {2, \infty} \| v ^ {\star} \| _ {2, \infty} \| \sigma^ {\star} \| \leq \frac {\mu r}{\sqrt {n _ {1} n _ {2}}} \| m ^ {\star} \|.
$$

here, the first inequality follows from the fact  $\| \boldsymbol{a}\boldsymbol{b}^{\top}\|_{\infty} \leq \| \boldsymbol{a}\|_{2,\infty}\| \boldsymbol{b}\|_{2,\infty}$  and the aforementioned one  $\| \boldsymbol{a}\boldsymbol{b}\|_{2,\infty} \leq \| \boldsymbol{a}\|_{2,\infty}\| \boldsymbol{b}\|$ , while the last inequality again relies on definition 3.1.

proof of lemma 3.21. note that the matrix  $\pmb{e} \coloneqq p^{-1}\mathcal{p}_{\omega}(m^{\star}) - m^{\star}$  can be expressed as the sum of  $n_1n_2$  i.i.d. random matrices

$$
\frac{1}{p}\mathcal{p}_{\omega}(m^{\star}) - m^{\star} = \sum_{i = 1}^{n_{1}}\sum_{j = 1}^{n_{2}}\underbrace{(p^{-1}\delta_{i,j} - 1)m_{i,j}^{\star}\boldsymbol{e}_{i}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}^{\top}}_{=: x_{i,j}}.
$$

here,  $\delta_{i,j}$  (which indicates whether the  $(i,j)$ -th entry is observed) follows an independent bernoulli distribution with parameter  $p$ , and  $\mathbf{e}_i$  stands for the  $i$ -th standard basis vector of appropriate dimensions. it is easily seen that for each  $(i,j)$ ,

$$
\mathbb {e} [ \boldsymbol {x} _ {i, j} ] = \mathbf {0} \quad \mathrm {a n d} \quad \| \boldsymbol {x} _ {i, j} \| \leq \frac {1}{p} \| \boldsymbol {m} ^ {\star} \| _ {\infty} \leq \frac {\mu r}{p \sqrt {n _ {1} n _ {2}}} \| \boldsymbol {m} ^ {\star} \|,
$$

# 3.9. tensor completion

where the last relation results from the entrywise upper bound (3.105b) on  $m^{\star}$ . in order to apply the matrix bernstein inequality (cf. corollary 3.3), we need to control the variance statistic

$$
v := \max  \left\{\left\| \sum_ {i, j} \mathbb {e} \left[ \boldsymbol {x} _ {i, j} \boldsymbol {x} _ {i, j} ^ {\top} \right] \right\|, \left\| \sum_ {i, j} \mathbb {e} \left[ \boldsymbol {x} _ {i, j} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {x} _ {i, j} \right] \right\| \right\}.
$$

regarding the first variance term, we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \sum_ {i, j} \mathbb {e} \left[ \boldsymbol {x} _ {i, j} \boldsymbol {x} _ {i, j} ^ {\top} \right] = \sum_ {i, j} \mathbb {e} \left[ \left(p ^ {- 1} \delta_ {i, j} - 1\right) ^ {2} \left(m _ {i, j} ^ {\star}\right) ^ {2} \boldsymbol {e} _ {i} \boldsymbol {e} _ {j} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {e} _ {j} \boldsymbol {e} _ {i} ^ {\top} \right] \\ = \frac {1 - p}{p} \sum_ {i, j} \left(m _ {i, j} ^ {\star}\right) ^ {2} \boldsymbol {e} _ {i} \boldsymbol {e} _ {i} ^ {\top} = \frac {1 - p}{p} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n _ {1}} \| m _ {i, \cdot} ^ {\star} \| _ {2} ^ {2} \boldsymbol {e} _ {i} \boldsymbol {e} _ {i} ^ {\top} \\ \preceq \frac {1 - p}{p} \| m ^ {\star} \| _ {2, \infty} ^ {2} i _ {n _ {1}} \preceq \frac {\mu r}{n _ {1} p} \| m ^ {\star} \| ^ {2} i _ {n _ {1}}. \\ \end{array}
$$

here, the first identity arises from the definition of  $\mathbf{x}_{i,j}$ , the second one calculates the variance of bernoulli random variables, and the last line relies on the upper bound (3.105a). similarly, the second term in the variance statistic enjoys the following characterization:

$$
\sum_ {i, j} \mathbb {e} \left[ \boldsymbol {x} _ {i, j} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {x} _ {i, j} \right] \preceq \frac {\mu r}{n _ {2} p} \| \boldsymbol {m} ^ {\star} \| ^ {2} \boldsymbol {i} _ {n _ {2}}.
$$

taking the above relations together and recalling that  $n_1 \leq n_2$  gives

$$
v \leq \frac {\mu r}{n _ {1} p} \| \boldsymbol {m} ^ {\star} \| ^ {2}.
$$

with the above bounds in place, invoking matrix bernstein (see corollary 3.3) reveals that: with probability at least  $1 - o(n_2^{-10})$ ,

$$
\| \boldsymbol {e} \| \lesssim \sqrt {\frac {\mu r \| \boldsymbol {m} ^ {\star} \| ^ {2} \log n _ {2}}{n _ {1} p}} + \frac {\mu r \| \boldsymbol {m} ^ {\star} \| \log n _ {2}}{p \sqrt {n _ {1} n _ {2}}} \asymp \sqrt {\frac {\mu r \| \boldsymbol {m} ^ {\star} \| ^ {2} \log n _ {2}}{n _ {1} p}},
$$

where the last inequality is valid as long as  $n_2p \gtrsim \mu r\log n_2$

# 3.9 tensor completion

tensor data, which can be viewed as a higher-order generalization of matrix data, are routinely used in science and engineering applications to capture multi-way interactions across variables of interest (kolda and bader, 2009; sidiropoulos et al., 2017; anandkumar et al., 2014). akin to matrix completion, the problem of tensor completion aims to reconstruct a (structured) tensor when the vast majority of its entries are unobserved, a task that spans a wide spectrum of applications including visual data inpainting, harmonic retrieval, seismic data analysis, and so on (liu et al. 2012; chen and chi, 2014; kreimer et al., 2013).

apparently, this task cannot possibly be accomplished without exploiting further structural assumptions on the tensor under consideration. inspired by the success of low-rank matrix completion, we explore the case where the unknown tensor enjoys certain low-rank structure (more specifically, low canonical-polyadic (cp) rank (kolda and bader, 2009)). for simplicity, we concentrate on order-three tensors (namely,  $\pmb{t} = [t_{i,j,k}] \in \mathbb{r}^{n_1 \times n_2 \times n_3}$ ), which already capture several fundamental challenges intrinsic to tensor estimation. in addition, we take the dimensionality  $n_1 = n_2 = n_3 = n$  for simplicity of presentation.

![](images/f11d8b86e4ae21572deb977aa8c898698ba3ed9bee59f0119726ffe0117ef689.jpg)  
figure 3.5: illustration of tensor completion, where we observe partial entries of an order-three tensor.

# 3.9.1 problem formulation and assumptions

notation. before describing our models, we introduce several notation that will be useful throughout. for any vectors  $\mathbf{a} = [a_i]_{1\leq i\leq n},\mathbf{b} = [b_i]_{1\leq i\leq n},\mathbf{c} = [c_i]_{1\leq i\leq n}\in \mathbb{r}^n$ , the tensor  $\mathbf{a}\otimes \mathbf{b}\otimes \mathbf{c}$  stands for an  $n\times n\times n$  array whose  $(i,j,k)$ -th entry is given by  $a_{i}b_{j}c_{k}$ . additionally, we denote by  $\mathbf{a}\otimes \mathbf{b}:= \left[ \begin{array}{c}a_1\mathbf{b}\\ \vdots \\ a_nb \end{array} \right]$  the kronecker product between  $\mathbf{a}$  and  $\mathbf{b}$ . for any tensor  $\pmb{t} = [t_{i,j,k}]_{1\leq i,j,k\leq n}\in \mathbb{r}^{n\times n\times n}$ , we say that  $\mathbf{a} = [a_{i,j}]\in \mathbb{r}^{n\times n^2}$  is the mode-1 matricization of  $\pmb{t}$ , denoted by

$$
\boldsymbol {a} = \operatorname {u n f o l d} (\boldsymbol {t}),
$$

if  $a_{i,(j - 1)n + k} = t_{i,j,k}$  for all  $(i,j,k)\in [n]\times [n]\times [n]$ .

models and assumptions. suppose the unknown order-three symmetric tensor  $\pmb{t}^{\star} = [t_{i,j,k}^{\star}]_{1\leq i,j,k\leq n}$  is a superposition of  $r$  ( $r < n$ ) rank-one symmetric tensors:

$$
\boldsymbol {t} ^ {\star} = \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {r} \boldsymbol {w} _ {i} ^ {\star} \otimes \boldsymbol {w} _ {i} ^ {\star} \otimes \boldsymbol {w} _ {i} ^ {\star} \in \mathbb {r} ^ {n \times n \times n}, \tag {3.108}
$$

where  $\{\pmb{w}_i^\star \in \mathbb{r}^n\}$  represents a set of latent tensor factors. what we have available are incomplete observations of the entries of  $\pmb{t}^{\star}$ . the observed data can be succinctly encoded by an index subset  $\omega \subseteq [n]\times [n]\times [n]$  (called a sampling set) and a tensor  $\pmb{t} = [t_{i,j,k}]_{1\leq i,j,k\leq n}$  as follows

$$
t _ {i, j, k} = \left\{ \begin{array}{l l} t _ {i, j, k} ^ {\star}, & \text {i f} (i, j, k) \in \omega , \\ 0, & \text {e l s e .} \end{array} \right. \tag {3.109}
$$

this subsection aims for an intermediate goal, namely, estimating the subspace spanned by  $\{\pmb{w}_i^{\star}\}_{1\leq i\leq r}$ , which often serves as a crucial initial stage towards reliable completion of the whole tensor. the interested reader is referred to montanari and sun (2018) and cai et al. (2021b) for subsequent stages of tensor completion algorithms.

similar to the matrix completion counterpart, we explore a random sampling pattern such that for all  $(i,j,k) \in [n] \times [n] \times [n]$ ,

$$
(i, j, k) \in \omega \quad \text {i n d e p e n d e n t l y w i t h p r o b a b i l i t y} p. \tag {3.110}
$$

in addition, we define for notational convenience that

$$
\nu_ {i} := \| \boldsymbol {w} _ {i} ^ {\star} \| _ {2} ^ {3}, \quad \nu_ {\min } := \min  _ {1 \leq i \leq r} \nu_ {i}, \quad \nu_ {\max } := \max  _ {1 \leq i \leq r} \nu_ {i}, \tag {3.111}
$$

# 3.9. tensor completion

where  $\nu_{i}$  reflects the size of the rank-1 component  $\pmb{w}_{i}^{\star} \otimes \pmb{w}_{i}^{\star} \otimes \pmb{w}_{i}^{\star}$ . the condition number of  $\pmb{t}^{\star}$  is then defined as  $\kappa := \nu_{\max} / \nu_{\min}$ .

we shall also introduce several incoherence parameters as follows.

definition 3.2. define the incoherence parameters of  $t^{\star}$  (cf. (3.108)) as

$$
\mu_ {1} := \max  _ {1 \leq i \leq r} \frac {n \| \boldsymbol {w} _ {i} ^ {\star} \| _ {\infty} ^ {2}}{\| \boldsymbol {w} _ {i} ^ {\star} \| _ {2} ^ {2}}, \quad \text {a n d} \quad \mu_ {2} := \max  _ {i \neq j} \frac {n | \langle \boldsymbol {w} _ {i} ^ {\star} , \boldsymbol {w} _ {j} ^ {\star} \rangle | ^ {2}}{\| \boldsymbol {w} _ {i} ^ {\star} \| _ {2} ^ {2} \| \boldsymbol {w} _ {j} ^ {\star} \| _ {2} ^ {2}}. \tag {3.112}
$$

let us explain these parameters in words: small  $\mu_{1}$  and  $\mu_{2}$  reflect that (i) the energy of each tensor factor  $\pmb{w}_{i}^{\star}$  is spread out across different entries, and (ii) the factors  $\{\pmb{w}_{i}^{\star}\}$  are not too correlated with each other. to simplify presentation, we set

$$
\mu := \max  \left\{\mu_ {1}, \mu_ {2} \right\}.
$$

# 3.9.2 algorithm

unfortunately, it is notoriously difficult to exploit the low-rank structure—and many other low-complexity structure—efficiently in the original tensor space (hillar and lim, 2013). to circumvent this issue, a natural strategy thus attempts to matricize the tensor data, followed by an application of suitable low-rank matrix estimation algorithms. specifically, let us unfold the tensor  $\pmb{t}^{\star}$  into an  $n \times n^{2}$  matrix  $\pmb{a}^{\star}$  as follows

$$
\boldsymbol {a} ^ {\star} := \operatorname {u n f o l d} \left(\boldsymbol {t} ^ {\star}\right) = \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {r} \boldsymbol {w} _ {i} ^ {\star} \left(\boldsymbol {w} _ {i} ^ {\star} \otimes \boldsymbol {w} _ {i} ^ {\star}\right) ^ {\top} \in \mathbb {r} ^ {n \times n ^ {2}}. \tag {3.113}
$$

the resulting matrix  $a^{\star}$  inherits the low-rank structure, as it clearly has rank at most  $r$ . we shall also matricize the observed data as

$$
\boldsymbol {a} := \operatorname {u n f o l d} (\boldsymbol {t}). \tag {3.114}
$$

in order to estimate the subspace  $u^{\star}$  spanned by  $\{w_i^\star\}_{1 \leq i \leq r}$  (which is the column space of  $a^{\star}$  as well), the spectral method studied here resorts to the rescaled gram matrix  $p^{-2}aa^{\top}$ . as a sanity check, if there is absolutely no missing data (i.e.,  $p = 1$ ), then  $p^{-2}aa^{\top}$  reduces to  $a^{\star}a^{\star \top}$ , whose column space coincides with that of  $a^{\star}$ . turning to the scenario with missing data, a close inspection reveals that

$$
\frac {1}{p ^ {2}} \mathbb {e} [ \boldsymbol {a} \boldsymbol {a} ^ {\top} ] = \boldsymbol {a} ^ {\star} \boldsymbol {a} ^ {\star \top} + \left(\frac {1}{p} - 1\right) \mathcal {p} _ {\text {d i a g}} (\boldsymbol {a} ^ {\star} \boldsymbol {a} ^ {\star \top}), \tag {3.115}
$$

where  $\mathcal{p}_{\mathrm{diag}}(\cdot)$  denotes the euclidean projection onto the set of matrices with zero off-diagonal entries. this, however, makes apparent a severe issue: in the highly subsampled regime (i.e., where  $p$  is small), the diagonal components might be excessively large and non-identical, thus destroying the low-rank structure in (3.115).

to mitigate their undesirable effects, it is advisable to properly adjust the sizes of the diagonal entries (montanari and sun, 2018; cai et al., 2021a). as it turns out, a simple yet plausible scheme is diagonal deletion, which exploits only the off-diagonal part as follows

$$
\boldsymbol {m} := \frac {1}{p ^ {2}} \mathcal {p} _ {\text {o f f - d i a g}} \left(\boldsymbol {a} \boldsymbol {a} ^ {\top}\right). \tag {3.116a}
$$

here,  $\mathcal{p}_{\mathrm{off - diag}}(\cdot)$  stands for the operator that zeros out all diagonal entries of a matrix. one can easily verify that, in expectation,

$$
\mathbb {e} [ m ] = \underbrace {\boldsymbol {a} ^ {\star} \boldsymbol {a} ^ {\star \top}} _ {=: m ^ {\star}} - \mathcal {p} _ {\text {d i a g}} \left(\boldsymbol {a} ^ {\star} \boldsymbol {a} ^ {\star \top}\right), \tag {3.116b}
$$

which stays quite close to the low-rank matrix  $m^{\star}$  as long as the diagonal entries of  $m^{\star}$  are small enough. the spectral method then proceeds by calculating the top- $r$  eigendecomposition  $\boldsymbol{u} \boldsymbol{\lambda} \boldsymbol{u}^{\top}$  of  $\boldsymbol{m}$  and returning  $\boldsymbol{u}$  as the subspace estimate. here, the columns of  $\boldsymbol{u} \in \mathbb{r}^{n \times r}$  are formed by the  $r$  leading eigenvectors of  $\boldsymbol{m}$ , while  $\boldsymbol{\lambda} \in \mathbb{r}^{r \times r}$  is a diagonal matrix containing the  $r$  leading eigenvalues.

remark 3.13. the diagonal deletion idea has been recommended not just for tensor completion, but also for problems including but not limited to bi-clustering (florescu and perkins, 2016), pca with missing data and/or heteroskedastic noise (cai et al., 2021a; abbe et al., 2020a), and contextual community detection (abbe et al., 2020a). instead of diagonal deletion, one might also consider properly rescaling the diagonal entries based on the sampling mechanism; see, e.g., montanari and sun (2018), lounici (2014), loh and wainwright (2012), zhang et al. (2021), and zhu et al. (2019).

# 3.9.3 performance guarantees

the aforementioned spectral method can be analyzed by means of the  $\ell_2$  perturbation theory as well. as usual, this requires first controlling the size of  $e\coloneqq m - m^{\star}$ , where  $m$  and  $m^{\star}$  are defined in (3.116a) and (3.116b), respectively.

lemma 3.24. consider the settings in section 3.9.1. there exists some universal constant  $c > 0$  such that with probability at least  $1 - o(n^{-7})$ ,

$$
\| \boldsymbol {e} \| \leq c \left(\frac {\mu^ {3 / 2} r \sqrt {\log n}}{n ^ {3 / 2} p} + \sqrt {\frac {\mu^ {2} r \log n}{n ^ {2} p}} + \frac {\mu r}{n}\right) \nu_ {\max } ^ {2}, \tag {3.117}
$$

provided that  $p \gtrsim \frac{\mu^{3/2} r \log^{2.5} n}{n^{3/2}}$  and that  $\mu \max \{ \log n, r^2 \kappa^4 \} \leq c_3 n$  for some sufficiently small constant  $c_3 > 0$ .

in order to apply the davis-kahan sin  $\theta$  theorem (cf. corollary 2.8), another step boils down to characterizing the eigengap of the matrix  $m^{\star} = a^{\star}a^{\star^{\top}}$  of interest. our result is this:

lemma 3.25. suppose that  $\mu r^2\kappa^4 \leq c_3n$  for some sufficiently small constant  $c_{3} > 0$ . then the  $i$ -th largest eigenvalue of  $a^{\star}a^{\star \top}$  obeys

$$
\lambda_ {i} \left(\boldsymbol {a} ^ {\star} \boldsymbol {a} ^ {\star \top}\right) \in \left[ \nu_ {\min } ^ {2} / 2, 2 \nu_ {\max } ^ {2} \right], \quad \text {i f} 1 \leq i \leq r;
$$

$$
\lambda_ {i} \left(\boldsymbol {a} ^ {\star} \boldsymbol {a} ^ {\star \top}\right) = 0, \quad \text {i f} i \geq r + 1.
$$

the preceding two lemmas, which will be established in section 3.9.4, readily lead to the following statistical guarantees for the spectral method presented in section 3.9.2.

theorem 3.26. consider the settings in section 3.9.1. suppose that

$$
\mu r ^ {2} \kappa^ {4} \log n \leq c _ {4} n \quad \text {a n d} \quad p \geq c _ {5} \frac {\mu^ {3 / 2} \kappa^ {2} r \log^ {2 . 5} n}{n ^ {3 / 2}} \tag {3.118}
$$

# 3.9. tensor completion

hold for some small (resp. large) enough constant  $c_4 > 0$  (resp.  $c_5 > 0$ ). then with probability at least  $1 - o(n^{-7})$ , one has

$$
\operatorname {d i s t} \left(\boldsymbol {u}, \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star}\right) \lesssim \frac {\mu^ {3 / 2} \kappa^ {2} r \sqrt {\log n}}{n ^ {3 / 2} p} + \sqrt {\frac {\mu^ {2} \kappa^ {4} r \log n}{n ^ {2} p}} + \frac {\mu \kappa^ {2} r}{n}.
$$

proof. in view of lemmas 3.24-3.25, one would have  $\| \pmb{e} \| \leq (1 - 1 / \sqrt{2}) \lambda_r(\pmb{m}^\star)$  under condition (3.118). corollary 2.8 combined with lemma 3.24 then tells us that, with probability at least  $1 - o(n^{-7})$ ,

$$
\operatorname {d i s t} \left(\boldsymbol {u}, \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star}\right) \leq \frac {2 \| \boldsymbol {e} \|}{\lambda_ {r} \left(\boldsymbol {m} ^ {\star}\right)} \lesssim \frac {\left(\frac {\mu^ {3 / 2} r \sqrt {\log n}}{n ^ {3 / 2} p} + \sqrt {\frac {\mu^ {2} r \log n}{n ^ {2} p}} + \frac {\mu r}{n}\right) \nu_ {\max } ^ {2}}{\nu_ {\min } ^ {2}}
$$

as desired.

theorem 3.26 is noteworthy for its implication on the sample complexity. to be precise, consider, for simplicity, the scenario where  $r, \mu, \kappa = o(1)$ . in order to achieve consistent estimation in the sense that  $\mathrm{dist}(\mathbf{u}, \mathbf{u}^{\star}) = o(1)$ , it suffices for the sample size—which sharply concentrates around  $n^3 p$  under our model—to exceed

$$
n ^ {3} p \gtrsim n ^ {3 / 2} \mathrm {p o l y} \log (n).
$$

the careful reader might immediately remark that this sample complexity remains substantially higher than the information-theoretic limit, the latter of which is  $nr = o(n)$  in this case since there are only  $nr$  free parameters. it is worth noting, however, that all polynomial-time algorithms developed in the literature for tensor completion require a sample size at least exceeding the order of  $n^{3/2}$  (barak and moitra, 2016). this hints at the (potential) existence of a computational barrier that prevents one from achieving the information-theoretic limit efficiently. viewed in this light, the spectral method presented herein already achieves near-optimal sample complexity—when restricted to computationally tractable algorithms—if the objective is consistent subspace estimation.

# 3.9.4 proof of auxiliary lemmas

proof of lemma 3.24. define the following zero-mean random matrix

$$
\boldsymbol {z} = p ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {a} - \boldsymbol {a} ^ {\star}.
$$

it is self-evident that

$$
p ^ {- 2} \left(\boldsymbol {a} \boldsymbol {a} ^ {\top} - \mathbb {e} \left[ \boldsymbol {a} \boldsymbol {a} ^ {\top} \right]\right) = \boldsymbol {a} ^ {\star} \boldsymbol {z} ^ {\top} + \boldsymbol {z} \boldsymbol {a} ^ {\star \top} + \left(\boldsymbol {z} \boldsymbol {z} ^ {\top} - \mathbb {e} \left[ \boldsymbol {z} \boldsymbol {z} ^ {\top} \right]\right),
$$

which implies that the identity holds for the off-diagonal part. by the definitions (3.116a) and (3.116b), it follows from the triangle inequality that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| m - m ^ {\star} \right\| \leq \left\| \mathcal {p} _ {\text {d i a g}} \left(a ^ {\star} a ^ {\star^ {\top}}\right) \right\| + 2 \left\| \mathcal {p} _ {\text {o f f - d i a g}} \left(a ^ {\star} z ^ {\top}\right) \right\| \\ + \left\| \mathcal {p} _ {\text {o f f - d i a g}} \left(\boldsymbol {z} \boldsymbol {z} ^ {\top} - \mathbb {e} \left[ \boldsymbol {z} \boldsymbol {z} ^ {\top} \right]\right) \right\|. \tag {3.119} \\ \end{array}
$$

in the sequel, we shall discuss how to control the three terms on the right-hand side of (3.119) separately.

step 1: bounding  $\| \mathcal{p}_{\mathrm{diag}}(\pmb{a}^{\star}\pmb{a}^{\star \top})\|$ . it is straightforward to verify that

$$
\left\| \mathcal {p} _ {\mathrm {d i a g}} \left(\boldsymbol {a} ^ {\star} \boldsymbol {a} ^ {\star^ {\top}}\right) \right\| = \max  _ {1 \leq l \leq n} \| \boldsymbol {a} _ {l, \cdot} ^ {\star} \| _ {2} ^ {2} = \| \boldsymbol {a} ^ {\star} \| _ {2, \infty} ^ {2}. \tag {3.120}
$$

it thus suffices to bound  $\| a^{\star}\|_{2,\infty}$ , which we shall discuss momentarily.

step 2: bounding  $\| \mathcal{p}_{\mathrm{off - diag}}(\pmb {z}\pmb {z}^{\top} - \mathbb{e}[\pmb {z}\pmb {z}^{\top}])\|$ . define a collection of independent zero-mean random matrices as follows

$$
\boldsymbol {q} _ {i} := \mathcal {p} _ {\mathrm {o f f - d i a g}} (\boldsymbol {z} _ {., i} \boldsymbol {z} _ {., i} ^ {\top}), \qquad 1 \leq i \leq n ^ {2},
$$

with which we can express

$$
\mathcal {p} _ {\text {o f f - d i a g}} \left(\boldsymbol {z} \boldsymbol {z} ^ {\top} - \mathbb {e} \left[ \boldsymbol {z} \boldsymbol {z} ^ {\top} \right]\right) = \sum_ {i} \left(\boldsymbol {q} _ {i} - \mathbb {e} \left[ \boldsymbol {q} _ {i} \right]\right) = \sum_ {i} \boldsymbol {q} _ {i}. \tag {3.121}
$$

here the last relation uses the fact that  $\mathcal{p}_{\mathrm{off - diag}}(\mathbb{e}[zz^{\top}]) = \sum_{i}\mathbb{e}[q_{i}] = 0$ . recognizing that the entries of  $z_{,i}$  are independently generated, one can see from straightforward calculations that  $\mathbb{e}[q_iq_i^\top ]$  is a diagonal matrix, whose diagonal entries satisfy

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left(\mathbb {e} \left[ \boldsymbol {q} _ {i} \boldsymbol {q} _ {i} ^ {\top} \right]\right) _ {l, l} = \mathbb {e} \left[ z _ {l, i} ^ {2} \right] \sum_ {j: j \neq l} \mathbb {e} \left[ z _ {j, i} ^ {2} \right] = \frac {1 - p}{p} \left(a _ {l, i} ^ {\star}\right) ^ {2} \sum_ {j: j \neq l} \frac {1 - p}{p} \left(a _ {j, i} ^ {\star}\right) ^ {2} \\ \leq \frac {1}{p ^ {2}} \left(a _ {l, i} ^ {\star}\right) ^ {2} \| a _ {., i} ^ {\star} \| _ {2} ^ {2} \leq \frac {1}{p ^ {2}} \left(a _ {l, i} ^ {\star}\right) ^ {2} \| a ^ {\star} \| _ {\infty , 2} ^ {2} \\ \end{array}
$$

for all  $1 \leq l \leq n$ . taking into account all samples yields

$$
\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n ^ {2}} \left(\mathbb {e} \left[ \boldsymbol {q} _ {i} \boldsymbol {q} _ {i} ^ {\top} \right]\right) _ {l, l} \leq \frac {1}{p ^ {2}} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n ^ {2}} \left(a _ {l, i} ^ {\star}\right) ^ {2} \| \boldsymbol {a} ^ {\star} \| _ {\infty , 2} ^ {2} \leq \frac {1}{p ^ {2}} \| \boldsymbol {a} ^ {\star} \| _ {2, \infty} ^ {2} \| \boldsymbol {a} ^ {\star} \| _ {\infty , 2} ^ {2}
$$

for any  $1 \leq l \leq n$ , which together with the diagonal structure of  $\mathbb{e}[q_i q_i^\top]$  leads to an upper bound on the variance statistic

$$
v := \left\| \sum_ {i} \mathbb {e} [ \boldsymbol {q} _ {i} \boldsymbol {q} _ {i} ^ {\top} ] \right\| = \left| \max _ {l} \left(\sum_ {i} \left(\mathbb {e} [ \boldsymbol {q} _ {i} \boldsymbol {q} _ {i} ^ {\top} ]\right) _ {l, l}\right) \right| \leq \frac {\| \boldsymbol {a} ^ {\star} \| _ {2 , \infty} ^ {2} \| \boldsymbol {a} ^ {\star} \| _ {\infty , 2} ^ {2}}{p ^ {2}}.
$$

in addition, we identify a suitable truncation level and claim that

$$
\mathbb {p} \left\{\| q _ {i} \| _ {2} \geq l \right\} \leq 2 n ^ {- 7} =: q _ {0}, \tag {3.122a}
$$

$$
\left\| \mathbb {e} \left[ q _ {i} \mathbb {1} \left\{\left\| q _ {i} \right\| _ {2} \leq l \right\} \right] \right\| \leq 4 n ^ {- 7} p ^ {- 2} \| a ^ {\star} \| _ {\infty , 2} ^ {2} =: q _ {1}, \tag {3.122b}
$$

where we define  $l \coloneqq 2\big(4\sqrt{\frac{\log n}{p}}\| a^{\star}\|_{\infty ,2} + \frac{6\log n}{p}\| a^{\star}\|_{\infty}\big)^{2}$ . armed with these observations, the truncated matrix bernstein inequality (see corollary 3.2) takes together with (3.121) reveals that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \mathcal {p} _ {\text {o f f - d i a g}} \left(\boldsymbol {z} \boldsymbol {z} ^ {\top} - \mathbb {e} \left[ \boldsymbol {z} \boldsymbol {z} ^ {\top} \right]\right) \right\| \lesssim \sqrt {v \log n} + l \log n + n ^ {2} q _ {1} \\ \lesssim \frac {\sqrt {\log n}}{p} \| \boldsymbol {a} ^ {\star} \| _ {2, \infty} \| \boldsymbol {a} ^ {\star} \| _ {\infty , 2} + \frac {\log^ {2} n}{p} \| \boldsymbol {a} ^ {\star} \| _ {\infty , 2} ^ {2} + \frac {\log^ {3} n}{p ^ {2}} \| \boldsymbol {a} ^ {\star} \| _ {\infty} ^ {2} \tag {3.123} \\ \end{array}
$$

with probability  $1 - o(n^{-7}) - nq_{0} = 1 - o(n^{-7})$ , provided that  $p \gtrsim n^{-5}$ .

step 3: bounding  $\| \mathcal{p}_{\mathrm{off - diag}}(\pmb {a}^{\star}\pmb{z}^{\top})\|$ . this term can be controlled in a similar fashion as  $\| \mathcal{p}_{\mathrm{off - diag}}(\pmb {z}\pmb {z}^{\top} - \mathbb{e}[\pmb {z}\pmb {z}^{\top}])\|$ . we thus omit the details and only state the result as follows:

$$
\begin{array}{l} \| \mathcal {p} _ {\mathrm {o f f - d i a g}} (\pmb {a} ^ {\star} \pmb {z} ^ {\top}) \| \lesssim \| \pmb {a} ^ {\star} \| _ {\infty , 2} \| \pmb {a} ^ {\star} \| \sqrt {\frac {\log n}{p}} \\ + \sqrt {\frac {\log^ {3} n}{p}} \| \boldsymbol {a} ^ {\star} \| _ {\infty , 2} ^ {2} + \| \boldsymbol {a} ^ {\star} \| _ {\infty , 2} \| \boldsymbol {a} ^ {\star} \| _ {\infty} \frac {\log^ {2} n}{p} \tag {3.124} \\ \end{array}
$$

holds with probability at least  $1 - o(n^{-7})$ .

step 4: to finish up, we are in need of bounding  $\| a^{\star}\|_{\infty ,2},\| a^{\star}\|_{2,\infty}$  and  $\| a^{\star}\|_{\infty}$ , which is accomplished in the following lemma.

lemma 3.27. suppose that  $\mu r^2\leq n$  . then one has

$$
\left\| \boldsymbol {a} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {\infty} \leq \frac {\mu^ {3 / 2} r \nu_ {\max}}{n ^ {3 / 2}}, \left\| \boldsymbol {a} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {\infty , 2} \leq \frac {\mu \sqrt {2 r} \nu_ {\max}}{n}, \left\| \boldsymbol {a} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {2, \infty} \leq \sqrt {\frac {2 \mu r}{n}} \nu_ {\max}.
$$

taking lemma 3.27 collectively with (3.120), (3.123), (3.124) and combining terms, we arrive at

$$
(3. 1 2 0) + (3. 1 2 3) + (3. 1 2 4) \lesssim \left(\frac {\mu^ {3 / 2} r \sqrt {\log n}}{n ^ {3 / 2} p} + \sqrt {\frac {\mu^ {2} r \log n}{n ^ {2} p}} + \frac {\mu r}{n}\right) \nu_ {\max } ^ {2}
$$

with probability at least  $1 - o(n^{-7})$ , provided that  $\mu \log n \leq n$  and  $p \gtrsim \frac{\mu^{3/2} r \log^{2.5} n}{n^{3/2}}$ . this taken together with (3.119) concludes the proof.

proof of the relation (3.122). we first make note of a connection between  $\mathbf{q}_i$  and  $\mathbf{z}_{\cdot,i}$  as follows

$$
\left\| q _ {i} \right\| \leq \left\| z _ {\cdot , i} z _ {\cdot , i} ^ {\top} \right\| + \left\| \mathcal {p} _ {\mathrm {d i a g}} \left(z _ {\cdot , i} z _ {\cdot , i} ^ {\top}\right) \right\| \leq 2 \left\| z _ {\cdot , i} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2}, \tag {3.125}
$$

which motivates us to first control the size of  $\mathbf{z}_{\cdot,i}$ . by construction, each entry  $z_{j,i}$  can be written as  $z_{j,i} = \left(\frac{1}{p}\delta_{j,i} - 1\right)a_{j,i}^{\star}$ , where  $\{\delta_{j,i}\}$  is a collection of independent bernoulli random variables with mean  $p$ . this observation allows one to derive

$$
\begin{array}{l} b _ {z} := \max  _ {i, j} | z _ {j, i} | \leq \frac {1}{p} \| \boldsymbol {a} ^ {\star} \| _ {\infty}; \\ v _ {z} := \mathbb {e} \left[ \| \boldsymbol {z} _ {\cdot , i} \| _ {2} ^ {2} \right] = \frac {1 - p}{p} \sum_ {j} \left(a _ {j, i} ^ {\star}\right) ^ {2} \leq \frac {1}{p} \| \boldsymbol {a} ^ {\star} \| _ {\infty , 2} ^ {2}. \\ \end{array}
$$

the matrix bernstein inequality (see corollary 3.3) then yields

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \mathbf {z} _ {., i} \right\| _ {2} \leq 4 \sqrt {v _ {z} \log n} + 6 b _ {z} \log n \\ \leq \left(4 \sqrt {\frac {\log n}{p}} \| \boldsymbol {a} ^ {\star} \| _ {\infty , 2} + \frac {6 \log n}{p} \| \boldsymbol {a} ^ {\star} \| _ {\infty}\right) =: \beta_ {z} \tag {3.126} \\ \end{array}
$$

with probability at least  $1 - 2n^{-7}$ , which combined with (3.125) gives

$$
\mathbb {p} \left\{\left\| q _ {i} \right\| _ {2} \geq 2 \beta_ {z} ^ {2} \right\} \leq 2 n ^ {- 7}. \tag {3.127}
$$

recalling that  $\mathbb{e}[\pmb{q}_i] = \mathbf{0}$ , one can derive

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \mathbb {e} \left[ q _ {i} \mathbb {1} \left\{\left\| q _ {i} \right\| _ {2} \leq 2 \beta_ {z} ^ {2} \right\} \right] \right\| = \left\| \mathbb {e} \left[ q _ {i} \right] - \mathbb {e} \left[ q _ {i} \mathbb {1} \left\{\left\| q _ {i} \right\| _ {2} > 2 \beta_ {z} ^ {2} \right\} \right] \right\| \\ = \left\| \mathbb {e} \left[ \boldsymbol {q} _ {i} \mathbb {1} \left\{\left\| \boldsymbol {q} _ {i} \right\| _ {2} > 2 \beta_ {z} ^ {2} \right\} \right] \right\| \stackrel {\text {(i)}} {\leq} \mathbb {p} \left\{\left\| \boldsymbol {q} _ {i} \right\| _ {2} > 2 \beta_ {z} ^ {2} \right\} \cdot \frac {2}{p ^ {2}} \| \boldsymbol {a} _ {, i} ^ {\star} \| _ {2} ^ {2} \\ \stackrel {\mathrm {(i i)}} {\leq} \frac {4}{n ^ {7} p ^ {2}} \| \boldsymbol {a} ^ {\star} \| _ {\infty , 2} ^ {2}. \\ \end{array}
$$

here, (i) relies on (3.125) and the fact  $\| \mathbf{z}_{\cdot ,i}\| _2\leq p^{-1}\| \mathbf{a}_{\cdot ,i}^{\star}\| _2$  (by construction), whereas (ii) results from the calculation in (3.126).

proof of lemma 3.25. define the normalized tensor factors as

$$
\overline {{\boldsymbol {w}}} _ {i} ^ {\star} := \boldsymbol {w} _ {i} ^ {\star} / \| \boldsymbol {w} _ {i} ^ {\star} \| _ {2} \qquad (1 \leq i \leq r),
$$

and it is convenient to introduce the following auxiliary matrices that contain information about them:

$$
\overline {{{\boldsymbol {w}}}} ^ {\star} := [ \overline {{{\boldsymbol {w}}}} _ {1} ^ {\star}, \dots , \overline {{{\boldsymbol {w}}}} _ {r} ^ {\star} ], \qquad \overline {{{\boldsymbol {w}}}} _ {\mathrm {l i f t}} ^ {\star} := [ \overline {{{\boldsymbol {w}}}} _ {1} ^ {\star} \otimes \overline {{{\boldsymbol {w}}}} _ {1} ^ {\star}, \dots , \overline {{{\boldsymbol {w}}}} _ {r} ^ {\star} \otimes \overline {{{\boldsymbol {w}}}} _ {r} ^ {\star} ].
$$

additionally, we introduce a diagonal matrix  $d^{\star} \in \mathbb{r}^{r \times r}$  whose diagonal entries are given by

$$
\left[ \pmb {d} ^ {\star} \right] _ {i, i} = \left\| \pmb {w} _ {i} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {2} ^ {3} = v _ {i}, \qquad 1 \leq i \leq r.
$$

the matrices introduced above allow one to express  $a^{\star} = \overline{w}^{\star}d^{\star}\overline{w}_{\mathrm{lift}}^{\star \top}$  and  $a^{\star}a^{\star \top}$  as follows

$$
\boldsymbol {a} ^ {\star} \boldsymbol {a} ^ {\star^ {\top}} = \overline {{\boldsymbol {w}}} ^ {\star} \boldsymbol {d} ^ {\star} \overline {{\boldsymbol {w}}} _ {\text {l i f t}} ^ {\star^ {\top}} \overline {{\boldsymbol {w}}} _ {\text {l i f t}} ^ {\star} \boldsymbol {d} ^ {\star} \overline {{\boldsymbol {w}}} ^ {\star^ {\top}}. \tag {3.128}
$$

clearly, the rank of  $a^{\star}a^{\star \top}$  is bounded above by  $r$ , and hence it suffices to lower bound  $\lambda_{i}(a^{\star}a^{\star \top})$  when  $i \leq r$ .

in order to characterize the spectrum of  $a^{\star}a^{\star \top}$ , we first look at the eigenvalues of  $\overline{w}^{\star \top} \overline{w}^{\star}$  and  $\overline{w}_{\mathrm{lift}}^{\star \top} \overline{w}_{\mathrm{lift}}^{\star}$ . write

$$
\overline {{\boldsymbol {w}}} ^ {\star \top} \overline {{\boldsymbol {w}}} ^ {\star} = \boldsymbol {i} _ {r} + \boldsymbol {r}, \quad \text {a n d} \quad \overline {{\boldsymbol {w}}} _ {\text {l i f t}} ^ {\star \top} \overline {{\boldsymbol {w}}} _ {\text {l i f t}} ^ {\star} = \boldsymbol {i} _ {r} + \boldsymbol {r} _ {\text {l i f t}} \tag {3.129}
$$

for some residual matrices  $\mathbf{r},\mathbf{r}_{\mathrm{lift}}\in \mathbb{r}^{r\times r}$  (which are off-diagonal matrices). by virtue of the definition (3.112), we immediately obtain

$$
\| \boldsymbol {r} \| _ {\infty} \leq \sqrt {\mu / n}, \qquad \mathrm {a n d} \qquad \| \boldsymbol {r} _ {\mathrm {l i f t}} \| _ {\infty} \leq \mu / n,
$$

thus indicating that

$$
\left\| \boldsymbol {r} \right\| \leq r \left\| \boldsymbol {r} \right\| _ {\infty} \leq r \sqrt {\mu / n}, \quad \left\| \boldsymbol {r} _ {\text {l i f t}} \right\| \leq r \left\| \boldsymbol {r} _ {\text {l i f t}} \right\| _ {\infty} \leq \mu r / n. \tag {3.130}
$$

putting these together with (3.129) and invoking weyl's inequality give

$$
\max  _ {i} \left| \lambda_ {i} \left(\overline {{\boldsymbol {w}}} ^ {\star \top} \overline {{\boldsymbol {w}}} ^ {\star}\right) - 1 \right| \leq \| \boldsymbol {r} \| \leq r \sqrt {\mu / n}, \tag {3.131}
$$

which together with the assumption  $\mu r^2\leq n$  further reveals that

$$
\left\| \overline {{\boldsymbol {w}}} ^ {\star} \right\| = \sqrt {\lambda_ {1} \left(\overline {{\boldsymbol {w}}} ^ {\star} ^ {\top} \overline {{\boldsymbol {w}}} ^ {\star}\right)} \leq \sqrt {1 + r \sqrt {\mu / n}} \leq 2. \tag {3.132}
$$

we now return to study  $a^{\star}a^{\star \top}$ . in view of (3.128) and (3.129), one can decompose  $a^{\star}a^{\star \top}$  into the following two terms

$$
a ^ {\star} a ^ {\star^ {\top}} = \underbrace {\overline {{w}} ^ {\star} \left(d ^ {\star}\right) ^ {2} \overline {{w}} ^ {\star^ {\top}}} _ {=: g _ {1}} + \underbrace {\overline {{w}} ^ {\star} d ^ {\star} r _ {\text {l i f t}} d ^ {\star} \overline {{w}} ^ {\star^ {\top}}} _ {=: g _ {2}}. \tag {3.133}
$$

making use of the bounds (3.130) and (3.132) immediately leads to

$$
\left\| \boldsymbol {g} _ {2} \right\| \leq \left\| \overline {{\boldsymbol {w}}} ^ {\star} \right\| ^ {2} \left\| \boldsymbol {d} ^ {\star} \right\| ^ {2} \left\| \boldsymbol {r} _ {\text {l i f t}} \right\| \leq 4 \mu r \nu_ {\max } ^ {2} / n.
$$

# 3.9. tensor completion

regarding  $g_{1}$ , it can be directly seen that the non-zero eigenvalues of  $g_{1}$  coincide with those of  $d^{\star}\overline{w}^{\star \top}\overline{w}^{\star}d^{\star}$ , where the latter can be decomposed into

$$
d ^ {\star} \overline {{w}} ^ {\star^ {\top}} \overline {{w}} ^ {\star} d ^ {\star} = (d ^ {\star}) ^ {2} + d ^ {\star} r d ^ {\star}.
$$

as a result, for any  $1 \leq i \leq r$  one can derive

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left| \lambda_ {i} \left(\boldsymbol {g} _ {1}\right) - \lambda_ {i} \left(\left(\boldsymbol {d} ^ {\star}\right) ^ {2}\right) \right| = \left| \lambda_ {i} \left(\boldsymbol {d} ^ {\star} \overline {{\boldsymbol {w}}} ^ {\star^ {\top}} \overline {{\boldsymbol {w}}} ^ {\star} \boldsymbol {d} ^ {\star}\right) - \lambda_ {i} \left(\left(\boldsymbol {d} ^ {\star}\right) ^ {2}\right) \right| \\ \leq \left\| d ^ {\star} r d ^ {\star} \right\| \leq \left\| d ^ {\star} \right\| ^ {2} \left\| r \right\| \leq r \sqrt {\frac {\mu}{n}} \nu_ {\max } ^ {2}. \\ \end{array}
$$

this taken together with the decomposition (3.133) leads to

$$
\left| \lambda_ {i} \left(\boldsymbol {a} ^ {\star} \boldsymbol {a} ^ {\star^ {\top}}\right) - \lambda_ {i} \left(\boldsymbol {g} _ {1}\right) \right| \leq \| \boldsymbol {g} _ {2} \| \leq \frac {4 \mu r \nu_ {\max } ^ {2}}{n},
$$

thus indicating that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left| \lambda_ {i} \left(\boldsymbol {a} ^ {\star} \boldsymbol {a} ^ {\star \top}\right) - \lambda_ {i} \left(\left(\boldsymbol {d} ^ {\star}\right) ^ {2}\right) \right| \\ \leq \left| \lambda_ {i} \left(\boldsymbol {g} _ {1}\right) - \lambda_ {i} \left(\left(\boldsymbol {d} ^ {\star}\right) ^ {2}\right) \right| + \left| \lambda_ {i} \left(\boldsymbol {a} ^ {\star} \boldsymbol {a} ^ {\star \top}\right) - \lambda_ {i} \left(\boldsymbol {g} _ {1}\right) \right| \\ \leq r \sqrt {\frac {\mu}{n}} \nu_ {\max } ^ {2} + \frac {4 \mu r \nu_ {\max } ^ {2}}{n} \leq 8 \max  \left\{\sqrt {\frac {\mu}{n}}, \frac {\mu}{n} \right\} r \nu_ {\max } ^ {2}. \\ \end{array}
$$

if  $16\max \left\{\frac{\mu}{n},\sqrt{\frac{\mu}{n}}\right\} r\nu_{\max}^{2}\leq \nu_{\min}^{2}$ , then one has  $|\lambda_i(\pmb {a}^\star \pmb{a}^{\star \top}) - \lambda_i((\pmb {d}^\star)^2)|\leq \nu_{\min}^2 /2$ . in addition, letting  $\nu_{(i)}$  be the  $i$ -th largest element in  $\{\nu_i\}_{1\leq i\leq r}$ , we have  $\lambda_i((d^\star)^2) = \nu_{(i)}^2$  and hence arrive at

$$
\nu_ {\min } ^ {2} / 2 \leq \nu_ {(i)} ^ {2} - \nu_ {\min } ^ {2} / 2 \leq \lambda_ {i} \left(\boldsymbol {a} ^ {\star} \boldsymbol {a} ^ {\star \top}\right) \leq \nu_ {(i)} ^ {2} + \nu_ {\min } ^ {2} / 2 \leq 2 \nu_ {\max } ^ {2}
$$

for any  $1 \leq i \leq r$ , as claimed.

proof of lemma 3.27. define the following two matrices containing information about the tensor factors:

$$
\boldsymbol {w} ^ {\star} := \left[ \boldsymbol {w} _ {1} ^ {\star}, \dots , \boldsymbol {w} _ {r} ^ {\star} \right] \in \mathbb {r} ^ {n \times r}, \tag {3.134a}
$$

$$
\boldsymbol {w} _ {\text {l i f t}} ^ {\star} := \left[ \boldsymbol {w} _ {1} ^ {\star} \otimes \boldsymbol {w} _ {1} ^ {\star}, \dots , \boldsymbol {w} _ {r} ^ {\star} \otimes \boldsymbol {w} _ {r} ^ {\star} \right] \in \mathbb {r} ^ {n ^ {2} \times r}. \tag {3.134b}
$$

given that  $\pmb{w}^{\star \top}\pmb{w}^{\star} = [\langle \pmb{w}_i^{\star},\pmb{w}_j^{\star}\rangle ]_{1\leq i,j\leq r}$ , its diagonal part satisfies

$$
\left\| \mathcal {p} _ {\mathrm {d i a g}} \left(\boldsymbol {w} ^ {\star^ {\top}} \boldsymbol {w} ^ {\star}\right) \right\| = \left\| \operatorname {d i a g} \left(\left[ \| \boldsymbol {w} _ {i} ^ {\star} \| _ {2} ^ {2} \right] _ {1 \leq i \leq r}\right) \right\| \leq \nu_ {\max } ^ {2 / 3}.
$$

in addition, the off-diagonal part of  $\pmb{w}^{\star \top}\pmb{w}^{\star}$  satisfies

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \mathcal {p} _ {\text {o f f - d i a g}} \left(\boldsymbol {w} ^ {\star \top} \boldsymbol {w} ^ {\star}\right) \right\| \leq \sqrt {\sum_ {i \neq j} \left| \langle \boldsymbol {w} _ {i} ^ {\star} , \boldsymbol {w} _ {j} ^ {\star} \rangle \right| ^ {2}} \leq \sqrt {\frac {\mu}{n} \sum_ {i \neq j} \| \boldsymbol {w} _ {i} ^ {\star} \| _ {2} ^ {2} \| \boldsymbol {w} _ {j} ^ {\star} \| _ {2} ^ {2}} \\ \leq \sqrt {\frac {\mu r ^ {2}}{n}} \max _ {i} \left\| \pmb {w} _ {i} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} = \nu_ {\mathrm {m a x}} ^ {2 / 3} \sqrt {\frac {\mu r ^ {2}}{n}}, \\ \end{array}
$$

where the second inequality relies on the definition (3.112) of the incoherence parameter, and the last relation follows from the definition of  $\nu_{\mathrm{max}}$  in (3.111). consequently, if  $\mu r^2 \leq n$ , then

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \boldsymbol {w} ^ {\star} \right\| ^ {2} = \left\| \boldsymbol {w} ^ {\star^ {\top}} \boldsymbol {w} ^ {\star} \right\| \leq \left\| \mathcal {p} _ {\text {d i a g}} \left(\boldsymbol {w} ^ {\star^ {\top}} \boldsymbol {w} ^ {\star}\right) \right\| + \left\| \mathcal {p} _ {\text {o f f - d i a g}} \left(\boldsymbol {w} ^ {\star^ {\top}} \boldsymbol {w} ^ {\star}\right) \right\| \\ \leq \nu_ {\max } ^ {2 / 3} + \nu_ {\max } ^ {2 / 3} \sqrt {\frac {\mu r ^ {2}}{n}} \leq 2 \nu_ {\max } ^ {2 / 3}. \tag {3.135} \\ \end{array}
$$

repeating similar arguments also reveals that

$$
\left\| \boldsymbol {w} _ {\text {l i f t}} ^ {\star} \right\| ^ {2} \leq 2 \nu_ {\max } ^ {4 / 3}. \tag {3.136}
$$

next, it is readily seen from the definition (3.112) that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \pmb {w} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {2, \infty} \leq \sqrt {r} \max _ {i} \| \pmb {w} _ {i} ^ {\star} \| _ {\infty} \leq \sqrt {\frac {\mu r}{n}} \max _ {i} \| \pmb {w} _ {i} ^ {\star} \| _ {2} = \sqrt {\frac {\mu r}{n}} \nu_ {\max} ^ {1 / 3}, \\ \left\| \pmb {w} _ {\mathrm {l i f t}} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {2, \infty} \leq \sqrt {r} \max _ {i} \| \pmb {w} _ {i} ^ {\star} \| _ {\infty} ^ {2} \leq \frac {\mu \sqrt {r}}{n} \max _ {i} \| \pmb {w} _ {i} ^ {\star} \| _ {2} ^ {2} = \frac {\mu \sqrt {r}}{n} \nu_ {\mathrm {m a x}} ^ {2 / 3}. \\ \end{array}
$$

combining these bounds with (3.135) and (3.136) immediately yields

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \boldsymbol {a} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {\infty , 2} = \left\| \boldsymbol {w} ^ {\star} \left(\boldsymbol {w} _ {\text {l i f t}} ^ {\star}\right) ^ {\top} \right\| _ {\infty , 2} \leq \| \boldsymbol {w} ^ {\star} \| \| \boldsymbol {w} _ {\text {l i f t}} ^ {\star} \| _ {2, \infty} \leq \frac {\mu \sqrt {2 r}}{n} \nu_ {\max }, \\ \left\| \boldsymbol {a} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {\infty} = \left\| \boldsymbol {w} ^ {\star} \left(\boldsymbol {w} _ {\text {l i f t}} ^ {\star}\right) ^ {\top} \right\| _ {\infty} \leq \| \boldsymbol {w} ^ {\star} \| _ {2, \infty} \| \boldsymbol {w} _ {\text {l i f t}} ^ {\star} \| _ {2, \infty} \leq \frac {\mu^ {3 / 2} r}{n ^ {3 / 2}} \nu_ {\max }, \\ \end{array}
$$

$$
\left\| \boldsymbol {a} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {2, \infty} = \left\| \boldsymbol {w} ^ {\star} \big (\boldsymbol {w} _ {\mathrm {l i f t}} ^ {\star} \big) ^ {\top} \right\| _ {2, \infty} \leq \| \boldsymbol {w} ^ {\star} \| _ {2, \infty} \| \boldsymbol {w} _ {\mathrm {l i f t}} ^ {\star} \| \leq \sqrt {\frac {2 \mu r}{n}} \nu_ {\max}.
$$

# 3.10 notes

this section provides further pointers to the applications studied in this chapter, and singles out a brief list of applications we have omitted.

before proceeding, it is worth pointing out several important facts. first, for many applications (e.g., phase retrieval, matrix and tensor completion), the spectral method alone does not allow for perfect reconstruction of the unknowns even when it is information-theoretically feasible to do so. instead, the spectral method frequently serves as a suitable initialization step for these applications, and its estimate can often be further refined by means of nonconvex optimization algorithms like gradient descent and alternating minimization; see chi et al. (2019) and jain and kar (2017) for overviews of recent advances. second, throughout this chapter, we have assumed that the underlying matrix is exactly low-rank, and in addition the spectral methods deployed know the correct rank. however, in reality, data matrices are rarely exactly low-rank. it is therefore of great importance to develop and analyze methods that can handle such misspecified cases. when the reconstruction error of the matrix is considered, several methods are capable of achieving graceful tradeoff between the estimation error and the approximation error, without knowing the correct rank, e.g. e.g., koltchinskii et al. (2011), chatterjee (2014), and negahban and wainwright (2011). in addition, further discussions (e.g., convex relaxation approaches and nonconvex landscape analysis) about several of these applications can be found in candès (2014), wainwright (2019), wright and ma (2020), and zhang et al. (2020d).

pca, factor models and covariance estimation. pca and factor models are among the most classic and extensively studied topics in statistics (anderson, 1962; fan et al., 2020b; wainwright, 2019). the model considered in section 3.3.1 has been studied by, for example, johnstone (2001), paul (2007), nadler (2008), perry et al. (2018), xie et al. (2020), wang and fan (2017), fan et al. (2018c), and bao et al. (2020) under the name of spiked covariance models, covering both the finite-sample regime and high-dimensional asymptotics. a more recent strand of work extended the theory to accommodate heteroskedastic noise and missing data (including heterogeneous missing patterns) (lounici, 2014; zhang et al., 2021; cai et al., 2021a; zhu et al., 2019), as well as exponential family distributions (liu et al., 2018). in addition to providing the distance control between the spectral estimate and the true principle subspace, koltchinskii and lounici (2016) and fan et al. (2019b) also studied the bias of the spectral estimate under various types of data distributions. it is clearly impossible to review the enormous literature in a monograph of this length; the interested reader is referred to the overview papers johnstone and paul (2018), fan et al. (2018a), vaswani et al. (2018), and balzano et al. (2018) and the recent books fan et al. (2020b) and wainwright (2019) for overviews of contemporary developments on this topic (with particular emphasis on high-dimensional data). in addition, this monograph does not account for the sparsity structure, or a superposition of low-rank and sparsity structure, where are commonly imposed on either the covariance matrix or the precision matrix (johnstone and lu, 2009; ma, 2013; vu and lei, 2012; cai et al., 2013; candès et al., 2011; chandrasekaran et al., 2011; chandrasekaran et al., 2010). these additional structural assumptions play a crucial role in further dimension reduction and are useful for, say, learning graphical models, video surveillance in computer vision, and portfolio allocation and risk managements in finance; see fan et al. (2020b), vidal et al. (2016), wainwright (2019), and wright and ma (2020) for more detailed discussions.

applications of pca in statistical and econometric modeling. pca has been widely applied to estimate dimension-reduced spaces in multiple-index models (li, 1992; duan and li, 1991; cook, 2007; xia et al., 2002; li, 2018), and latent factors in econometric modeling (forni et al., 2000; stock and watson, 2002; bai and ng, 2002; bai, 2003; bai, 2009; ahn and horenstein, 2013; fan et al., 2015; fan et al., 2016). for recent reviews of this topic, we refer the readers to stock and watson (2016) for dynamic factor models with applications to macroeconomics, to bai and wang (2016) for time series and panel data models, to fan et al. (2021b) for robust factor models and large covariance estimation, and to fan et al.
