as a natural starting point to build confidence intervals, we seek to develop comprehensive understanding about the distribution of the spectral estimator. in general, obtaining a non-asymptotic

yet tractable distributional characterization of a nonconvex estimator like the spectral method could be remarkably challenging. fortunately, the  $\ell_{\infty}$  and  $\ell_{2,\infty}$  perturbation theory introduced previously (e.g., theorem 4.2) allows one to make progress for some important scenarios.

let us revisit the setting in section 4.2.1, and consider the following estimator of the unknown low-rank matrix  $m^{\star}$ :

$$
\widehat {\boldsymbol {m}} = \left[ \widehat {m} _ {i, j} \right] _ {1 \leq i, j \leq n} = \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {\lambda} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\top}, \tag {4.63}
$$

obtained via the spectral method. the aim is to develop tractable distributional guarantees for each entry of  $\widehat{m} - m^{\star}$ .

towards this end, we first examine whether our previous results shed light on certain distributional properties of  $\widehat{m} - m^{\star}$ . informally, theorem 4.2 (in particular, (4.31b)) reveals that

$$
\boldsymbol {u} \operatorname {s g n} (\boldsymbol {h}) \approx m \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \left(\boldsymbol {\lambda} ^ {\star}\right) ^ {- 1} = \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} + \boldsymbol {e} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \left(\boldsymbol {\lambda} ^ {\star}\right) ^ {- 1}. \tag {4.64}
$$

assuming tightness of this first-order approximation, one further derives

$$
\begin{array}{l} \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {\lambda} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\top} - \boldsymbol {m} ^ {\star} \stackrel {\text {(i)}} {\approx} \boldsymbol {u} \operatorname {s g n} (\boldsymbol {h}) \boldsymbol {\lambda} ^ {\star} \left(\boldsymbol {u} \operatorname {s g n} (\boldsymbol {h})\right) ^ {\top} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \boldsymbol {\lambda} ^ {\star} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star^ {\top}} \\ \stackrel {\text {(i i)}} {\approx} \left(u \operatorname {s g n} (h) - u ^ {\star}\right) \lambda^ {\star} u ^ {\star \top} + u ^ {\star} \lambda^ {\star} \left(u \operatorname {s g n} (h) - u ^ {\star}\right) ^ {\top} \\ \stackrel {\text {(i i i)}} {\approx} \boldsymbol {e} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \left(\boldsymbol {\lambda} ^ {\star}\right) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {\lambda} ^ {\star} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star \top} + \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \boldsymbol {\lambda} ^ {\star} \left(\boldsymbol {e} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \left(\boldsymbol {\lambda} ^ {\star}\right) ^ {- 1}\right) ^ {\top} \\ = e u ^ {\star} u ^ {\star \top} + u ^ {\star} u ^ {\star \top} e, \tag {4.65} \\ \end{array}
$$

where (i) holds as long as  $\mathsf{sgn}(\pmb {h})\pmb{\lambda}^{\star}\mathsf{sgn}(\pmb {h})^{\top}\approx \pmb{\lambda}$  (which has already been illuminated in the analysis of corollary 4.3 and will be solidified momentarily), (ii) is obtained by dropping the higher-order term  $(u\mathsf{sgn}(\pmb {h}) - u^{\star})\pmb{\lambda}^{\star}\bigl (\pmb {u}\mathsf{sgn}(\pmb {h}) - \pmb{u}^{\star}\bigr)^{\top}$ , and (iii) relies upon the approximation (4.64).

given that (4.65) is a linear map of the noise matrix  $\pmb{e}$ , this essentially forms a first-order approximation of  $\widehat{\pmb{m}}$ , which in turn enables a tractable distributional theory for  $\widehat{\pmb{m}}$ . observe that each entry of the matrix in (4.65) is a weighted superposition of the independent zero-mean entries of  $\pmb{e}$ . equipped with this observation, some variant of the central limit theorem suggests that each entry of  $\widehat{\pmb{m}} - \pmb{m}^{\star}$  is approximately zero-mean gaussian, as formalized by the theorem below. for notational convenience, we shall define a projection matrix

$$
\boldsymbol {p} ^ {\star} = \left[ p _ {i, j} ^ {\star} \right] _ {1 \leq i, j \leq n} := \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star \top}, \tag {4.66}
$$

and impose a lower bound requirement on the noise variance:

$$
\sigma_ {\min } ^ {2} \leq \sigma_ {i, j} ^ {2} \leq \sigma^ {2}, \quad 1 \leq i, j \leq n. \tag {4.67}
$$

theorem 4.10. suppose that the assumptions of theorem 4.2 hold. for any  $1 \leq i, j \leq n$ , set

$$
v _ {i, j} ^ {\star} = \left\{ \begin{array}{l l} \sum_ {l = 1} ^ {n} \sigma_ {i, l} ^ {2} p _ {l, j} ^ {\star 2} + \sum_ {l = 1} ^ {n} p _ {i, l} ^ {\star 2} \sigma_ {l, j} ^ {2} + 2 \sigma_ {i, j} ^ {2} p _ {i, i} ^ {\star} p _ {j, j} ^ {\star}, & \text {i f} i \neq j, \\ 4 \sum_ {l = 1} ^ {n} \sigma_ {i, l} ^ {2} p _ {l, i} ^ {\star 2}, & \text {i f} i = j. \end{array} \right. \tag {4.68}
$$

assume that  $\sigma / \sigma_{\min} = o(1)$ , and that

$$
\frac {\left\| \boldsymbol {u} _ {j , \cdot} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} + \left\| \boldsymbol {u} _ {i , \cdot} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2}}{\left\| \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {\mathrm {f}} ^ {2}} \gtrsim \frac {b ^ {2} \kappa^ {2} \mu^ {2} r ^ {2} \log^ {2} n}{\sigma^ {2} n ^ {2}} + \frac {\sigma^ {2} \mu^ {2} r \kappa^ {4} \log^ {3} n}{\left(\lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}\right) ^ {2}}. \tag {4.69}
$$

then the estimator (4.63) obeys

$$
\sup _ {z \in \mathbb {r}} \left| \mathbb {p} \left(\widehat {m} _ {i, j} - m _ {i, j} ^ {\star} \leq z \sqrt {v _ {i , j} ^ {\star}}\right) - \phi (z) \right| = o (1),
$$

where  $\phi (\cdot)$  represents the cumulative density function (cdf) of the standard gaussian distribution.

the proof of this theorem is postponed to section 4.11. in a nutshell, theorem 4.10 tells us that  $\widehat{\pmb{m}}$  is a nearly unbiased estimator of the truth  $m^{\star}$ , as long as the signal strength—as captured by  $\|u_{i,j}^{\star}\|_2$  and  $\|u_{j,j}^{\star}\|_2$  when estimating the  $(i,j)$ -th entry—is sufficiently large (cf. (4.69)). the resulting estimation error in each entry is well approximated by a zero-mean gaussian random variable, whose variance can be determined in a tractable fashion. as can be easily verified, the variance  $v_{i,j}^{\star}$  is precisely the variance of the  $(i,j)$ -th entry of  $\pmb{e}\pmb{u}^{\star}\pmb{u}^{\star\top} + \pmb{u}^{\star}\pmb{u}^{\star\top}\pmb{e}$  (as singled out in (4.65)). the above distributional theory is non-asymptotic, which lends itself well to high-dimensional applications.

# 4.6.2 inference and uncertainty quantification

the gaussian approximation unveiled in theorem 4.10, which is dictated by a single parameter  $v_{i,j}^{\star}$ , paves the way for statistical inference and uncertainty quantification tailored to this model. in order to construct a valid confidence interval for each entry of  $m^{\star}$ , everything boils down to identifying an estimator that approximates the variance parameter  $v_{i,j}^{\star}$ , ideally in a data-driven yet faithful manner.

in view of the variance characterization (4.68), computing  $v_{i,j}^{\star}$  requires information about both the noise variances  $\{\sigma_{i,j}^{2}\}_{1\leq i,j\leq n}$  and the projection matrix  $p^{\star}$  (cf. (4.66)). however, estimating the noise variances is in general statistically infeasible, given that we only have access to a single observation (i.e.,  $m_{i,j}$ ) related to each individual variance  $\sigma_{i,j}^{2}$ . fortunately, the variance  $v_{i,j}^{\star}$  involves only the summation or equivalently the average of these individual variances, whose stochastic errors will be averaged out. this leads us to the following surrogate

$$
\widetilde {v} _ {i, j} = \left\{ \begin{array}{l l} \sum_ {l = 1} ^ {n} e _ {i, l} ^ {2} p _ {l, j} ^ {\star 2} + \sum_ {l = 1} ^ {n} p _ {i, l} ^ {\star 2} e _ {l, j} ^ {2} + 2 e _ {i, j} ^ {2} p _ {i, i} ^ {\star i} p _ {j, j} ^ {\star}, & \text {i f} i \neq j, \\ 4 \sum_ {l = 1} ^ {n} e _ {i, l} ^ {2} p _ {l, i} ^ {\star 2}, & \text {i f} i = j, \end{array} \right. \tag {4.70}
$$

which is clearly an unbiased estimator of  $v_{i,j}^{\star}$ . given the statistical independence of  $\{e_{i,j}\}_{i\geq j}$ , we can expect to have  $\widetilde{v}_{i,j}\approx v_{i,j}^{\star}$ , owing to the concentration of measure.

however, the above surrogate  $\widetilde{v}_{i,j}$  remains practically incomputable, due to the absence of knowledge about both  $\pmb{e}$  and  $p^{\star}$ . to address this issue, we propose the following plug-in estimator:

$$
\widehat {v} _ {i, j} = \left\{ \begin{array}{l l} \sum_ {l = 1} ^ {n} \widehat {e} _ {i, l} ^ {2} \widehat {p} _ {l, j} ^ {2} + \sum_ {l = 1} ^ {n} \widehat {p} _ {i, l} ^ {2} \widehat {e} _ {l, j} ^ {2} + 2 \widehat {e} _ {i, j} ^ {2} \widehat {p} _ {i, i} \widehat {p} _ {j, j}, & \text {i f} i \neq j, \\ 4 \sum_ {l = 1} ^ {n} \widehat {e} _ {i, l} ^ {2} \widehat {p} _ {l, i} ^ {2}, & \text {i f} i = j, \end{array} \right. \tag {4.71}
$$

where  $\hat{\pmb{e}} = [\hat{e}_{i,j}]_{1\leq i,j\leq n}$  and  $\hat{\pmb{p}} = [\hat{p}_{i,j}]_{1\leq i,j\leq n}$  stand for estimators of  $\pmb{e}$  and  $p^{\star}$ , respectively. in particular, we employ the following specific estimators of  $\pmb{e}$  and  $p^{\star}$ , again adopting the plug-in strategy:

$$
\widehat {\boldsymbol {e}} := \boldsymbol {m} - \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {\lambda} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\top}, \tag {4.72a}
$$

$$
\widehat {\boldsymbol {p}} := \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\top}, \tag {4.72b}
$$

where  $\pmb{u}$  and  $\pmb{\lambda}$  are, as usual, computed via eigendecomposition of  $\pmb{m}$ . for a prescribed coverage level  $1 - \alpha$  (with  $0 < \alpha < 1$ ), we construct the following confidence interval for the  $(i,j)$ -th entry of  $\pmb{m}^{\star}$ , motivated by the gaussian approximation in theorem 4.10:

$$
\mathrm {c i} _ {i, j} ^ {1 - \alpha} := \left[ \widehat {m} _ {i, j} \pm \phi^ {- 1} (1 - \alpha / 2) \sqrt {\widehat {v} _ {i , j}} \right]. \tag {4.73}
$$

here and throughout, for any  $b > 0$ , we let  $[a \pm b]$  abbreviate the interval  $[a - b, a + b]$ , and we use  $\phi^{-1}(\cdot)$  to represent the inverse cdf of the standard gaussian distribution.

as encouraging news, the above construction of entrywise confidence intervals is provably valid with high probability, as revealed by the following theorem. the proof is postponed to section 4.12.

theorem 4.11. consider the settings and assumptions in section 4.2.1, and suppose that  $\sigma / \sigma_{\min} = o(1)$ ,  $\kappa^4 \mu^2 r^2 \log n \leq n$  and  $\sigma \kappa \sqrt{n \log n} \lesssim |\lambda_r^\star|$ . consider any  $1 \leq i, j \leq n$ , and assume that

$$
\frac {\left\| \boldsymbol {u} _ {j , \cdot} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} + \left\| \boldsymbol {u} _ {i , \cdot} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2}}{\left\| \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {\mathrm {f}} ^ {2}} \gtrsim \frac {b \kappa^ {2} \mu^ {2} r ^ {2} \log^ {2} n}{\sigma n ^ {3 / 2}} + \frac {\sigma \mu^ {2} r \kappa^ {3} \log^ {3} n}{\left| \lambda_ {r} ^ {\star} \right| \sqrt {n}}. \tag {4.74}
$$

for any fixed coverage level  $1 - \alpha \in (0,1)$ , the confidence interval  $\mathsf{cl}_{i,j}^{1 - \alpha}$  constructed in (4.73) obeys

$$
\mathbb {p} \left(m _ {i, j} ^ {\star} \in \mathrm {c l} _ {i, j} ^ {1 - \alpha}\right) = 1 - \alpha + o (1). \tag {4.75}
$$

theorem 4.11 confirms that the confidence interval proposed above meets the prescribed coverage requirement, provided that the associated signal strength is not too low (see (4.74)). in addition to its statistical validity, the proposed procedure enjoys several features that make it practically appealing:

- adaptive to unknown noise levels and distributions. the above inference procedure is fully data-driven, which does not require prior knowledge about the noise levels or noise distributions. as alluded to previously, it is in general impossible to estimate the noise variance in each entry, and hence a data-driven yet valid approach is of critical value.  
- adaptive to heteroskedastic noise. our statistical guarantees hold without relying on homogeneity of noise components. in other words, this inference procedure automatically accommodates heteroskedastic noise, a scenario where the variance of the noise components might vary across different locations.

careful readers might remark that theorem 4.11 is concerned with statistical inference for a single entry. interestingly, the distributional theory presented in section 4.6.1 (see also lemma 4.18 in the proof of theorem 4.10) might also be instrumental in pursuing simultaneous inference, namely, the problem of constructing a confidence region that simultaneously accounts for more than one unknown entries. we omit such an extension for the sake of conciseness.

# 4.7 application: confidence intervals for matrix completion

as an illustration of the applicability of the inference procedure described in section 4.6.2, we develop concrete consequences of theorem 4.11 in application to noisy matrix completion—an extension of the formulation in section 3.8 to noisy settings.

model: noisy matrix completion. suppose that we are asked to reconstruct a symmetric rank- $r$  matrix  $m^{\star} = [m_{i,j}^{\star}]_{1\leq k,l\leq n}\in \mathbb{r}^{n\times n}$  with eigendecomposition  $m^{\star} = u^{\star}\lambda^{\star}u^{\star^{\top}}$ . we only get to acquire noisy observations of a subset of the entries of  $m^{\star}$ ; more precisely, there exists a sampling set  $\omega \subseteq [n]\times [n]$  such that we observe

$$
m _ {k, l} ^ {\star} + \eta_ {k, l} \quad \text {i f} (k, l) \in \omega . \tag {4.76}
$$

here,  $\{\eta_{k,l} \mid k \geq l\}$  denotes independent gaussian noise obeying

$$
\eta_ {k, l} = \eta_ {l, k} \stackrel {\text {i . i . d .}} {\sim} \mathcal {n} \left(0, \sigma_ {\eta} ^ {2}\right), \quad k \geq l. \tag {4.77}
$$

as before, we focus on the random sampling model such that each location  $(k,l)$  with  $k\geq l$  is included in the sampling set  $\omega$  independently with probability  $p$ . further, assume that  $m^{\star}$  has eigenvalues obeying (4.22), condition number  $\kappa$  (cf. (4.23)), and incoherence parameter  $\mu$  (cf. (4.24)). can we build a confidence interval for each entry  $m_{i,j}^{\star}$ , on the basis of the output of the spectral method?

computing entrywise confidence intervals. in order to apply the inference procedure in section 4.6.2, it suffices to determine the data matrix  $m = [m_{i,j}]_{1 \leq i,j \leq n}$ , which can be selected as usual. specifically, a possible inference procedure proceeds as follows:

- set  $m$  such that for any  $1 \leq i, j \leq n$ ,

$$
m _ {i, j} = \left\{ \begin{array}{l l} \frac {1}{p} \left(m _ {i, j} ^ {\star} + \eta_ {i, j}\right), & \text {i f} (i, j) \in \omega , \\ 0, & \text {e l s e}, \end{array} \right. \tag {4.78}
$$

which clearly obeys  $\mathbb{e}[m] = m^{\star}$

- compute the estimate  $\widehat{m}$  (cf. (4.63)) via the spectral method.  
- for a given coverage level  $1 - \alpha$  and a given pair  $(i,j)$ , construct the confidence interval  $\mathsf{cl}_{i,j}^{1 - \alpha}$  according to (4.73), with auxiliary parameters provided in (4.71) and (4.72).

performance guarantees and implications. when specialized to noisy matrix completion, our inference theory in theorem 4.11 leads to the following statistical guarantees.

theorem 4.12. consider the noisy matrix completion setting in this section. suppose that  $\kappa^4\mu^2 r^2\log n\leq n$ ,  $\frac{\max_{k,l}|m_{k,l}^{\star}|}{\min_{k,l}|m_{k,l}^{\star}|} = o(1)$

$$
n p \gtrsim \kappa^ {4} r \log n \quad \text {a n d} \quad \sigma_ {\eta} \kappa \sqrt {\frac {n \log n}{p}} \lesssim | \lambda_ {r} ^ {\star} |. \tag {4.79}
$$

consider any  $1 \leq i, j \leq n$ , and assume that

$$
\frac {\left\| \boldsymbol {u} _ {j , \cdot} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} + \left\| \boldsymbol {u} _ {i , \cdot} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2}}{\left\| \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {\mathrm {f}} ^ {2}} \gtrsim \frac {\mu^ {2} r ^ {2} \kappa^ {4} \log^ {3} n}{n \sqrt {n p}} + \frac {\sigma_ {\eta} \mu^ {2} r \kappa^ {3} \log^ {3} n}{\left| \lambda_ {r} ^ {\star} \right| \sqrt {n p}}. \tag {4.80}
$$

for any fixed coverage level  $1 - \alpha \in (0,1)$ , the confidence interval  $\mathsf{cl}_{i,j}^{1 - \alpha}$  constructed in (4.73) obeys

$$
\mathbb {p} \left(m _ {i, j} ^ {\star} \in \mathrm {c l} _ {i, j} ^ {1 - \alpha}\right) = 1 - \alpha + o (1). \tag {4.81}
$$

in order to help interpret the applicable range of theorem 4.12, let us focus on the simple scenario with  $\kappa, \mu, r \asymp 1$  to simplify discussion.

- first of all, condition (4.79) can be simplified as

$$
n p \gtrsim \log n \quad \text {a n d} \quad \sigma_ {\eta} \sqrt {\frac {n \log n}{p}} \lesssim | \lambda_ {r} ^ {\star} |.
$$

the first condition on the sampling size coincides with the fundamental requirement even if the goal is merely to enable reliable estimation (candès and tao, 2010), whereas the second condition on the signal-to-noise ratio is also necessary—up to some log factor—to ensure an estimation quality better than that of a random guess (cai et al., 2021a, theorem 3.3).

- next, we move on to interpret the other condition (4.80) imposed in our theory, which simplifies to

$$
\frac {\| \boldsymbol {u} _ {j , \cdot} ^ {\star} \| _ {2} ^ {2} + \| \boldsymbol {u} _ {i , \cdot} ^ {\star} \| _ {2} ^ {2}}{\| \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \| _ {\mathrm {f}} ^ {2}} \gtrsim \frac {\log^ {3} n}{n \sqrt {n p}} + \frac {\sigma_ {\eta} \log^ {3} n}{| \lambda_ {r} ^ {\star} | \sqrt {n p}}.
$$

let us consider the most challenging case where  $np \gtrsim \text{poly} \log n$  and  $\sigma_{\eta} \sqrt{\frac{n \text{poly} \log n}{p}} \lesssim |\lambda_r^\star|$  (for some sufficiently large poly-log factor). in such a case, the above condition only requires

$$
\frac {\left\| \boldsymbol {u} _ {j , \cdot} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} + \left\| \boldsymbol {u} _ {i , \cdot} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2}}{\left\| \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {\mathrm {f}} ^ {2}} \gtrsim \frac {1}{n \text {p o l y} \log n},
$$

indicating that the associated signal power  $\| u_{j,\cdot}^{\star}\| _2^2 +\| u_{i,\cdot}^{\star}\| _2^2$  is allowed to be much smaller than the average signal power across all rows (which can be captured by  $\| u^{\star}\|_{\mathrm{f}}^{2} / n$ ).

in a nutshell, the validity of our inference procedure is ensured for broad settings. additionally, we have conducted a series of numerical experiments to examine the entrywise distributions of  $m$ . as illustrated in figure 4.3, the normalized estimation error  $(\widehat{v}_{i,j})^{-1/2}(\widehat{m}_{i,j} - m_{i,j}^{\star})$  is close in distribution to a standard gaussian random variable, which corroborates our theory on the confidence interval construction.

before concluding, we would like to remark that: while the distributional theory for spectral methods allows for valid construction of confidence intervals for an unseen entry, it is oftentimes not among the most effective statistical inference procedures that one can put forward. there exist other alternatives that are provably more efficient, including but not limited to inference procedures based on convex relaxation and nonconvex optimization (chen et al., 2019c; xia and yuan, 2021) and the ones based on more refined spectral methods (yan et al., 2021; chernozhukov et al., 2021)

proof of theorem 4.12. given that  $\eta_{i,j}$  is a gaussian random variable and hence possibly unbounded, we find it convenient to introduce a truncated version as follows

$$
\widetilde {\eta} _ {i, j} = \eta_ {i, j} \mathbb {1} \left\{\left| \eta_ {i, j} \right| \leq 5 \sigma_ {\eta} \sqrt {\log n} \right\}, \quad 1 \leq i, j \leq n.
$$

and

$$
\widetilde {m} _ {i, j} = \left\{ \begin{array}{l l} \frac {1}{p} \big (m _ {i, j} ^ {\star} + \widetilde {\eta} _ {i, j} \big), & \text {i f} (i, j) \in \omega , \\ 0, & \text {e l s e .} \end{array} \right.
$$

![](images/fc114807f880646d00c85df432a4be4aaf291e6b049a4d693170b48cbff2b21c.jpg)  
(a)

![](images/efdd9d5cb55704491b5b63ee0a3a76021ba32bbeff8080307099030f519c8754.jpg)  
(b)  
figure 4.3: entrywise numerical distribution for noisy matrix completion. we generate  $m^{\star} = u^{\star}u^{\star \top}$  with  $u^{\star}$  being a random orthonormal matrix, and analyze the behavior of  $z_{1,2} = (\widehat{v}_{1,2})^{-0.5}(\widehat{m}_{1,2} - m_{1,2}^{\star})$ , where  $\widehat{m}$  is defined in (4.63) and  $\widehat{v}_{i,j}$  is defined in (4.71). the results are reported for 500 monte carlo trials when  $p = 0.3$ ,  $n = 1000$ ,  $\sigma_{\eta} = 10^{-4}$ , and  $r = 3$ . (a) histogram of the empirical distribution of  $z_{1,2}$ ; (b) q-q (quantile-quantile) plot of  $z_{1,2}$  vs. the standard normal distribution.

repeating the analysis in section 3.2.3, we can show that

$$
\mathbb {p} \big \{m = \widetilde {m} \big \} = \mathbb {p} \big \{\eta_ {i, j} = \widetilde {\eta} _ {i, j}, \forall i, j \in [ n ] \big \} \geq 1 - n ^ {- 1 0},
$$

meaning that  $m$  and  $\widetilde{m}$  are equivalent with high probability. as a result, we shall concentrate on validating the confidence interval computed based on  $\widetilde{m}$  in the subsequent analysis. before proceeding, we record several key properties about  $\widetilde{\eta}_{i,j}$  as follows:

$$
\mathbb {e} [ \widetilde {\eta} _ {i, j} ] = 0, \quad \mathbb {e} [ \widetilde {\eta} _ {i, j} ^ {2} ] = (1 - o (1)) \sigma_ {\eta} ^ {2}, \quad | \widetilde {\eta} _ {i, j} | \leq 5 \sigma_ {\eta} \sqrt {\log n}. \tag {4.82}
$$

the proof follows by invoking theorem 4.11, as long as the conditions required therein are satisfied. to begin with, the associated variance parameters are given by

$$
\begin{array}{l} \sigma_ {i, j} ^ {2} := \mathbb {e} \left[ \left(\widetilde {m} _ {i, j} - m _ {i, j} ^ {\star}\right) ^ {2} \right] \\ = p \mathbb {e} \left[ \left(\frac {1 - p}{p} m _ {i, j} ^ {\star} + \frac {1}{p} \widetilde {\eta} _ {i, j}\right) ^ {2} \right] + (1 - p) \big (m _ {i, j} ^ {\star} \big) ^ {2} \\ = \frac {(1 - p) ^ {2}}{p} \left(m _ {i , j} ^ {\star}\right) ^ {2} + \frac {1}{p} \mathbb {e} \left[ \widetilde {\eta} _ {i, j} ^ {2} \right] + (1 - p) \left(m _ {i, j} ^ {\star}\right) ^ {2} \\ = \frac {1 - p}{p} \big (m _ {i, j} ^ {\star} \big) ^ {2} + \frac {1 - o (1)}{p} \sigma_ {\eta} ^ {2}, \\ \end{array}
$$

thus leading to

$$
\begin{array}{l} \sigma_ {\mathrm {m i n}} ^ {2} := \min _ {i, j} \sigma_ {i, j} ^ {2} = \frac {1 - p}{p} \min _ {i, j} \left(m _ {i, j} ^ {\star}\right) ^ {2} + \frac {1 - o (1)}{p} \sigma_ {\eta} ^ {2}, \\ \sigma^ {2} := \max _ {i, j} \sigma_ {i, j} ^ {2} = \frac {1 - p}{p} \| \boldsymbol {m} ^ {\star} \| _ {\infty} ^ {2} + \frac {1 - o (1)}{p} \sigma_ {\eta} ^ {2}. \\ \end{array}
$$

apparently,  $\sigma^2 / \sigma_{\min}^2 = o(1)$  holds true under the assumptions of theorem 4.12. in addition, the random variables  $\{\widetilde{m}_{i,j} - m_{i,j}^{\star}\}$  are all bounded obeying

$$
\left| \widetilde {m} _ {i, j} - m _ {i, j} ^ {\star} \right| \leq \frac {1 - p}{p} \left| m _ {i, j} ^ {\star} \right| + \frac {1}{p} \left| \widetilde {\eta} _ {i, j} \right| \leq \frac {(1 - p) \| m ^ {\star} \| _ {\infty} + \sigma_ {\eta} \sqrt {5 \log n}}{p} =: b.
$$

these bounds readily imply that

$$
\frac {b}{\sigma} \asymp \frac {\frac {(1 - p) \| m ^ {\star} \| _ {\infty} + \sigma_ {\eta} \sqrt {5 \log n}}{p}}{\sqrt {\frac {1 - p}{p}} \| m ^ {\star} \| _ {\infty} + \frac {1}{\sqrt {p}} \sigma_ {\eta}} \lesssim \sqrt {\frac {\log n}{p}}. \tag {4.83}
$$

moving to the condition  $\sigma \kappa \sqrt{n\log n} \lesssim |\lambda_r^\star|$  in theorem 4.12, it can be guaranteed if

$$
\| m ^ {\star} \| _ {\infty} \kappa \sqrt {\frac {(1 - p) n \log n}{p}} \lesssim | \lambda_ {r} ^ {\star} | \quad \text {a n d} \quad \sigma_ {\eta} \kappa \sqrt {\frac {n \log n}{p}} \lesssim | \lambda_ {r} ^ {\star} |.
$$

given the assumption  $\frac{\max_{k,l}|m_{k,l}^{\star}|}{\min_{k,l}|m_{k,l}^{\star}|} = o(1)$ , one has

$$
\left\| m ^ {\star} \right\| _ {\infty} \asymp \frac {1}{n} \left\| m ^ {\star} \right\| _ {\mathrm {f}} \leq \frac {\sqrt {r}}{n} \left\| m ^ {\star} \right\| = \frac {\kappa \sqrt {r}}{n} \left| \lambda_ {r} ^ {\star} \right|. \tag {4.84}
$$

as a consequence, the condition  $\sigma \kappa \sqrt{n\log n} \lesssim |\lambda_r^\star|$  can be ensured under condition (4.79).

it remains to certify condition (4.74). by virtue of the above calculations of  $\sigma$  and  $b$  as well as the property (4.83), it is easily seen that condition (4.74) is valid as long as the following holds:

$$
\begin{array}{l} \frac {\left\| \boldsymbol {u} _ {j , \cdot} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} + \left\| \boldsymbol {u} _ {i , \cdot} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2}}{\left\| \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {\mathrm {f}} ^ {2}} \gtrsim \frac {\kappa^ {2} \mu^ {2} r ^ {2} \log^ {5 / 2} n}{n \sqrt {n p}} + \frac {\sqrt {1 - p} \left\| \boldsymbol {m} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {\infty} \mu^ {2} r \kappa^ {3} \log^ {3} n}{\left| \lambda_ {r} ^ {\star} \right| \sqrt {n p}} \\ + \frac {\sigma_ {\eta} \mu^ {2} r \kappa^ {3} \log^ {3} n}{| \lambda_ {r} ^ {\star} | \sqrt {n p}}. \\ \end{array}
$$

taking this together with the relation (4.84), we can demonstrate straightforwardly that condition (4.74) is guaranteed to hold as long as condition (4.80) is satisfied. this completes the proof.

# 4.8 appendix a: proof of theorem 4.2

to simplify notation, we assume throughout the proof that  $\lambda_r^\star > 0$ , namely,

$$
\left| \lambda_ {1} ^ {\star} \right| \geq \dots \geq \left| \lambda_ {r - 1} ^ {\star} \right| \geq \lambda_ {r} ^ {\star} > 0. \tag {4.85}
$$

the challenge of the proof arises due to the complicated statistical dependency between  $m$  and  $u$ , and the leave-one-out analysis paves a plausible path to decouple the dependency.

# 4.8.1 construction of leave-one-out auxiliary estimates

as elucidated in the rank-1 matrix denoising example in section 4.1, the key to enabling fine-grained analysis is to seek assistance from a collection of leave-one-out estimates. akin to section 4.1.3, for each  $1 \leq l \leq n$ , we construct two auxiliary matrices  $\pmb{m}^{(l)}$  and  $\pmb{e}^{(l)} = [e_{i,j}^{(l)}]_{1 \leq i,j \leq n}$  as follows:

$$
\boldsymbol {m} ^ {(l)} := \boldsymbol {m} ^ {\star} + \boldsymbol {e} ^ {(l)}, \quad e _ {i, j} ^ {(l)} := \left\{ \begin{array}{l l} e _ {i, j}, & \text {i f} i \neq l \text {a n d} j \neq l, \\ 0, & \text {e l s e ,} \end{array} \right. \tag {4.86}
$$

which are generated by simply discarding all random noise incurred in the  $l$ -th column/row of the data matrix. in addition, let  $\lambda_1^{(l)}, \dots, \lambda_n^{(l)}$  be the eigenvalues of  $m^{(l)}$  sorted by

$$
\left| \lambda_ {1} ^ {(l)} \right| \geq \left| \lambda_ {2} ^ {(l)} \right| \geq \dots \geq \left| \lambda_ {n} ^ {(l)} \right|, \tag {4.87}
$$

and denote by  $\pmb{u}_i^{(l)}$  the eigenvector of  $m^{(l)}$  associated with  $\lambda_i^{(l)}$ . the leave-one-out spectral estimates  $\pmb{u}^{(l)}$  and  $\pmb{\lambda}^{(l)}$  are, therefore, given by

$$
\boldsymbol {u} ^ {(l)} := \left[ \boldsymbol {u} _ {1} ^ {(l)}, \dots , \boldsymbol {u} _ {r} ^ {(l)} \right] \in \mathbb {r} ^ {n \times r}; \quad \boldsymbol {\lambda} ^ {(l)} := \operatorname {d i a g} \left(\left[ \lambda_ {1} ^ {(l)}, \dots , \lambda_ {r} ^ {(l)} \right]\right). \tag {4.88}
$$

we emphasize again that the main advantage of introducing the leave-one-out estimate  $\pmb{u}^{(l)}$  stems from its statistical independence from the  $l$ -th row of  $\pmb{m}$ , which substantially simplifies the analysis for the  $l$ -th row of the estimate. in principle, our analysis employs the leave-one-out estimates to help decouple delicate statistical dependency in a row-by-row fashion. another crucial aspect of the analysis lies in the exploitation of the proximity of all these auxiliary estimates, a feature that is enabled by the "stability" of the spectral method.

# 4.8.2 preliminary facts

before embarking on the  $\ell_{\infty}$  and  $\ell_{2,\infty}$  analyses, we gather a couple of useful facts, whose proofs are postponed to section 4.8.4. in what follows,  $\theta$  (resp.  $\theta^{(l)}$ ) denotes a diagonal matrix whose diagonal entries are the principal angles between  $u$  (resp.  $u^{(l)}$ ) and  $u^{\star}$ . in addition, we find it helpful to introduce the following matrices

$$
\boldsymbol {h} := \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \quad \text {a n d} \quad \boldsymbol {h} ^ {(l)} := \boldsymbol {u} ^ {(l) \top} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star}, \tag {4.89}
$$

which turn out to be close to being orthonormal.

the first set of results follows from the statistical nature of the perturbation matrix  $\pmb{e}$  (cf. assumption 4.1), which is immediately available from the matrix tail bounds.

lemma 4.13. consider the setting in section 4.2. there is some constant  $c_2 > 0$  such that with probability at least  $1 - o(n^{-7})$ ,

$$
\max  _ {l} \| \boldsymbol {e} ^ {(l)} \| \leq \| \boldsymbol {e} \| \leq c _ {2} \sigma \sqrt {n}. \tag {4.90}
$$

moreover, for any fixed matrix  $a \in \mathbb{r}^{n \times d}$  with  $d \leq n$ , one has

$$
\left\| \boldsymbol {e} \boldsymbol {a} \right\| _ {2, \infty} \leq 4 \sigma \sqrt {\log n} \| \boldsymbol {a} \| _ {\mathrm {f}} + (6 b \log n) \| \boldsymbol {a} \| _ {2, \infty}. \tag {4.91}
$$

remark 4.3. in view of (4.91), with probability at least  $1 - 2n^{-5}$

$$
\begin{array}{l} \| \boldsymbol {e} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \| _ {2, \infty} \leq 4 \sigma \sqrt {\log n} \| \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \| _ {\mathrm {f}} + (6 b \log n) \| \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \| _ {2, \infty} \\ = 4 \sigma \sqrt {r \log n} + 6 b \sqrt {\frac {\mu r \log^ {2} n}{n}} = (4 + 6 c _ {\mathrm {b}}) \sigma \sqrt {r \log n}, \tag {4.92} \\ \end{array}
$$

which relies on the definition (4.24) and the definition of  $c_{\mathfrak{b}}$  in (4.29). as a result,

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| m u ^ {\star} \right\| _ {2, \infty} \leq \left\| m ^ {\star} u ^ {\star} \right\| _ {2, \infty} + \left\| e u ^ {\star} \right\| _ {2, \infty} \\ \leq \sqrt {\frac {\mu r}{n}} \left| \lambda_ {1} ^ {\star} \right| + (4 + 6 c _ {\mathrm {b}}) \sigma \sqrt {r \log n}, \tag {4.93} \\ \end{array}
$$

which follows from the fact  $m^{\star}u^{\star} = u^{\star}\lambda^{\star}$  (so that  $\| m^{\star}u^{\star}\|_{2,\infty}\leq \| u^{\star}\|_{2,\infty}\| \lambda^{\star}\| = \sqrt{\mu r / n} |\lambda_1^{\star}|)$ .

with the size of the perturbations (i.e.,  $\| \pmb{e}^{(l)}\|$  and  $\| \pmb{e}\|$ ) under control, the  $\ell_2$  perturbation theory established in chapter 2 leads to the following set of conclusions.

lemma 4.14. suppose that  $c_{2}\sigma \sqrt{n} \leq (1 - 1 / \sqrt{2})\lambda_{r}^{\star}$ , where  $c_{2}$  is the same constant as in lemma 4.13. then with probability at least  $1 - o(n^{-7})$ , one has

$$
\operatorname {d i s t} \left(\boldsymbol {u}, \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star}\right) \leq \frac {2 c _ {2} \sigma \sqrt {n}}{\lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}}, \quad \operatorname {d i s t} \left(\boldsymbol {u} ^ {(l)}, \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star}\right) \leq \frac {2 c _ {2} \sigma \sqrt {n}}{\lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}}, \tag {4.94a}
$$

$$
\left\| \sin \theta \right\| \leq \frac {c _ {2} \sigma \sqrt {2 n}}{\lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}}, \quad \left\| \sin \theta^ {(l)} \right\| \leq \frac {c _ {2} \sigma \sqrt {2 n}}{\lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}}, \tag {4.94b}
$$

$$
\max  _ {1 \leq j \leq r} | \lambda_ {j} | \geq \lambda_ {r} ^ {\star} - c _ {2} \sigma \sqrt {n}, \quad \max  _ {1 \leq j \leq r} | \lambda_ {j} ^ {(l)} | \geq \lambda_ {r} ^ {\star} - c _ {2} \sigma \sqrt {n}, \tag {4.94c}
$$

$$
\max  _ {j: j > r} \left| \lambda_ {j} \right| \leq c _ {2} \sigma \sqrt {n}, \quad \max  _ {j: j > r} \left| \lambda_ {j} ^ {(l)} \right| \leq c _ {2} \sigma \sqrt {n} \tag {4.94d}
$$

hold simultaneously for all  $1 \leq l \leq n$ . in addition,

$$
\left\| \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {h} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {\mathrm {f}} \leq \frac {2 c _ {2} \sigma \sqrt {r n}}{\lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}}. \tag {4.94e}
$$

with probability exceeding  $1 - 2n^{-5}$ .

remark 4.4. lemmas 4.13 and 4.14 allow us to bound the eigengap and perturbation size as follows

$$
\left| \lambda_ {r} ^ {(l)} \right| - \left| \lambda_ {r + 1} ^ {(l)} \right| \geq \lambda_ {r} ^ {\star} - c _ {2} \sigma \sqrt {n} - c _ {2} \sigma \sqrt {n} \geq \lambda_ {r} ^ {\star} / 2, \tag {4.95a}
$$

$$
\begin{array}{l} \| m - m ^ {(l)} \| \leq \| m - m ^ {\star} \| + \| m ^ {\star} - m ^ {(l)} \| = \| e \| + \| e ^ {(l)} \| \\ \leq 2 c _ {2} \sigma \sqrt {n} \leq (1 - 1 / \sqrt {2}) \left(\left| \lambda_ {r} ^ {(l)} \right| - \left| \lambda_ {r + 1} ^ {(l)} \right|\right), \tag {4.95b} \\ \end{array}
$$

which are valid as long as  $20c_{2}\sigma \sqrt{n} \leq \lambda_{r}^{\star}$ . these will prove useful when bounding the approximation error of  $\pmb{u}$  using  $\pmb{u}^{(l)}$ .

another collection of results is concerned with  $\pmb{h}$  and  $\pmb{h}^{(l)}$ .

lemma 4.15. suppose that the assumptions of lemma 4.14 hold. with probability at least  $1 - o(n^{-7})$ ,

$$
\| \boldsymbol {h} ^ {- 1} \| \leq 2, \quad \| \left(\boldsymbol {h} ^ {(l)}\right) ^ {- 1} \| \leq 2, \tag {4.96a}
$$

$$
\left\| \boldsymbol {h} - \operatorname {s g n} (\boldsymbol {h}) \right\| \leq \frac {2 c _ {2} ^ {2} n \sigma^ {2}}{\left(\lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}\right) ^ {2}}, \quad \left\| \boldsymbol {h} ^ {(l)} - \operatorname {s g n} \left(\boldsymbol {h} ^ {(l)}\right) \right\| \leq \frac {2 c _ {2} ^ {2} n \sigma^ {2}}{\left(\lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}\right) ^ {2}} \tag {4.96b}
$$

hold simultaneously for all  $1 \leq l \leq n$ .

remark 4.5. as a consequence of (4.96) and proposition 2.1, one has

$$
\| \boldsymbol {a} \| = \| \boldsymbol {a h h} ^ {- 1} \| \leq \| \boldsymbol {a h} \| \| \boldsymbol {h} ^ {- 1} \| \leq 2 \| \boldsymbol {a h} \|, \tag {4.97a}
$$

$$
\| \boldsymbol {a} \| \| \leq \| \left. \left. \left. \boldsymbol {a} \boldsymbol {h} ^ {(l)} \right. \right. \right\| \| \left(\boldsymbol {h} ^ {(l)}\right) ^ {- 1} \| \leq 2 \| \left. \left. \left. \boldsymbol {a} \boldsymbol {h} ^ {(l)} \right. \right. \right\| \tag {4.97b}
$$

for any matrix  $\mathbf{a}$ . here,  $\| \mathbf{a} \|$  could either be the frobenius norm or the  $\ell_{2,\infty}$  norm  $\| \cdot \|_{2,\infty}$ .

# 4.8.3 leave-one-out analysis

now we move on to the main part of the analysis, which is further decomposed into four steps.

# step 1: decomposing the  $\ell_{2,\infty}$  estimation error of  $u$

by virtue of the proximity of  $\pmb{h}$  and  $\mathsf{sgn}(\pmb{h})$  unveiled in lemma 4.15, we are allowed to employ  $\pmb{u}\pmb{h}$  as a surrogate for  $u\mathsf{sgn}(\pmb{h})$ , which is more convenient to work with. as it turns out, the discrepancy between  $\pmb{u}\pmb{h}$  and the first-order approximation  $mu^{\star}(\lambda^{\star})^{-1}$ , and the discrepancy between  $\pmb{u}\pmb{h}$  and the truth, can be bounded by three important terms, as asserted below. the proof is built upon elementary algebra and basic  $\ell_2$  perturbation bounds in section 4.8.2, and is deferred to section 4.8.4.

lemma 4.16. suppose that  $2c_{2}\sigma \sqrt{n} \leq \lambda_{r}^{\star}$  for some sufficiently large constant  $c_{2} > 0$ . then with probability at least  $1 - o(n^{-7})$ , one has

$$
\left\| \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {h} - \boldsymbol {m} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \left(\boldsymbol {\lambda} ^ {\star}\right) ^ {- 1} \right\| _ {2, \infty} \leq \mathcal {e} _ {1} + \mathcal {e} _ {2}, \tag {4.98a}
$$

$$
\left\| \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {h} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {2, \infty} \leq \mathcal {e} _ {1} + \mathcal {e} _ {2} + \mathcal {e} _ {3}, \tag {4.98b}
$$

where  $\mathcal{e}_1\coloneqq \frac{2\|m(uh - u^\star)\|_{2,\infty}}{\lambda_r^\star}$ ,  $\mathcal{e}_2\coloneqq \frac{4\|mu^\star\|_{2,\infty}\|e\|}{(\lambda_r^\star)^2}$ , and  $\mathcal{e}_3\coloneqq \frac{\|eu^\star\|_{2,\infty}}{\lambda_r^\star}$ .

lemma 4.16 leaves us with three important terms to deal with. the term  $\mathcal{e}_1$  is most complicated as it involves the product of two random matrices, whereas  $\mathcal{e}_2$  and  $\mathcal{e}_3$  can be controlled straightforwardly through our preliminary facts in section 4.8.2. specifically, the term  $\mathcal{e}_2$  can be bounded by combining (4.93) with the bound (4.90) on  $\pmb{e}$  to obtain

$$
\mathcal {e} _ {2} \leq \frac {4 c _ {2} \kappa \sigma \sqrt {\mu r}}{\lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}} + \frac {4 c _ {2} (4 + 6 c _ {\mathrm {b}}) \sigma^ {2} \sqrt {r n \log n}}{(\lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}) ^ {2}}. \tag {4.99}
$$

regarding the term  $\mathcal{e}_3$ , the inequality (4.92) readily gives

$$
\mathcal {e} _ {3} \leq \frac {\left(4 + 6 c _ {\mathrm {b}}\right) \sigma \sqrt {r \log n}}{\lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}}. \tag {4.100}
$$

turning to controlling the remaining term  $\mathcal{e}_1$ , a closer inspection, however, reveals substantial challenges, due to the complicated statistical dependency between  $m$  and  $u$ . to further complicate matters, the term  $\mathcal{e}_1$  as we shall demonstrate momentarily—depend on some intrinsic properties of interest about  $u$  (e.g.,  $\| u h - u^{\star}\|_{2,\infty}$ ), which might lead to circular reasoning if not handled properly. in order to circumvent this issue, we intend to establish the following relation

$$
\mathcal {e} _ {1} \leq \mathcal {e} _ {1, 1} + \rho_ {1} \| \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {h} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \| _ {2, \infty} \tag {4.101}
$$

for some quantity  $\mathcal{e}_{1,1} > 0$  that does not involve  $\| \boldsymbol{u}\boldsymbol{h} - \boldsymbol{u}^{\star}\|_{2,\infty}$  as well as some contraction factor  $0 < \rho_{1} \leq 1/2$ . assuming the relation (4.101) holds for the moment, we have the following useful claim (the proof is straightforward and again postponed to section 4.8.4).

lemma 4.17. if conditions (4.98) and (4.101) hold with  $0 < \rho_{1} \leq 1/2$ , then we have

$$
\left\| \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {h} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {2, \infty} \leq 2 \left(\mathcal {e} _ {1, 1} + \mathcal {e} _ {2} + \mathcal {e} _ {3}\right), \tag {4.102a}
$$

$$
\left\| \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {h} - \boldsymbol {m} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \left(\boldsymbol {\lambda} ^ {\star}\right) ^ {- 1} \right\| _ {2, \infty} \leq 2 \left(\mathcal {e} _ {1, 1} + \mathcal {e} _ {2} + \rho_ {1} \mathcal {e} _ {3}\right), \tag {4.102b}
$$

$$
\left\| \boldsymbol {u} \operatorname {s g n} (\boldsymbol {h}) - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {2, \infty} \leq 4 \left(\mathcal {e} _ {1, 1} + \mathcal {e} _ {2} + \mathcal {e} _ {3}\right) + \frac {4 c _ {2} ^ {2} \sigma^ {2} \sqrt {\mu r n}}{\left(\lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}\right) ^ {2}}, \tag {4.102c}
$$

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \boldsymbol {u} \operatorname {s g n} (\boldsymbol {h}) - \boldsymbol {m} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \left(\boldsymbol {\lambda} ^ {\star}\right) ^ {- 1} \right\| _ {2, \infty} \\ \leq 3 \mathcal {e} _ {1, 1} + 3 \mathcal {e} _ {2} + \left(2 \rho_ {1} + \frac {8 c _ {2} ^ {2} \sigma^ {2} n}{\left(\lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}\right) ^ {2}}\right) \mathcal {e} _ {3} + \frac {4 c _ {2} ^ {2} \sigma^ {2} \sqrt {\mu r n}}{\left(\lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}\right) ^ {2}}. \tag {4.102d} \\ \end{array}
$$

remark 4.6. when  $\mathcal{e}_3$  is the dominant term, the bound (4.102b) might be stronger than (4.102a) if  $\rho_1$  is small.

with this lemma in mind, everything boils down to (i) establishing the relation (4.101) and (ii) deriving a tight bound on  $\mathcal{e}_{1,1}$ , which form the main content of the rest of the proof. in light of the triangle inequality

$$
\left\| m \left(\boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {h} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star}\right) \right\| _ {2, \infty} \leq \left\| \boldsymbol {e} \left(\boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {h} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star}\right) \right\| _ {2, \infty} + \left\| m ^ {\star} \left(\boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {h} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star}\right) \right\| _ {2, \infty},
$$

we dedicate the next two steps to bounding  $\| e(uh - u^{\star})\|_{2,\infty}$  and  $\| m^{\star}(uh - u^{\star})\|_{2,\infty}$  respectively.

# step 2: bounding  $\| e(uh - u^{\star})\|_{2,\infty}$  via leave-one-out analysis

to obtain tight row-wise control of  $e(uh - u^{\star})$ , one needs to carefully decouple the statistical dependency between  $e$  and  $u$ , which is where the leave-one-out idea comes into play.

step 2.1: a convenient decomposition. we start by invoking the triangle inequality to decompose the target quantity as follows

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \boldsymbol {e} (\boldsymbol {u h} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star}) \right\| _ {2, \infty} = \max _ {l} \left\| \boldsymbol {e} _ {l, \cdot} (\boldsymbol {u h} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star}) \right\| _ {2} \\ \leq \max _ {l} \left\{\left\| \boldsymbol {e} _ {l, \cdot} (\boldsymbol {u} ^ {(l)} \boldsymbol {h} ^ {(l)} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star}) \right\| _ {2} + \left\| \boldsymbol {e} _ {l, \cdot} (\boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {h} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {(l)} \boldsymbol {h} ^ {(l)}) \right\| _ {2} \right\} \\ \leq \max  _ {l} \left\{\| \boldsymbol {e} _ {l, \cdot} \left(\boldsymbol {u} ^ {(l)} \boldsymbol {h} ^ {(l)} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star}\right) \| _ {2} + \| \boldsymbol {e} \| \| \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {h} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {(l)} \boldsymbol {h} ^ {(l)} \| _ {\mathrm {f}} \right\}. \tag {4.103} \\ \end{array}
$$

in words, when controlling the  $l$ -th row of  $\pmb{e}(\pmb{u}\pmb{h} - \pmb{u}^{\star})$ , we attempt to employ  $\pmb{u}^{(l)}\pmb{h}^{(l)}$  as a surrogate of  $\pmb{u}\pmb{h}$ . the benefits to be harvested from this decomposition are:

- the statistical independence between  $\pmb{e}_{l}$ , and  $\pmb{u}^{(l)}\pmb{h}^{(l)}$  allows for convenient upper bounds on  $\| \pmb{e}_{l,}(u^{(l)}\pmb{h}^{(l)} - \pmb{u}^{\star})\|_{2}$ ;  
-  $\mathbf{u} \mathbf{h}$  and  $\mathbf{u}^{(l)} \mathbf{h}^{(l)}$  are expected to be exceedingly close, so that the discrepancy incurred by replacing  $\mathbf{u} \mathbf{h}$  with  $\mathbf{u}^{(l)} \mathbf{h}^{(l)}$  is negligible.

in what follows, we flesh out the proof details.

step 2.2: the proximity of  $uh$  and  $u^{(l)}h^{(l)}$ . given that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {h} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {(l)} \boldsymbol {h} ^ {(l)} \right\| _ {\mathrm {f}} = \left\| \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {(l)} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {(l) \top} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {\mathrm {f}} \\ \leq \left\| \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\top} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {(l)} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {(l) \top} \right\| _ {\mathrm {f}} \left\| \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \right\| \\ = \left\| \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\top} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {(l)} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {(l) \top} \right\| _ {\mathrm {f}}, \tag {4.104} \\ \end{array}
$$

it boils down to bounding  $\| \pmb{u}\pmb{u}^{\top} - \pmb{u}^{(l)}\pmb{u}^{(l)^{\top}}\|_{\mathrm{f}}$ . under simple conditions on the eigengap and the perturbation size (see (4.95) in remark 4.4), the davis-kahan theorem (cf. corollary 2.8) yields

$$
\begin{array}{l} \| \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\top} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {(l)} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {(l) \top} \| _ {\mathrm {f}} \leq \frac {2 \| (\boldsymbol {m} - \boldsymbol {m} ^ {(l)}) \boldsymbol {u} ^ {(l)} \| _ {\mathrm {f}}}{| \lambda_ {r} ^ {(l)} | - | \lambda_ {r + 1} ^ {(l)} |} \\ \leq \frac {4 \| (\boldsymbol {m} - \boldsymbol {m} ^ {(l)}) \boldsymbol {u} ^ {(l)} \| _ {\mathrm {f}}}{\lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}}. \tag {4.105} \\ \end{array}
$$

it remains to develop an upper bound on  $\| (m - m^{(l)})u^{(l)}\|$ . the way we construct  $m^{(l)}$  (see section 4.8.1) allows us to express

$$
\left(\boldsymbol {m} - \boldsymbol {m} ^ {(l)}\right) \boldsymbol {u} ^ {(l)} = \boldsymbol {e} _ {l} \boldsymbol {e} _ {l, *} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {(l)} + \left(\boldsymbol {e} _ {*, l} - e _ {l, l} \boldsymbol {e} _ {l}\right) \boldsymbol {e} _ {l} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {(l)}.
$$

this together with the triangle inequality and the fact (4.97) gives

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \left(\boldsymbol {m} - \boldsymbol {m} ^ {(l)}\right) \boldsymbol {u} ^ {(l)} \right\| _ {\mathrm {f}} \leq \left\| \boldsymbol {e} _ {l,} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {(l)} \right\| _ {2} + \left\| \boldsymbol {e} _ {., l} - e _ {l, l} \boldsymbol {e} _ {l} \right\| _ {2} \left\| \boldsymbol {u} ^ {(l)} \right\| _ {2, \infty} \\ \leq \left\| \boldsymbol {e} _ {l, \cdot} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {(l)} \right\| _ {2} + 2 \left\| \boldsymbol {e} \right\| \left\| \boldsymbol {u} ^ {(l)} \boldsymbol {h} ^ {(l)} \right\| _ {2, \infty} \\ \leq \left\| \boldsymbol {e} _ {l, \cdot} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {(l)} \right\| _ {2} + 2 \left\| \boldsymbol {e} \right\| \left\| \boldsymbol {u h} \right\| _ {2, \infty} + 2 \left\| \boldsymbol {e} \right\| \left\| \boldsymbol {u h} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {(l)} \boldsymbol {h} ^ {(l)} \right\| _ {\mathrm {f}}. \\ \end{array}
$$

substitution into (4.104) and (4.105) gives

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {h} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {(l)} \boldsymbol {h} ^ {(l)} \right\| _ {\mathrm {f}} \leq \left\| \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\top} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {(l)} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {(l) \top} \right\| _ {\mathrm {f}} \\ \leq \frac {4 \| \boldsymbol {e} _ {l ,} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {(l)} \| _ {2} + 8 \| \boldsymbol {e} \| \| \boldsymbol {u h} \| _ {2 , \infty} + 8 \| \boldsymbol {e} \| \| \boldsymbol {u h} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {(l)} \boldsymbol {h} ^ {(l)} \| _ {\mathrm {f}}}{\lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}}. \\ \end{array}
$$

as long as  $\| \pmb {e}\| /\lambda_r^\star \leq 1 / 16$ , one can further rearrange terms to obtain

$$
\left\| \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {h} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {(l)} \boldsymbol {h} ^ {(l)} \right\| _ {\mathrm {f}} \leq \frac {8 \left\| \boldsymbol {e} _ {l , .} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {(l)} \right\| _ {2} + 1 6 \left\| \boldsymbol {e} \right\| \left\| \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {h} \right\| _ {2 , \infty}}{\lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}}. (4. 1 0 6)
$$

in addition, the fact (4.97) combined with the triangle inequality yields

$$
\frac {1}{2} \left\| \boldsymbol {e} _ {l, \cdot} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {(l)} \right\| _ {2} \leq \left\| \boldsymbol {e} _ {l, \cdot} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {(l)} \boldsymbol {h} ^ {(l)} \right\| _ {2} \leq \left\| \boldsymbol {e} _ {l, \cdot} (\boldsymbol {u} ^ {(l)} \boldsymbol {h} ^ {(l)} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star}) \right\| _ {2} + \left\| \boldsymbol {e} _ {l, \cdot} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {2},
$$

which taken collectively with (4.106) reveals that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \| \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {h} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {(l)} \boldsymbol {h} ^ {(l)} \| _ {\mathrm {f}} \leq \frac {1 6 \| \boldsymbol {e} _ {l , \star} (\boldsymbol {u} ^ {(l)} \boldsymbol {h} ^ {(l)} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star}) \| _ {2}}{\lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}} \\ + \frac {1 6 \left\{\left\| \boldsymbol {e} _ {l ,} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {2} + \left\| \boldsymbol {e} \right\| \left\| \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {h} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {2 , \infty} + \left\| \boldsymbol {e} \right\| \left\| \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {2 , \infty} \right\}}{\lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}}. \tag {4.107} \\ \end{array}
$$

step 2.3: bounding  $\| \pmb{e}_{l,\cdot}(\pmb{u}^{(l)}\pmb{h}^{(l)} - \pmb{u}^{\star})\|_{\pmb{2}}$ . recognizing that  $\pmb{e}_{l,\cdot}$  is statistically independent of  $\pmb{u}^{(l)}$  (since  $\pmb{u}^{(l)}$  is computed without using  $\pmb{e}_{l,\cdot}$ ), we invoke lemma 4.13 (more precisely, we use the proof of this lemma) to demonstrate that with probability exceeding  $1 - 2n^{-6}$ ,

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \boldsymbol {e} _ {l, \cdot} (\boldsymbol {u} ^ {(l)} \boldsymbol {h} ^ {(l)} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star}) \right\| _ {2} \\ \leq 4 \sigma \sqrt {\log n} \| \boldsymbol {u} ^ {(l)} \boldsymbol {h} ^ {(l)} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \| _ {\mathrm {f}} + (6 b \log n) \| \boldsymbol {u} ^ {(l)} \boldsymbol {h} ^ {(l)} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \| _ {2, \infty} \\ \leq 4 \sigma \sqrt {\log n} \| \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {h} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \| _ {\mathrm {f}} + (6 b \log n) \| \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {h} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \| _ {2, \infty} \\ + (1 0 b \log n) \| \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {h} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {(l)} \boldsymbol {h} ^ {(l)} \| _ {\mathrm {f}} \tag {4.108} \\ \end{array}
$$

holds simultaneously for all  $1 \leq l \leq n$ , where the last line results from the triangle inequality and the fact  $4\sigma \sqrt{\log n} + 6b\log n \leq 10b\log n$ .

step 2.4: combining the above bounds. the careful reader would immediately remark that the inequalities (4.107) and (4.108) are convoluted, both of which involve the terms  $\| \pmb{e}_{l,\cdot}(\pmb{u}^{(l)}\pmb{h}^{(l)} - \pmb{u}^{\star})\|_{2}$  and  $\| \pmb{u}\pmb{h} - \pmb{u}^{(l)}\pmb{h}^{(l)}\|_{\mathrm{f}}$ . fortunately, one can substitute (4.108) into (4.107) to produce a cleaner bound. by doing so and exploiting the condition  $320b\log n\leq \lambda_r^\star$ , we rearrange terms to reach

$$
\begin{array}{l} \| \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {h} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {(l)} \boldsymbol {h} ^ {(l)} \| _ {\mathrm {f}} \leq \frac {3 2 \| \boldsymbol {e} _ {l} , \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \| _ {2} + 3 2 \| \boldsymbol {e} \| \| \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \| _ {2 , \infty}}{\lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}} \\ + \frac {1 2 8 \sigma \sqrt {\log n} \left\| \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {h} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {\mathrm {f}} + (3 2 c _ {2} \sigma \sqrt {n} + 1 9 2 b \log n) \left\| \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {h} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {2 , \infty}}{\lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}}, \\ \end{array}
$$

where we have also used the upper bound on  $\| \pmb{e}\|$  derived in (4.94). meanwhile, plugging the above inequality into (4.108) yields

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \boldsymbol {e} _ {l, \cdot} \left(\boldsymbol {u} ^ {(l)} \boldsymbol {h} ^ {(l)} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star}\right) \right\| _ {2} \leq \frac {3 2 0 b \log n}{\lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}} \left(\left\| \boldsymbol {e} _ {l, \cdot} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {2} + \left\| \boldsymbol {e} \right\| \left\| \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {2, \infty}\right) \\ + 5 \sigma \sqrt {\log n} \| \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {h} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \| _ {\mathrm {f}} + (7 b \log n) \| \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {h} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \| _ {2, \infty}, \tag {4.109} \\ \end{array}
$$

provided that  $\max \{\sigma \sqrt{n}, b\log n\} \leq c_3\lambda_r^\star$  for some small constant  $c_{3}$

substituting the preceding two bounds into (4.103) and combining terms reveal the existence of some constant  $c_4 > 0$  such that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \boldsymbol {e} (\boldsymbol {u h} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star}) \right\| _ {2, \infty} \\ \leq \alpha_ {0} + \alpha_ {1} + c _ {4} (\sigma \sqrt {n} + b \log n) \| \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {h} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \| _ {2, \infty} \tag {4.110} \\ \end{array}
$$

provided that  $\max \{\sigma \sqrt{n}, b \log n\} \leq c_3 \lambda_r^\star$  for some constant  $c_3 > 0$  small enough, where

$$
\begin{array}{l} \alpha_ {0} := \frac {3 2 c _ {2} \sigma \sqrt {n} + 3 2 0 b \log n}{\lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}} \left(\| \boldsymbol {e} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \| _ {2, \infty} + \| \boldsymbol {e} \| \| \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \| _ {2, \infty}\right), \tag {4.111} \\ \alpha_ {1} := 6 \sigma \sqrt {\log n} \| \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {h} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \| _ {\mathrm {f}}. \\ \end{array}
$$

before continuing, note that we are already well-equipped to bound the above two quantities. first,  $\alpha_0$  can be bounded by

$$
\begin{array}{l} \alpha_ {0} \leq \frac {3 2 c _ {2} \sigma \sqrt {n} + 3 2 0 b \log n}{\lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}} \\ \cdot \left(4 \sigma \sqrt {r \log n} + 6 b \sqrt {\frac {\mu r}{n}} \log n + c _ {2} \sigma \sqrt {\mu r}\right), \tag {4.112} \\ \end{array}
$$

where we have used the bounds concerning  $\pmb{e}$  from lemma 4.13 as well as the definition (4.24). regarding  $\alpha_{1}$ , it is seen from (4.94e) that

$$
\alpha_ {1} \leq \frac {1 2 c _ {2} \sigma^ {2} \sqrt {r n \log n}}{\lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}}. \tag {4.113}
$$

step 3: bounding  $\| m^{\star}(uh - u^{\star})\|_{2,\infty}$

we make the key observation that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| m ^ {\star} \left(\boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {h} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star}\right) \right\| _ {2, \infty} = \left\| \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \boldsymbol {\lambda} ^ {\star} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star \top} \left(\boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {h} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star}\right) \right\| _ {2, \infty} \\ \leq \left\| \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {2, \infty} \left\| \boldsymbol {\lambda} ^ {\star} \right\| \left\| \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star^ {\top}} \left(\boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {h} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star}\right) \right\| \\ = \sqrt {\frac {\mu r}{n}} \| \boldsymbol {\lambda} ^ {\star} \| \| \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\top} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star \top} \| ^ {2}. \tag {4.114} \\ \end{array}
$$

here, the last identity holds true due to the following observation

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star \top} \left(\boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {h} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star}\right) \right\| = \left\| \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star \top} \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star \top} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \right\| = \left\| \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star \top} \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} - \boldsymbol {i} \right\| \\ = \| \boldsymbol {y} (\cos^ {2} \theta) \boldsymbol {y} ^ {\top} - \boldsymbol {i} \| = \| \cos^ {2} \theta - \boldsymbol {i} \| \\ = \| \sin^ {2} \theta \| = \| \sin \theta \| ^ {2}, \\ \end{array}
$$

where we denote by  $\pmb{x}(\cos \theta)\pmb{y}^{\top}$  the svd of  $\pmb{u}^{\top}\pmb{u}^{\star}$ , with  $\pmb{x}$  and  $\pmb{y}$  being orthonormal matrices and  $\theta$  the diagonal matrix consisting of the principal angles between  $\pmb{u}$  and  $\pmb{u}^{\star}$  (see the definition in (2.5)). the above bounds combined with (4.94b) indicate that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| m ^ {\star} \left(\boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {h} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star}\right) \right\| _ {2, \infty} \leq \left| \lambda_ {1} ^ {\star} \right| \sqrt {\frac {\mu r}{n}} \| \sin \theta \| ^ {2} \\ \leq \frac {4 c _ {2} ^ {2} \kappa \sigma^ {2} \sqrt {\mu r n}}{\lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}} =: \alpha_ {2}. \tag {4.115} \\ \end{array}
$$

# step 4: putting all pieces together

combining the bounds (4.110) and (4.115) in steps 2-3 and using the definition of  $\mathcal{e}_1$  (see lemma 4.16) give

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathcal {e} _ {1} \leq \frac {2 \| \boldsymbol {e} (\boldsymbol {u h} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star}) \| _ {2 , \infty} + 2 \| \boldsymbol {m} ^ {\star} (\boldsymbol {u h} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star}) \| _ {2 , \infty}}{\lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}} \\ \leq \mathcal {e} _ {1, 1} + \rho_ {1} \| \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {h} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \| _ {2, \infty}, \tag {4.116} \\ \end{array}
$$

where

$$
\mathcal {e} _ {1, 1} := \frac {2 \left(\alpha_ {0} + \alpha_ {1} + \alpha_ {2}\right)}{\lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}} \quad \text {a n d} \quad \rho_ {1} := \frac {2 c _ {4} \left(\sigma \sqrt {n} + b \log n\right)}{\lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}}. \tag {4.117}
$$

this matches precisely the relation hypothesized in (4.101). in particular, one has  $0 < \rho \leq 1/2$  as long as  $4c_4(\sigma \sqrt{n} + b\log n) \leq \lambda_r^\star$ , which holds whenever  $\sigma \sqrt{n\log n} \leq c_\sigma \lambda_r^\star$  for a sufficiently small  $c_\sigma > 0$  in view of our assumption on  $b$  (cf. (4.29)).

recall that  $\mathcal{e}_{1,1}$ ,  $\mathcal{e}_2$ ,  $\mathcal{e}_3$ ,  $\alpha_0$ ,  $\alpha_1$ ,  $\alpha_2$  and  $\rho_1$  have been controlled in (4.117), (4.99), (4.100), (4.112), (4.113), (4.115) and (4.117), respectively. in addition, recall our assumption (4.29) and suppose that  $\sigma \sqrt{n} \leq c_{\sigma} \lambda_r^\star$  for some sufficiently small constant  $c_{\sigma} > 0$ . with these bounds and assumptions in mind, invoking lemma 4.17 and combining terms immediately conclude the proof.

remark 4.7. we shall also make note of an immediate consequence of the above argument as follows

$$
\left\| \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {h} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {2, \infty} \lesssim \frac {\sigma \kappa \sqrt {\mu r} + \sigma \sqrt {r \log n}}{\lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}}, \tag {4.118}
$$

which will prove useful for deriving other important results.

# 4.8.4 proof of auxiliary lemmas

proof of lemma 4.13. under assumption 4.1, theorem 3.4 (in particular (3.9)) reveals the existence of some constant  $c_2 > 0$  such that

$$
\max _ {l} \left\| \boldsymbol {e} ^ {(l)} \right\| \leq \| \boldsymbol {e} \| \leq c _ {2} \sigma \sqrt {n},
$$

holds with probability exceeding  $1 - o(n^{-7})$ .

when it comes to  $\mathbf{ea}$ , we proceed by viewing its  $l$ -th row  $\mathbf{e}_{l,'}\mathbf{a}$  as a sum of independent random vectors as follows

$$
\pmb {e} _ {l, \cdot} \pmb {a} = \sum_ {j} e _ {l, j} \pmb {a} _ {j, \cdot} =: \sum_ {j} \pmb {z} _ {j},
$$

which can be controlled by the matrix bernstein inequality. specifically, it is seen from assumption 4.1 that

$$
v := \sum_ {j} \mathbb {e} \left[ \| \boldsymbol {z} _ {j} \| _ {2} ^ {2} \right] \leq \sigma^ {2} \sum_ {j} \| \boldsymbol {a} _ {j, \cdot} \| _ {2} ^ {2} = \sigma^ {2} \| \boldsymbol {a} \| _ {\mathrm {f}} ^ {2};
$$

$$
l := \max _ {j} \| z _ {j} \| _ {2} = \max _ {j} | e _ {l, j} | \| z _ {j, \cdot} \| _ {2} \leq b \| z \| _ {2, \infty}.
$$

invoke the matrix bernstein inequality (cf. corollary 3.3) and take the union bound to demonstrate that: with probability exceeding  $1 - 2n^{-6}$ ,

$$
\left\| \boldsymbol {e} _ {l, \cdot} \boldsymbol {a} \right\| _ {2} \leq 4 \sqrt {v \log n} + 6 l \log n \leq 4 \sigma \sqrt {\log n} \| \boldsymbol {a} \| _ {\mathrm {f}} + (6 b \log n) \| \boldsymbol {a} \| _ {2, \infty}
$$

holds simultaneously for all  $1 \leq l \leq n$ , thus concluding the proof.

proof of lemma 4.14. lemma 4.13 tells us that

$$
\max  _ {l} \| \boldsymbol {e} ^ {(l)} \| \leq \| \boldsymbol {e} \| \leq c _ {2} \sigma \sqrt {n} \tag {4.119}
$$

holds with probability exceeding  $1 - o(n^{-7})$ . repeating the argument in the proof of corollary 2.8 reveals that

$$
\max  _ {1 \leq j \leq r} | \lambda_ {j} | \geq \lambda_ {r} ^ {\star} - \| \boldsymbol {e} \|, \quad \max  _ {1 \leq j \leq r} | \lambda_ {j} ^ {(l)} | \geq \lambda_ {r} ^ {\star} - \| \boldsymbol {e} ^ {(l)} \|, \tag {4.120a}
$$

$$
\max  _ {j: j > r} \left| \lambda_ {j} \right| \leq \| \boldsymbol {e} \|, \quad \max  _ {j: j > r} \left| \lambda_ {j} ^ {(l)} \right| \leq \| \boldsymbol {e} ^ {(l)} \|, \tag {4.120b}
$$

which taken together with (4.119) validates (4.94c)-(4.94d).

regarding (4.94a) and (4.94b), apply corollary 2.8 to reach

$$
\operatorname {d i s t} \left(\boldsymbol {u}, \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star}\right) \leq \sqrt {2} \| \sin \theta \| \leq \frac {2 \| \boldsymbol {e} \|}{\lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}} \leq \frac {2 c _ {2} \sigma \sqrt {n}}{\lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}} \tag {4.121}
$$

as long as  $\| \pmb{e}\| \leq c_2\sigma \sqrt{n}\leq (1 - 1 / \sqrt{2})\lambda_r^\star$ , where we have used  $\lambda_{r + 1}^{\star} = 0$ . the bound on  $\mathrm{dist}\bigl (\pmb {u}^{(l)},\pmb {u}^{\star}\bigr)$  follows from the same argument.

additionally, regarding  $\mathbf{u}\mathbf{h} - \mathbf{u}^{\star}$  we can derive

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {h} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {\mathrm {f}} = \left\| \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star \top} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {\mathrm {f}} \\ \leq \left\| \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\top} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star^ {\top}} \right\| \left\| \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {\mathrm {f}} \\ = \| \sin \boldsymbol {\theta} \| \| \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \| _ {\mathrm {f}} \leq \frac {2 c _ {2} \sigma \sqrt {r n}}{\lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}}, \tag {4.122} \\ \end{array}
$$

where the last line arises from lemma 2.5, (4.94b), and  $\| \pmb{u}^{\star}\|_{\mathrm{f}} = \sqrt{r}$

proof of lemma 4.15. we shall only prove the result for  $\pmb{h}$ ; the proof for  $\pmb{h}^{(l)}$  follows from identical arguments and is hence omitted.

from our discussion in section 2.2.2, one can express the svd of  $\pmb{h} = \pmb{u}^{\top}\pmb{u}^{\star}$  as  $\pmb{h} = \pmb{x}(\cos \theta)\pmb{y}^{\top}$ , where the columns of  $\pmb{x}$  (resp.  $\pmb{y}$ ) are the left (resp. right) singular vectors of  $\pmb{h}$ ,

and  $\theta$  is a diagonal matrix composed of the principal angles between  $\mathbf{u}$  and  $\mathbf{u}^{\star}$ . in light of this and the definition (4.30), we can establish (4.96b) as follows

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \boldsymbol {h} - \operatorname {s g n} (\boldsymbol {h}) \right\| = \left\| \boldsymbol {x} \left(\cos \theta - \boldsymbol {i}\right) \boldsymbol {y} ^ {\top} \right\| = \left\| \boldsymbol {i} - \cos \theta \right\| \\ \leq \left\| \boldsymbol {i} - \cos^ {2} \theta \right\| = \| \sin \theta \| ^ {2} \\ \leq \frac {2 c _ {2} ^ {2} \sigma^ {2} n}{\left(\lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}\right) ^ {2}}, \tag {4.123} \\ \end{array}
$$

where the middle line holds since  $1 - \cos \theta \leq 1 - \cos^2\theta$  , and the last line follows from (4.94b).

coming back to the claim (4.96a), it suffices to justify that  $\sigma_{\min}(\pmb{h}) \geq 1/2$ . recognizing that  $\mathsf{sgn}(\pmb{h}) = \pmb{x}\pmb{y}^{\top}$ , we see that all singular values of  $\mathsf{sgn}(\pmb{h})$  equal 1. thus, weyl's inequality together with (4.123) gives

$$
\sigma_ {\min } (\boldsymbol {h}) \geq \sigma_ {\min } \left(\operatorname {s g n} (\boldsymbol {h})\right) - \| \boldsymbol {h} - \operatorname {s g n} (\boldsymbol {h}) \| \geq 1 - \frac {2 c _ {2} ^ {2} \sigma^ {2} n}{\left(\lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}\right) ^ {2}} \geq \frac {1}{2},
$$

with the proviso that  $2c_{2}\sigma \sqrt{n}\leq \lambda_{r}^{\star}$

proof of lemma 4.16. we start by connecting  $uh\lambda^{\star}$  more explicitly with  $mu^{\star}$  as follows (the invertibility of  $\lambda$  can be deduced from (4.94c))

$$
\boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {h} \boldsymbol {\lambda} ^ {\star} = \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {\lambda} \boldsymbol {\lambda} ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {h} \boldsymbol {\lambda} ^ {\star} = \boldsymbol {m} \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {\lambda} ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \boldsymbol {\lambda} ^ {\star}, \tag {4.124}
$$

which relies on the definition (4.89) and the eigendecomposition  $mu = u\lambda$ . in addition, the eigendecomposition  $m^{\star}u^{\star} = u^{\star}\lambda^{\star}$  gives

$$
\begin{array}{l} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \boldsymbol {\lambda} ^ {\star} = \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {m} ^ {\star} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} = \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {m} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {e} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \\ = \boldsymbol {\lambda} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {e} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star}, \tag {4.125} \\ \end{array}
$$

which taken collectively with (4.124) demonstrates that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {h} \boldsymbol {\lambda} ^ {\star} = \boldsymbol {m} \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {\lambda} ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {\lambda} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} - \boldsymbol {m} \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {\lambda} ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {e} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \\ = \boldsymbol {m} \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {h} - \boldsymbol {m} \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {\lambda} ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {e} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \\ = m u ^ {\star} + m \left(u h - u ^ {\star}\right) - m u \lambda^ {- 1} u ^ {\top} e u ^ {\star}. \\ \end{array}
$$

consequently, the difference between  $uh\lambda^{\star}$  and  $mu^{\star}$  obeys

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {h} \boldsymbol {\lambda} ^ {\star} - \boldsymbol {m} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {2, \infty} \leq \left\| \boldsymbol {m} \left(\boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {h} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star}\right) \right\| _ {2, \infty} \\ + \left\| m u \lambda^ {- 1} u ^ {\top} e u ^ {\star} \right\| _ {2, \infty}. \tag {4.126} \\ \end{array}
$$

regarding the second term in (4.126), one can deduce that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \boldsymbol {m} \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {\lambda} ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {e} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {2, \infty} \\ \leq \left\| m u \right\| _ {2, \infty} \left\| \boldsymbol {\lambda} ^ {- 1} \right\| \cdot \left\| u \right\| \cdot \left\| e \right\| \cdot \left\| u ^ {\star} \right\| \\ \stackrel {\text {(i)}} {\leq} \frac {2 \| m u \| _ {2 , \infty} \| e \|}{\lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}} \stackrel {\text {(i i)}} {\leq} \frac {4 \| m u h \| _ {2 , \infty} \| e \|}{\lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}} \\ \stackrel {(i i i)} {\leq} \frac {4 \| m (\boldsymbol {u h} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star}) \| _ {2 , \infty} \| \boldsymbol {e} \|}{\lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}} + \frac {4 \| m \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \| _ {2 , \infty} \| \boldsymbol {e} \|}{\lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}} \\ \stackrel {(\mathrm {i v})} {\leq} \| m \left(\boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {h} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star}\right) \| _ {2, \infty} + \frac {4 \| \boldsymbol {m} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \| _ {2 , \infty} \| \boldsymbol {e} \|}{\lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}}. \tag {4.127} \\ \end{array}
$$

here, (i) follows from the facts  $\| \pmb{u}\| = \| \pmb{u}^{\star}\| = 1$  and  $|\lambda_r|\geq \lambda_r^\star -c_2\sigma \sqrt{n}\geq \lambda_r^\star /2$  (see lemma 4.14), (ii) holds due to (4.97), (iii) invokes the triangle inequality, whereas (iv) holds true provided that  $4\| e\| \leq \lambda_r^\star$ . combine (4.126) and (4.127) to reach

$$
\left\| \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {h} \boldsymbol {\lambda} ^ {\star} - \boldsymbol {m} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {2, \infty} \leq 2 \left\| \boldsymbol {m} (\boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {h} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star}) \right\| _ {2, \infty} + \frac {4 \left\| \boldsymbol {m} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {2 , \infty} \left\| \boldsymbol {e} \right\|}{\lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}},
$$

which together with the fact that  $\| (\pmb{\lambda}^{\star})^{-1}\| = 1 / \lambda_r^{\star}$  and the elementary relation  $\| \mathbf{a}\|_{2,\infty} = \| a\pmb{\lambda}^{\star}(\pmb{\lambda}^{\star})^{-1}\|_{2,\infty} \leq \| \pmb{a}\pmb{\lambda}^{\star}\|_{2,\infty}\| (\pmb{\lambda}^{\star})^{-1}\|$  yields the desired claim (4.98a).

when it comes to the second claim (4.98b), combining (4.98a) with the triangle inequality

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {h} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {2, \infty} = \left\| \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {h} - \boldsymbol {m} ^ {\star} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \left(\boldsymbol {\lambda} ^ {\star}\right) ^ {- 1} \right\| _ {2, \infty} \\ \leq \left\| \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {h} - \boldsymbol {m} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \left(\boldsymbol {\lambda} ^ {\star}\right) ^ {- 1} \right\| _ {2, \infty} + \left\| \boldsymbol {e} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \left(\boldsymbol {\lambda} ^ {\star}\right) ^ {- 1} \right\| _ {2, \infty} \\ \leq \left\| \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {h} - \boldsymbol {m} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \left(\boldsymbol {\lambda} ^ {\star}\right) ^ {- 1} \right\| _ {2, \infty} + \left\| \boldsymbol {e} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {2, \infty} / \lambda_ {r} ^ {\star} \\ \end{array}
$$

immediately establishes the advertised bound. here the last relation again arises from the elementary inequality  $\| \boldsymbol{a}\boldsymbol{b}\|_{2,\infty} \leq \| \boldsymbol{a}\|_{2,\infty}\| \boldsymbol{b}\|$ .

proof of lemma 4.17. first of all, taking condition (4.101) collectively with (4.98b) and rearranging terms yield (4.102a):

$$
\left\| \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {h} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {2, \infty} \leq \frac {1}{1 - \rho_ {1}} \left(\mathcal {e} _ {1, 1} + \mathcal {e} _ {2} + \mathcal {e} _ {3}\right) \leq 2 \left(\mathcal {e} _ {1, 1} + \mathcal {e} _ {2} + \mathcal {e} _ {3}\right), \tag {4.128}
$$

where the last inequality follows from  $\rho \leq 1/2$ . substituting (4.101) and (4.102a) into (4.98a) then gives (4.102b):

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {h} - \boldsymbol {m} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \left(\boldsymbol {\lambda} ^ {\star}\right) ^ {- 1} \right\| _ {2, \infty} \leq \mathcal {e} _ {1, 1} + 2 \rho_ {1} \left(\mathcal {e} _ {1, 1} + \mathcal {e} _ {2} + \mathcal {e} _ {3}\right) + \mathcal {e} _ {2} \\ \leq 2 \mathcal {e} _ {1, 1} + 2 \mathcal {e} _ {2} + 2 \rho_ {1} \mathcal {e} _ {3}, \tag {4.129} \\ \end{array}
$$

where once again we use the assumption that  $\rho \leq 1/2$ . in addition, the following observation connects  $uh$  with  $u\operatorname{sgn}(h)$ :

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {h} - \boldsymbol {u} \operatorname {s g n} (\boldsymbol {h}) \right\| _ {2, \infty} \leq \| \boldsymbol {u} \| _ {2, \infty} \| \boldsymbol {h} - \operatorname {s g n} (\boldsymbol {h}) \| \\ \stackrel {\mathrm {(i)}} {\leq} \frac {2 c _ {2} ^ {2} \sigma^ {2} n}{(\lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}) ^ {2}} \| \boldsymbol {u} \| _ {2, \infty} \stackrel {\mathrm {(i i)}} {\leq} \frac {4 c _ {2} ^ {2} \sigma^ {2} n}{(\lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}) ^ {2}} \| \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {h} \| _ {2, \infty} \\ \stackrel {(\mathrm {i i i})} {\leq} \frac {4 c _ {2} ^ {2} \sigma^ {2} n}{\left(\lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}\right) ^ {2}} \left\| \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {h} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {2, \infty} + \frac {4 c _ {2} ^ {2} \sigma^ {2} n}{\left(\lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}\right) ^ {2}} \sqrt {\frac {\mu r}{n}}, \tag {4.130} \\ \end{array}
$$

where (i) results from (4.96b), (ii) relies on (4.97a), and (iii) comes from the triangle inequality  $\| u h \|_{2, \infty} \leq \| u h - u^{\star} \|_{2, \infty} + \| u^{\star} \|_{2, \infty}$  and the definition (4.24).

the preceding bound together with the triangle inequality gives (4.102c):

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \boldsymbol {u} \operatorname {s g n} (\boldsymbol {h}) - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {2, \infty} \leq \left\| \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {h} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {2, \infty} + \left\| \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {h} - \boldsymbol {u} \operatorname {s g n} (\boldsymbol {h}) \right\| _ {2, \infty} \\ \leq \left(1 + \frac {4 c _ {2} ^ {2} \sigma^ {2} n}{\left(\lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}\right) ^ {2}}\right) \| \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {h} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \| _ {2, \infty} + \frac {4 c _ {2} ^ {2} \sigma^ {2} n}{\left(\lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}\right) ^ {2}} \sqrt {\frac {\mu r}{n}} \\ \leq 4 \left(\mathcal {e} _ {1, 1} + \mathcal {e} _ {2} + \mathcal {e} _ {3}\right) + \frac {4 c _ {2} ^ {2} \sigma^ {2} \sqrt {\mu r n}}{\left(\lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}\right) ^ {2}}, \\ \end{array}
$$

where the last inequality holds as long as  $4c_2^2\sigma^2 n \leq (\lambda_r^\star)^2$ . additionally, the inequalities (4.129) and (4.130) further allow us to deduce (4.102d):

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \boldsymbol {u} \operatorname {s g n} (\boldsymbol {h}) - \boldsymbol {m} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \left(\boldsymbol {\lambda} ^ {\star}\right) ^ {- 1} \right\| _ {2, \infty} \\ \leq \left\| u h - m u ^ {\star} \left(\lambda^ {\star}\right) ^ {- 1} \right\| _ {2, \infty} + \left\| u h - u \operatorname {s g n} (h) \right\| _ {2, \infty} \\ \leq 2 \mathcal {e} _ {1, 1} + 2 \mathcal {e} _ {2} + 2 \rho_ {1} \mathcal {e} _ {3} + \frac {8 c _ {2} ^ {2} \sigma^ {2} n}{\left(\lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}\right) ^ {2}} \left(\mathcal {e} _ {1, 1} + \mathcal {e} _ {2} + \mathcal {e} _ {3}\right) + \frac {4 c _ {2} ^ {2} \sigma^ {2} \sqrt {\mu r n}}{\left(\lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}\right) ^ {2}} \\ \leq 3 \mathcal {e} _ {1, 1} + 3 \mathcal {e} _ {2} + \left(2 \rho_ {1} + \frac {8 c _ {2} ^ {2} \sigma^ {2} n}{\left(\lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}\right) ^ {2}}\right) \mathcal {e} _ {3} + \frac {4 c _ {2} ^ {2} \sigma^ {2} \sqrt {\mu r n}}{\left(\lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}\right) ^ {2}}. \\ \end{array}
$$

here, the penultimate line combines (4.102a), (4.102b) and (4.130), while the last inequality relies on the assumption  $8c_{2}^{2}\sigma^{2}n \leq (\lambda_{r}^{\star})^{2}$ .

# 4.9 appendix b: proof of corollary 4.3

moving on to the proof of corollary 4.3, we start by pointing out the main issue that deserves particular attention. roughly speaking, we have learned from theorem 4.2 (and its analysis) that  $u^{\star} \approx u h$  under mild conditions, which naturally suggests that

$$
\boldsymbol {m} ^ {\star} = \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \boldsymbol {\lambda} ^ {\star} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star \top} \approx \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {h} \boldsymbol {\lambda} ^ {\star} \boldsymbol {h} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\top}.
$$

as a result, in order to enable  $m^{\star} \approx u\lambda u^{\top}$ , one would need to ensure  $h\lambda^{\star}h^{\top} \approx \lambda$ .

the above argument, while highly informal, reveals the core idea underlying the proof. our proof is based upon the following observation

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {\lambda} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\top} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \boldsymbol {\lambda} ^ {\star} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star^ {\top}} \right\| _ {\infty} \leq \underbrace {\left\| \boldsymbol {u} \left(\boldsymbol {\lambda} - \boldsymbol {h} \boldsymbol {\lambda} ^ {\star} \boldsymbol {h} ^ {\top}\right) \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\top} \right\| _ {\infty}} _ {=: \gamma_ {1}} \\ + \underbrace {\left\| \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {h} \boldsymbol {\lambda} ^ {\star} \boldsymbol {h} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\top} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \boldsymbol {\lambda} ^ {\star} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star \top} \right\| _ {\infty}} _ {=: \gamma_ {2}}, \tag {4.131} \\ \end{array}
$$

which leaves us with two terms to cope with.

# step 1: bounding  $\gamma_{1}$

regarding  $\gamma_{1}$  defined in (4.131), it is seen that

$$
\gamma_ {1} \leq \| \boldsymbol {u} \| _ {2, \infty} ^ {2} \| \boldsymbol {\lambda} - \boldsymbol {h} \boldsymbol {\lambda} ^ {\star} \boldsymbol {h} ^ {\top} \| \leq \frac {4 \mu r}{n} \| \boldsymbol {\lambda} - \boldsymbol {h} \boldsymbol {\lambda} ^ {\star} \boldsymbol {h} ^ {\top} \|. \tag {4.132}
$$

in the last relation, we have exploited the fact that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \boldsymbol {u} \right\| _ {2, \infty} = \left\| \boldsymbol {u} \operatorname {s g n} (\boldsymbol {h}) \right\| _ {2, \infty} \leq \left\| \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {2, \infty} + \left\| \boldsymbol {u} \operatorname {s g n} (\boldsymbol {h}) - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {2, \infty} \\ \leq 2 \sqrt {\mu r / n}, \tag {4.133} \\ \end{array}
$$

where the last inequality relies on (4.31a) and the assumption  $\sigma \sqrt{n} (\kappa +\sqrt{\log n})\leq c_1\lambda_r^\star$  for some sufficiently small constant  $c_{1} > 0$

it then boils down to bounding  $\| \pmb{\lambda} - \pmb{h}\pmb{\lambda}^{\star}\pmb{h}^{\top}\|$ . towards this, it is seen from the identity (4.125) and the definition  $\pmb{h} = \pmb{u}^{\top}\pmb{u}^{\star}$  that

$$
h \lambda^ {\star} h ^ {\top} - \lambda = \lambda h h ^ {\top} - u ^ {\top} e u ^ {\star} h ^ {\top} - \lambda ,
$$

which together with the triangle inequality reveals that

$$
\left\| \boldsymbol {h} \boldsymbol {\lambda} ^ {\star} \boldsymbol {h} ^ {\top} - \boldsymbol {\lambda} \right\| \leq \left\| \boldsymbol {\lambda} \left(\boldsymbol {h} \boldsymbol {h} ^ {\top} - \boldsymbol {i}\right) \right\| + \left\| \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {e} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \boldsymbol {h} ^ {\top} \right\|. \tag {4.134}
$$

the rest of this step is devoted to controlling the above two terms.

with regards to the first term on the right-hand side of (4.134), we make the observation that

$$
\| \boldsymbol {h} \boldsymbol {h} ^ {\top} - \boldsymbol {i} \| = \| \cos^ {2} \boldsymbol {\theta} - \boldsymbol {i} \| = \| \sin^ {2} \boldsymbol {\theta} \| \leq \frac {2 c _ {2} ^ {2} \sigma^ {2} n}{(\lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}) ^ {2}},
$$

where, as usual,  $\theta$  denotes a diagonal matrix composed of the principal angles between  $\mathbf{u}$  and  $\mathbf{u}^{\star}$ , and the last inequality results from lemma 4.14. this combined with weyl's inequality and lemma 4.14 leads to

$$
\begin{array}{l} \| \boldsymbol {\lambda} (\boldsymbol {h} \boldsymbol {h} ^ {\top} - \boldsymbol {i}) \| \leq \| \boldsymbol {\lambda} \| \| \boldsymbol {h} \boldsymbol {h} ^ {\top} - \boldsymbol {i} \| \leq \left(\| \boldsymbol {\lambda} ^ {\star} \| + \| \boldsymbol {e} \|\right) \| \boldsymbol {h} \boldsymbol {h} ^ {\top} - \boldsymbol {i} \| \\ \leq \left(\left| \lambda_ {1} ^ {\star} \right| + c _ {2} \sigma \sqrt {n}\right) \frac {2 c _ {2} ^ {2} \sigma^ {2} n}{\left(\lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}\right) ^ {2}} \leq \frac {4 c _ {2} ^ {2} \kappa \sigma^ {2} n}{\lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}}, \tag {4.135} \\ \end{array}
$$

provided that  $c_{2}\sigma \sqrt{n} \leq \lambda_{r}^{\star} \leq |\lambda_{1}^{\star}|$ .

when it comes to the second term on the right-hand side of (4.134), let us introduce an orthonormal matrix  $\pmb{r} \coloneqq \arg \min_{\pmb{q} \in \mathcal{o}^{r \times r}} \| \pmb{u}\pmb{q} - \pmb{u}^{\star} \|$ , which helps us derive

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {e} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \boldsymbol {h} ^ {\top} \right\| \leq \left\| \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {e} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \right\| = \left\| \boldsymbol {r} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {e} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \right\| \\ \leq \left\| \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {e} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \right\| + \left\| \left(\boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {r} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star}\right) ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {e} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \right\| \\ \leq \left\| \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star \top} \boldsymbol {e} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \right\| + \| \boldsymbol {e} \| \operatorname {d i s t} (\boldsymbol {u}, \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star}). \tag {4.136} \\ \end{array}
$$

here, the first inequality holds since  $\| h\| = \| u^{\top}u^{\star}\| \leq 1$ , while the last line follows since  $\| u^{\star}\| = 1$  and  $\| ur - u^{\star}\| = \mathrm{dist}(u,u^{\star})$ . in addition, we claim that with probability at least  $1 - 2n^{-7}$

$$
\left\| \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star^ {\top}} \boldsymbol {e} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \right\| \leq (6 + 1 2 c _ {\mathrm {b}}) \sigma \sqrt {r \log n}. \tag {4.137}
$$

if this claim were valid, then one could continue the derivation (4.136) and invoke lemma 4.14 to demonstrate that

$$
\left\| \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {e} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \boldsymbol {h} ^ {\top} \right\| \leq (6 + 1 2 c _ {\mathrm {b}}) \sigma \sqrt {r \log n} + \frac {2 c _ {2} ^ {2} \sigma^ {2} n}{\lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}}. \tag {4.138}
$$

to finish up, substituting (4.135) and (4.138) into (4.134) yields

$$
\left\| \boldsymbol {h} \boldsymbol {\lambda} ^ {\star} \boldsymbol {h} ^ {\top} - \boldsymbol {\lambda} \right\| \leq \frac {6 c _ {2} ^ {2} \kappa \sigma^ {2} n}{\lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}} + (6 + 1 2 c _ {\mathrm {b}}) \sigma \sqrt {r \log n}, \tag {4.139}
$$

which combined with (4.132) leads to

$$
\gamma_ {1} \lesssim \frac {\sigma^ {2} \kappa \mu r}{\lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}} + \frac {\sigma \mu \sqrt {r ^ {3} \log n}}{n}. \tag {4.140}
$$

# step 2: bounding  $\gamma_{2}$

before proceeding, we recall from (4.118) that

$$
