\left\| \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {h} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {2, \infty} \lesssim \frac {\sigma \kappa \sqrt {\mu r} + \sigma \sqrt {r \log n}}{\lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}} \lesssim \frac {\sigma \kappa \sqrt {\mu r \log n}}{\lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}} \tag {4.141}
$$

recognizing the basic decomposition

$$
\gamma_ {2} = \left\| \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {h} \boldsymbol {\lambda} ^ {\star} \boldsymbol {h} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\top} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \boldsymbol {\lambda} ^ {\star} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star^ {\top}} \right\| _ {\infty} = \left\| \boldsymbol {a} _ {1} + \boldsymbol {a} _ {1} ^ {\top} + \boldsymbol {a} _ {2} \right\| _ {\infty}
$$

with  $\mathbf{a}_1 \coloneqq (\mathbf{u}\mathbf{h} - \mathbf{u}^\star)\mathbf{\lambda}^\star\mathbf{u}^{\star \top}$  and  $\mathbf{a}_2 \coloneqq (\mathbf{u}\mathbf{h} - \mathbf{u}^\star)\mathbf{\lambda}^\star(\mathbf{u}\mathbf{h} - \mathbf{u}^\star)^\top$ , we can control each of these terms separately. firstly, observe that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \boldsymbol {a} _ {1} \right\| _ {\infty} \leq \left\| \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {h} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {2, \infty} \left\| \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {2, \infty} \left\| \boldsymbol {\lambda} ^ {\star} \right\| \\ \lesssim | \lambda_ {1} ^ {\star} | \sqrt {\frac {\mu r}{n}} \cdot \frac {\sigma \kappa \sqrt {\mu r \log n}}{\lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}} \asymp \sigma \kappa^ {2} \mu r \sqrt {\frac {\log n}{n}}, \\ \end{array}
$$

where we have made use of (4.141). similarly,

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \boldsymbol {a} _ {2} \right\| _ {\infty} \leq \left\| \boldsymbol {u h} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {2, \infty} ^ {2} \left\| \boldsymbol {\lambda} ^ {\star} \right\| \lesssim | \lambda_ {1} ^ {\star} | \frac {\sigma^ {2} \kappa^ {2} \mu r \log n}{\left(\lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}\right) ^ {2}} \\ \succ \frac {\sigma^ {2} \kappa^ {3} \mu r \log n}{\lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}} \lesssim \sigma \kappa^ {2} \mu r \sqrt {\frac {\log n}{n}}, \\ \end{array}
$$

provided that  $\sigma \kappa \sqrt{n\log n} \lesssim \lambda_r^\star$ . consequently,

$$
\gamma_ {2} \leq 2 \| \boldsymbol {a} _ {1} \| _ {\infty} + \| \boldsymbol {a} _ {2} \| _ {\infty} \lesssim \sigma \kappa^ {2} \mu r \sqrt {\frac {\log n}{n}}.
$$

# step 3: putting all this together

combining the above bounds, we demonstrate that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {\lambda} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\top} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \boldsymbol {\lambda} ^ {\star} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star^ {\top}} \right\| _ {\infty} \leq \gamma_ {1} + \gamma_ {2} \\ \lesssim \sigma \kappa^ {2} \mu r \sqrt {\frac {\log n}{n}} + \frac {\kappa \mu r \sigma^ {2}}{\lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}} + \frac {\sigma \mu r \sqrt {r \log n}}{n} \asymp \sigma \kappa^ {2} \mu r \sqrt {\frac {\log n}{n}}, \tag {4.142} \\ \end{array}
$$

provided that  $\sigma \sqrt{n} \lesssim \lambda_r^\star$ . this concludes the proof of corollary 4.3, as long as the claim (4.137) can be validated.

# proof of the claim (4.137)

let us start by expressing  $\pmb{u}^{\star \top}\pmb{e}\pmb{u}^{\star}$  as a sum of independent random matrices as follows

$$
\boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star \top} \boldsymbol {e} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} = \sum_ {i, j: i \geq j} e _ {i, j} \left\{\left(\boldsymbol {u} _ {i, \cdot} ^ {\star}\right) ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {u} _ {j, \cdot} ^ {\star} + \left(\boldsymbol {u} _ {j, \cdot} ^ {\star}\right) ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {u} _ {i, \cdot} ^ {\star} \right\} =: \sum_ {i, j: i \geq j} \boldsymbol {z} _ {i, j}.
$$

from the elementary inequality  $(\mathbf{a} + \mathbf{a}^{\top})^{2}\preceq 2\mathbf{a}\mathbf{a}^{\top} + 2\mathbf{a}^{\top}\mathbf{a}$ , we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathbb {e} \left[ \boldsymbol {z} _ {i, j} ^ {2} \right] \preceq \sigma^ {2} \left\{\left(\boldsymbol {u} _ {i, \cdot} ^ {\star}\right) ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {u} _ {j, \cdot} ^ {\star} + \left(\boldsymbol {u} _ {j, \cdot} ^ {\star}\right) ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {u} _ {i, \cdot} ^ {\star} \right\} \left\{\left(\boldsymbol {u} _ {i, \cdot} ^ {\star}\right) ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {u} _ {j, \cdot} ^ {\star} + \left(\boldsymbol {u} _ {j, \cdot} ^ {\star}\right) ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {u} _ {i, \cdot} ^ {\star} \right\} ^ {\top} \\ \preceq 2 \sigma^ {2} \big \{\| \boldsymbol {u} _ {j, \cdot} ^ {\star} \| _ {2} ^ {2} (\boldsymbol {u} _ {i, \cdot} ^ {\star}) ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {u} _ {i, \cdot} ^ {\star} + \| \boldsymbol {u} _ {i, \cdot} ^ {\star} \| _ {2} ^ {2} (\boldsymbol {u} _ {j, \cdot} ^ {\star}) ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {u} _ {j, \cdot} ^ {\star} \big \}, \\ \end{array}
$$

thus indicating that

$$
\begin{array}{l} v = \big \| \sum_ {i, j: i \geq j} \mathbb {e} \big [ \boldsymbol {z} _ {i, j} ^ {2} \big ] \big \| \leq 2 \sigma^ {2} \big \| \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \sum_ {j = 1} ^ {n} \| \boldsymbol {u} _ {j, \cdot} ^ {\star} \| _ {2} ^ {2} \big (\boldsymbol {u} _ {i, \cdot} ^ {\star} \big) ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {u} _ {i, \cdot} ^ {\star} \big \| \\ = 2 \sigma^ {2} \| \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \| _ {\mathrm {f}} ^ {2} \left\| \sum_ {i} \left(\boldsymbol {u} _ {i, \cdot} ^ {\star}\right) ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {u} _ {i, \cdot} ^ {\star} \right\| = 2 \sigma^ {2} r \| \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star \top} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \| = 2 \sigma^ {2} r. \\ \end{array}
$$

in addition, each matrix  $\mathbf{z}_{i,j}$  can be bounded in size by

$$
\max  _ {i, j} \| \boldsymbol {z} _ {i, j} \| \leq 2 \max  _ {i, j} \left| e _ {i, j} \right| \max  _ {i, j} \left\| \boldsymbol {u} _ {i, \cdot} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {2} \left\| \boldsymbol {u} _ {j, \cdot} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {2} \leq 2 b \frac {\mu r}{n} =: l,
$$

where we have used the definition of the incoherence parameter  $\mu$ . apply the matrix bernstein inequality (see corollary 3.3) to reach

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star \top} \boldsymbol {e} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \right\| \leq 4 \sqrt {v \log n} + 6 l \log n \leq \sigma \sqrt {3 2 r \log n} + 1 2 b \frac {\mu r \log n}{n} \\ \leq (6 + 1 2 c _ {\mathrm {b}}) \sigma \sqrt {r \log n} \\ \end{array}
$$

with probability exceeding  $1 - 2n^{-7}$ . here, the last line holds since

$$
b \frac {\mu r \log n}{n} \leq c _ {\mathsf {b}} \sigma \sqrt {\frac {n}{\mu \log n}} \cdot \frac {\mu r \log n}{n} = c _ {\mathsf {b}} \sigma \sqrt {r \log n} \sqrt {\frac {\mu r}{n}} \leq c _ {\mathsf {b}} \sigma \sqrt {r \log n},
$$

which relies on the assumption (4.29) and the basic fact  $\mu \leq n / r$ .

# 4.10 appendix c: proof of theorem 4.4

a symmetrization trick. as alluded to previously, the proof is built on a "symmetric dilation" trick that helps symmetrize a general matrix. we start with the following definition.

definition 4.3 (symmetric dilation). for any matrix  $\mathbf{a} \in \mathbb{r}^{n_1 \times n_2}$ , its symmetric dilation  $s(\mathbf{a}) \in \mathbb{r}^{(n_1 + n_2) \times (n_1 + n_2)}$  is defined to be

$$
\mathcal {s} (\boldsymbol {a}) = \left[ \begin{array}{c c} \boldsymbol {0} & \boldsymbol {a} \\ \boldsymbol {a} ^ {\top} & \boldsymbol {0} \end{array} \right].
$$

apart from the symmetry of  $s(a)$ , which is immediate from its definition, the main benefit of the symmetric dilation lies in the correspondence between the eigendecomposition of  $s(a)$  and the singular value decomposition of  $a$ . more specifically, let  $u\sigma v^{\top}$  be the svd of  $a$ . then one has the following eigendecomposition for  $s(a)$ :

$$
\mathcal {s} (\boldsymbol {a}) = \frac {1}{\sqrt {2}} \left[ \begin{array}{l l} \boldsymbol {u} & \boldsymbol {u} \\ \boldsymbol {v} & - \boldsymbol {v} \end{array} \right] \cdot \left[ \begin{array}{l l} \boldsymbol {\sigma} & \boldsymbol {0} \\ \boldsymbol {0} & - \boldsymbol {\sigma} \end{array} \right] \cdot \frac {1}{\sqrt {2}} \left[ \begin{array}{l l} \boldsymbol {u} & \boldsymbol {u} \\ \boldsymbol {v} & - \boldsymbol {v} \end{array} \right] ^ {\top}. \tag {4.143}
$$

here, the columns of  $\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left[ \begin{array}{cc}u & u\\ v & -v \end{array} \right]$  are orthonormal and represent the eigenvectors of  $s(a)$ , whereas  $\left[ \begin{array}{cc}\sigma & 0\\ 0 & -\sigma \end{array} \right]$  contains all (non-zero) eigenvalues of  $s(a)$ .

utilizing this "symmetric dilation" trick, we can translate the observation model  $m = m^{\star} + e$  into the following equivalent form

$$
\mathcal {s} (m) = \mathcal {s} (m ^ {\star}) + \mathcal {s} (e),
$$

which is in line with the symmetric observation model stated in (4.26).

verifying conditions. to invoke the general theory in section 4.2, one is required to first examine the spectral properties of  $s(m^{\star})$  and  $s(m)$ , as well as the assumptions on the noise part  $s(e)$ .

recall that  $m^{\star} = u^{\star}\sigma^{\star}v^{\star \top}$ , which together with the relation (4.143) reveals that: (i)  $\mathcal{s}(m^{\star})$  has rank  $2r$  and condition number  $\kappa$ ; (ii) the nonzero eigenvalues of  $\mathcal{s}(m^{\star})$  and the corresponding eigenvectors are reflected respectively in the matrices

$$
\overline {{\boldsymbol {\lambda}}} ^ {\star} := \left[ \begin{array}{c c} \boldsymbol {\sigma} ^ {\star} & \boldsymbol {0} \\ \boldsymbol {0} & - \boldsymbol {\sigma} ^ {\star} \end{array} \right] \quad \text {a n d} \quad \overline {{\boldsymbol {u}}} ^ {\star} := \frac {1}{\sqrt {2}} \left[ \begin{array}{c c} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} & \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \\ \boldsymbol {v} ^ {\star} & - \boldsymbol {v} ^ {\star} \end{array} \right] \tag {4.144}
$$

similarly, given that the svd of  $m$  is  $m = u\sigma v^{\top} + u_{\perp}\sigma_{\perp}v_{\perp}^{\top}$ , we see that the 2r-leading eigenvalues of  $s(m)$  and the corresponding eigenvectors are represented respectively by the matrices

$$
\overline {{\boldsymbol {\lambda}}} := \left[ \begin{array}{c c} \boldsymbol {\sigma} & \boldsymbol {0} \\ \boldsymbol {0} & - \boldsymbol {\sigma} \end{array} \right] \quad \text {a n d} \quad \overline {{\boldsymbol {u}}} := \frac {1}{\sqrt {2}} \left[ \begin{array}{c c} \boldsymbol {u} & \boldsymbol {u} \\ \boldsymbol {v} & - \boldsymbol {v} \end{array} \right].
$$

further, the incoherence parameter  $\overline{\mu}$  of  $s(m^{\star})$  (cf. (4.24)) obeys

$$
\begin{array}{l} \overline {{\mu}} := \frac {(n _ {1} + n _ {2})}{2 r} \| \overline {{\boldsymbol {u}}} ^ {\star} \| _ {2, \infty} ^ {2} \stackrel {\mathrm {(i)}} {=} \frac {(n _ {1} + n _ {2})}{2 r} \max \left\{\| \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \| _ {2, \infty} ^ {2}, \| \boldsymbol {v} ^ {\star} \| _ {2, \infty} ^ {2} \right\} \\ \stackrel {(\mathrm {i i})} {\leq} \frac {\left(n _ {1} + n _ {2}\right)}{\min  \left\{n _ {1} , n _ {2} \right\}} \frac {\mu}{2} \stackrel {(\mathrm {i i i})} {=} \frac {\left(n _ {1} + n _ {2}\right)}{2 n _ {1}} \mu . \tag {4.145} \\ \end{array}
$$

here, the relation (i) is based on the definition (4.144), the inequality (ii) follows from the incoherence of  $m^{\star}$  (cf. definition 3.1), while the last one (iii) holds under the assumption  $n_1 \leq n_2$ .

when it comes to the "symmetrized" noise part, it is straightforward to verify that under assumption 4.2, the matrix  $s(\pmb{e})$  satisfies assumption 4.1 with precisely the quantities  $\sigma, b$  and  $c_{\mathrm{b}}$ .

$\ell_{2,\infty}$  and  $\ell_{\infty}$  guarantees. with the above preparations in place, apply theorem 4.2 (more specifically (4.118)) to demonstrate that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \| \overline {{\boldsymbol {u}}} \overline {{\boldsymbol {u}}} ^ {\top} \overline {{\boldsymbol {u}}} ^ {\star} - \overline {{\boldsymbol {u}}} ^ {\star} \| _ {2, \infty} \lesssim \frac {\sigma \kappa \sqrt {\mu r} + \sigma \sqrt {r \log n}}{\sigma_ {r} ^ {\star}} \\ \lesssim \frac {\sigma \kappa \sqrt {n _ {2} \mu r / n _ {1}} + \sigma \sqrt {r \log n}}{\sigma_ {r} ^ {\star}}, \tag {4.146} \\ \end{array}
$$

where the last relation follows from (4.145). further, note that

$$
\overline {{u}} \overline {{u}} ^ {\top} \overline {{u}} ^ {\star} - \overline {{u}} ^ {\star} = \frac {1}{\sqrt {2}} \left[ \begin{array}{c c} u h _ {u} - u ^ {\star}, & u h _ {u} - u ^ {\star} \\ v h _ {v} - v ^ {\star}, & - (v h _ {v} - v ^ {\star}) \end{array} \right],
$$

where  $\pmb{h}_{u} \coloneqq \pmb{u}^{\top}\pmb{u}^{\star}$  and  $\pmb{h}_{v} \coloneqq \pmb{v}^{\top}\pmb{v}^{\star}$ . combining this with the upper bound (4.146) then yields

$$
\max \left\{\| \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {h} _ {u} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \| _ {2, \infty}, \| \boldsymbol {v} \boldsymbol {h} _ {v} - \boldsymbol {v} ^ {\star} \| _ {2, \infty} \right\} \lesssim \frac {\sigma \kappa \sqrt {n _ {2} \mu r / n _ {1}} + \sigma \sqrt {r \log n}}{\sigma_ {r} ^ {\star}}.
$$

we can then repeat the same analysis as in the proof of lemma 4.17 to connect  $\| u h_{u} - u^{\star}\|_{2,\infty}$  (resp.  $\| v h_{v} - v^{\star}\|_{2,\infty}$ ) with  $\| u\mathrm{sgn}(h_u) - u^{\star}\|_{2,\infty}$  (resp.  $\| v\mathrm{sgn}(h_v) - v^{\star}\|_{2,\infty}$ ), and obtain the desired bound in (4.41); the details are omitted here for the sake of conciseness.

we now proceed to the second claim (4.42). towards this, invoke corollary 4.3 and the inequality (4.145) to obtain

$$
\left\| \overline {{\boldsymbol {u}}} \overline {{\boldsymbol {\lambda}}} \overline {{\boldsymbol {u}}} ^ {\top} - \mathcal {s} (\boldsymbol {m} ^ {\star}) \right\| _ {\infty} \lesssim \sigma \kappa^ {2} \overline {{\mu}} r \sqrt {\frac {\log n}{n}} \lesssim \sigma \kappa^ {2} \mu r \sqrt {\frac {n \log n}{n _ {1} ^ {2}}}.
$$

this in conjunction with the following observations

$$
\bar {u} \bar {\lambda} \bar {u} ^ {\top} = s (u \sigma v ^ {\top})
$$

$$
\left\| \mathcal {s} \left(\boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {\sigma} \boldsymbol {v} ^ {\top}\right) - \mathcal {s} \left(\boldsymbol {m} ^ {\star}\right) \right\| _ {\infty} = \left\| \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {\sigma} \boldsymbol {v} ^ {\top} - \boldsymbol {m} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {\infty}
$$

immediately establishes the second claim.

# 4.11 appendix d: proof of theorem 4.10

as before (see (4.85)), we assume throughout the proof that  $\lambda_r^\star > 0$  for the purpose of simplifying notation.

# 4.11.1 proof outline

we now outline the proof of our distributional guarantees in theorem 4.10. the first step consists of justifying the heuristic first-order approximation in (4.65). this is stated in the lemma below, with the proof deferred to section 4.11.2.

lemma 4.18 (first-order approximation). suppose that the assumptions of theorem 4.2 hold. then with probability at least  $1 - o(n^{-5})$ , one can write

$$
\mathbf {u} \operatorname {s g n} (\mathbf {h}) - \mathbf {u} ^ {\star} = \underbrace {\mathbf {e} \mathbf {u} ^ {\star} \left(\boldsymbol {\lambda} ^ {\star}\right) ^ {- 1}} _ {=: z} + \boldsymbol {\psi}, \tag {4.147a}
$$

$$
m - m ^ {\star} = \underbrace {e u ^ {\star} u ^ {\star^ {\top}} + u ^ {\star} u ^ {\star^ {\top}} e} _ {=: w} + \phi \tag {4.147b}
$$

for some matrices  $\psi$  and  $\phi$  obeying

$$
\| \psi \| _ {2, \infty} \lesssim \frac {\sigma^ {2} \kappa \sqrt {\mu r n \log^ {2} n}}{(\lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}) ^ {2}} + \frac {\kappa \sigma^ {2} \sqrt {\mu r n}}{(\lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}) ^ {2}} + \frac {\sigma}{\lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}} \sqrt {\frac {\mu r ^ {2} \log n}{n}}, \tag {4.148a}
$$

$$
\left\| \boldsymbol {\phi} \right\| _ {\infty} \lesssim \frac {\sigma^ {2} \mu r \kappa^ {2} \log n}{\lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}} + \frac {\sigma \kappa \mu \sqrt {r ^ {3} \log n}}{n}. \tag {4.148b}
$$

remark 4.8. in addition to quantifying the goodness of the approximation (4.147b), lemma 4.18 also delivers a more refined characterization for the first-order approximation  $u \operatorname{sgn}(h) - u^{\star} \approx eu^{\star}(\lambda^{\star})^{-1}$  in comparison to theorem 4.2. as it turns out, this result (4.147a) also assists in performing statistical inference on the low-rank factors  $u^{\star}$ . the interested reader is referred to yan et al. (2021) for details.

in turn, lemma 4.18 motivates one to pin down the distribution of the matrix  $\mathbf{w}$  in (4.147b). this can be accomplished by invoking the berry-esseen theorem (e.g., chen et al. (2010, theorem 3.7)), which gives rise to the following distributional characterization. the proof of this lemma can be found in section 4.11.3.

lemma 4.19 (gaussian approximation). suppose that the assumptions of theorem 4.2 hold, and that

$$
\frac {\left\| \boldsymbol {u} _ {j , \cdot} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} + \left\| \boldsymbol {u} _ {i , \cdot} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2}}{\left\| \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {\mathrm {f}} ^ {2}} \gtrsim \frac {b ^ {2} \kappa^ {2} \mu^ {2} r ^ {2} \log^ {2} n}{\sigma_ {\min } ^ {2} n ^ {2}} + \frac {\sigma^ {4} \mu^ {2} r \kappa^ {4} \log^ {3} n}{\sigma_ {\min } ^ {2} \left(\lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}\right) ^ {2}}. \tag {4.149}
$$

let  $\pmb{w} = \pmb{e}\pmb{u}^{\star}\pmb{u}^{\star\top} + \pmb{u}^{\star}\pmb{u}^{\star\top}\pmb{e}$ . for any  $1 \leq i, j \leq n$ , one has

$$
\sup  _ {z \in \mathbb {r}} \left| \mathbb {p} \left(w _ {i, j} \leq z \sqrt {v _ {i , j} ^ {\star}}\right) - \phi (z) \right| = o (1), \tag {4.150a}
$$

$$
\left\| \boldsymbol {\phi} \right\| _ {\infty} = o \left(\sqrt {v _ {i , j} ^ {\star}}\right), \tag {4.150b}
$$

where  $v_{i,j}^{\star}$  is defined in (4.68), and  $\phi(\cdot)$  denotes the cdf of the standard gaussian distribution.

to finish up, invoke lemma 4.18 and (4.150b) to yield

$$
m _ {i, j} - m _ {i, j} ^ {\star} = w _ {i, j} + \delta_ {\phi} \sqrt {v _ {i , j} ^ {\star}} \qquad \mathrm {w i t h} \delta_ {\phi} = o (1).
$$

a little algebra further gives

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left| \mathbb {p} \left(m _ {i, j} - m _ {i, j} ^ {\star} \leq z \sqrt {v _ {i , j} ^ {\star}}\right) - \phi (z) \right| = \left| \mathbb {p} \left(w _ {i, j} \leq (z - \delta_ {\phi}) \sqrt {v _ {i , j} ^ {\star}}\right) - \phi (z) \right| \\ \leq \left| \mathbb {p} \left(w _ {i, j} \leq (z - \delta_ {\phi}) \sqrt {v _ {i , j} ^ {\star}}\right) - \phi (z - \delta_ {\phi}) \right| + | \phi (z - \delta_ {\phi}) - \phi (z) | \\ \leq o (1) + \delta_ {\phi} = o (1), \\ \end{array}
$$

where the last line invokes lemma 4.19 and the fact that  $|\phi(u) - \phi(v)| \leq |u - v|$  for any  $u, v \in \mathbb{r}$ . this completes the proof of theorem 4.10, as long as lemmas 4.18 and 4.19 can be established. the rest of this section is thus devoted to proving lemmas 4.18 and 4.19.

# 4.11.2 proof of lemma 4.18

before proceeding to the proof, we make note of several preliminary facts that are all direct consequences of the analysis of theorem 4.2 and corollary 4.3. the proof of these preliminary results can be found in section 4.11.4.

lemma 4.20. with probability exceeding  $1 - o(n^{-5})$ , one can write

$$
\boldsymbol {u} \lambda \operatorname {s g n} (\boldsymbol {h}) = \boldsymbol {m} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} + \delta_ {1}, \tag {4.151a}
$$

$$
\operatorname {s g n} (\boldsymbol {h}) \boldsymbol {\lambda} ^ {\star} \operatorname {s g n} (\boldsymbol {h}) ^ {\top} = \boldsymbol {\lambda} + \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {2} \tag {4.151b}
$$

for some matrices  $\pmb{\delta}_{1}$  and  $\pmb{\delta}_{2}$  obeying

$$
\left\| \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {1} \right\| _ {2, \infty} \lesssim \frac {\sigma^ {2} \kappa \sqrt {\mu r n \log^ {2} n}}{\lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}}, \tag {4.152a}
$$

$$
\left\| \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {2} \right\| \lesssim \frac {\kappa \sigma^ {2} n}{\lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}} + \sigma \sqrt {r \log n}. \tag {4.152b}
$$

we are now ready to embark on the proof of lemma 4.18. in order to analyze the behavior of  $u \operatorname{sgn}(\mathbf{h})$ , we first point out the following decomposition:

$$
\begin{array}{l} \boldsymbol {u} \operatorname {s g n} (\boldsymbol {h}) \boldsymbol {\lambda} ^ {\star} = \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {\lambda} \operatorname {s g n} (\boldsymbol {h}) + \boldsymbol {u} (\operatorname {s g n} (\boldsymbol {h}) \boldsymbol {\lambda} ^ {\star} - \boldsymbol {\lambda} \operatorname {s g n} (\boldsymbol {h})) \\ = m u ^ {\star} + \delta_ {1} + u (\operatorname {s g n} (h) \lambda^ {\star} \operatorname {s g n} (h) ^ {\top} - \lambda) \operatorname {s g n} (h) \\ = \boldsymbol {m} ^ {\star} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} + \boldsymbol {e} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} + \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {1} + \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {2} \operatorname {s g n} (\boldsymbol {h}) \\ = \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \boldsymbol {\lambda} ^ {\star} + \boldsymbol {e} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} + \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {1} + \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {2} \operatorname {s g n} (\boldsymbol {h}), \\ \end{array}
$$

where the second and the third identities result from lemma 4.20 (cf. (4.151)). the key point of this decomposition is to establish a connection between  $\mathbf{u} \mathrm{sgn}(\mathbf{h}) \mathbf{\lambda}^{\star}$  and  $\mathbf{u}^{\star} \mathbf{\lambda}^{\star} + \mathbf{e} \mathbf{u}^{\star}$ , with the assistance of the matrices  $\delta_{1}$  and  $\delta_{2}$  studied in lemma 4.20. a little algebra then yields

$$
\boldsymbol {u} \operatorname {s g n} (\boldsymbol {h}) - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} = \underbrace {\boldsymbol {e} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \left(\boldsymbol {\lambda} ^ {\star}\right) ^ {- 1}} _ {=: z} + \underbrace {\boldsymbol {\delta} _ {1} \left(\boldsymbol {\lambda} ^ {\star}\right) ^ {- 1} + \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {2} \operatorname {s g n} (\boldsymbol {h}) \left(\boldsymbol {\lambda} ^ {\star}\right) ^ {- 1}} _ {=: \psi}. \tag {4.153}
$$

in view of lemma 4.20, the residual matrix  $\psi$  obeys

$$
\begin{array}{l} \| \psi \| _ {2, \infty} \leq \| \delta_ {1} \| _ {2, \infty} \| (\lambda^ {\star}) ^ {- 1} \| + \| u \| _ {2, \infty} \| \delta_ {2} \| \| \operatorname {s g n} (h) \| \| (\lambda^ {\star}) ^ {- 1} \| \\ \lesssim \frac {1}{\lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}} \| \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {1} \| _ {2, \infty} + \frac {1}{\lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}} \sqrt {\frac {\mu r}{n}} \| \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {2} \| \\ \lesssim \frac {\sigma^ {2} \kappa \sqrt {\mu r n \log^ {2} n}}{(\lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}) ^ {2}} + \frac {\kappa \sigma^ {2} \sqrt {\mu r n}}{(\lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}) ^ {2}} + \frac {\sigma}{\lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}} \sqrt {\frac {\mu r ^ {2} \log n}{n}} \tag {4.154} \\ \end{array}
$$

as claimed.

the next step lies in analyzing the matrix estimator  $m = u\lambda u^{\top}$ . towards this, we make the observation that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \boldsymbol {m} - \boldsymbol {m} ^ {\star} = \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {\lambda} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\top} - \boldsymbol {m} ^ {\star} \\ = u \left(\operatorname {s g n} (h) \lambda^ {\star} \operatorname {s g n} (h) ^ {\top}\right) u ^ {\top} - u \delta_ {2} u ^ {\top} - m ^ {\star} \\ = \left(\boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} + \boldsymbol {z} + \psi\right) \boldsymbol {\lambda} ^ {\star} \left(\boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} + \boldsymbol {z} + \psi\right) ^ {\top} - \boldsymbol {u} \delta_ {2} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\top} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \boldsymbol {\lambda} ^ {\star} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star^ {\top}} \\ = z \boldsymbol {\lambda} ^ {\star} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star^ {\top}} + \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \boldsymbol {\lambda} ^ {\star} \boldsymbol {z} ^ {\top} + \boldsymbol {\phi} \\ = e u ^ {\star} u ^ {\star \top} + u ^ {\star} u ^ {\star \top} e + \phi , \tag {4.155} \\ \end{array}
$$

where the second line relies on lemma 4.20 (cf. (4.151b)), the third identity makes use of (4.153), and the residual matrix  $\phi$  is defined as

$$
\phi := \psi \lambda^ {\star} (u \operatorname {s g n} (h)) ^ {\top} + u ^ {\star} \lambda^ {\star} \psi^ {\top} + z \lambda^ {\star} (u \operatorname {s g n} (h) - u ^ {\star}) ^ {\top} - u \delta_ {2} u ^ {\top}. \tag {4.156}
$$

in addition, it is seen from (4.92) that

$$
\| \boldsymbol {z} \| _ {2, \infty} \leq \left\| \boldsymbol {e u} ^ {\star} \left(\boldsymbol {\lambda} ^ {\star}\right) ^ {- 1} \right\| _ {2, \infty} \leq \frac {1}{\lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}} \| \boldsymbol {e u} ^ {\star} \| _ {2, \infty} \lesssim \frac {\sigma \sqrt {r \log n}}{\lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}}. \tag {4.157}
$$

consequently, one can deduce that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \boldsymbol {\phi} \right\| _ {\infty} \leq \left\| \boldsymbol {\lambda} ^ {\star} \right\| \left\{\| \boldsymbol {z} \| _ {2, \infty} ^ {2} + \| \boldsymbol {z} \| _ {2, \infty} \| \boldsymbol {u} \operatorname {s g n} (\boldsymbol {h}) - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \| _ {2, \infty} \right\} \\ + \left\| \boldsymbol {\lambda} ^ {\star} \right\| \left\| \boldsymbol {\psi} \right\| _ {2, \infty} \left(\left\| \boldsymbol {u} \right\| _ {2, \infty} + \left\| \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {2, \infty}\right) + \left\| \boldsymbol {u} \right\| _ {2, \infty} ^ {2} \| \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {2} \| \\ \lesssim \left| \lambda_ {1} ^ {\star} \right| \left\{\frac {\sigma^ {2} r \log n}{(\lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}) ^ {2}} + \frac {\sigma \sqrt {r \log n}}{\lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}} \cdot \frac {\sigma \kappa \sqrt {\mu r \log n}}{\lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}} \right\} \\ + \left| \lambda_ {1} ^ {\star} \right| \sqrt {\frac {\mu r}{n}} \left\{\frac {\sigma^ {2} \kappa \sqrt {\mu r n \log^ {2} n}}{(\lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}) ^ {2}} + \frac {\kappa \sigma^ {2} \sqrt {\mu r n}}{(\lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}) ^ {2}} + \frac {\sigma}{\lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}} \sqrt {\frac {\mu r ^ {2} \log n}{n}} \right\} \\ + \frac {\mu r}{n} \left\{\frac {\kappa \sigma^ {2} n}{\lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}} + \sigma \sqrt {r \log n} \right\} \\ \lesssim \frac {\sigma^ {2} \mu r \kappa^ {2} \log n}{\lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}} + \frac {\sigma \kappa \mu \sqrt {r ^ {3} \log n}}{n}, \\ \end{array}
$$

where the second inequality follows from (4.154), (4.157), (4.133), theorem 4.2, and lemma 4.20.

# 4.11.3 proof of lemma 4.19

in what follows, we shall only focus on the case with  $i \neq j$ . the case with  $i = j$  can be analyzed in an analogous manner; we omit it for the sake of brevity. before proceeding to the proof, we make

note of a couple of basic facts about  $p^{\star}$  that will prove useful. the first property asserts that, for any  $1 \leq j \leq n$ ,

$$
\begin{array}{l} \sum_ {l = 1} ^ {n} p _ {j, l} ^ {\star 2} = \sum_ {l = 1} ^ {n} p _ {l, j} ^ {\star 2} = \left\| \left(\boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star \top}\right) _ {\cdot , j} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} = \left\| \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \left(\boldsymbol {u} _ {j, \cdot} ^ {\star}\right) ^ {\top} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} = \boldsymbol {u} _ {j, \cdot} ^ {\star} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star \top} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \left(\boldsymbol {u} _ {j, \cdot} ^ {\star}\right) ^ {\top} \\ = \left\| u _ {j, \cdot} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} = p _ {j, j} ^ {\star}. \tag {4.158} \\ \end{array}
$$

the second property is concerned with the term  $\| p^{\star}\|_{\infty}$

$$
\| \boldsymbol {p} ^ {\star} \| _ {\infty} = \| \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star^ {\top}} \| _ {\infty} \leq \| \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \| _ {2, \infty} ^ {2} \leq \frac {\mu r}{n}, \tag {4.159}
$$

where the last inequality follows from the incoherence assumption.

in view of the definition (4.66) of  $p^{\star}$ , we can express  $w = ep^{\star} + p^{\star}e$ , which reveals that

$$
w _ {i, j} = \sum_ {l: l \neq j} e _ {i, l} p _ {l, j} ^ {\star} + \sum_ {l: l \neq i} p _ {i, l} ^ {\star} e _ {l, j} + e _ {i, j} \left(p _ {i, i} ^ {\star} + p _ {j, j} ^ {\star}\right). \tag {4.160}
$$

in other words,  $w_{i,j}$  can be viewed as a weighted sum of independent random variables  $\{e_{i,l} \mid l \neq j\} \cup \{e_{l,j} \mid l \neq i\} \cup \{e_{i,j}\}$ . to pin down the distribution of  $w_{i,j}$ , we resort to a non-asymptotic version of the celebrated berry-esseen theorem; see chen et al. (2010, theorem 3.7) for a proof using stein's method.

theorem 4.21 (the berry-esseen bound). let  $\xi_1, \ldots, \xi_n$  be independent zero-mean random variables satisfying  $\sum_{i=1}^{n} \mathsf{var}(\xi_i) = v$ . then the quantity  $s = \frac{1}{\sqrt{v}} \sum_{i=1}^{n} \xi_i$  satisfies

$$
\sup  _ {z \in \mathbb {r}} | \mathbb {p} (s \leq z) - \phi (z) | \leq 1 0 \gamma , \quad \text {w h e r e} \gamma = \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \frac {\mathbb {e} [ | \xi_ {i} | ^ {3} ]}{v ^ {3 / 2}}.
$$

according to the berry-esseen bound (cf. theorem 4.21), proving the approximate gaussianity of  $w_{i,j}$  boils down to characterizing the second and the third moments of these random variables under consideration.

let us start with the variance statistics. given that  $\{e_{i,j} \mid i \geq j\}$  are independently generated, we can straightforwardly see that

$$
\begin{array}{l} v _ {i, j} ^ {\star} = \sum_ {l: l \neq j} \sigma_ {i, l} ^ {2} p _ {l, j} ^ {\star 2} + \sum_ {l: l \neq i} p _ {i, l} ^ {\star 2} \sigma_ {l, j} ^ {2} + \sigma_ {i, j} ^ {2} (p _ {i, i} ^ {\star} + p _ {j, j} ^ {\star}) ^ {2} \\ = \operatorname {v a r} \left(w _ {i, j}\right). \tag {4.161} \\ \end{array}
$$

we now develop a lower bound on this variance term. given that  $\pmb{p}^{\star} \succeq \mathbf{0}$ , one has  $p_{i,i}^{\star}, p_{j,j}^{\star} \geq 0$  which combined with (4.158) reveals that

$$
v _ {i, j} ^ {\star} \geq \sigma_ {\min } ^ {2} \left\{\sum_ {l = 1} ^ {n} p _ {l, j} ^ {\star 2} + \sum_ {l = 1} ^ {n} p _ {i, l} ^ {\star 2} \right\} = \sigma_ {\min } ^ {2} \left\{\| u _ {j, \cdot} ^ {\star} \| _ {2} ^ {2} + \| u _ {i, \cdot} ^ {\star} \| _ {2} ^ {2} \right\}. \tag {4.162}
$$

next, we move on to bound the third moments. utilizing the independence of  $\{e_{i,j} \mid i \geq j\}$  once

again gives

$$
\begin{array}{l} \gamma := \frac {\sum_ {l : l \neq j} \mathbb {e} \big [ | e _ {i , l} | ^ {3} \big ] | p _ {l , j} ^ {\star} | ^ {3} + \sum_ {l : l \neq i} | p _ {i , l} | ^ {3} \mathbb {e} \big [ | e _ {l , j} | ^ {3} \big ] + \mathbb {e} \big [ | e _ {i , j} | ^ {3} \big ] | p _ {i , i} ^ {\star} + p _ {j , j} ^ {\star} | ^ {3}}{\left(v _ {i , j} ^ {\star}\right) ^ {3 / 2}} \\ \leq \frac {2 b \| \boldsymbol {p} ^ {\star} \| _ {\infty}}{\left(v _ {i , j} ^ {\star}\right) ^ {3 / 2}} \left\{\sum_ {l: l \neq j} \mathbb {e} \left[ e _ {i, l} ^ {2} \right] p _ {l, j} ^ {\star 2} + \sum_ {l: l \neq i} p _ {i, l} ^ {\star 2} \mathbb {e} \left[ e _ {l, j} ^ {2} \right] + \mathbb {e} \left[ e _ {i, j} ^ {2} \right] \left| p _ {i, i} ^ {\star} + p _ {j, j} ^ {\star} \right| ^ {2} \right\} \\ = \frac {2 b \| \boldsymbol {p} ^ {\star} \| _ {\infty}}{\left(v _ {i , j} ^ {\star}\right) ^ {3 / 2}} \cdot v _ {i, j} ^ {\star} = \frac {2 b \| \boldsymbol {p} ^ {\star} \| _ {\infty}}{\left(v _ {i , j} ^ {\star}\right) ^ {1 / 2}}, \tag {4.163} \\ \end{array}
$$

where we have used the assumption that  $|e_{i,j}| \leq b$ , and the last line arises from the expression (4.161). substituting (4.159) and (4.162) into (4.163) and invoking the elementary identity  $\| \pmb{u}^{\star} \|_{\mathrm{f}}^{2} = r$ , we arrive at

$$
\gamma \leq \frac {2 b \mu r}{n \sigma_ {\mathrm {m i n}} \sqrt {\| \boldsymbol {u} _ {j , \cdot} ^ {\star} \| _ {2} ^ {2} + \| \boldsymbol {u} _ {i , \cdot} ^ {\star} \| _ {2} ^ {2}}} = \frac {2 b \mu \sqrt {r} \| \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \| _ {\mathrm {f}}}{n \sigma_ {\mathrm {m i n}} \sqrt {\| \boldsymbol {u} _ {j , \cdot} ^ {\star} \| _ {2} ^ {2} + \| \boldsymbol {u} _ {i , \cdot} ^ {\star} \| _ {2} ^ {2}}} = o (1),
$$

provided that condition (4.149) holds.

with the above calculations in place, invoking theorem 4.21 immediately leads to

$$
\sup  _ {z \in \mathbb {r}} \left| \mathbb {p} \left(w _ {i, j} \leq \sqrt {v _ {i , j} ^ {\star}} z\right) - \phi (z) \right| \leq 1 0 \gamma = o (1).
$$

as claimed in (4.150a).

finally, we turn to proving the bound (4.150b). by virtue of lemma 4.18 and (4.162), we know that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \| \phi \| _ {\infty} \lesssim \frac {\sigma^ {2} \mu r \kappa^ {2} \log n}{\lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}} + \frac {\sigma \kappa \mu \sqrt {r ^ {3} \log n}}{n} \\ = o \left(\sigma_ {\min} \sqrt {\| \boldsymbol {u} _ {j , \cdot} ^ {\star} \| _ {2} ^ {2} + \| \boldsymbol {u} _ {i , \cdot} ^ {\star} \| _ {2} ^ {2}}\right) \leq o (\sqrt {v _ {i , j} ^ {\star}}), \\ \end{array}
$$

with the proviso that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \sqrt {\left\| \boldsymbol {u} _ {j , \cdot} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} + \left\| \boldsymbol {u} _ {i , \cdot} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2}} \gtrsim \frac {\sigma^ {2} \mu r \kappa^ {2} \log^ {3 / 2} n}{\sigma_ {\min} \lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}} + \frac {\sigma \kappa \mu \sqrt {r ^ {3}} \log n}{\sigma_ {\min} n} \\ = \left(\frac {\sigma^ {2} \mu \sqrt {r} \kappa^ {2} \log^ {3 / 2} n}{\sigma_ {\operatorname* {m i n}} \lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}} + \frac {\sigma \kappa \mu r \log n}{\sigma_ {\operatorname* {m i n}} n}\right) \| u ^ {\star} \| _ {\mathrm {f}}. \tag {4.164} \\ \end{array}
$$

recognizing the trivial bound  $\sigma^2 = \max_{i,j} \mathbb{e}[e_{i,j}^2] \leq b^2$ , we know that condition (4.164) holds as long as

$$
\frac {\left\| \boldsymbol {u} _ {j , \cdot} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} + \left\| \boldsymbol {u} _ {i , \cdot} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2}}{\left\| \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {\mathrm {f}} ^ {2}} \gtrsim \frac {\sigma^ {4} \mu^ {2} r \kappa^ {4} \log^ {3} n}{\sigma_ {\min } ^ {2} \left(\lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}\right) ^ {2}} + \frac {b ^ {2} \kappa^ {2} \mu^ {2} r ^ {2} \log^ {2} n}{\sigma_ {\min } ^ {2} n ^ {2}}, \tag {4.165}
$$

which is precisely condition (4.149). this concludes the proof of lemma 4.19.

# 4.11.4 proof of lemma 4.20

to begin with, let us begin by proving (4.152a). from the definition of the quantity  $\mathcal{e}_1$  (see lemma 4.16), we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \boldsymbol {m} (\boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {h} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star}) \right\| _ {2, \infty} = \lambda_ {r} ^ {\star} \mathcal {e} _ {1} / 2 \\ \lesssim \left(\alpha_ {0} + \alpha_ {1} + \alpha_ {2}\right) + \left(\sigma \sqrt {n} + b \log n\right) \| \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {h} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \| _ {2, \infty} \\ \lesssim \left(\alpha_ {0} + \alpha_ {1} + \alpha_ {2}\right) + \left(\sigma \sqrt {n} + b \log n\right) \frac {\sigma \kappa \sqrt {\mu r \log n}}{\lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}} \\ \succ \frac {\sigma^ {2} \kappa \sqrt {\mu r n \log^ {2} n}}{\lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}}, \\ \end{array}
$$

where the first inequality comes from (4.116) and (4.117), the second inequality is a consequence of theorem 4.2, and the last line relies on our previous bounds on  $\alpha_0, \alpha_1, \alpha_2$  (see (4.112), (4.113) and (4.115)) and holds as long as  $b \lesssim \sigma \sqrt{n / (\mu \log n)}$ . additionally, from the elementary identity  $\mathbf{muh} = \mathbf{u}\mathbf{\lambda}\mathbf{h}$ , we obtain

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| u \lambda \operatorname {s g n} (h) - m u h \right\| _ {2, \infty} = \left\| u \lambda \operatorname {s g n} (h) - u \lambda h \right\| _ {2, \infty} \\ \leq \left\| \boldsymbol {u} \right\| _ {2, \infty} \left\| \boldsymbol {\lambda} \right\| \left\| \operatorname {s g n} (\boldsymbol {h}) - \boldsymbol {h} \right\| \\ \lesssim \sqrt {\frac {\mu r}{n}} | \lambda_ {1} ^ {\star} | \cdot \frac {\sigma^ {2} n}{(\lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}) ^ {2}} = \frac {\kappa \sigma^ {2} \sqrt {\mu r n}}{\lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}}, \\ \end{array}
$$

where the last line results from lemma 4.15, the fact (4.133), and the following inequality

$$
\left\| \boldsymbol {\lambda} \right\| \leq \left\| \boldsymbol {\lambda} ^ {\star} \right\| + \left\| \boldsymbol {e} \right\| \leq | \lambda_ {1} ^ {\star} | + o (\sigma \sqrt {n}) \leq 2 | \lambda_ {1} ^ {\star} |. \tag {4.166}
$$

taking together the above bounds and applying the triangle inequality immediately establish (4.152a). next, we turn to the proof of the bound (4.152b). note that it has been shown in (4.139) that

$$
\left\| \boldsymbol {h} \boldsymbol {\lambda} ^ {\star} \boldsymbol {h} ^ {\top} - \boldsymbol {\lambda} \right\| \lesssim \frac {\kappa \sigma^ {2} n}{\lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}} + \sigma \sqrt {r \log n}.
$$

in addition, the triangle inequality leads to

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \operatorname {s g n} (\boldsymbol {h}) \boldsymbol {\lambda} ^ {\star} \operatorname {s g n} (\boldsymbol {h}) ^ {\top} - \boldsymbol {h} \boldsymbol {\lambda} ^ {\star} \boldsymbol {h} ^ {\top} \right\| \\ \leq \left\| \operatorname {s g n} (\boldsymbol {h}) \boldsymbol {\lambda} ^ {\star} \left(\operatorname {s g n} (\boldsymbol {h}) - \boldsymbol {h}\right) ^ {\top} \right\| + \left\| \left(\operatorname {s g n} (\boldsymbol {h}) - \boldsymbol {h}\right) \boldsymbol {\lambda} ^ {\star} \boldsymbol {h} ^ {\top} \right\| \\ \leq \left(\| \operatorname {s g n} (\boldsymbol {h}) \| + \| \boldsymbol {h} \|\right) \| \boldsymbol {\lambda} ^ {\star} \| \| \operatorname {s g n} (\boldsymbol {h}) - \boldsymbol {h} \| \\ \leq 2 \left| \lambda_ {1} ^ {\star} \right| \| \operatorname {s g n} (\boldsymbol {h}) - \boldsymbol {h} \| \\ \lesssim | \lambda_ {1} ^ {\star} | \frac {\sigma^ {2} n}{(\lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}) ^ {2}} = \frac {\kappa \sigma^ {2} n}{\lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}}, \\ \end{array}
$$

where the third line follows since  $\| \mathsf{sgn}(\pmb {h})\| = 1$  and  $\| h\| \leq \| u\| \| u^{\star}\| = 1$ , and the last inequality comes from (4.123). combining the above two results and invoking the triangle inequality lead to the advertised bound (4.152b).

# 4.12 appendix e: proof of theorem 4.11

with the distributional guarantees in theorem 4.10 in place, the only remaining task boils down to verifying the statistical accuracy of the variance estimator  $\widehat{v}_{i,j}$ . this can be achieved via the following lemma, whose proof is provided in section 4.12.1.

lemma 4.22. suppose that the assumptions of theorem 4.2 hold. in addition, assume that  $\kappa^4\mu^2 r^2\log n\leq n$ ,  $\sigma \sqrt{n}\lesssim |\lambda_r^\star | / \kappa$  and

$$
\left\| \boldsymbol {u} _ {j, \cdot} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} + \left\| \boldsymbol {u} _ {i, \cdot} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} \gtrsim \frac {b \sigma \kappa^ {2} \mu^ {2} r ^ {2} \log n}{\sigma_ {\min } ^ {2} n ^ {3 / 2}} + \frac {\sigma^ {3} \kappa \mu^ {2} r ^ {2} \log n}{\sigma_ {\min } ^ {2} | \lambda_ {r} ^ {\star} | \sqrt {n}}. \tag {4.167}
$$

with probability exceeding  $1 - o(n^{-5})$ , one has

$$
\left| \widehat {v} _ {i, j} - v _ {i, j} ^ {\star} \right| = o \left(v _ {i, j} ^ {\star}\right). \tag {4.168}
$$

lemma 4.22 essentially enables us to express

$$
\widehat {v} _ {i, j} = \left(1 + \zeta_ {v}\right) ^ {2} v _ {i, j} ^ {\star} \quad \text {w i t h} \zeta_ {v} = o (1).
$$

as a consequence, we can further demonstrate that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left| \mathbb {p} \left(m _ {i, j} ^ {\star} \in \mathrm {c l} _ {i, j} ^ {1 - \alpha}\right) - (1 - \alpha) \right| = \left| \mathbb {p} \left(\left| \widehat {m} _ {i, j} - m _ {i, j} ^ {\star} \right| \leq z _ {\alpha / 2} \sqrt {\widehat {v} _ {i , j}}\right) - (1 - \alpha) \right| \\ = \left| \mathbb {p} \left(\left| \widehat {m} _ {i, j} - m _ {i, j} ^ {\star} \right| \leq (1 + \zeta_ {v}) z _ {\alpha / 2} \sqrt {v _ {i , j} ^ {\star}}\right) - (1 - \alpha) \right| \\ \leq \left| \phi \left((1 + \zeta_ {v}) z _ {\alpha / 2}\right) - \phi \left(- (1 + \zeta_ {v}) z _ {\alpha / 2}\right) - (1 - \alpha) \right| + o (1) \\ \leq \left| \phi \left(z _ {\alpha / 2}\right) - \phi \left(- z _ {\alpha / 2}\right) - (1 - \alpha) \right| + 2 \left| \phi \left((1 + \zeta_ {v}) z _ {\alpha / 2}\right) - \phi \left(z _ {\alpha / 2}\right) \right| + o (1) \\ \leq 2 \zeta_ {v} z _ {\alpha / 2} = o (1), \\ \end{array}
$$

where the first inequality follows from theorem 4.10 and  $z_{\alpha /2}\coloneqq \phi^{-1}(1 - \alpha /2)$ , the second inequality applies the triangle inequality, and the validity of the last line can be seen from the basic fact  $|\phi (u) - \phi (v)|\leq |u - v|$ .

when  $\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}\asymp \sigma$  , condition (4.167) simplifies to

$$
\frac {\left\| \boldsymbol {u} _ {j , \cdot} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} + \left\| \boldsymbol {u} _ {i , \cdot} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2}}{\left\| \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {\mathrm {f}} ^ {2}} \gtrsim \frac {b \kappa^ {2} \mu^ {2} r \log n}{\sigma n ^ {3 / 2}} + \frac {\sigma \kappa \mu^ {2} r \log n}{\left| \lambda_ {r} ^ {\star} \right| \sqrt {n}}. \tag {4.169}
$$

we still need to ensure that condition (4.69) is satisfied. it is seen that

$$
\frac {b ^ {2} \kappa^ {2} \mu^ {2} r ^ {2} \log^ {2} n}{\sigma^ {2} n ^ {2}} + \frac {\sigma^ {2} \mu^ {2} r \kappa^ {4} \log^ {3} n}{(\lambda_ {r} ^ {\star}) ^ {2}} \lesssim \frac {b \kappa^ {2} \mu^ {2} r ^ {2} \log^ {2} n}{\sigma n ^ {3 / 2}} + \frac {\sigma \mu^ {2} r \kappa^ {3} \log^ {3} n}{| \lambda_ {r} ^ {\star} | \sqrt {n}},
$$

which holds if  $\sigma \kappa \sqrt{n} \lesssim |\lambda_r^\star|$  and  $b \lesssim \sigma \sqrt{n}$ . as a result, if condition (4.74) holds, then both (4.169) and (4.69) are satisfied. this finishes the proof, as long as lemma 4.22 can be established.

# 4.12.1 proof for lemma 4.22

as before, we shall only present the proof for the case with  $i \neq j$  for the sake of conciseness. in order to justify the goodness of the estimator  $\widehat{v}_{i,j}$ , we find it convenient to first look at the surrogate estimator introduced in (4.70), i.e.,

$$
\widetilde {v} _ {i, j} = \sum_ {l = 1} ^ {n} e _ {i, l} ^ {2} p _ {l, j} ^ {\star 2} + \sum_ {l = 1} ^ {n} p _ {i, l} ^ {\star 2} e _ {l, j} ^ {2} + 2 e _ {i, j} ^ {2} p _ {i, l} ^ {\star} p _ {j, j} ^ {\star}, \tag {4.170}
$$

in the sequel, our proof consists of two main steps:

- show that the surrogate  $\widetilde{v}_{i,j}$  is a reliable estimate of the truth  $v_{i,j}^{\star}$ , namely,  $\widetilde{v}_{i,j} \approx v_{i,j}^{\star}$ .  
- show that the estimator in use and the surrogate estimator are sufficiently close, namely,  $\widehat{v}_{i,j} \approx \widetilde{v}_{i,j}$ .

# step 1: show that  $\widetilde{v}_{i,j} \approx v_{i,j}^{\star}$

firstly, the fact that the  $e_{i,j}$ 's are zero-mean random variables immediately reveals that  $\widetilde{v}_{i,j}$  is an unbiased estimate of  $v_{i,j}^{\star}$ , that is,

$$
\mathbb {e} \big [ \widetilde {v} _ {i, j} \big ] = v _ {i, j} ^ {\star}.
$$

secondly, given that the  $e_{i,j}$ 's are statistically independent, we intend to invoke the bernstein inequality to control the difference  $\widetilde{v}_{i,j} - v_{i,j}^{\star} = \widetilde{v}_{i,j} - \mathbb{e}\big[\widetilde{v}_{i,j}\big]$ . to do so, one first calculates that

$$
l _ {0} := \max  \left\{\max  _ {l} e _ {i, l} ^ {2} p _ {l, j} ^ {\star 2}, \max  _ {l} e _ {l, j} ^ {2} p _ {i, l} ^ {\star 2}, e _ {i, j} ^ {2} (p _ {i, i} ^ {\star} + p _ {j, j} ^ {\star}) ^ {2} \right\} \leq 4 b ^ {2} \| \boldsymbol {p} ^ {\star} \| _ {\infty} ^ {2}
$$

and

$$
\begin{array}{l} v _ {0} := \sum_ {l: l \neq j} \mathsf {v a r} (e _ {i, l} ^ {2}) p _ {l, j} ^ {\star 4} + \sum_ {l: l \neq i} \mathsf {v a r} (e _ {l, j} ^ {2}) p _ {i, l} ^ {\star 4} + \mathsf {v a r} (e _ {i, j} ^ {2}) (p _ {i, i} ^ {\star} + p _ {j, j} ^ {\star}) ^ {4} \\ \lesssim \sum_ {l = 1} ^ {n} \mathbb {e} \left[ e _ {i, l} ^ {4} \right] p _ {l, j} ^ {\star 4} + \sum_ {l = 1} ^ {n} \mathbb {e} \left[ e _ {l, j} ^ {4} \right] p _ {i, l} ^ {\star 4} + \mathbb {e} \left[ e _ {i, j} ^ {4} \right] p _ {i, i} ^ {\star 2} p _ {j, j} ^ {\star 2} \\ \lesssim b ^ {2} \| \boldsymbol {p} ^ {\star} \| _ {\infty} ^ {2} \left\{\sum_ {l = 1} ^ {n} \mathbb {e} \left[ e _ {i, l} ^ {2} \right] p _ {l, j} ^ {2} + \sum_ {l = 1} ^ {n} \mathbb {e} \left[ e _ {l, j} ^ {2} \right] p _ {i, l} ^ {\star 2} + \mathbb {e} \left[ e _ {i, j} ^ {2} \right] p _ {i, i} ^ {\star} p _ {j, j} ^ {\star} \right\} \\ \lesssim \sigma^ {2} b ^ {2} \| \boldsymbol {p} ^ {\star} \| _ {\infty} ^ {2} \left\{\sum_ {l = 1} ^ {n} p _ {l, j} ^ {\star 2} + \sum_ {l = 1} ^ {n} p _ {i, l} ^ {\star 2} \right\} \\ \lesssim \sigma^ {2} b ^ {2} \| \boldsymbol {p} ^ {\star} \| _ {\infty} ^ {3}, \\ \end{array}
$$

where the last line follows from (4.158). invoking the bernstein inequality (cf. corollary 3.3) reveals that with probability exceeding  $1 - o(n^{-5})$ ,

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left| \widetilde {v} _ {i, j} - v _ {i, j} ^ {\star} \right| = \left| \widetilde {v} _ {i, j} - \mathbb {e} \left[ \widetilde {v} _ {i, j} \right] \right| \lesssim \sqrt {v _ {0} \log n} + l _ {0} \log n \\ \lesssim \sigma b \sqrt {\| \boldsymbol {p} ^ {\star} \| _ {\infty} ^ {3} \log n} + b ^ {2} \| \boldsymbol {p} ^ {\star} \| _ {\infty} ^ {2} \log n \\ \lesssim \frac {\sigma b \mu^ {3 / 2} r ^ {3 / 2} \sqrt {\log n}}{n ^ {3 / 2}} + \frac {\mu^ {2} r ^ {2} b ^ {2} \log n}{n ^ {2}}, \tag {4.171} \\ \end{array}
$$

where the last inequality results from (4.159).

# step 2: show that  $\widehat{v}_{i,j} \approx \widetilde{v}_{i,j}$

in order to accomplish this, we are in need of controlling the difference between  $e_{i,j}$  (resp.  $p_{i,j}^{\star}$ ) and  $\widehat{e}_{i,j}$  (resp.  $\widehat{p}_{i,j}$ ). to this end, apply the entrywise estimation guarantees in corollary 4.3 to yield

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \widehat {\boldsymbol {e}} - \boldsymbol {e} \right\| _ {\infty} = \left\| (\boldsymbol {m} - \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {\lambda} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\top}) - (\boldsymbol {m} - \boldsymbol {m} ^ {\star}) \right\| _ {\infty} \\ = \left\| \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {\lambda} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\top} - \boldsymbol {m} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {\infty} \lesssim \sigma \kappa^ {2} \mu r \sqrt {\frac {\log n}{n}}, \tag {4.172} \\ \end{array}
$$

and as a result,

$$
\left\| \widehat {\boldsymbol {e}} \right\| _ {\infty} \leq \left\| \boldsymbol {e} \right\| _ {\infty} + \left\| \widehat {\boldsymbol {e}} - \boldsymbol {e} \right\| _ {\infty} \lesssim b + \sigma \kappa^ {2} \mu r \sqrt {\frac {\log n}{n}} \asymp b, \tag {4.173}
$$

provided that  $b \gtrsim \sigma \kappa^2 \mu r \sqrt{\frac{\log n}{n}}$  (which is trivially satisfied if  $\kappa^2 \mu r \sqrt{\frac{\log n}{n}} \leq 1$ ). moving on to the error term  $\widehat{p}_{i,j} - p_{i,j}^\star$ , we observe that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \widehat {\boldsymbol {p}} - \boldsymbol {p} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {\infty} = \left\| \boldsymbol {u} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\top} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star^ {\top}} \right\| _ {\infty} \\ \leq \left\| \left(\mathbf {u} \operatorname {s g n} (\mathbf {h}) - \mathbf {u} ^ {\star}\right) \mathbf {u} ^ {\star^ {\top}} \right\| _ {\infty} + \left\| \mathbf {u} \operatorname {s g n} (\mathbf {h}) \left(\mathbf {u} \operatorname {s g n} (\mathbf {h}) - \mathbf {u} ^ {\star}\right) ^ {\top} \right\| _ {\infty} \\ \leq \left\| \boldsymbol {u} \operatorname {s g n} (\boldsymbol {h}) - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {2, \infty} \left\{\left\| \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {2, \infty} + \left\| \boldsymbol {u} \operatorname {s g n} (\boldsymbol {h}) \right\| _ {2, \infty} \right\} \\ \leq \left\| \boldsymbol {u} \operatorname {s g n} (\boldsymbol {h}) - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {2, \infty} \left\{\left\| \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {2, \infty} + \left\| \boldsymbol {u} \right\| _ {2, \infty} \right\}. \\ \end{array}
$$

taking this together with the bound (4.31a) in theorem 4.2, the incoherence assumption, and the inequality (4.133), we arrive at

$$
\left\| \hat {\boldsymbol {p}} - \boldsymbol {p} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {\infty} \lesssim \frac {\left(\sigma \kappa \sqrt {\mu r} + \sigma \sqrt {r \log n}\right)}{\left| \lambda_ {r} ^ {\star} \right|} \sqrt {\frac {\mu r}{n}}. \tag {4.174}
$$

this taken together with (4.159) indicates that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \hat {\boldsymbol {p}} \right\| _ {\infty} \leq \left\| \boldsymbol {p} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {\infty} + \left\| \hat {\boldsymbol {p}} - \boldsymbol {p} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {\infty} \\ \lesssim \frac {\mu r}{n} + \frac {\left(\sigma \kappa \sqrt {\mu r} + \sigma \sqrt {r \log n}\right)}{\left| \lambda_ {r} ^ {\star} \right|} \sqrt {\frac {\mu r}{n}} \asymp \frac {\mu r}{n}, \tag {4.175} \\ \end{array}
$$

with the proviso that  $\sigma \sqrt{n} \lesssim |\lambda_r^\star| / \kappa$  and  $\sigma \sqrt{n \log n} \lesssim |\lambda_r^\star|$ .

armed with the preceding bounds, we are now positioned to control  $\widehat{v}_{i,j} - \widetilde{v}_{i,j}$ . from the definition of  $\widetilde{v}_{i,j}$  and  $v_{i,j}^{\star}$ , we recognize that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left| \widehat {v} _ {i, j} - \widetilde {v} _ {i, j} \right| \leq \underbrace {\left| \sum_ {l = 1} ^ {n} \left(\widehat {e} _ {i , l} ^ {2} \widehat {p} _ {l , j} ^ {2} - e _ {i , l} ^ {2} p _ {l , j} ^ {* 2}\right) \right|} _ {=: \alpha_ {1}} + \underbrace {\left| \sum_ {l = 1} ^ {n} \left(\widehat {p} _ {i , l} ^ {2} \widehat {e} _ {l , j} ^ {2} - p _ {i , l} ^ {* 2} e _ {l , j} ^ {2}\right) \right|} _ {=: \alpha_ {2}} \\ + 2 \underbrace {\left| \widehat {e} _ {i , j} ^ {2} \widehat {p} _ {i , i} \widehat {p} _ {j , j} - e _ {i , j} ^ {2} p _ {i , i} ^ {\star} p _ {j , j} ^ {\star} \right|} _ {=: \alpha_ {3}}, \tag {4.176} \\ \end{array}
$$

leaving us with three terms to cope with. regarding the first term  $\alpha_{1}$  on the right-hand side of (4.176), it can be easily verified that

$$
\alpha_ {1} \leq \left| \sum_ {l = 1} ^ {n} \left(\widehat {e} _ {i, l} ^ {2} \widehat {p} _ {l, j} ^ {2} - e _ {i, l} ^ {2} \widehat {p} _ {l, j} ^ {2}\right) \right| + \left| \sum_ {l = 1} ^ {n} \left(e _ {i, l} ^ {2} \widehat {p} _ {l, j} ^ {2} - e _ {i, l} ^ {2} p _ {l, j} ^ {\star 2}\right) \right|
$$

$$
\leq \left(\max _ {l} \left| \widehat {e} _ {i, l} ^ {2} - e _ {i, l} ^ {2} \right|\right) \sum_ {l = 1} ^ {n} \widehat {p} _ {l, j} ^ {2} + \left(\max _ {l} \left| \widehat {p} _ {l, j} ^ {2} - p _ {l, j} ^ {\star 2} \right|\right) \sum_ {l = 1} ^ {n} e _ {i, l} ^ {2}
$$

$$
\begin{array}{l} \stackrel {\mathrm {(i)}} {\leq} \left(\| \boldsymbol {e} \| _ {\infty} + \| \widehat {\boldsymbol {e}} \| _ {\infty}\right) \left\| \widehat {\boldsymbol {e}} - \boldsymbol {e} \right\| _ {\infty} \widehat {p} _ {j, j} \\ + \left(\| \boldsymbol {p} ^ {\star} \| _ {\infty} + \| \widehat {\boldsymbol {p}} \| _ {\infty}\right) \left\| \widehat {\boldsymbol {p}} - \boldsymbol {p} ^ {\star} \right\| _ {\infty} \| \boldsymbol {e} \| ^ {2} \\ \end{array}
$$

$$
\stackrel {\mathrm {(i i)}} {\lesssim} b \sigma \kappa^ {2} \mu r \sqrt {\frac {\log n}{n}} \cdot \frac {\mu r}{n} + \frac {\mu r}{n} \cdot \frac {(\sigma \kappa \sqrt {\mu r} + \sigma \sqrt {r \log n})}{| \lambda_ {r} ^ {\star} |} \sqrt {\frac {\mu r}{n}} \cdot \sigma^ {2} n.
$$

here, (i) makes use of (4.158) (with  $p^{\star}$  replaced by  $\widehat{p}$ ), whereas (ii) holds true due to (4.172), (4.173), (4.159), (4.174), (4.175), and lemma 4.13. the second term  $\alpha_{2}$  on the right-hand side of

(4.176) can be bounded in the same manner and we omit it here for brevity. when it comes to the last term  $\alpha_{3}$  on the right-hand side of (4.176), one has

$$
\begin{array}{l} \alpha_ {3} \leq \left| \widehat {e} _ {i, j} ^ {2} - e _ {i, j} ^ {2} \right| \left| \widehat {p} _ {i, i} \widehat {p} _ {j, j} \right| + e _ {i, j} ^ {2} \left| \widehat {p} _ {i, i} \widehat {p} _ {j, j} - p _ {i, i} ^ {\star} p _ {j, j} ^ {\star} \right| \\ \lesssim \| \widehat {\pmb {e}} - \pmb {e} \| _ {\infty} \left\{\| \pmb {e} \| _ {\infty} + \| \widehat {\pmb {e}} \| _ {\infty} \right\} \| \widehat {\pmb {p}} \| _ {\infty} ^ {2} \\ + b ^ {2} \| \hat {\boldsymbol {p}} - \boldsymbol {p} ^ {\star} \| _ {\infty} \left\{\| \boldsymbol {p} ^ {\star} \| _ {\infty} + \| \hat {\boldsymbol {p}} \| _ {\infty} \right\} \\ \lesssim \sigma \kappa^ {2} \mu r \sqrt {\frac {\log n}{n}} \cdot b \cdot \left(\frac {\mu r}{n}\right) ^ {2} + b ^ {2} \cdot \frac {(\sigma \kappa \sqrt {\mu r} + \sigma \sqrt {r \log n})}{| \lambda_ {r} ^ {\star} |} \sqrt {\frac {\mu r}{n}} \cdot \frac {\mu r}{n} \\ \lesssim b \sigma \kappa^ {2} \mu r \sqrt {\frac {\log n}{n}} \cdot \frac {\mu r}{n} + \frac {\mu r}{n} \cdot \frac {(\sigma \kappa \sqrt {\mu r} + \sigma \sqrt {r \log n})}{| \lambda_ {r} ^ {\star} |} \sqrt {\frac {\mu r}{n}} \cdot \sigma^ {2} n, \\ \end{array}
$$

where the penultimate inequality is a consequence of (4.172), (4.173), (4.159), (4.174) and (4.175), and the last line holds true as long as  $\mu r \leq n$  (cf. (4.25)) and  $b \lesssim \sigma \sqrt{n}$ . combining the above inequalities allows one to reach

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left| \hat {v} _ {i, j} - \tilde {v} _ {i, j} \right| \leq \alpha_ {1} + \alpha_ {2} + \alpha_ {3} \\ \lesssim b \sigma \kappa^ {2} \mu r \sqrt {\frac {\log n}{n}} \cdot \frac {\mu r}{n} + \frac {\mu r}{n} \cdot \frac {\sigma \kappa \sqrt {\mu r} + \sigma \sqrt {r \log n}}{| \lambda_ {r} ^ {\star} |} \sqrt {\frac {\mu r}{n}} \cdot \sigma^ {2} n \\ \succsim \frac {b \sigma \kappa^ {2} \mu^ {2} r ^ {2} \sqrt {\log n}}{n ^ {3 / 2}} + \frac {\sigma^ {3} \kappa \mu^ {2} r ^ {2} + \sigma^ {3} \mu^ {3 / 2} r ^ {2} \sqrt {\log n}}{| \lambda_ {r} ^ {\star} | \sqrt {n}} \\ \end{array}
$$

with probability at least  $1 - o(n^{-5})$ .

# step 3: combining the above bounds

putting together the results in the previous steps, we can readily derive

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left| \widehat {v} _ {i, j} - v _ {i, j} ^ {\star} \right| \leq \left| \widetilde {v} _ {i, j} - v _ {i, j} ^ {\star} \right| + \left| \widehat {v} _ {i, j} - \widetilde {v} _ {i, j} \right| \\ \lesssim \left(\frac {\sigma b \mu^ {3 / 2} r ^ {3 / 2} \sqrt {\log n}}{n ^ {3 / 2}} + \frac {\mu^ {2} r ^ {2} b ^ {2} \log n}{n ^ {2}}\right) \\ + \left(\frac {b \sigma \kappa^ {2} \mu^ {2} r ^ {2} \sqrt {\log n}}{n ^ {3 / 2}} + \frac {\sigma^ {3} \kappa \mu^ {2} r ^ {2} + \sigma^ {3} \mu^ {3 / 2} r ^ {2} \sqrt {\log n}}{| \lambda_ {r} ^ {\star} | \sqrt {n}}\right) \\ \asymp \frac {b \sigma \kappa^ {2} \mu^ {2} r ^ {2} \sqrt {\log n}}{n ^ {3 / 2}} + \frac {\sigma^ {3} \kappa \mu^ {2} r ^ {2} \sqrt {\log n}}{\left| \lambda_ {r} ^ {\star} \right| \sqrt {n}}, \tag {4.177} \\ \end{array}
$$

where the last relation is guaranteed as long as  $b \lesssim \sigma \sqrt{n / \log n}$ . consequently, if condition (4.167) holds, then it follows from (4.177) and the lower bound (4.162) that

$$
| \widehat {v} _ {i, j} - v _ {i, j} ^ {\star} | \lesssim \frac {1}{\sqrt {\log n}} v _ {i, j} ^ {\star},
$$

thus concluding the proof.

# 4.13 notes

leave-one-out analysis. the core idea of leave-one-out analysis, which drops a small amount of randomness to decouple complicated statistical dependency, is deeply rooted in the probability and

statistics literature. for instance, an idea of this kind was invoked by stein (1972) to help establish normal approximation, was paired with the stieltjes transform to establish the limiting spectral law of random matrices (see, e.g., (tao, 2012, section 2.4.3)), and bears some resemblance to the cavity method in statistical physics (mezard and montanari, 2009). when it comes to statistical estimation, a prominent series of work that unveiled the striking effectiveness of leave-one-out analysis was el karoui et al. (2013) and el karoui (2018), which characterized rigorously the sharp statistical performance (including pre-constants) of m-estimators in high dimension (i.e., a challenging regime where the number of samples is comparable to the number of unknown parameters). the deep analysis framework developed in these papers inspired much of the follow-up work presented in this chapter. particularly worth mentioning are: (1) zhong and boumal (2018): which was the first to determine the entrywise behavior of the generalized projected power method; (2) abbe et al. (2020b) and chen et al. (2019b): which extended the leave-one-out analysis idea to establish entrywise eigenvector perturbation; and (3) ma et al. (2020) and chen et al. (2019a): which characterized tight convergence guarantees for nonconvex optimization algorithms with the aid of leave-one-out ideas. for readers' reference, we list below several topics for which leave-one-out analyses prove useful:

- maximum likelihood estimation and m-estimation: el karoui et al. (2013), el karoui (2018), lei et al. (2018), sur et al. (2019), sur and candès (2019), chen et al. (2019b), and chen et al. (2020b);  
- spectral methods: chen et al. (2019b), abbe et al. (2020b), ma et al. (2020), cai et al. (2021a), lei (2019), abbe et al. (2020a), ling (2020), and chen et al. (2020b);  
- nonconvex optimization for statistical estimation: ma et al. (2020), chen et al. (2019a), li et al. (2021c), chen et al. (2020a), cai et al. (2019a), dong and shi (2018), chen et al. (2021d), and wang et al. (2021b);  
- semidefinite relaxation for low-rank matrix factorization: zhong and boumal (2018), ding and chen (2020), chen et al. (2020d), chen et al. (2021c), and chen et al. (2021d);  
- uncertainty quantification and confidence intervals: javanmard and montanari (2018), chen et al. (2019c), cai et al. (2020), and yan et al. (2021);  
cross validation: xu et al. (2019);  
- reinforcement learning: agarwal et al. (2020), li et al. (2020a), pananjady and wainwright (2020), zhang et al. (2020b), cui and yang (2021), and wang et al. (2021a).

$\ell_{\infty}$  and  $\ell_{2,\infty}$  perturbation theory. the  $\ell_{\infty}$  and  $\ell_{2,\infty}$  perturbation theory for eigenspace and singular subspaces have been investigated in the literature (fan et al., 2018b; cape et al., 2019; eldridge et al., 2018), but only scatteredly until very recently. a modern and systematic framework was established recently, empowered by the leave-one-out analysis idea. its efficacy and tightness were first demonstrated by abbe et al. (2020b) in a setting that subsumes the one presented herein (with applications to sbms, phase synchronization and matrix completion), and by chen et al. (2019b) in an asymmetric setting (with application to top- $k$  ranking). the theoretical framework has subsequently been extended in several aspects. for instance, (1) cai et al. (2021a) investigated

the "unbalanced" scenario where the column dimension far exceeds the row dimension of the matrix, resulting in near-optimal fine-grained guarantees for pca, bi-clustering and tensor completion; (2) lei (2019) expanded the setting by accounting for more flexible noise distributions (including the ones exhibiting certain dependency structure), leading to tight guarantees for, e.g., spectral clustering with more than two communities, and hierarchical clustering; (3) abbe et al. (2020a) explored a more general  $\ell_p$  perturbation theory that subsumes the  $\ell_{\infty}$  perturbation theory as special cases. another plausible approach to study  $\ell_{\infty}$  eigenvector perturbation is to analyze instead the dynamics of an iterative procedure (e.g., the power method) that converges to the leading eigenvector (zhong and boumal, 2018), again using the leave-one-out ideas. this iterative approach offers a perspective complementary to the analysis framework presented herein, while at the same time playing a pivotal role when studying nonconvex optimization algorithms (see chi et al. (2019)). moving beyond the leave-one-out analysis framework,  $\ell_{\infty}$  and  $\ell_{2,\infty}$  eigenspace perturbation theory has been derived via other powerful tools as well, e.g., the neumann trick (eldridge et al., 2018; chen et al., 2021b; cheng et al., 2021), the procrustes analysis (cape et al., 2019), and more specialized techniques tailored to gaussian ensembles (koltchinskii and xia, 2016; koltchinskii and lounici, 2016; koltchinskii et al., 2020). perturbations of linear forms and bilinear forms of eigenvectors have also been investigated in the literature (koltchinskii and xia, 2016; koltchinskii and lounici, 2016; chen et al., 2021b; cheng et al., 2021; fan et al., 2020a; koltchinskii et al., 2020), which are beyond the scope of this work. finally, distributional theory and uncertainty quantification for spectral methods, which have been recently studied by xia (2021), cheng et al. (2021), fan et al. (2020a), yan et al. (2021), and agterberg et al. (2021), are still in their infancy. the results presented in section 4.6 follow the analysis framework developed in yan et al. (2021).

this monograph offered a coherent statistical treatment for spectral methods, resulting in appealing theoretical guarantees for a wide spectrum of data science applications ranging from structured signal reconstruction and factor analysis to clustering and ranking. the important role of statistical thinking cannot be overstated. as has been illuminated, the suite of modern statistical techniques not merely empowers classical  $\ell_2$  perturbation theory by delivering tight euclidean error bounds, but also enables fine-grained  $\ell_{\infty}$  and  $\ell_{2,\infty}$  performance guarantees that cannot be derived from classical matrix perturbation theory alone. we highlighted a unified recipe that underlies our application-driven analyses, which will be readily applicable to tackle many other problems.

the vignettes presented herein only reflect the tip of an iceberg regarding the capability of spectral methods. there are multiple aspects about spectral methods that remain inadequately explored and are worthy of future investigation. we conclude this monograph by pointing out a few of them.

- precise performance characterization. the analysis herein falls short of pinpointing a precise trade-off curve between the statistical accuracy and sample complexity of spectral methods, and might even be off by some logarithmic factor. for algorithms that exhibit order-wise equivalent behavior, comparing their performances requires finer statistical characterization, ideally with sharp pre-constants.  
- handling dependency structure. thus far, the  $\ell_{\infty}$  and  $\ell_{2,\infty}$  perturbation theory we have presented is restricted to the case where the entries of the data samples are independently generated. there is no shortage of applications where the data samples might exhibit across-entry dependency, examples including blind deconvolution (ahmed et al., 2013) and phase retrieval with coded diffraction patterns (candes et al., 2015a; gross et al., 2017). handling such scenarios might require ideas beyond the current leave-one-out framework.  
- functional estimation. in many decision making applications, what ultimately matters might not be full information about an eigenvector of a matrix, but rather, some deterministic functions (e.g., certain linear functionals or polynomials) about the entries of this eigenvector. however, naive "plug-in" estimators—namely, estimating the eigenvector first and plugging it

into the target functional—might suffer from significant estimation bias, even in the case of a linear functional. a systematic bias-correction paradigm is therefore needed to enable optimal functional estimation.

- small eigengaps. all theory presented in this monograph imposes a stringent requirement on the associated eigengap, that is, it needs to exceed the spectral norm of a noise or perturbation matrix. while this eigengap criterion might be unavoidable in generic matrix perturbation theory (which takes a worst-case perspective), there is often no statistical lower bound that rules out the possibility of reliable eigenspace estimation when the eigengap drops below the perturbation size. it would be of fundamental importance to understand how a small eigengap impacts the efficacy of spectral methods under various statistical models.  
- weak and sparse factors. as mentioned previously, low-rank matrices often admit factor-model interpretations. in many applications, one has to deal with weak factors, on which only a small fraction of the variables have non-negligible loadings. this gives rise to sparse patterns on the loading matrix or the eigenvectors of the covariance matrix. to utilize such a sparsity structure, a simple method is to apply marginal screening techniques (fan and lv, 2008; fan and fan, 2008). examples of this kind include supervised pca (bair et al., 2006), pca on "targeted predictors" (bai and ng, 2008), and sparse pca (zou et al., 2006; johnstone and lu, 2009; ma, 2013). it remains to develop a more systematic and unified theory concerning how to efficiently exploit such special structures in low-rank factorizations, taking into account both statistical and computational considerations.  
- heterogeneous missing patterns. when it comes to missing data, the theory presented herein adopts a uniform sampling model where every entry is independently observed with the same probability. in practice, however, one might encounter non-uniform sampling mechanisms, where the sampling probabilities are non-identical across different entries. how to develop an effective spectral method to automatically account for heterogeneous observation patterns, ideally without knowing the detailed sampling probabilities a priori?  
- confidence regions and hypothesis testing for individual eigenvectors. given the output of a spectral method, one might be asked to produce valid confidence regions for an unknown individual eigenvector of interest, a task that has not been fully resolved by the existing literature. another closely related task is hypothesis testing for individual eigenvectors: given two random samples, how to develop viable statistical tests regarding whether these two samples are associated with the same individual eigenvectors or not. an even more challenging task is concerned with performing efficient statistical inference on some deterministic functions of an individual eigenvector, which remains largely unknown.
